# # through C

## 1984 and Philosophy - Is Resistance Futile

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "A" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "i" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Thanks" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "Are We Living in 1984" **·** Utopia and Dystopia — The editors answer Asimov's and Posner's dismissals of 1984 as obsolete by pointing to the Iraq War's 935 false statements, Snowden's NSA surveillance disclosures, weaponized social media, and Kellyanne Conway's 'alternative facts' as evidence that we may live in Orwell's world in 2018. They argue technology is not the antidote to Orwellian concerns, despite Apple's famous '1984 won't be like Nineteen Eighty-Four' ad, since telescreen-like smartphones and the Party's history-rewriting bureaucracy are both forms of 'political technology'. The real antidote is philosophy, which trains us to examine assumptions and resist bad arguments, as Winston feared when he thought any Party intellectual could overthrow him in debate.
- ch. 10 "I War Is Peace" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "1. Revolutionary from the Waist Down" **·** Michel Foucault — Power/Knowledge — Storrie and Martin use Foucault's critique of the repressive hypothesis to argue that Winston is wrong to think sex is naturally subversive and the Party merely represses it. Power over sexuality is productive: the Soviet sexual revolution, Ceausescu's Decree 770 and its 'Decree Generation', Pornosec's pornography for pacifying proles, and tacitly tolerated prostitution all channel erotic energy into state power rather than simply forbid it. Reading O'Brien's torture of Winston as a sustained scene of rape and castration (following Cass Sunstein), they conclude that only understanding power's productive use of sex, as Julia does, makes effective resistance possible; it is Winston who turns out to be merely 'a revolutionary from the waist down'.
- ch. 12 "2. Physical Jerks Ungood" **·** Aristotle — Man as Political Animal; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Di Nucci compares the Party's mandatory morning Physical Jerks to the modern middle-class ritual of pre-work exercise, asking who needs an oppressor when we oppress ourselves. He argues exercise carries a hidden political price: the time, energy, and solitude it consumes (Orwell shows exercise making it harder for Winston to think and remember) leave citizens too exhausted for the political engagement Aristotle considered necessary to the good life. Like Juvenal's bread and circuses, now 'panem et iphones', and like private schooling, fitness culture also deepens class inequality because working-class people lack the time and energy to access it.
- ch. 13 "3. Why Don’t the Proles Just Take Over" **·** Ideology and False Consciousness; Class Struggle; Cultural Hegemony — Littmann asks why Oceania's proles, 85 percent of the population who could 'blow the Party to pieces tomorrow morning', never revolt, answering with Winston's diagnosis: until they become conscious they will never rebel. The Party keeps them unconscious through its media monopoly, crimestop, doublethink, reality control, perpetual war hysteria, and a nationalist identity that cuts them off from the international proletariat. He maps each mechanism onto the modern US, where six corporations control about 90 percent of media, citizens tailor their own reality bubbles, opponents are assumed to argue in bad faith, and partisan 'nationalisms' block common cause, arguing that American political conflict is mostly over facts rather than values.
- ch. 14 "4. Non-State Enemies of Freedom" **·** John Stuart Mill, Isaiah Berlin, Hannah Arendt, Karl Popper, Catharine MacKinnon — On Liberty; Two Concepts of Liberty; Totalitarianism; The Open Society — Nash argues 1984 has been misread as a critique of state power alone; through Newspeak's silencing of thought (in the tradition Austin, MacKinnon, and Langton developed) it shows how any concentration of power oppresses via control of words, data, and information. Drawing on Goldstein's manual's debt to James Burnham's managerial revolution and Sheldon Wolin's 'inverted totalitarianism', she reads the non-centralized Oceania as a warning about non-state enemies: corporations, think tanks running the 'Tobacco Strategy', and global institutions peddling misleading poverty statistics. Popper, Arendt, and Berlin exemplify the state-focused liberalism she thinks blinded us to these horizontal threats, while the Newspeak appendix's past tense shows Orwell expected totalitarianism to be beatable through evidence and democracy.
- ch. 15 "5. Ministry of Truth Handbook Excerpt on the Strategic Use of Fallacious Reasoning for Thoughtcrime Prevention" **·** Plato, Aristotle — The Noble Lie — Rard's satirical Party handbook explains how to keep a population from thinking: keep the proles too poor and exhausted to reason, and deploy fallacies strategically, from ad hominem goat-caricatures of Goldstein at the Two Minutes Hate and straw man versions of his arguments, to destroying contrary evidence, appealing to fear of the old capitalist world, and Room 101's appeal to force, so false beliefs can be manufactured as easily as true ones. It presents Plato's noble lie as the Ministry's founding inspiration, calling the reader's mission 'a continuation of Plato's dream'. It also cites Aristotle's view that emotion must be disciplined for reason to work, which the Party inverts by deliberately triggering fear and hatred to disable rational thought.
- ch. 16 "6. Big Brother Ltd" **·** John Locke, Adam Smith — Natural Rights — Botello-Samson and Mobley extend Fromm's point that 1984 indicts authoritarian power in general by reading Oceania alongside neoliberalism, which grew out of Locke's liberalism of natural rights and limited government yet demands activist state power to create the conditions for markets. Doublethink reappears as 'accountable independence' in administrative law (Chevron deference, and Garcetti v. Ceballos stripping whistleblower protection) and as Washington Consensus food policy, in which developing nations were pushed off agricultural self-sufficiency while the US kept its own subsidies. Both Ingsoc and neoliberal governance reduce everything to economic elements and use Newspeak-like buzzwords such as 'stakeholder' and 'customer-driven government' to hide the contradiction between democratic rhetoric and rule by experts and elites.
- ch. 17 "II Freedom Is Slavery" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "7. Big Brother, We’re Watching You!" **·** Judith Jarvis Thomson, Thomas Nagel, James Rachels, Karl Popper, Jeremy Bentham — The Open Society — Tännsjö argues mass surveillance is here to stay, and since absolute privacy is technically impossible while targeted surveillance only stigmatizes the dissidents who most need protection, we should embrace reciprocal mass surveillance: radical transparency in which citizens can 'watch Big Brother back', secret-service records are opened, and whistleblowers like Snowden and Manning are honored as heroes. On the way he dismantles the standard defenses of privacy, including the honor-based right to control one's image, moral rights (following Thomson's denial of a distinct right to privacy), and dignity-based autonomy arguments, while conceding through Nagel and Rachels what would be lost if inner thoughts became readable. Winston's opening wish to see Big Brother as Big Brother sees him becomes the chapter's model of an open surveillance society.
- ch. 19 "8. Human Enhancement for Freedom" **·** Peter Singer, Martha Nussbaum, Nick Bostrom, John Rawls, Aristotle — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Existential Risk; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Koï asks whether biomedical enhancement could make humans resistant to the totalitarian subjugation shown in 1984, where the Party keeps proles too poor and distracted to think and dims Outer Party minds with Newspeak, erased history, and propaganda. Testing Persson and Savulescu's program of enhancing intelligence and moral empathy, she finds intelligence enhancement an educated gamble, since the gifted are also the most likely to embrace extremism and to start totalitarian movements, and empathy politically selective: Nazis directed it only at the in-group and even invoked it to justify the 'euthanasia' murders, while Party propaganda exploits citizens' empathy for horror stories of pre-revolution child labor. Her conclusion is that whatever capacity we enhance, the oligarchy could turn it to its own use: none of us will ever be immune to the love of Big Brother.
- ch. 20 "9. 24 7 Newsleep" **·** Sigmund Freud, Aristotle — The Panopticon (surveillance); The Unconscious — Buchanan argues the Party 'sleep-hacks' its citizens because sleep is an active state of thought, a lineage he traces from Aristotle's theory of dreaming through Freud's Interpretation of Dreams, and is therefore a site of potential resistance that must be colonized. Between the Physical Jerks, telescreen trumpet-calls, a Thought Police able to inspect anyone 'asleep or awake' without warning, and O'Brien's manipulation of Winston's sleep schedule during torture, Winston is trapped in 'Newsleep', a derealized blur of dream and reality that makes doublethink the only available way of thinking. He extends the diagnosis to our own 24/7 economy of phones and Trump's all-hours tweets, concluding that Orwell's warning is that we must guard our sleep.
- ch. 21 "10. Love, Truluv" **·** Hannah Arendt, Niccolò Machiavelli, Aristotle, Plato — Totalitarianism; Philosophy of Love — Sandefur argues that what makes totalitarianism unique is its demand that subjects love their ruler, and that this project is doomed because love is selective, unequal, internal, and inalienable, invoking Aristotle's critique of Plato's community of wives and children, Machiavelli on whether rulers should be loved or feared, and Arendt on conscience as internal dialogue and totalitarianism as total rule. Winston and Julia's sex is a political act asserting the individual against the collective, while the Two Minutes Hate channels repressed erotic energy into sanctioned ecstasy directed at Big Brother, as at Khomeini's funeral. What finally breaks Winston in Room 101 is not torture as such but his surrender of Julia, the one thing that was truly his, which collapses the distinction between love and rape.
- ch. 22 "11. When Cruelty Is Not Enough" **·** Richard Rorty, Hannah Arendt, Theodor Adorno, Friedrich Nietzsche, Sigmund Freud — The Banality of Evil; Civilization and Its Discontents — Conway argues the real horror of 1984 is not the torture chamber but O'Brien's worldmaking: he cultivated Winston and Julia's affair from lust into a hopeful little home precisely so that destroying it, and hope itself, the Party's true enemy, would be total. Against Rorty's influential reading of O'Brien as a torturer who breaks Winston for pleasure, Conway adds two amendments: Arendt's thoughtless technocrat, with O'Brien as an Eichmann figure whose Party affirms his competence and insulates him from rebuke, and Horkheimer and Adorno's warning that technical rationality has become a secular religion whose 'priests of power' sanctify cruelty. The result is a portrait of a warm-blooded, situationally sympathetic sadist who genuinely excels at building the selves and worlds he is then charged to dismantle.
- ch. 23 "12. No Crack in the Wall" **·** Theodor Adorno — Utopia and Dystopia — Silberbusch defends 1984's bleakness with Adorno, for whom 'only exaggeration is true' and unreduced awareness of the worst torture is the first step toward resistance, so that the dystopia negatively preserves the utopia it forbids. She then finds cracks in the wall: Julia's spontaneous 'I love you', the ring-doves and bluebells of the Golden Country, and the 'mute protest in your bones' are the unassimilable other that the Party's megalomaniac calculating rationality, its identity thinking taken to totality, cannot control. Against O'Brien's claim that 'we create human nature', she cites Primo Levi's Muselmänner to argue that humanity survived even Auschwitz, so Big Brother has not truly won.
- ch. 24 "13. Hangings, Shootings, and Other Funny Stuff in 1984" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Henri Bergson, Plato — Laughter (the comic as mechanical encrusted on the living) — Hietalahti combines Kierkegaard's thesis that the comic lies in contradiction, Plato's warning that laughter is malicious and must be chained by law, and Bergson's account of laughter as social punishment to explain Oceania's humor: the cinema audience laughs at refugees being machine-gunned, citizens clamor for public hangings, and the barroom joke about living in treetops humiliates the old man, because shared fear and hatred set the norms of what counts as funny. Doublethink explains why slogans like 'War is peace' provoke no laughter in Oceania, and the Ministry of Truth's amusement industry shows the Party deliberately weaponizes fun. The upshot is reflexive: Orwell indicts our own unexamined sense of humor, including the slogan that everything may be ridiculed, not merely Oceania's.
- ch. 25 "14. The Seduction of Winston Smith" **·** Michel Foucault — The Panopticon (surveillance); Power/Knowledge — Mark Alfano reads the novel's ending ('He loved Big Brother') as a seduction story: following John Forrester, a seducer opens with 'I know what you're thinking', which O'Brien enacts when he voices Winston's unspoken thought under torture and demands not mere obedience but enthusiastic consent. Big Brother wins Winston over through four epistemic methods: total surveillance (telescreens, Thought Police, Parsons's own children), the Ministry of Truth's annihilation of records like the photograph of Jones, Aaronson and Rutherford, the destruction of self-trust in the 'how many fingers?' sessions, and the isolation of intimates until Winston betrays Julia. Alfano closes by mapping all four methods onto post-9/11 mass surveillance and Trump-era attacks on records, perception, and the press.
- ch. 26 "15. Happy in Oceania" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Dewey, Viktor Frankl, Epicurus — Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); Man's Search for Meaning (logotherapy); The Four Noble Truths; The Unexamined Life — Ćirić and Ćurko ask what philosophy could have done for Winston, imagining a counterfactual Oceania where he reads Walpole's line that the world is 'a comedy to those that think, a tragedy to those that feel' and learns from Julia that another worldview is possible. They present philosophy as therapy: Kant's notion of worldview (Weltanschauung), Epicurean ataraxia, the Buddhist four noble truths, Frankl's logotherapy, and Stoic premeditation of the worst as resources for reworking the beliefs that made Winston live in fear. The Party's true target, they argue, is critical thinking — from Dewey's ideal of education as learning to think to Frankfurt's On Bullshit — and Kant's Enlightenment motto 'have the courage to use your own understanding' is the weapon that could still defeat Ingsoc.
- ch. 27 "III Ignorance Is Strength" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "16. Bad Faith and Make-Believe" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, René Descartes — Bad Faith; Methodological Doubt (evil demon) — Iskra Fileva asks how a rational person can be brought to believe that two plus two equals five, arguing that Orwell's psychology of belief is realistic rather than exaggerated. Fear, Newspeak and crimestop get citizens to deliberately blunt their own rationality, and 'sanity is statistical' peer pressure — modeled on the Asch conformity experiments and on Descartes' methodological evil-demon doubt turned psychological — makes doublethink, consciously held contradiction, possible; Winston's hope about Goldstein is analyzed with Sartre's idea that self-deception is something we do. O'Brien's zeal to convert heretics before killing them betrays the Party's own nagging self-doubt, which is Fileva's ground for hope that truth keeps reasserting itself.
- ch. 29 "17. Through a Telescreen Darkly" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Plato, Bertrand Russell, Augustine of Hippo — Language Games — Lavinia Marin uses Wittgenstein's 'meaning is use' and forms of life to argue that Newspeak cannot win by dictionary design alone: the Party must freeze a single form of life, which requires erasing history and memory. Recalling Russell's failed project of taming language with logic, Augustine's picture of words as names, and Plato's ancient distrust of writing, and drawing on Vilém Flusser's account of how linear writing creates logical thought, objectivity and history, she argues the telescreens push Oceania back into orality — chant, duckspeak, blind faith — where analysis is impossible. Winston's notebook is the counter-apparatus: writing anchors him in a linear, record-keeping relation to the past, which is why blank paper terrifies the Party.
- ch. 30 "18. Thoughtcrime or Feelingcrime" **·** Harry Frankfurt, Aristotle, John Locke — Second-Order Desires (personhood); Personal Identity; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Alba Montes Sánchez argues that the real crime in Oceania is not thoughtcrime but 'feelingcrime': Winston lands in the Ministry of Love only once he falls in love, because beliefs alone do not motivate action while emotions and loves do — the problem of akrasia that puzzled Aristotle, since Winston carries his proof of Party lies for eleven years without acting. Using Harry Frankfurt's account of love as an involuntary, non-utilitarian, rigidly focused, self-affirming concern that organizes a person's priorities, she explains why Julia is irreplaceable and why Room 101 must destroy Winston's deepest allegiance rather than just his opinions. Surveying theories of individuality from Locke's memory to recognition and narrative, she concludes that a stubborn residue of selfhood survives in what we love — which is why Ingsoc must make war on love itself.
- ch. 31 "19. Nineteen Eighty-Four’s Religion" **·** Karl Marx — The Death of God; Ideology and False Consciousness; Utopia and Dystopia — Crossley and Markou trace Orwell's ambivalence about religion — a lifelong atheist who loved the Church of England's liturgy — into the novel: Goldstein's book repeats the socialist critique of priests who 'softened by promises of compensation in an imaginary world beyond the grave' propped up hierarchy, yet the Party then occupies religion's vacant place itself, with O'Brien as 'priest of power', Goldstein as arch-heretic, and 'Thou art' replacing the old despotisms' 'Thou shalt not'. They argue Orwell thought a purer socialism needed the communal goods of religion, from the proles' fuzzy 'Christianness' to the radical Christian tradition of the Diggers and Levellers, and read Ingsoc as that utopian tradition betrayed. The novel's warning is therefore double: what fills the gap when religion dies, and how religion itself can be a blueprint for totalitarianism.
- ch. 32 "20. Wheat Can Become Rye!" **·** Karl Marx, Thomas Kuhn — Ideology and False Consciousness — William Goodwin reads Syme as Orwell's portrait of the scientist who serves totalitarianism: the genuine intellectual integrity that Newspeak work requires — consciousness of what the language is for — is exactly why he must be vaporized, since 'orthodoxy is unconsciousness' and totalitarianism can tolerate only pseudo-inquiry, never real inquiry. The chapter sets this beside the Lysenko affair, where dialectical materialism and Stalin's favor overrode Mendelian genetics ('wheat can become rye') and purged Soviet biologists, and beside J.D. Bernal, whose scientism, vague Marxist euphemisms and proto-Newspeak made him a real-world Syme defending Lysenko and Stalin against his own interests. Goodwin extends the lesson to today's 'fake news' and 'junk science' politics: once ideology replaces inquiry with self-deception, the destruction of objective truth that Orwell feared becomes complete.
- ch. 33 "21. Controlling Thought through Tweets" **·** John Searle — Edoardo Pérez reads 1984 through speech act theory — Austin's and Searle's distinction between locution, illocution and perlocution — and theories of meaning: the Party's slogans ('War is Peace') perform rather than describe, and Syme's Newspeak, like Twitter's character limits, emojis and slang, narrows the range of thinkable locutions until only conditioned reactions remain, as in the Two Minutes Hate or the viral retweet. He borrows Jay Rosen's notion of 'mindcasting' — broadcasting assertions whose effect, not meaning, does the work — to show that Julia's 'I love you' note compels Winston's belief regardless of what 'love' means, just as O'Brien's torture reshapes Winston until he genuinely believes he loves Big Brother. Twitter bots and engineered disinformation, Pérez concludes, may make the feed a more efficient Ministry of Truth than the telescreen ever was.
- ch. 34 "22. The Irrelevance of Truth" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Martin Heidegger, Richard Rorty, Immanuel Kant — Perspectivism; The Question Concerning Technology; Will to Power — Jan Kyrre Berg Friis argues, with Nietzsche, that truth is a 'mobile army of metaphors', a relational event between people rather than an objective correspondence, and that the will to truth is subordinate to the will to power — the difference being that Trump-style 'alternative truth' is dishonest domination while Nietzsche's askesis is self-transformation. He layers in the Buddhist and Zen (Dogen) diagnosis of perception as conceptual distortion, Descartes' ego cogito as the fatal Western turn, and Heidegger's warning that calculative, technological thinking covers over our tacit lived background, producing exactly the one-dimensional minds a Ministry of Truth can feed. Rorty's advice to drop the truth-talk, Kant's insight that reality is sifted through the perceiver, and the cognitive-science of confirmation bias explain why facts no longer bite in the post-truth newsfeed.
- ch. 35 "23. oldthinkful duckspeak refs opposites rewrite fullwise upsub antefiling" **·** Aristotle, Plato, Avicenna (Ibn Sina) — Keith Begley gives a technical reading of Newspeak's grammar: Syme's 'ungood' for 'bad' destroys gradable opposites like hot/cold and good/bad by converting contrariety into plain contradiction, so that Newspeak could only articulate false dichotomies such as Bush's 'with us or with the enemy', while 'duckspeak' and 'blackwhite' behave like Oldspeak auto-antonyms whose ambiguity lets the Party reinterpret doctrine at will. The heart of blackwhite, he argues, is trained denial of the principle of non-contradiction — formulated by Plato in Republic IV and defended by Aristotle in the Metaphysics as a principle of the world itself — and Winston's 'four, five, six' under torture shows the resulting 'controlled insanity'. Against the Party he sets Avicenna's point that whoever denies the principle can be corrected by torture, which is precisely O'Brien's method, and ends by noting philosophers are the true Thought Police of non-contradiction.
- ch. 36 "IV Epilogue" *(front)*
- ch. 37 "24. Post-Factual Democracy" **·** Hannah Arendt, Jürgen Habermas, Niccolò Machiavelli — Totalitarianism; The Public Sphere; Democracy — Hendricks and Vestergaard open with Trump's inauguration lies and Kellyanne Conway's 'alternative facts' to define post-factual democracy: a state in which opportune political narratives replace facts and evidence as the basis of debate, opinion formation and policymaking. Unlike Machiavelli, who taught that lies must at least be concealed, the post-factual politician pays no price for exposed falsehood once social media, the attention economy and populist us-versus-them narratives engineered for indignation and fear have stripped journalists and fact-checkers of authority — Arendt's remark that mass leaders show 'extreme contempt for facts as such' before fitting reality to their lies. Using Habermas's deliberative model and even minimal accountability theories of voting, they argue post-factuality destroys democratic legitimacy, and defend a 'factual democracy' that trusts science and journalism without becoming a technocracy — the real-world echo of the Party's command to reject the evidence of your eyes.
- ch. 38 "Bibliography" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "Unpersons" *(notes)*
- ch. 40 "Destroy These Words" *(notes)*

## 30 Rock and Philosophy - We Want to Go to There

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "30 Rock and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "The Blackwell Philosophy and Pop Culture Series" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Copyright Page" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Acknowledgments" *(notes)*
- ch. 8 "Introduction" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Editor J. Jeremy Wisnewski argues that Plato, who used the popular dialogue form to draw people into philosophy and filled his dialogues with jokes about self-importance and incompetence, would have been a television writer today. He claims 30 Rock is a modern analogue: a show that imitates a show, just as Plato's Republic is a written speech reporting a fictional dialogue, an imitation twice removed from living philosophical exchange. Invoking Aristotle's claim that philosophy begins in wonder, the introduction frames the book as answers to the questions the TGS writers' room keeps raising about race, the good life, friendship, and knowledge.
- ch. 9 "PART ONE - WHAT WOULD OPRAH DO ETHICS AND THE GOOD LIFE AT 30 ROCK" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Olympic Tetherball A Final Lesson on Moral Frailty" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle — Deontology; Practical Wisdom (phronesis); Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — The chapter asks whether Kenneth the page is a Kantian rule absolutist who never makes exceptions, or an Aristotelian 'contextual absolutist' who sees that the right act depends on circumstances, as when he runs the Hill Witch ruse to scare Tracy into eating well. Jack's lifeboat hypothetical and rigged-elevator test bounce off Kenneth's refusal to believe in hypothetical situations, but Jack's gift of illegal cable exposes Kenneth's moral frailty when he enjoys the stolen channels. It concludes that Kenneth's gullibility, trust, and loyalty make his simple moral vision both admirable and easily corrupted, and that the real lesson is facing our failures with resolve rather than shame.
- ch. 11 "When Life Gives You Liz’s Lemons, Can You Make Lemonade" **·** Karl Marx, Theodor Adorno, Sigmund Freud — Alienation; Ideology and False Consciousness — Hinzmann reads Liz Lemon's workaholism through Marx and his successors: she gives NBC-Universal-Shinehardt her best years, but her alienation is social and existential rather than the classic alienated labor of the factory, since her creative job leaves her unable to connect with people or to choose love, as with Dennis and Floyd. Drawing on Adorno and the Frankfurt School, the chapter argues Liz cannot see her job as the problem because capitalist ideologies like meritocracy and free will make self-blame feel natural, and the 'Jackie Jormp-Jomp' fight-club episode shows even a life of leisure turns miserable without purpose. Freud's dictum that happiness requires work and love frames the diagnosis: Liz's work gives her life meaning precisely by shutting out everything else that could.
- ch. 12 "Aristotle to the Rescue" **·** Aristotle — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Virtue Ethics (arete); Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Yim applies Aristotle's three kinds of friendship from the Nicomachean Ethics to the show: Tracy with Grizz and Dot Com in 'Hardball', and Tracy with Jenna in 'Flu Shot', are friendships of utility that dissolve when the benefit ends, while Liz and Jenna's long shared history looks deep but remains mutual usefulness between ambitious colleagues. Liz and Jack, by contrast, evolve from David-versus-Goliath adversaries in the pilot into something like virtuous friendship, culminating in 'Retreat to Move Forward' when Liz humbles herself with a terrible improv routine and a shirt-ripping dance to save Jack's standing before the Six Sigma crowd. The chapter argues the show thereby dramatizes that self-sacrificial goodwill is what makes a friendship worth the risk.
- ch. 13 "“Well, It Got Big Laughs”" **·** Confucius, Aristotle — The Golden Mean; Ren (benevolence/humaneness); Practical Wisdom (phronesis); Li (ritual propriety) — Barkman uses Confucius (ren, yi, li, and zheng ming, treating each person according to what he or she is) and Aristotle (eudaimonia, virtue as a habituated mean between extremes, practical wisdom) to indict 30 Rock for valorizing Jack's 'semi-virtuous path', the smug middle between best and worst announced in 'Do-Over'. He catalogues injustices the show laughs off: Liz's serial lies about her age, hobbies, and adoption; Jack's priority flu shots for the powerful in 'Flu Shot'; Tracy's insubordination about dogfighting in 'Rosemary's Baby'; and Kenneth's servility as everyone's punching bag. On liberality and piety, Jack's confession that 'capitalism is my religion' and Liz's 'I pretty well do whatever Oprah tells me' show the show settling for semi-virtue, which the chapter says even comedy must not endorse.
- ch. 14 "PART TWO - THE GIRLIE SHOW GENDER, RACE, AND SEXUAL ORIENTATION AT 30 ROCK" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "Tracy Plays the Race Card" **·** Kwame Anthony Appiah, Frantz Fanon — Black Skin, White Masks — Wisnewski argues races are socially real but biologically spurious, deploying Appiah's critique of racialism through Tracy's crisis over Dr. Spaceman's DNA results and his dream in which Dream Jefferson tells him 'It's not about who you were. It's about who you are right now.' Fanon's account of the sexual myth of black virility explains why Tracy performs the sex-addicted wildman stereotype while Harvard grad Toofer gets branded not 'really' black. Liz's accidental racism ('Bling bling, that is ghetto fabulous' to Angie, assuming Tracy cannot read) shows racist assumptions operating beneath conscious intent, and the 'Natural Order' equality experiment with Liz and Tracy shows that equal treatment cannot mean identical treatment: equality demands equal consideration of different interests.
- ch. 16 "Being Gay for Jamie (or, How to Rethink Sexuality)" **·** Sigmund Freud, Judith Butler — Heteronormativity — The chapter uses Devon Banks's failed Practicology conversion and Liz's blind date with Gretchen Thomas to argue sexual orientation is not chosen, then brings in Freud's view that infants are polymorphously perverse with culture training undirected desire, which explains Athenian pederasty, same-sex behavior in over 200 mammalian species, and Frank going 'gay for Jamie' in 'Cougars'. It argues homophobia rests on anchoring gender to sex under a normative heterosexuality, so Jack reads Liz's shoes, the Floyd bromance, and Devon's every remark as sexual signs. Against Liz's claim that being gay for just one person 'isn't a thing', the chapter contends our either/or homo/hetero categories arbitrarily essentialize orientation given the messy chromosomal and genital facts about biological sex.
- ch. 17 "Liz Meets the Elephant in the Room" **·** Aristotle, Jean-Paul Sartre — Existence Precedes Essence; Feminism; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Barkman reads 'Rosemary's Baby' as staging second-wave feminism in Rose Howard's comfort-sacrificing activism against third-wave feminism in Liz, who sells her dignity for the $10,000 Followship Award and likes comfort and the status quo. Sartre's existence precedes essence supplies third-wave feminism's non-essentialist claim that anyone can be a feminist in any manner, while an Aristotelian essentialist standard of human flourishing shows Liz's 'have it all' optimism, from the wedding dress bought in 'SeinfeldVision' to the adoption schemes of 'Do-Over', is a delusion because career and family each demand everything. Jack's confession that 'you have to choose' lands the chapter's verdict that the third-wave promise leaves women in Liz's predicament.
- ch. 18 "PART THREE - DEALBREAKER! CORPORATIONS, PROFESSIONS, AND POLITICS" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "“Medicine’s Not a Science!”" **·** Karl Marx, Jürgen Habermas, Plato — Commodity Fetishism; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Decker explains the paradox of Dr. Leo Spaceman, incompetent ('Science is whatever we want it to be'), paternalistic, yet beloved by Tracy and Jack, through social theory: Parsons's pattern variables and professional ethics codes that Leo flouts, Marx's commodity fetishism, which makes patients desire medicalized enhancements the way direct-to-consumer pharma ads teach them to want Cialis and Viagra. Habermas's colonization of the lifeworld by systems organizations names the deeper diagnosis: managed-care bureaucracy and the market have reorganized medicine's social meanings, so 'medicine's not a science' because health itself has become a fashionable commodity. The chapter adds that 30 Rock works as satire because it shows the lifeworld pushing back against the system.
- ch. 20 "Too Big to Function" **·** Alasdair MacIntyre, Adam Smith — Virtues, Practices and Traditions — Terjesen argues Jack's combined title, Vice President of East Coast Television and Microwave Oven Programming, exhibits the corporate mentality: following Robert Jackall's Moral Mazes, credit flows up (Jack's Trivection oven) and blame flows down (Greenzo, the fireworks fiasco), so managers learn to avoid decisive action. Peter French's thesis that corporations are moral persons explains why GE runs fine with Don Geiss in a coma and Kathy Geiss playing with unicorns, so no one at the top needs to understand the businesses they oversee. MacIntyre's distinction between practices with internal goods and external rewards shows profit-thinking erasing comedy's internal good, turning TGS into 'pos-mens' and product integration, and Adam Smith's attack on distant, ignorant regulators is turned against conglomerate central management itself.
- ch. 21 "Where’s Kenneth We Need to Blame Someone!" **·** Karl Marx — Alienation — Michaud takes seriously Jack's love of GE: if Peter French is right that corporations have actorship through their corporate internal decision structure, showing intentionality, rational decision, and behavior change as in the Challenger launch that no individual willed, then GE is a moral person. Following Marvin Brown, he draws the uncomfortable conclusion that we have enslaved corporations: they are designed by stockholders to want only profit, programmed toward corporate suicide in buyouts, with no ethics built into the CID structure. Marx's wage slavery of workers unable to express their creative will is mirrored from above, and the chapter closes by relocating blame: when corporations do evil, the culpable party is us, the free-market supporters who designed the system.
- ch. 22 "“I Truly Don’t Like You as a Person. Can’t One Human Being Not Like Another" **·** Communitarianism — Michaud sets Liz's communitarianism, in which communities constitute identity and positive rights matter, against Jack's libertarianism of negative rights, self-making, and 'Capitalism is my religion', and asks how their friendship can be real if the philosophies are incompatible. The reconciliation runs through two concessions: Jack proves no one is self-made since his mother, GE, and Liz shape him, and he even takes a mail-room job rather than leave GE, while communitarianism is shown to make sense only because individuals first value their own welfare. Liz and Jack's friendship thus models a rational compromise in which community and individual interest turn out to need each other.
- ch. 23 "PART FOUR - MIND GRAPES (TO NOURISH YOUR THINKING)" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "The Danger of Thinking “Science Is Whatever We Want It to Be”; or, the" **·** Plato — Allegory of the Cave; Atman and Brahman; Karma and Rebirth — Barkman reads the 30 Rock cast as prisoners who prefer delusion, using Plato's allegory of the cave and the Upanishadic scheme of samsara, karma, maya, and moksha, in which Atman is realized as one with Brahman, as twin diagnoses. Drew's 'bubble' of good looks, Liz's false memory of being a harmless nerd in 'Reunion' and her belief she can have it all, Jack's pride about his terrible sketch ideas, Kenneth's unexamined dogma ('I can't handle the truth!'), Jenna's age-hiding, and Tracy's white myths about diabetes and Colorado all illustrate ignorance of reality that costs happiness. The Hindu-Platonic lesson is that knowledge of reality, not Dr. Spaceman's 'science is whatever we want it to be', is the route to happiness, because ignorance is not bliss.
- ch. 25 "Liz Lemon and High School The More Things Change, the More They Don’t" **·** David Hume, Augustine of Hippo, Gilbert Ryle, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Bundle Theory of Self; Augustine on Time; Personal Identity — Shores uses Jack's cookie jar collection and his fiftieth-birthday home movies in 'Apollo, Apollo', plus Liz's catastrophic high-school reunion in 'Reunion', to examine personal identity over time. Hume's bundle theory denies any self beyond a flux of perceptions, Whitehead offers three senses of a person over time, and Augustine locates past, present, and future in the mind as memory, sight, and expectation, which explains why Jack cannot recover his tenth-birthday joy by buying the Apollo Lunar Module toy yet feels 'like a kid' after laughing until he vomits. Ryle's 'elusiveness of I' and Rousseau on happiness as a fleeting state frame the conclusion that we continually reinterpret the past from the present, so the self is a stretched-out temporal continuity.
- ch. 26 "You Can Take the Man out of Cleveland . ." **·** John Searle — Moral Relativism — Da Silva and Found take Liz's stint as a Cleveland model in the episode 'Cleveland' as an entry into social constructivism: following Ian Hacking's account of socially constructed Xs and Marcia Eaton's socially constructed aesthetic response, taste is learned, culture-bound, and class-marked, so Jack's Manhattan standards condemn Liz's Burger King attire while Cleveland calls her a model. They then warn that aesthetic relativism ('you can't question taste') leaves no ground for condemning Tracy's solid gold boots or for resisting the powerful, since celebrities and the Black Crusaders have platforms that let them impose their standards. Searle's non-relativist account of social facts is floated as an escape route, and Liz's persistence as a New Yorker shows identity and values are constructed gradually through social belonging.
- ch. 27 "“Nothing Unusual. Russian Mobs, Invisible Motorcycles, Sex Pooping.”" **·** Plato, David Hume — Arp uses Tracy Jordan's truth bombs, from the moon as Oprah's spy satellite to vampires who golf and Godzilla's indifference to humans, to teach the Theaetetus analysis of knowledge as justified true belief together with the correspondence theory of truth. He shows why Tracy's claims fail, as with 'it's still illegal to be black in Arizona', and when they succeed, as with the Jamaican meat-pie building and his stories about his dad, cataloguing justification by perception, testimony, and deductive arguments like Tracy's 'I can't be normal' syllogism about Quincy Jones. Clifford's ethics of belief supplies the moral punchline that believing on insufficient evidence is wrong always and everywhere, so Tracy's delusions are not just funny but immoral, and Hume's proportioning of belief to evidence is the corrective.
- ch. 28 "Final Act" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Judith Butler, Friedrich Nietzsche, Plato — Bad Faith; Gender Performativity; Radical Freedom and Responsibility; Authenticity — Shaw argues everyone at 30 Rock performs a role that shields them from Sartrean radical freedom: Kenneth is Sartre's cafe waiter in bad faith, believing television is America's true art form and willing to 'die at my post' for NBC, while Jack psyches himself up with corporate slogans and Jenna's 'Black Tie' candor about marrying the last Hapsburg to be taken care of is the rare moment of good faith. Butler's gender performativity explains Jack's masculinity as a repeated script, from his Glengarry Glen Ross 'always be talking' allusion to Baldwin's brass-balls speech to his homosocial man-crushes on Don Geiss and Dick Cheney. Plato's wariness of imitation, Nietzsche on hypocrisy hardening into character, and Santayana's tragic mask frame the closing thought: the actors know they are performing, but the characters, like us, do not.
- ch. 29 "APPENDIX 1" *(notes)*
- ch. 30 "APPENDIX 2" *(notes)*
- ch. 31 "CONTRIBUTORS" *(notes)*
- ch. 32 "INDEX" *(notes)*

## Adventure Time and Philosophy - The Handbook for Heroes

- ch. 1 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The editor's introduction argues that Adventure Time is philosophy in disguise: its theme-song promise of 'very distant lands' describes the philosopher's trek into unpopular, even hated ideas, and the show treats 'crazy' questions as fun rather than frightening. It names the questions the book will chase—whether Finn was fated to lose his arm, whether the Ice King is really a bad guy, what evil really means—and claims that since common sense has always turned out to be wrong (matter isn't solid, the Earth moves), the childlike willingness to reconsider everything is a philosophical virtue. Philosophy, like the show, is an adventure best taken with a friend.
- ch. 8 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Karl Popper — Falsificationism; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Karavitis argues Finn's 'Finn-losophy' is a metaphysics that prefers magic over Princess Bubblegum's science, and that this choice shapes his ethics: magic is the 'easy way out' requiring no understanding (Jake's stretching, the Glasses of Nerdicon), while science demands observation, experiment, and falsifiable ideas—'reality is the ultimate spell-checker.' Because magic promises instant gratification, Finn rushes into danger ('Wizard,' 'Donny,' the Goblin Kingdom) behind an inflexible hero code, and his altruism misfires, as the never-ending prize-winning battles of the 'Mystery Train' episode illustrate. The chapter concludes that your view of how the world works—magical or scientific—determines whether you can grow ethically at all.
- ch. 10 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger — The Question Concerning Technology — Leonard reads Ooo through Heidegger's contrast between technological and poetic frames of mind: Princess Bubblegum's BGCCTV, Goliad, and the James clone mission treat candy people as manipulable stock, while the Lich is technological thinking perfected—pure efficiency aimed at 'the extinction of all life'—and Finn is the poet who values things for what they are, risks Heidegger's 'venture,' and battles Death in 'Death in Bloom' as if in a concert. On Heidegger's definition of art as 'the setting-itself-into-work of truth,' Marceline's songs and Jake's imagined lava show creation disclosing reality. Magic, spread by the Mushroom War, sits between the two modes, and only Finn's poetic exuberance can check Bubblegum's slide toward order at any cost.
- ch. 11 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Hobbes — The Social Contract — Caballeros argues that political legitimacy in Ooo runs through parenthood: Princess Bubblegum rules the citizens she created ('You made me... you are my Glob'), the Ice King is redeemed when we learn Simon protected child Marceline, and the Flame King and Hunson Abadeer forfeit standing as fathers. Using Hobbes's view that a ruler is legitimate insofar as he protects his subjects, the chapter asks whether Bubblegum's protection-cum-manipulation—sabotaging Finn to let James die, jailing Tree Trunks—still counts, and it contrasts justified rebellion (Flame Princess deposing her father, Marceline resisting Abadeer) with Lumpy Space Princess's disastrous one. Good parenting, like good government, earns obedience; bad parenting loses the crown.
- ch. 12 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Hobbes, John Locke, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — The State of Nature; The Social Contract; Natural Rights; Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society — Gale and Barkman treat post-Mushroom-War Ooo as a state-of-nature laboratory for liberalism's three founders. The Forest Wizard who cages Finn in 'Storytelling' is Hobbes's Leviathan, kept in power by fear yet moderated by self-interest (Goblin King Xergiok's fall shows the leash); Princess Bubblegum's Candy Kingdom is Locke's civil society where citizens trade private revenge for impartial law—her lawful bow to Lemongrab shows she rules by contract, not fear; and 'City of Thieves' illustrates Rousseau's claim that property corrupts natural goodness, with Finn and Jake as his uncorrupted natural people. The chapter concludes that the rights language these thinkers forged is what keeps both Ooo and our world from sliding back into a war of all against all.
- ch. 13 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Thomas Aquinas — Doctrine of Double Effect; The Categorical Imperative; Utilitarianism; The Drowning Child (obligation to aid) — Montoya tests Simon's choice to sacrifice his sanity by wearing the crown to save child Marceline against three ethical theories. Aquinas's doctrine of double effect clears him because the intended good (saving her life) outweighs the merely foreseen side effect (her tears, his possible future evils); Kant's categorical imperative rules out universalizing 'let children die to save myself,' since a world of universal using destroys the rule itself; and a fountain case where Finn must choose between world-saving plans and a drowning candy child separates act from rule utilitarianism, with rule utilitarianism preserving the duty to rescue. All three converge on the verdict that Simon, not Finn, is Ooo's greatest hero.
- ch. 14 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Forgiveness; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Moral Luck — McCrossin asks why Finn and Jake forgive Marceline although she tries to kill Jake, evicts them twice, drains Princess Bubblegum, and never shows remorse. Jake's change of heart ('my fear was based on ignorance') comes from witnessing her father Hunson Abadeer's cruelty and her abandoned childhood, so her bad acts flow from causes outside her control rather than pure choice. The chapter presses the remaining problem: we normally forgive the penitent offender, not the unrepentant one, and Marceline—unlike the moping vampires of Twilight, Underworld, and True Blood—never regrets, leaving readers to decide whether understanding without remorse can ground trust and friendship.
- ch. 15 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** David Hume — Problem of Induction; Philosophical Skepticism — Michaud plays the 'David Hume' Card Wars deck against Princess Bubblegum's confidence in science: following Hume's problem of induction, every scientific law rests on observation and is only probabilistic—we never observe the necessary connection between cause and effect, whether in magnetism, Jake's hunger-induced jerkiness, or the first law of thermodynamics. Humans are pattern-makers who project causal order onto the world (seeing Gunter in clouds, blaming one bubblegum incident), while quantum physics reveals genuine randomness that Einstein hated. Ooo's residents, who watch Tree Trunks explode and simply move on, may grasp reality better than we do, because they never smuggle in stable, lawlike expectations that the show—and the universe—keep violating.
- ch. 17 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Free Will vs Determinism; Compatibilism; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Littmann uses Adventure Time as a stockpile of thought experiments against free will: Finn excuses Billy (possessed by the Lich), hug-wolf Finn, amulet-Marceline, red-starved Marceline, the magically animated hat Little Dude, and crown-cursed Simon, yet punishes the Lich and Lemongrab, whose destructive desires are exactly as unchosen—Lemongrab even protests 'you made me like this.' Since deterministic causation and randomness exhaust the options, we are 'machines made of meat' wearing ice crowns inside our skulls, so deserved suffering makes no sense. The chapter concludes that just-deserts retribution (Princess Cookie's life sentence, America's prisons) should give way to deterrence, protection, and rehabilitation—freeing the Simon Petrikovs underneath.
- ch. 18 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Free Will vs Determinism; Fate and Foreknowledge; Compatibilism — Miller argues from Jake's croak dream, the Cosmic Owl, and the Dungeon Train's future crystal that Ooo's future is real, visible, and changeable—hence a case of soft determinism: the dream of Jake dying in space is what caused Finn to accompany him and avert it, so the only being who could have intended the change, the Owl, is the sole certain free agent in Ooo. Every alternate Finn is missing his right arm—Shoko the thief, Farmworld Finn, pillow-world Finn, the Dungeon Train elder—hinting at an epic destiny, yet Finn altered the train future once he saw where it led Jake. The chapter closes on the open question of whether choosing one's fate differs from having it thrust on one, since every choice is caused by the past no one can change.
- ch. 19 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Gottfried Leibniz — The Best of All Possible Worlds; The Problem of Evil; Possible Worlds — Montoya asks whether Prismo, Adventure Time's wish-granting stand-in for God, produced the best possible world when he restored Ooo over Farmworld. After explaining Leibniz's answer to the problem of evil—that an all-good, all-knowing God permits some evil because every possible world contains some, and ours contains the least—the chapter compares Farmworld (no Lich, but Finn becomes the Ice King, Jake mutates into the Lich, Marceline dies ancient and insane) with Ooo, where the same characters flourish as heroes. Prismo, no omnibenevolent deity, remakes Ooo out of friendship with Jake, and Farmworld's horrors vindicate the Leibnizian point that wishing one evil out of existence can unleash far worse.
- ch. 20 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Death of God; Substance Monism (Deus sive Natura) — Namvol argues that 'Glob' is the post-apocalyptic corpse of the Christian God: combining Don Cupitt's claim that gods live only in worship and practice with Nietzsche's 'God is dead,' he traces how the Mushroom War destroyed the believers, churches, and rituals that kept God alive, leaving Ooo an empty exclamation. Pantheism fails as a rescue because evil is native to Ooo's nature (Gunter, the eternally self-existing Abadeer), so God cannot simply have become the land. Against Dostoevsky's 'without God all things are permitted,' the chapter sides with Žižek—that morality's shadow, guilt, outlives God—as when Finn's self-regulation kicks in after 'All the Little People' and 'Dungeon Train,' making Finn, not Glob, the true savior of a godless world.
- ch. 21 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Muniz uses the 'Ocean of Fear' material to stage the paradox of fiction—we know Finn, Jake, and the Lich are drawings, yet we genuinely fear for the child hero in danger—and works through its three sentences: we respond emotionally only to what we believe real, we disbelieve fiction, yet we respond to fiction constantly. Distinguishing passive startle from active dwelling, he tests escape routes: fear at Marceline's abandonment may really be fear for our own children, the robot attack in 'Business Men' may become 'real' to a viewer, and some philosophers call our responses mere 'fictional emotions.' Finding no sentence safely deniable, he concludes that our emotional responses to Adventure Time are, strictly speaking, irrational—but no less honest for it.
- ch. 23 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Hans-Georg Gadamer — Philosophical Hermeneutics (fusion of horizons) — Jones reads Adventure Time through Gadamer's account of play in Truth and Method: play is the 'mode of being' of the artwork, and Finn's all-in commitment—the 'sacred seriousness' that makes a spoilsport of anyone who mocks the rules, as in 'Slumber Party Panic' or Finn's bloodlust in 'Morituri Te Salutamus'—is why the show counts as art. The finished episode is Gadamer's 'transformation into structure,' and like a festival or a favorite board game it exists in its return, episode after episode. Interpreting the show is itself play: holding our world and Ooo's world in two palms brought together is Gadamer's fusion of horizons, through which Finn corrects our concepts of a boy or a hero and makes new understanding, even empathy, possible.
- ch. 24 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Plato, David Hume, George Berkeley — Allegory of the Cave; Subjective Idealism (esse est percipi); Bundle Theory of Self; Philosophical Skepticism — Arp uses King Worm's dream-within-dreams to pose the perception-reality problem: distinguishing perceiver, perceptions, and perceived, he maps the triad onto Plato's allegory of the cave, then contrasts epistemological and metaphysical realism (the fallen tree fort still makes sound waves unheard; Pythagoras discovered rather than invented his theorem) with idealism and antirealism, whose dead end is solipsism—Lumpy Space Princess locked in a private movie theater of perceptions. Hume's picture of the mind as a theater of successive perceptions and Berkeley's Hylas-and-Philonous worry that we are trapped inside perceptual reality give the skeptical threat its force. The chapter sides with Jake—'You gotta focus on what's real, man'—while conceding that after 'King Worm' Finn and Jake can never be certain they escaped the hypnotism.
- ch. 25 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** J.M.E. McTaggart, Henri Bergson, David Lewis — The A-Series and B-Series of Time; Duration (durée); Possible Worlds — Through a hot-tub dialogue in Prismo's Time Room, the chapter walks Jake through the main theories of time: McTaggart's A-theory versus B-theory, plus presentism, the growing block, eternalism and four-dimensionalism, illustrated with the mirror-Finn dream, Farmworld Finn, Jake's unchangeable croak dream, and the Ancient Psychic Tandem War Elephant as a thing stretched across past, present and future selves. Booko's description of the multiverse becomes a springboard for David Lewis's possible-worlds realism and Everett's many-worlds reading of Prismo's 'alternate wish worlds'. Finally, quotes from McTaggart and Bergson delivered as Finn's Glasses of Nerdicon rant argue that time resists spatial and scientific analysis and must be lived, which the authors tie to relativity, black-hole cosmology and Prismo's Time Room as the one dimension outside time.
- ch. 26 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Sigmund Freud, Michel Foucault — The Unconscious — Framing the 'Frost & Fire' episode as an imagined storyboard meeting, the chapter asks whether Finn's erotic dream of Flame Princess is a Freudian wish-fulfillment rising from his subconscious after he deliberately lies to provoke the Ice King-Flame Princess fight so the dream will return. Via Foucault's Care of the Self, it introduces Artemidorus's method of reading dreams for the real and moral consequences they portend, suggesting Finn's dream already warns him that his deception will cost him the relationship, with the Cosmic Owl whispering that he 'screwed up'. The writers conclude that sleeping and waking lives bleed into each other, and that Finn's refusal to read the warning leaves him burned: broken hearts, a torched Ice Kingdom, and the princess gone.
- ch. 27 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Gilbert Ryle — Personal Identity; The Ghost in the Machine — The chapter asks whether Marceline has lost Simon Petrikov forever now that he is the Ice King, and tests every criterion of personal identity - body, memories, beliefs, personality, soul - finding each one either something that changes over time without ending a person (Finn survives losing his arm, Jake his fear of vampires) or something shared that then explains nothing. Borrowing Ryle's deflation of the soul as just the way the brain works - like Lemongrab renaming and remaking the Candy Kingdom, killing it without touching a single building - the author concludes Simon and the Ice King are two different people sharing one piece of machinery, like two operating systems on one computer. The dark payoff: bringing Simon back permanently would delete the Ice King, and by the same reasoning our own past selves are equally gone, a 'thousand deaths' a day that the chapter offers as Marceline's consolation, with Death's line 'you're gonna be the Ice King' as the haunting exception.
- ch. 29 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Alan Turing, René Descartes, John Searle — The Turing Test; The Chinese Room; The Cogito — Arguing against the dogma that only organic beings can be conscious, the chapter defends BMO's aliveness and personhood against Finn and Jake's teasing and the contrast with servile Neptr. Using Pepperell's criteria of 'motivated meaning' and layered consciousness, it shows BMO changing her own batteries, escaping the giant bird, fearing the silent-contest 'doppelgangers', resisting the factory's personality wipe, and rehearsing personhood with Football in the mirror. Against Descartes's claim that no machine can truly use language and Searle's Chinese Room - reimagined as Gunter in a dungeon flipping response cards - the author sides with Turing: since we credit other humans with minds on conversation and behavior alone, refusing BMO the same credit is mere speciesism, and personhood finally rests on social recognition of another as a person.
- ch. 30 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Hans-Georg Gadamer — Philosophical Hermeneutics (fusion of horizons) — The chapter argues that what sets Adventure Time above generic sword-and-sorcery is that its stories are stories of play, from Card Wars duels to the pillow-fort world of 'Puhoy', where Finn lives out an entire alternate life with Rosalinen. Drawing on Huizinga's claim that play founds all culture, Gadamer's view that play is a presentation that reveals truth, and Fink's account of play as purposeless, world-creating magic, it shows Finn learning what a hero is by 'playing' the hero's role, BMO transforming a chicken into the noir suspect Lorraine, and Jake's imaginary lava genuinely burning Finn in 'Rainy Day Daydream'. Play-worlds like Ooo hold a mirror to the real one - Finn's experiments on the little people force Jake to confront what breaking up his relationship would mean - so make-believe turns out to be how we seriously learn who we are.
- ch. 31 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Judith Butler — Gender Performativity; Feminism — The chapter uses Adventure Time's gender play - Fionna and Cake, princesses holding nearly all political power in Ooo, Jake as terrified helicopter dad, Mannish Man's parody of manliness - to ask what being a man or woman really means. Following Judith Butler's Gender Trouble, it argues sex does not fix gender: the Lumpy Space King and Queen share one body, Flame King is a flame in a suit, and Butler's critique of the MIT 'master gene' experiment shows how the assumption that men and women are opposites smuggles cultural convention into science. Finn is read as performing masculinity - Billy worship, dungeon codes, the endless 'Dungeon Train' - caught in a crisis of masculinity between what society says a man is and who he is, with the ghost of Shoko in 'The Vault' and the accepted grass sword as steps toward a heroism that is his own.
- ch. 32 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Alan Turing — The Turing Test; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Staging a modified Turing Test in which the author interrogates BMO about Football, Bubble, her broken egg, her glitch and the Mo Co factory, the chapter asks whether her answers display human-like intelligence or merely a machine's 'pretend' intelligence. Beemo's evasions about emotion ('I felt like crying') become the pivot for asking whether anyone can define feeling beyond the words they were taught for it, since we are told as children that crying means sadness. Turing's conversational test is then weighed against our bias that a boxy Gameboy cannot really think - you are, the author notes, proof that arranged matter can love and feel - and the chapter closes on the ethics of the question: Princess Bubblegum's hacksaw shows that convenience is exactly the criterion we must never use to deny personhood, as slaveholders once did.
- ch. 33 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Epictetus — Stoicism; Practical Wisdom (phronesis); Preferred Indifferents — Re-examining 'The Enchiridion!' trial by trial - the gnome illusions, the ogre whose dollar Finn steals and then returns, the dark mage's brain world, the wallop given the Key-per in devil pajamas - the chapter argues Finn wins the hero's handbook without being righteous: he is brave, tender and ingenious, but gullible, hasty and violent, slaying whatever he is told is evil and running from the gnome test. Epictetus's Stoic Enchiridion supplies the standard the show's book lacks: only virtue matters, wealth, fame and comfort are indifferents, and a real handbook teaches how to lose, age and admit mistakes, not just how to win, which is exactly what Billy's quit-heroism shows Ooo's greatest hero never learned. Because bravery without good judgment could as easily serve a Nazi, a terrorist or the Lich, Finn's lack of practical wisdom means the 'most righteous hero' in Ooo is an impetuous boy - proof of how morally broken the Land of Ooo is.
- ch. 35 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Socrates, Plato, Friedrich Nietzsche — Master and Slave Morality; The Unexamined Life; Justice in the Republic — Comparing Finn with Gilgamesh, Achilles, Cuchulainn and Beowulf, the chapter shows how deeply he belongs to the archaic heroic code: a warrior who loves combat, seeks glory in tough-guy contests, fights dirty like Odysseus, and is fiercely loyal to Jake, his friends and his community. Against this tradition, Finn's unprecedented willingness to help any stranger who cries for help and his refusal to kill the 'unaligned' ant mark the post-heroic morality that began when Socrates told Athenians to care for their souls rather than status and Plato designed a city serving the common good - the author explicitly frames this through Nietzsche's complaint that Greek philosophy derailed the healthy, competitive heroic values. The chapter sides with Finn and the self-sacrificing Lemonjohn over Achilles, concluding that the secret of the Enchiridion is doing whatever the common good requires, warrior or not.
- ch. 36 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Epictetus, Immanuel Kant — The Golden Mean; The Categorical Imperative; The Dichotomy of Control — The chapter assembles real-life versions of Finn's Enchiridion, starting with Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics: the Golden Mean locates each virtue between two vices, so true courage is knowing when to fight and when to run - cowardice and 'stupidteous' recklessness as deficiency and excess - a balance Finn's all-out bravery lacks. Epictetus's Stoic Handbook (which inspired the emperor Marcus Aurelius) teaches that happiness depends only on one's own reactions and that events are good or bad by how we take them, making it a manual even the chronically disappointed Ice King could use, while Christine de Pizan's book of chivalry prepares heroes for defeat and Erasmus's Handbook of the Christian Knight with Praise of Folly makes Finn and Jake's 'foolish' mercy look righteous. Finally Kant's Groundwork supplies duty for duty's sake: by the categorical imperative, Finn was entitled to steal the ogre's dollar only if, as an ogre who had swallowed Jake, he would accept the same theft from a hero.
- ch. 37 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Martha Nussbaum, John Stuart Mill, Immanuel Kant — Virtue Ethics (arete); Practical Wisdom (phronesis); Eudaimonia (flourishing); The Capabilities Approach — The chapter argues Finn is not yet a true hero but is on a quest to become one: against rule systems like Mill's greatest-happiness utilitarianism and Kantian respect for persons, virtue ethics asks not 'what should I do' but 'who should I be', and Aristotle's point that children lack fully developed practical reason explains why Finn's good intentions misfire - removing Donny exposes the house people to Why-wolves, and Penny's trick humiliates him in the City of Thieves. Martha Nussbaum's ideal of every person as free and of equal dignity grounds heroism in the common good, and her work on literature and popular culture explains how watching Finn's exaggerated choices trains our own moral judgment. Citing Franco and Zimbardo's psychology of real heroes - empathy, risk tolerance, persistence - and closing with Seneca's 'how nobly you live', it concludes that since Aristotelian virtue is a habit, anyone can start by admitting they suck at heroism and practicing, as Jake's advice puts it.
- ch. 38 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" **·** Practical Wisdom (phronesis); Virtue Ethics; Moral Luck — Reading Finn's first season as a three-stage hero's journey - winning the Enchiridion on Mount Cragdor, being tested in 'Business Time', 'City of Thieves', 'Ocean of Fear' and 'Henchman', then doubly confirmed when Billy recognizes him - the chapter extracts a working handbook of heroism from his stumbles. Its lessons: heroism needs reason as much as courage (thinking through the gnome 'illusions', knocking himself unconscious to beat the Fear Feaster), plain luck plays a real and uneven part in saving Jake from the ogre, retribution should be temporary (the ogre's returned dollar), and heroes must check their sources (Penny's con). The deepest tension is that justice and compassion, instinct and reason, violence and non-violence must be perpetually balanced - Billy's nonviolent way becomes its own injustice when Finn medicates villagers without consent - so a real hero is simply someone who never stops trying to hold the balance.
- ch. 39 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 40 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 41 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 42 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 43 "Adventure Time and Philosophy" *(front)*

## Alice in Wonderland and Philosophy - Curiouser and Curiouser

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Alice in Wonderland and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "The Blackwell Philosophy and Pop Culture Series" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright Page" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Acknowledgements" *(notes)*
- ch. 7 "Introduction" **·** Personal Identity — Frames the volume with Morpheus's red-pill speech in The Matrix, treating the rabbit-hole as our culture's portal of escape and asking how to make sense of what we find in Wonderland: hookah-smoking caterpillars, babies that turn into pigs, grins without cats, and trials where the sentence comes before the evidence. It takes Alice's 'Who in the world am I? Ah, that's the great puzzle!' as the book's guiding question, tying knowledge of who we are and can become to memory of who we have been, and previews chapters on the feminist Alice, the Red Queen and nuclear strategy, mushroom trips, and the connection between Alice, procrastination, and the Spice Girls.
- ch. 8 "PART ONE - “WAKE UP, ALICE DEAR”" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "NOTES" **·** Feminism — Argues that Alice, in Carroll's and Disney's versions, is a feminist model who navigates Wonderland by her own wits, unlike passive princesses such as Cinderella and Aurora who need male rescue. Megan S. Lloyd reads Alice's fall down the rabbit-hole as a rejection of her sister's idle female role, the Pigeon's complaints and the Duchess's shaken pig-baby as showing that motherhood is not a requirement of female worth, and the Mad Hatter's 'Then you shouldn't talk' as the old-boy network punishing an assertive woman. Alice's final 'You're nothing but a pack of cards!' completes her growth into a powerful presence, and the chapter closes by asking what twenty-first-century young women facing double standards would do if they asked 'What would Alice do?'
- ch. 10 "“Some People Have No More Sense Than a Baby!”" **·** G.E. Moore — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Uses the White Queen's 'jam to-morrow and jam yesterday—but never jam to-day' to introduce Elijah Millgram's jam-yesterday-jam-tomorrow goods: jobs, parenting, and Utah hiking that feel drudging in every moment yet are remembered and anticipated as wonderful. Mark D. White brings in G. E. Moore's organic unity, illustrated by the Spice Girls' 'Wannabe', Wham!, and Led Zeppelin, to ask how a whole made entirely of bad parts can be good 'on the whole', then treats procrastination as akrasia (weakness of will) and endorses Millgram's remedy: manufacture short-term 'jam today' goals like graduation ceremonies and marked lookout points to motivate timely action.
- ch. 11 "Grand Masters of the Universe" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Richard Rorty, Jacques Derrida — Language Games; The Private Language Argument — Chases Cold War nuclear strategists down the rabbit-hole, reading the Mad Tea Party as the MAD (mutually assured destruction) tea party where 'to reduce the risk of nuclear war, the risk must be increased' passes for wisdom. Ron Hirschbein uses Wittgenstein's language games (Alice was his favorite English prose), Rorty's final vocabulary, and Derrida's remark that a nuclear war has never 'taken place' to argue that Nukespeak, deterrence, the 'Delicate Balance of Terror', and the post hoc ergo propter hoc argument for deterrence are ungrounded, unfalsifiable word games. The Queen of Hearts's 'Off with his head!' prefigures decapitation strategy and the Hitler card, and the looking-glass chessboard becomes the arms race in which the public are expendable pawns.
- ch. 12 "A Closer Look at Alice’s Trial" **·** Socrates, Thomas Hobbes, John Locke, John Rawls — The Social Contract; The State of Nature; The Veil of Ignorance — Argues Alice is right to attack the court of the King and Queen of Hearts because she never entered a social contract with Wonderland: unlike Socrates, who stayed in Athens and so had to abide by the verdict (Plato's Crito), Alice fell in involuntarily and is trying to leave. Dennis Knepp casts the Queen of Hearts as Hobbes's absolute sovereign whose public 'Off with their heads!' terrorizes the playing cards into obedience ('Covenants, without the Sword, are but Words'), while Locke's limited, consent-based government and Rawls's contractualism mark the better alternative the Hearts court fails to meet. Since Alice stands outside their covenant, she is her own more-than-a-mile-high Leviathan, rightly defending herself in the state of nature.
- ch. 13 "PART TWO - “THAT’S LOGIC”" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "“Consider Your Verdict!”" **·** David Hume, Socrates, Plato — Problem of Induction; Socratic Method (elenchus); The Unexamined Life — Splits Wonderland's nonsense into the tolerable kind (strange but lawful regularities, like mushroom side-effects and walking the opposite way to reach the Red Queen, which Alice masters by experiment) and the intolerable kind (the creatures' eristic logic). Using Hume's fork of relations of ideas versus matters of fact, George Dunn and Brian McDonald argue Alice rightly concludes the pig-baby is merely an out-of-the-way matter of fact, while the White Queen's 'jam every other day: to-day isn't any other day' and the King's beheading of the bodiless Cheshire Cat repeat the Sophists' tricks from Plato's Euthydemus, which Socrates deflates from the standpoint of common sense and the examined life. The Knave's trial ('sentence first, verdict afterwards') lands on cable-news pundits who, like the show trials of Stalin, argue from predetermined conclusions.
- ch. 15 "The Lasting Legacy of Logic" **·** David Hume, Aristotle, Karl Popper — Problem of Induction; Category Mistake; Falsificationism — Treats Carroll the Oxford logician as a teacher hiding five lessons in Wonderland: watch your assumptions (the King infers the Knave's guilt from an unsigned letter), know what your words mean (Humpty Dumpty on 'glory'), don't confuse categories (the King's 'Nobody' and the Hatter's personified Time), don't switch subjects and predicates ('I mean what I say' versus 'I say what I mean'), and don't confuse necessary with sufficient conditions (Alice's Ada and Mabel arguments, with a hammer-and-egg illustration). David S. Brown grounds the lessons in Hume on causes discoverable only by experience, Aristotle's law of non-contradiction and categories, the Marx Brothers' 'Either he's dead or my watch has stopped', and Popper's point that a successful prediction cannot prove a hypothesis, only a failed one can refute it.
- ch. 16 "Wonderland and the Real World" **·** David Hume, W.V.O. Quine — Problem of Induction; Indeterminacy of Translation; The Web of Belief — Extracts three problems of induction from Alice's misadventures: predicting the future (Hume's problem: nothing rationally compels Humpty Dumpty to agree the world will not turn into Wonderland at 6 a.m. tomorrow), proving whose dream it is (Quine's underdetermination of theory by evidence: Tweedledee can accommodate any evidence within the theory that Alice exists only in the Red King's dream), and translating Wonderese (Quine's indeterminacy: pointing at the White Rabbit cannot fix whether natives mean the rabbit, its parts, or its stages, nor what 'muchness' means). Brendan Shea shows Alice succeeding where the creatures fail by the criteria Hume and Quine endorse: experimentation, simplicity, conservatism of belief, and a principle of charity in translation.
- ch. 17 "“Language Is Worth a Thousand Pounds a Word”" *(no index entry yet)* — Pits Donald Davidson's notorious claim that 'there is no such thing as a language' against the Wonderland dialogues, asking whether communication rests on shared rules (the institutional view) or on speaker intentions (the invention view). Daniel Whiting argues that 'curiouser and curiouser', the Mock Turtle's 'uglification', and the Mouse's tale/tail homophony show grasp of rules is neither necessary nor sufficient for understanding, as Davidson holds, but that Humpty Dumpty's 'which is to be master—that's all', the Red Queen's 'when you've once said a thing, that fixes it', and Alice's talks with the Sheep and the March Hare show the invention view is solipsistic too, so rule-governed established meanings still do real work in conversation.
- ch. 18 "PART THREE - “WE’RE ALL MAD HERE”" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "Perceptions Are Fine, but Dull Reality Is Still Better" **·** Plato, George Berkeley — Subjective Idealism (esse est percipi); Allegory of the Cave; Philosophical Skepticism — Distinguishes perceiver, perceptions, and perceived external objects to ask whether Alice, or anyone, can get beyond her perceptions to a mind-independent world, entering through Robert Arp's own painkiller hallucination of stones that were really Jell-O. The chapter sets epistemological idealism and metaphysical antirealism (the movie-theater-in-the-mind picture, traced to Plato's cave and Berkeley's suggestion that we are trapped inside perceptual reality) against the epistemological and metaphysical realism it finds in Alice and Carroll: fossilized ferns, dodo birds, and the Pythagorean theorem are discovered rather than invented, and at story's end everything rightly changes 'to dull reality'.
- ch. 20 "The Real Is Fake and the Fake Is Real" **·** Zhuangzi, Plato, William James — Zhuangzi's Perspectivism (butterfly dream); Philosophical Skepticism — Takes Morpheus's red-pill speech at face value to ask whether Alice is a drug story, then argues the deeper point: the distinction between normal and distorted experience is exaggerated or specious because all perception is conditioned by expectation, mood, and chemistry, whether on caffeine or psilocybin. Scott F. Parker reads the Red King's dream alongside Plato's Theaetetus dream argument and Zhuangzi's butterfly dream to show we can never prove we are awake, then weighs William James's nitrous-oxide revelations and Aldous Huxley's mescaline 'Mind at Large' as reasons altered states might sometimes show reality more truly, concluding that the real is fake, the fake is real, and Wonderland's syndrome is now a recognized medical condition.
- ch. 21 "Dull Reality The Tragic View of Life" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Perspectivism; The Apollonian and the Dionysian; Will to Power — A Nietzschean interpretation: Alice's unastonished pursuit of the White Rabbit exemplifies the 'will to ignorance' hidden within the will to truth, since every perspective is created by selecting and simplifying while we convince ourselves we have discovered rather than invented. Rick Mayock reads the Caterpillar's 'Who are YOU?' and the mushroom as lessons in perspectivism and in the self as a grammatical fiction (no doer behind the deed, no lightning behind the flash), the Queen's beheadings and ruleless croquet as a flux of competing wills to power, and the Hatter's madness as Dionysian against Alice's Apollonian calm, with Greek tragedy supplying the metaphysical comfort that makes 'dull reality' livable.
- ch. 22 "Deleuze Jam Today" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Immanuel Kant, Henri Bergson, Gilles Deleuze, Zeno of Elea — Duration (durée); Augustine on Time; Transcendental Idealism; Zeno's Paradoxes — Walks Alice through four theories of time: Augustine's three presents in Confessions (Alice 'was a different person then'; the past exists only as present memory), Kant's time as a subjective form of intuition presupposed by experience (the White Rabbit's anxiety about being late already requires it), Bergson's indivisible duration against spatialized clock time (running fastest with the Red Queen yet ending under the same tree; the Hatter stuck at six o'clock; Zeno's Achilles resolved by indivisible acts), and Deleuze's chronos and aion from The Logic of Sense. Mark W. Westmoreland reads 'jam to-morrow and jam yesterday—but never jam to-day' as pure aion: the present never arrives, only an endlessly subdividing past-future.
- ch. 23 "PART FOUR - “WHO IN THE WORLD AM I ”" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "A Nonsense Too Far" **·** Plato, Socrates, Diogenes the Cynic — Socratic Method (elenchus); Cynicism (living according to nature) — Places Carroll's nonsense in a Greco-Roman tradition of philosophically useful absurdity: Socrates exposing Euthyphro's definitions of piety as nonsense, Plato's tongue-in-cheek Republic calculation that the tyrant's life is 729 times worse, and Diogenes ridiculing slavery even while sold on the auction block.
