Charles Taliaferro and Elizabeth Olson argue the jokes land only because Alice stays sane, curious, polite, and self-controlled, the stable foil who keeps the death threats and cruelty of Wonderland and the Looking-Glass on the amusing side of the ledger, and that nonsense goes 'too far' in Kafka's The Trial, where losing the sane, good center turns absurdity into meaninglessness.
- ch. 25 "From Backwards -Working Memory, or Working Backwards from Memory" **·** John Locke, Plato, Augustine of Hippo, Henri Bergson, Friedrich Nietzsche, Sigmund Freud — Personal Identity (memory theory); Personal Identity; Matter and Memory — Treats the Caterpillar's 'Who are YOU?' and Alice's 'Who in the world am I? Ah, that's the great puzzle!' as the classic memory-and-identity problem, rallying Plato's wax tablet and Meno doctrine that learning is recollection, Locke's memory criterion of the same person, and Diderot's dialogue on how without memory there would be no connected self. Tyler Shores reads Alice's botched recitations as Bergson's impersonal habit-memory swamping personal memory, her name-forgetting in the wood through Nietzsche's Untimely Meditations on the necessity of forgetting, the Red King's dream as Freud's alienated memory, and the White Queen's memory that 'works both ways' as a thought experiment tying memory, time, and self together.
- ch. 26 "CONTRIBUTORS" *(notes)*
- ch. 27 "INDEX" *(notes)*

## Alien and Philosophy - I Infest Therefore I Am

- ch. 1 "Introduction" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Jean-Paul Sartre, Albert Camus, Karl Marx, Plato — The Übermensch; The Absurd; The Ethics of Care; Just War Theory — Book introduction: recounts how the 1979 cast were the first casualties of the Chestburster scene (real butcher-shop organs, Veronica Cartwright fainting), then previews the volume's payload - personhood questions about Ash, Bishop, David and the Ripley clone; Marx on why Weyland-Yutani sacrifices crews for profit; Plato's Republic on children in warfare via Newt; Lovecraftian cosmic horror; feminist readings of Ripley as care ethicist and mother; and continental takes - Xenomorphs as Nietzsche's Ubermensch, Camus on Ripley-8's refusal to commit suicide, and Sartre on a Xenomorph's right to resist us.
- ch. 2 "Part I Identity and Moral Considerability" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "“There is nothing in the desert, and no man needs nothing”" **·** John Locke, René Descartes — Personal Identity (memory theory); Mind–Body Dualism; The Cogito — Argues David in Prometheus qualifies as a Lockean person - intelligence, rule-following rationality, self-reflection via an active power of thought (his self-modeling on Lawrence of Arabia, his judged desire to be free of Weyland's programming), and mental pleasure and pain - while Ash fails the test, reacting only passively to company orders like Locke's unfree tennis ball. Against Weyland's and Shaw's Cartesian soul-based denial ('you're a robot'), this leaves open that beings humans create could share our moral status, making David's ostracism arbitrary and cruel.
- ch. 4 "“It’s a robot! A goddamn droid!”" **·** Peter Singer, Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham — Speciesism; Autonomy of the Will; Utilitarianism; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Tests three grounds of moral status against the franchise: species membership (Singer's charge of speciesism - would the human-DNA Newborn count?), Kantian autonomy (which makes Bishop matter but excludes babies and animals, and is itself doubtful once his behavioral inhibitors fix his choices), and Bentham's sentience ('can they suffer?' - Bishop's 'I hurt. Do me a favor. Disconnect me'). Settling on interests and preferences, the chapter argues synthetics with desires - even Ash's admiration for the 'perfect organism' - can be moral patients, so Weyland-Yutani's treatment of them as tools and slaves is wrong.
- ch. 5 "This is Rumor Control. Here are the Facts!" **·** Adam Smith — The Invisible Hand — Uses the Weyland corporations' treatment of crews - Special Order 937, Vickers's 'that makes you an employee,' Burke's Facehugger scheme, the debriefing that mourns a $42 million ship but never the crew - to pit stockholder theory (Friedman, Berle) against libertarian contract theory (Machan) and stakeholder theory (Freeman). The libertarian middle way fails because in deep space crews have neither Hirschman's exit nor voice, so only stakeholder theory explains why hiding workplace hazards and marking employees expendable wrongs them; the market's invisible hand behaves like Ash's very visible murdering hand.
- ch. 6 "Part II Ethics" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Special Order 937—Crew Expendable" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — Deontology; Utilitarianism — Reads Weyland-Yutani as 'greed is good' cost-benefit reasoning embodied by Ash, Mother, and Burke (Gordon Gekko's Wall Street speech transplanted into space), and asks who can be held accountable when every employee fills only a narrow role and scapegoats absorb blame. It then weighs Kantian deontology - Bishop's inviolable no-harm rules, the absolute wrongness of killing crew for profit - against utilitarian reasoning: Ripley's quarantine refusal to save the many, Parker's refusal to flame the alien holding Lambert, and the uncomfortable thought that acquiring the Xenomorph might genuinely benefit billions.
- ch. 8 "The End of History" **·** Karl Marx — Alienation; Commodity Fetishism — Diagnoses the Alien universe through Marx's Grundrisse: workers like Parker and Fifield ('I'm here to make money') live only to have and consume, wage labor is avoided like the plague (Pvt. Frost's 'I hate this job'), money replaces the faith people should place in each other, and employees become disposable commodities (Vickers to Shaw: 'that makes you an employee'). Only the conscience-free android Ash fits the Corporation's ideal 'pure capitalist,' and because the saga's future stays capitalist, it vindicates Fukuyama's end-of-history thesis over Marx's prediction of collapse.
- ch. 9 "Hope for the Future" **·** John Dewey, Plato, Charles Sanders Peirce — Pragmatism; Democracy as a Way of Life; Theory of Forms — Applies Dewey's The Public and Its Problems to Alien and Aliens: the real villain is not the Xenomorph but a United Americas too weak to govern Weyland-Yutani, whose crude cost-benefit erasure of the Nostromo crew's interests shows a state that has lost the public's voice. Ash's arrogant 'perfect organism' objectivity, the appeal to blameworthy 'human nature,' and faith that big business runs everything illustrate Dewey's targets - Plato's static Forms, economic determinism, and rugged individualism - and since beliefs are habits of action (Peirce), the Company's deeds expose its professed principles as negligible fictions.
- ch. 10 "Not Bad, for a [Wo]man" **·** Carol Gilligan, Nel Noddings, Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — The Ethics of Care; Caring as the Ground of Ethics; The Trolley Problem; Speciesism — Argues Ripley's crossed-heart promise to Newt shows partialist care rightly overriding impartial justice: Kant, Mill, and Rachels demand everyone count for one, but Gilligan's critique of Kohlberg and Noddings's carer-cared-for relation - completed in Newt's 'I knew you'd come' - explain why Ripley duct-tapes two guns together and descends into the hive. Her flexible, relational problem-solving (the lighter-sprinkler trick against the Facehuggers, learning the pulse rifle) beats Gorman's mission-first command, and the trolley-style arithmetic she rejects when the one on the tracks is her little girl is not a moral failing; even nuking the Queen's eggs raises Singer-style speciesism questions.
- ch. 11 "Part III Moral Psychology" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "Easing Back on the APC’s Throttle" **·** Michael Walzer, Cicero, Thomas Aquinas, Thomas Hobbes, John Locke — Just War Theory; The State of Nature — Treats Engineers, Xenomorphs, and humans as states and reads the APC debate through Thucydides' Melian dialogue ('the strong do what they will') and the jus in bello criteria of discrimination, military necessity, and proportionality. It concludes that Ripley's 'nuke the site from orbit' and Vasquez's nerve gas are vengeance masquerading as realism - the Xeno threat is no longer imminent, so Walzer's 'supreme emergency' is unavailable - while Hudson's withdrawal (even Burke's species-preservation plea) fits both realism and just war restraint; the Ben Tre 'we destroyed the town to save it' fiasco in Vietnam is the cautionary parallel.
- ch. 13 "Should Your Child Watch Aliens" **·** Plato — Justice in the Republic — Maps Aliens onto Plato's Republic: Hadley's Hope is an isolated city of fixed roles, Newt is an apprentice guardian taken along on campaign, and Ripley is her auxiliary supervisor who knows which military ventures are safe (making her look away from the cocooned colonists on the monitors). Exposure to violence has made Newt mentally tough and courageous in Plato's exact sense of retaining what reason dictates about what is and is not to be feared - she stays calm in the APC and leads Ripley and Hicks through the vents while Hudson panics - so the authors conclude parents should discuss violent media like Aliens with children rather than shield them.
- ch. 14 "Alien Nation" **·** Karl Marx — Alienation; Class Struggle — Reads Alien as a film about alienation in Marx's fourfold sense from the 1844 Manuscripts: the crew is estranged from the product of its labor (the retrieved Xenomorph turns on them), from the labor process (Brett and Parker's bonus dispute, Dallas shilling for the Company that deems him disposable), from species-being (infantile dependence on Mother, whose Special Order 937 merely formalizes an expendability they already live), and from one another (they never unite against the Company or suspect Ash). In Aliens the Colonial Marines repeat the failure, while the coordinated alien 'nation' under its queen - possible victims of Engineer colonization - makes the humans look like the inferior species.
- ch. 15 "Part IV Horror" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "Final Transmission" **·** Aristotle — Argues Alien is the best science-fiction horror film because it executes H.P. Lovecraft's principles of weird fiction: fear of the unknown (the alien's shifting life cycle, acid blood as 'cosmic fear' at violations of natural law), an atmosphere of realism through mundane space-truckers, cramped and imperfect technology, and pseudo-scientific jargon, monsters that are genuinely rather than cosmetically alien, suggestion over explicit gore (Brett and Parker hauled off-screen), claustrophobia, paranoia via the disguised android Ash, and horrific bodily violation in the Facehugger and Chestburster. Aristotle's Poetics supplies the matching doctrine that even astounding stories should default to the necessary or probable.
- ch. 17 "The Melding" *(no index entry yet)* — Extends Noel Carroll's art-horror theory from fusion-figure monsters to 'art-horror environments': the series' distinctive disgust comes not only from the Xenomorph's impure fusion of species traits but from the accidental contamination of clean human spaces - acid blood tunneling deck by deck through the Nostromo, hive webbing repurposing Hadley's Hope as a nursery ('kill me'), and Ripley absorbed into the Queen's fleshy mass at the end of Resurrection. These are accidents that could have been avoided and that break horror's normal movement from disruption back to restored order, since contaminated ships and colonies can only be nuked, never redeemed.
- ch. 18 "Death" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer, Michel Foucault, G.W.F. Hegel, Plato — Power/Knowledge; Philosophical Pessimism; Tripartite Soul — Reads Alien3's Fury 161 through impurity and contagion: Mary Douglas's 'matter out of place,' Foucault's confinement of the mad (the YY-chromosome inmates quarantined as social infection; Golic's fixation on the 'dragon' as Schopenhauer's madness of a fixed idea), Hegel and Schelling's philosophies of biology in which disease, decline, and death are necessary moments of life, and Plato's virtue-as-health of the soul. Ripley's fall into the molten lead embraces Schelling's 'indifference' of freedom and its enemy - she refuses Bishop's quack offer to 'deal with the malignancy' so Weyland-Yutani can never harvest the queen inside her.
- ch. 19 "Part V Sex and Gender" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "I’m an Alien. And That’s OK" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Sigmund Freud — Embodiment (the lived body) — Reads Ripley's arc through Alison Stone's maternal subjectivity: her mothering of Jonesy and Newt shows the maternal is relational as much as bodily (neither ever gestated in her), Mother (MU/TH/UR) and the Queen stage the 'malevolent mother' against her ('Get away from her, you BITCH!'), and Alien3's unwanted pregnancy forces a violent separation in which only death asserts her selfhood. In Resurrection, Ripley-8 relives Helene Deutsch's quasi-infantile emotional fusion with her hybrid child before killing it, illustrating why Stone demands nonviolent differentiation rather than a complete break from the maternal body - 'I'm a stranger here myself.'
- ch. 21 "A Professional, a Mother, and Someone Who Cares" **·** Carol Gilligan, Nel Noddings — The Ethics of Care; Feminism — Asks whether Ripley is a feminist and answers 'only sometimes': her quarantine refusal ('Yes, I read you. The answer is negative') and her reasons for killing the Alien3 specimen ('a lot of innocent people will die') invoke rules, chain of command, and anonymous future victims - Kohlberg's post-conventional reasoning, not Gilligan's care voice - whereas the hot-chocolate scene with Newt and the unhesitating hive rescue exhibit the care-focused feminism of Gilligan and Noddings. Against the status-oriented reading of Vasquez's 'have you ever been mistaken for a man?' exchange, Ripley emerges as atypical of either stereotype: a professional, a mother, and someone who cares.
- ch. 22 "Equal Opportunity Offenders" **·** Marilyn Frye — Objectification; Feminism — Argues the Facehugger stages rape with the sexes reversed: because the aliens violate male and female bodies impartially - Kane's penetration, forced pregnancy, and death in childbirth; the cocooned Dallas begging Ripley to kill him - the films strip male viewers of the patriarchal privilege of treating sexual violence as someone else's threat and even of biological immunity to forced pregnancy. The Marines' 'colonists' daughters' banter, the prisoners' treatment of Ripley on Fury 161, and Johner's basketball comment exemplify a rape culture (Dworkin: rape is committed by exemplars of our norms) persisting into the twenty-second century, while Frye and Shafer's 'domain' explains why bodily violation attacks personhood itself.
- ch. 23 "Part VI Continental Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "“You are…a beautiful, beautiful, butterfly”" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; The Last Man; The Death of God — Argues the Xenomorph realizes Nietzsche's Ubermensch: it is what Ash describes - unclouded by 'conscience, remorse, or delusions of morality' - and perfects its predatory function down to its acidic blood, while paycheck-fixated crewmen like Brett and Parker are Nietzsche's complacent 'last men,' the type Zarathustra's townspeople clamor for. The alien's egg-Facehugger-Chestburster-adult metamorphosis maps onto the camel, lion, and child of the three metamorphoses of the spirit, and the chapter closes by wondering whether the creature's monstrosity might have made Nietzsche himself reconsider the vital perfection he preached.
- ch. 25 "One Must Imagine Ripley Happy" **·** Albert Camus — The Absurd; The Myth of Sisyphus; Rebellion and Revolt — Reads Ripley-8 through Camus's Myth of Sisyphus: discovering the room of failed clones confronts her with the absurd gap between her longing to be Ellen Ripley and the world's answer that she is lab-grown Number 8. Of Camus's three responses she refuses physical suicide (declining Call's knife - 'What makes you think I would let you do that?') and philosophical suicide (mocking the auton's programmed crossing herself in the chapel), instead living in revolt - 'I rebel--therefore we exist' - shared with Call, so that her serial extermination of Xenomorphs across four films becomes Sisyphean toil, and one must imagine Ripley happy.
- ch. 26 "Its Structural Perfection is Only Matched by its Autonomy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Anthropocentrism; The Ethics of Political Violence; Existence Precedes Essence — Defends the Xenomorphs: if the Queen's tactical intelligence (incubating her eggs under the reactor, ordering drones to hold fire, cutting the power) and the species' hive organization satisfy Sartre's criteria of self-creating persons, then their 'attacks' are legitimate self-defense on the model of Sartre's preface to Fanon's Wretched of the Earth, where the colonized's violence restores a denied personhood. Read without anthropocentrism, Alien is the story of a newborn extraterrestrial hunted to death by humans, and Weyland-Yutani, the Marines, and the colonists of LV-426 - not the aliens - are the series' true villains.
- ch. 27 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 28 "End User License Agreement" *(front)*

## American Horror Story and Philosophy - Life Is But a Nightmare

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Thanks" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "The Devil Made Us Write This" *(no index entry yet)* — The editors' introduction argues that American Horror Story is 'American' in its maximalist excess (the pilot's creepy house, breathing disemboweled cat, and bloody murder), in recycling every major American horror trope from vampires, witches, and ghosts to freak shows and slasher clowns, and in its social commentary on marginalized groups, race relations, and civil rights - so that stripped of horror it is about life in America, and underneath the gore it contains 'a lot of great philosophy', which the book's sixteen chapters extract.
- ch. 10 "I. Goddesses don’t speak in whispers. They scream" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "1. What’s So Scary about Demonic Possession" **·** René Descartes, John Locke — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Mind–Body Dualism; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Using the Asylum possession arc - the boy who rips open the pig's heart, the demon exposing Sister Jude's hit-and-run secret and whispering 'You're Bloody Face' to Thredson, and Sister Mary Eunice's corruption - the chapter argues that possession stories terrify because they break our identity judgments through time, the same judgments on which moral responsibility, criminal punishment (the Manson family parole cases, Phineas Gage), trust, and personal safety depend. Descartes's mind-body union and psychological-continuity theories of identity explain why sameness of body can no longer guarantee sameness of person, and why we fear our own identity being erased next.
- ch. 12 "2. A Death Worth Living" **·** Bernard Williams — Immortality and the Soul; The Meaning of Life; The Myth of Sisyphus — Argues that dying in the Murder House or the Hotel Cortez removes death's usual harms - Ben Harmon can reunite with his ghost family and keep counseling patients, and Liz Taylor's chosen death brings reunion with Tristan plus open-ended projects protecting the hotel - but then poses Bernard Williams's boredom problem: finite projects run out over eternity and replacing them would make you a different person. Richard Taylor's Sisyphus and the Hotel's contented Laundress supply the answer that repetitive tasks embraced as meaningful can sustain even an immortal afterlife.
- ch. 13 "3. My Sister, My Self" **·** W.V.O. Quine — Ship of Theseus; Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Teleporter Paradox (personal fission) — Staged as a remembered backstage conversation among Elsa's freak show performers, the chapter has conjoined twins Bette and Dot work through the Ship of Theseus, the psychological-continuity theory of identity and its coma and false-memory counterexamples, Quine's claim that persons are only convenient posits rather than real entities, split-brain cases suggesting one body can host two minds, and fission worries about what would happen if they were separated - to ask whether they are one person or two, concluding it is a genuine paradox with no agreed answer.
- ch. 14 "4. How to Live through a Horror Story and Still Find Your Self" *(no index entry yet)* — A survival guide built on Charles Taylor's philosophy of the self: reality 'discloses' different possibilities depending on one's attunement (Ben Harmon sees the young Moira, the Harmons and the Roanoke families misread their houses), the medieval enchanted cosmos of porous selves explains Sister Mary Eunice's demon, the cursed rubber suit, and Billie Dean Howard's trauma-soaked houses, while the buffered self of the disenchanted universe - Arden locating the devil in the frontal gyrus, John Lowe demanding evidence - invites gaslighting, and full porousness brings its own horrors (Kyle, Cricket). The lesson: keep a pragmatic, open mind and share reality with a community.
- ch. 15 "5. Other People’s Body Parts" **·** Aristotle, Maurice Merleau-Ponty, Avicenna (Ibn Sina), Plato — Aristotle on the Soul; Embodiment (the lived body); Potentiality and Actuality — Explains Kyle Spencer's horror at the body Madison and Zoe assembled from other people's parts through the history of soul-body theories: Aristotle's De Anima account of the soul as the faculties of a living body (plus the Physiognomonics claim that small people have small minds - poor Ma Petite), Plato's Timaeus on body parts designed for philosophy and reincarnation into bodies fitted to one's vices, Avicenna's soul as a faculty-binding nexus that chooses its bodily vehicle like a vehicle serves its owner, and Merleau-Ponty's habitual body (the Axeman's axe as part of his body, phantom limbs), which best explains why walking around on someone else's legs 'has got to mess with you a little'.
- ch. 16 "II. When bad things keep happening to good people, you start to question what is right and what is wrong" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "6. Horror Can Be Great Drama" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Golden Mean — Applies Aristotle's Poetics wholesale - mimesis of possible actions, the six elements of tragedy, complete plots of proper magnitude, reversal and recognition, hamartia, and catharsis as the perfecting of fear and pity - to argue that Murder House (Ben's lust as tragic flaw and his double reversal to redemption in death) and Asylum (Sister Jude's irascibility and pride punished then reformed) are genuine high tragedies, and then uses the Nicomachean Ethics (virtue as a mean between extremes; justice as both retributive and rehabilitative) to show the seasons teach virtue, while Coven, Freak Show, and Hotel fall short because their characters' fates are undeserved and the moral message goes murky.
- ch. 18 "7. And the Most Despicable Jessica Lange Character Is . ." **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle — The Categorical Imperative; The Ethics of Care; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); The Golden Mean — Ranks Jessica Lange's characters from least to most despicable using moral philosophy: Sister Jude earns credit for acting from duty (reporting the Nazi Dr. Arden at personal risk, Kant-style) and for genuinely feeling remorse; Elsa Mars partly honors the matriarchal obligations of care ethics but lacks Aristotle's habitual virtue and moderation, selling Bette and Dot to Dandy for fame; Constance Langdon abuses and effectively murders her disabled children; and Fiona Goode, motivated exclusively by youth, beauty, and power, murders her Supreme and torments Cordelia - never once acting out of concern for others.
- ch. 19 "8. Night of the Living Disabled" **·** Philosophy of Disability — Reads Constance's treatment of Addy and Beau Langdon through the eugenic roots of the horror genre (Angela Smith's Hideous Progeny, The Black Stork, the Production Code): outward bodily difference assumed to prove inner defect, and the disabled treated as better off dead - Addy finding peace only as 'a pretty girl at last' after death, Beau suffocated in the attic. Against the medical model the chapter defends Alison Kafer's political-relational model and crip futurity (Tate's 'He only suffered because of you'), arguing that ableism, not disability itself, makes their lives unlivable and that imagining disabled futures differently is a matter of survival.
- ch. 20 "9. Voodoo Hoodoo" **·** Immortality and the Soul — Explains Coven's Spirit Wars through the real history and philosophy of New Orleans Voodoo: the historical Marie Laveaux as healer and Voodoo Queen, Tituba as the link between witchcraft and Voodoo in Salem, the loa and spirit possession (Maya Deren's gros bon ange), zombies and the bokor, Papa Legba as trickster gatekeeper rather than Satan, and the seven gates of Guinee. It argues that Laveau's annual innocent and Fiona's failed soul-selling enact the Voodoo cosmology in which hell is the earthly entrapment of the soul - and, as Legba says, 'everybody pays.'
- ch. 21 "10. Too Freaking Bad" **·** Philosophy of Disability — Reads Freak Show through Erving Goffman's Stigma - virtual versus actual social identity, the three types of stigma, discredited versus discreditable conditions, and spoiled identity - and through an analysis of exploitation, dignity, and consent: Elsa's claim that the real monsters are 'the people outside this tent', the courts' defense of freaks' right to perform, and the argument that Ryan Murphy's own casting - real disabled performers as window dressing behind able-bodied stars playing freaks, with roles too scarce for true consent - replicates the exploitation the season condemns, which may be the real horror story.
- ch. 22 "III. I have long stopped asking why the mad do mad things" *(front)*
- ch. 23 "11. 999 Fingers" **·** The Buddha, Friedrich Nietzsche — The Four Noble Truths; Karma and Rebirth; Impermanence (anicca); The Death of God — Voiced by a composite being shredded together from every AHS character, the chapter retells the Buddha's path - Siddhartha sheltered from suffering like a pampered prince, enlightenment as watching 'every episode of American Horror Story', Mara's temptations, and Angulimala with his garland of 999 fingers converted to monkhood - to read Briarcliff's cruelty, Sister Jude's devil, and Bloody Face's son Johnny as suffering born of clinging, in a contingent, moment-to-moment world of karma and rebirth, while Nietzsche's killed God, eternal recurrence, and ressentiment frame the question of who is to blame when there is no creator.
- ch. 24 "12. A Month from Now, I’ll Be a Balding, Toothless Skeleton" **·** The Buddha — The Four Noble Truths; Impermanence (anicca); Non-Self (anatman) — Reads Fiona Goode's war against fading - the experimental RM47 serum, rejecting the Axeman's escape because he would watch her decay, the Papa Legba bargain that collapses because she has no soul - alongside Delphine LaLaurie's blood facials and Marie Laveau's child sacrifices, as illustrations of Buddhist dukkha and the three thirsts (kama for sense pleasure, bhava for existence, vibhava for annihilation). Legba's 'you don't have a soul' becomes an accidentally liberating glimpse of anatta, and Cordelia's dying advice - 'feel the fear... let it all go' - is the mindfulness, echoing Buddhaghosa's corpse meditations, that might have freed Fiona.
- ch. 25 "13. Burn the Witch!" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Augustine of Hippo — Natural Law; Feminism; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Argues Coven is a feminist counterpoint to Aquinas's Natural Law - the reason-grounded doctrine of universal human nature historically wielded against 'unnatural' women, from Zoe's vagina-dentata power to Misty burned in the bayou - and, drawing on Augustine's Confessions ('Things that exist are good', so corruptibility implies goodness to lose) and Lady Macbeth's 'unsex me here', shows that women who are both witch and woman break any single natural moral code; Myrtle Snow, who embraces her double nature and goes 'proudly to the flame', proves Natural Law works only as Cupp-style discernment, not as commandments.
- ch. 26 "IV. I am tough, but I’m no cookie" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "14. The Absurdity at the Heart of Horror" **·** Albert Camus — The Absurd; The Myth of Sisyphus; The Meaning of Life — Uses Camus's Myth of Sisyphus to argue that AHS's deepest horror is absurdity - the human quest for meaning in a meaningless universe: the Harmons' doomed attempts to protect each other in the Murder House mirror Sisyphus's futile push, Dr. Arden's experiments on aliens who mock him ('Arden you quack who would make a better duck?') show comprehension of the universe is beyond reach, the Countess's bored immortality and Ma Petite's casual murder stage the confrontation between human longing and a universe that cannot care, and the answer to 'why do bad things happen to good people?' is simply: 'because they do. There is no reason.'
- ch. 28 "15. Red Harvest in Roanoke" **·** Ecofeminism; Anthropocentrism; Animal Rights (subjects-of-a-life); Wilderness and Nature Aesthetics — Reads Roanoke through ecocriticism and ecofeminism: the culture-nature dualism collapses as the urban Millers' wooded retreat turns predatory (rain of teeth, pig-men); the Butcher's backstory - a colonial woman starved by men whose blood-sacrifice pact makes the land flourish - aligns women and Nature as co-exploited by patriarchy, with Shelby's 'irrational' warnings ignored and Lee's maternal fury a force of nature; the animalizing of victims (pig-headed roasting, the Polks' barn-raised children, Matt's tryst with the Witch of the Wood) dissolves the human/animal hierarchy; and the media crew's attempt to package wilderness for ratings ends with Nature striking back at Agnes in the woods.
- ch. 29 "16. Please Don’t Kill Me! (But if You Do, Could You Make a Video )" **·** Michel de Montaigne — Immortality and the Soul; Being-Toward-Death; The Meaning of Life — Reads Roanoke through Stephen Cave's Mortality Paradox, Ernest Becker's Denial of Death, and Terror Management Theory: the Blood Moon ghosts embody inevitable death, dramatic irony mirrors our denial, reality-TV fame and Twitter followers are the modern immortality project (Agnes's 'I just wanted to be on TV', the superfans killed mid-broadcast), and the Polks' ritual cannibalism is symbolic power over extinction. Montaigne's 'to study philosophy is to learn to die' and his savoring of fleeting moments close the chapter, with Shelby - who alone submits to death without fiction - as the figure who actually escapes the terror.
- ch. 30 "American Haunting Story" *(no index entry yet)* — A companion travelogue cataloging real haunted American sites paralleling each season - the Los Feliz Murder House and Winchester Mystery House behind Murder House, the Trans-Allegheny Asylum and Waverly Hills Sanatorium behind Asylum, Marie Laveau's tomb in St. Louis Cemetery #1 behind Coven, the Stanley Hotel of The Shining - from haunted houses, asylums, cemeteries, and prisons to the RMS Queen Mary and the Lizzie Borden B&B. It argues hauntings are a pervasive feature of American history and folklore, and makes no philosophical argument of its own.
- ch. 31 "Bibliography" *(notes)*
- ch. 32 "The Coven" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "Index" *(notes)*

## Amy Schumer and Philosophy - Brainwreck!

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Dear Amy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "I. Persona Non Grata" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "1. Amy’s Self-Confidence and Self-Deprecation" **·** Sigmund Freud — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Argues that Schumer's self-deprecating and gallows humor succeeds when it models transcendence, connection, and acceptance, drawing on Freud's relief theory of laughter as a discharge of repressed energy. Atkinson reads the Trainwreck funeral scene where Amy turns on Aaron after her father's burial, plus the memoir's 'grape' and clam-chowder consent jokes, as gallows humor inviting audiences to laugh both at and with her. She contrasts the failing Snatched tapeworm and flirting scenes, which degrade Emily rather than showing her overcoming her circumstances, and frames the whole practice as humility rather than humiliation.
- ch. 10 "2. “Brave”" *(no index entry yet)* — Uses the superiority theory of humor to ask who the butt of a self-deprecating joke really is. Alloy first worries that jokes like 'I've only been with four people' reaffirm the standards behind slut-shaming and body-shaming, then argues that good self-deprecation makes the shaming standard itself the butt, reading 'Last Fuckable Day' and the '12 Angry Men Inside Amy Schumer' jury sketch as ridiculing the chastity, sobriety, and thinness norms imposed on women. That is why Schumer is 'brave': not for the underwear photo, but for mocking those who called it brave.
- ch. 11 "3. Schumer’s Selfishness" **·** Adam Smith, James Rachels, Sigmund Freud — Ethical Egoism; Is–Ought Problem — Weighs psychological egoism, the thesis that every action is ultimately self-interested, against apparent altruism through Schumer's cluelessly selfish characters: the 'Cancer Excuse' sketch where Amy exploits Tig Notaro's diagnosis, God's dismay at her in 'Herpes Scare', and Emily Middleton guilt-tripping her mother into an Ecuador trip in Snatched. Arp deploys Adam Smith's Chinese-earthquake passage from The Theory of Moral Sentiments and Rachels's Lincoln piglets story before rejecting the argument for ethical egoism on is-ought and coherence grounds, concluding Schumer's selfish jerks are comic negative examples of how not to live.
- ch. 12 "4. To Be or Not to Be Perfect" **·** Plato, René Descartes, Aristotle — Mind–Body Dualism — Asks whether chasing externally imposed perfection is worthwhile and reads Schumer's comedy as an invitation to drop society's 'suggested' standards, from the impossible pre-sex grooming ritual of 'Sex Prep' to the pole-dancing hypocrisy of 'Judging Strippers'. Flieswasser tests three models of human perfection: the Platonic and Cartesian privileging of mind over body, monotheistic modesty, and Aristotle's telos as an individual purpose. She then lands on the Japanese aesthetics of wabi-sabi and kintsugi, where broken pieces are rejoined into something more beautiful, as the truest picture of Schumer's self-acceptance.
- ch. 13 "II. Schumer the Philosopher" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "5. The Funny Thing about Being Yourself" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Richard Rorty — Eternal Recurrence; Amor Fati (love of fate); Authenticity — Reads Schumer's comedy as Nietzschean self-creation: like the tightrope walker over the abyss she confronts who she is, says 'Yes' to life in the spirit of the eternal recurrence and amor fati, and 'gives style' to her character by wearing mistakes as badges of honor, notably reclaiming her paparazzi photos in the Leather Special. Balke then casts her as Rorty's ironist, whose continual burning of old material keeps her free of a 'final vocabulary' in a contingent world, with 'Amy Goes Deep' doing the Rortian work of testing herself against others. He closes with George Herbert Mead's account of a self formed through social interaction with significant others.
- ch. 15 "6. No Wisdom to Offer You" **·** Socrates, Plato — Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); Socratic Method (elenchus) — Distinguishes public intellectuals like Chomsky, who deliver their own conclusions, from public philosophers who get audiences thinking for themselves, and argues Schumer is the latter. Her memoir claim that she has 'no wisdom to offer' is set beside the Socratic ignorance of Plato's Apology, where wisdom is worth nothing. Sketches like 'Football Town Nights', whose coach condemns rape only to praise violent domination, and 'The Trial of Bill Cosby' withhold conclusions and force viewers to work out for themselves the links between football and rape culture, or between un-conflicted entertainment and justice.
- ch. 16 "7. Amy Schumer or the Incongruities" **·** Sigmund Freud, Michel Foucault — The Unconscious — Pits Freud's psychoanalytic theory of wit, where harmless nonsense smuggles tendentious sexual content past the superego's censor, against the incongruity theory of comic amusement, using 'Boner Doctor' as the test case. Carroll argues the psychoanalytic account makes the superego absurdly gullible, citing Foucault on how thoroughly sexuality is now discussed, and cannot capture what is specific to Schumer. Incongruity theory can: sketches like 'Sexting' and Trainwreck's doll analogy for infidelity generate laughter from violated norms of ladylike decorum, exposing her feminism as a sustained subversion of the modest, uninterested stereotype of womanhood.
- ch. 17 "8. Sexistentialism" **·** Albert Camus — The Absurd; The Myth of Sisyphus; Existentialism — Reads Schumer through Camus's absurd: Trainwreck's gender-reversed commitment-phobe and the sketch where friends kill themselves rather than accept a compliment expose the futility of finding meaning in patriarchal gender scripts. Browning argues that advocating change against so systemic a sexism is a Sisyphean task, and that the film's romantic-comedy ending is 'existential' because Amy freely chooses commitment and owns the absurdity rather than escaping it. The chapter ends by asking whether Schumer's jokes are a battle cry whose very pointlessness might be the point.
- ch. 18 "9. Girls Just Wanna Have Mediated Fun" **·** Alienation; Ideology and False Consciousness — Applies Guy Debord's society of the spectacle to Inside Amy Schumer: relationships and self-image become 'mediated' by women's magazines and advertising, so Amy hides under the bed in 'Sex Prep' rather than be seen unretouched, a manufactured boy band dictates 'natural' beauty in 'Girl, You Don't Need Makeup', and 'Publicity Stunt' ends with thin arms in photos mattering more than the prom disaster. Celebrity culture, reality-TV breakups, and 'Sext Photographer' illustrate having and appearing displacing being, while the spectacle's alienation and false consciousness surface in 'Judging Strippers'. The verite 'Amy Goes Deep' interviews and end-of-episode bloopers puncture the spectacle with glimpses of the real.
- ch. 19 "III. Misbehaving" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "10. Multiple Murderer Emily Middleton" **·** Aristotle — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Moral Luck — Treats Snatched as a series of case studies in personal responsibility: who originated an action, who created the situation, and what was intended. Emily's non-refundable tickets and drunken night out are hers, but she did not kidnap herself, and the film's title card ('The kidnappers did bad stuff, too') lures viewers out of victim-blaming. Her shovel killing of Morgado's nephew and accidental spear-gun killing of his son are judged through Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics III.2 on acts done in ignorance of circumstances, excusing the 'multiple murderer' while showing how perspective and intent shift blame.
- ch. 21 "11. The Unruly Woman" **·** Plato — Tripartite Soul; Justice in the Republic — Sets Plato's Republic case against the poets, with its censored storytellers, heroes who bear misfortune quietly, and worry that comedy feeds the soul's appetitive part, against Schumer's program of celebrating flaws. Young Amy raiding her roommate's hidden Twinkies and lasagna illustrates the appetitive soul overruling reason, and memoir chapters like 'Secret Bad Habits' do exactly what Plato feared by licensing readers to enjoy what they would be ashamed to confess. Li argues that the unruly women of Schumer, Dunham, Broad City, and Kaling legitimately answer Plato's ancient quarrel by representing messy, complicated female experience rather than modeling virtue.
- ch. 22 "12. Putting the Fun Back in Non-refundable" **·** David Hume, Friedrich Nietzsche, Plato — Theory of Forms — Defends Snatched against its 37 percent Rotten Tomatoes score by retracing art criticism from Plato's claim, grounded in his theory of copies and originals, that art is an inferior imitation of reality, to Hume's 'Of the Standard of Taste', where value judgments track social values and refined experts may devalue works that offend their morals. Against moralistic critics, the chapter argues some art, like Guernica or 'Football Town Nights', exists to upset us. It then reads the film through Nietzsche's Genealogy of Morals: Emily's guilt-free firing scene and her cajoling her deflated mother toward Ecuador enact the refusal of socially manufactured guilt, putting the 'fun' back in non-refundable.
- ch. 23 "IV Women Are Funny" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "13. The Laughter and the Fury" **·** Aristotle — The Male Gaze; Feminism — Situates Inside Amy Schumer between Aristotle's Poetics dictum that valor in a woman is inappropriate, his Politics claim of female inferiority, and the 1970s French feminists: Cixous's 'Laugh of the Medusa' breaking up truth with laughter and Irigaray's strategic mimesis. Kolb reads stereotype-performing sketches with violent endings, such as the 'I'm So Bad' women devouring a waiter's face and the 'You Are Gorgeous' anthem rewarding a faked miscarriage and an N-word rap, as resubmitting women to stereotypes to explode them. Casting-couch opener '2 Girls, 1 Cup', 'Focus Group', and '12 Angry Men' hold up a mirror to the male gaze and to the rage it produces when women stop performing for it.
- ch. 25 "14. Five Stereotypes that Sustain Grape Zones" **·** Miranda Fricker — Epistemic Injustice; Intersectionality — Explains 'grape', Schumer's grey area between rape and consent, through Miranda Fricker's hermeneutical gaps: bundles of stereotypes about the stranger rapist, the chaste victim, the resisting victim, the racialized 'barbarian rapist', and the unrapeable strong-ass woman leave holes in shared knowledge, so non-stereotypical rapes go unrecognized and unprosecuted. The 'Football Town Nights' players' excuses and the Leather Special's fat-but-brave monologue ridicule these schemas, while Brock Turner's lenient sentence versus Brian Banks's imprisonment shows how intersecting racist and sexist biases protect white rapists and discredit victims. The chapter concludes that debunking the five stereotypes shrinks the grape zones.
- ch. 26 "15. Are There Certain Things We Should Not Talk About" **·** Simone de Beauvoir — Woman as the Other; One Is Not Born a Woman; Objectification — Reads Schumer's taboo-breaking through The Second Sex: man makes woman the mysterious Other, and silences around menstruation, aging, and wives' desire keep that mystery intact. 'Last Fuckable Day' dramatizes the expiry of the idolized 'real woman', 'Sex Prep' shows the metamorphosis into a sex-representation collapsing into hiding under the bed, and '12 Angry Men' stages a male jury defining a woman's value. Ehrnberger argues Schumer's demystifying candor, from publishing her teenage journals to calling herself a 'flawed fuckup', dissolves the Other's aura and restores power, exactly as Beauvoir predicted when women assert themselves as human beings.
- ch. 27 "16. Raucous Feminist or Racially Insensitive" **·** Kimberlé Crenshaw, bell hooks, Betty Friedan — Intersectionality; Feminism; The Feminine Mystique (the problem with no name) — Weighs Schumer's Peabody-honored 'raucous feminist' image against charges of racial insensitivity by contrasting liberal feminism, exemplified by Friedan's problem that has no name, with intersectionality: hooks's rejoinder that Friedan spoke only for white middle-class women and Crenshaw's multiply-burdened identities. Ware reads the 'Mostly Sex Stuff' jokes about Asian women and third-world bikini waxers, the Beyonce 'Formation' video with Goldie Hawn, and Schumer's dismissive tweets as speaking 'for women' while erasing difference, though the 'Herpes Scare' caricature shows her sometimes winking from inside an intentionally vapid persona. The verdict: Schumer can be both a genuine and an imperfect feminist who must still take accountability.
- ch. 28 "17. Switching It Up" **·** Immanuel Kant — Objectification; Feminism — Argues Trainwreck and the stand-up material on sexual 'ownership' expose patriarchal double standards: the same promiscuity that makes men players makes women sluts, while LeBron James's gossipy, overprotective best friend and John Cena's devoted, unthreatening boyfriend cast male stars in traditional rom-com female roles. Browning brings in Kant's view that sexual desire treats persons as objects to be discarded like squeezed lemons, conceding that counting conquests objectifies partners while rejecting Kant's celibate conclusion. Schumer's comedy instead shows women owning their orgasms, turning sex from competition into mutual gratification.
- ch. 29 "V. Afterthoughts" *(front)*
- ch. 30 "18. Oppression Pretty Much Sucks" **·** Iris Marion Young, John Rawls, W.E.B. Du Bois, Sigmund Freud — Double Consciousness; Justice as Fairness; The Veil of Ignorance — Reads I Feel Pretty through feminist critiques of oppression: Bartky's Fashion-Beauty Complex turns the desire to feel pretty into a parasitic scheme of creams and credit-card debt, Du Bois's double consciousness captures Renee post-head-injury seeing herself as beautiful against the world's verdict, and Freud's account of hating those most like us explains the Highlander-style rivalry between women. The chapter then dismantles Rawls's distributive justice via Iris Marion Young, arguing rights are not goods to hand out and that 'imagine everyone is the same' thinking entrenches privilege. The alternative is celebrating difference, which is exactly what Schumer's prettiness-as-self-celebration performs.
- ch. 31 "19. How to Feel Pretty" **·** Objectification; The Male Gaze — A satirical monologue voiced by 'The Patriarchy' that catalogs women's impossible, contradictory beauty demands: a painted face that must look natural, effortless long hair, a body that is simultaneously too fat and too thin, sexual availability that must never look eager, accomplishments that are unattractive, and finally a pretty death. The screed makes vivid that the standards exist only to keep women judging themselves as garbage, nailing the dynamic with Schumer-specific gags like 'Milk Milk Lemonade' as both a women-are-people reminder and a butt showcase, and Amy's 'catch a dick' line, before mocking workplace 'merit' talk that promotes Jim.
- ch. 32 "References" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "Author Bios" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "Index" *(notes)*

## Anime and Philosophy - Wide Eyed Wonder

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Anime and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "What These Otaku Are Saying about" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Popular Culture and Philosophy Series Editor George A. Reisch" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Previews and Coming Attractions" *(no index entry yet)* — The editors' introduction argues that anime — from Akira, Ghost in the Shell, and Neon Genesis Evangelion to My Neighbor Totoro, Nausicaa, and Grave of the Fireflies — is not children's fare but stories about 'monsters, witches, robots, children, and spirits who grapple with questions of societal violence, ethics, morality, justice, heroism, identity, and the soul.' It frames the book's project as finding what lies beneath anime's visual signature of big eyes and giant robots, illustrating the tone with epigraphs from Wolf's Rain ('If life has no purpose, then you're already dead') and Princess Tutu on fate versus defiance. It closes with housekeeping: anime means Japan-originated animation for this book, Western name order will be used, and the contributing artists and industry supporters are thanked.
- ch. 7 "Accidental Anime" *(no index entry yet)* — A distributor's memoir, not a philosophical essay: Margo Zimmerman recounts falling into anime at Manga Entertainment via Ninja Scroll and Perfect Blue, and the art-versus-business balance of bringing a niche genre to Western audiences. Its philosophy-adjacent cases are concrete: otaku demanded authenticity over adaptation (fans rejected the Southern-accent dubbing of Mima's country mother in Perfect Blue, and the contested translation of End of Evangelion's final line 'kimochi warui' — 'How disgusting' vs 'I feel sick' — still divides viewers), and she credits anime's serious commentary on mythology, philosophy, nuclear power, and humanity with influencing Western storytelling, citing The Matrix's obvious debt to Ghost in the Shell. No taxonomy philosopher or concept is actually deployed.
- ch. 8 "Body" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Wave Goodbye from the Catbus" **·** The Ghost in the Machine; Embodiment (the lived body); The Extended Mind — Heinricy argues anime is the richest medium for posthuman theory because it shows bodies 'in a state of becoming' rather than as grotesque boundary violations: Miyazaki's Catbus and Totoro, Haku's dragon transformation and Chihiro's pig parents in Spirited Away, and the cursed pig-headed aviator of Porco Rosso all treat morphing bodies as possibility, not horror. She sorts anime posthumanism into two valences: a 'Ghost in the Machine' dream of consciousness leaving the body altogether (Motoko merging with the net in Ghost in the Shell), which she rebuts via N. Katherine Hayles's point that consciousness is embodied and would change with any new body, and hybrid configurations like mecha (Gundam, Robotech) where body and machine drive each other. Following Donna Haraway, she contends we are all already cyborgs — vaccines, engineered food, treated water — and credits Japan's nuclear history (via Susan Napier) and its shape-shifting folklore for why anime embraces rather than fears the transformed body.
- ch. 10 "Looking Inwards" **·** The Male Gaze; Objectification; Feminism — Barber, Bryce, and Davis test Donna Haraway's emancipatory cyborg thesis against Gunslinger Girl, where the Social Welfare Agency 'adopts' orphaned and abused girls (Henrietta, Rico, Triela, Elsa, Claes), rebuilds their bodies as cybernetic weapons, and drug-conditions them into loyal assassins paired with male handlers. They argue the series realizes none of Haraway's post-gender potential: conditioning erodes memory and autonomy (Rico coolly shoots the porter Emilio who befriended her; Elsa murders her handler Lauro and kills herself; Triela suffers pointless menstrual cramps in a wombless body), while the girls' kawaii styling, teddy bears, and school uniforms make them objects of the male gaze whose cuteness disguises lethal power. The cyborgs' victimization ultimately reinforces traditional gender hierarchy and mirrors conformity-pressured Japanese youth, so the cyborg has 'regressed' from the autonomous heroines of Ghost in the Shell or Battle Angel Alita.
- ch. 11 "The Transforming Body" **·** Martin Heidegger — Embodiment (the lived body); Mind–Body Dualism; Dasein (being-there); The Extended Mind — McBlane reads Oshii's Ghost in the Shell as staging the collision between a humanist framework — Cartesian ghost-in-shell dualism where consciousness is the seat of identity — and a posthuman 'mindbody' of distributed cognition in which the body is an informational circuit extending into the net, so a data body is still a body. Motoko's crisis (is the real me the organic brain, the ghost, or nothing?) deepens through the hacked garbage-truck man ('There's nothing sadder than a puppet without a ghost') and resolves only in her fusion with the Puppet Master, whose line 'Your effort to remain what you are is what limits you' enacts the shift from one ghost to multiple, distributed subjectivities ending in a little girl's body with 'The net is vast.' Heidegger's Dasein and being-in-the-world supply the claim that access to Being expands beyond the human into technological and informational modes, while the film's angelic transcendence imagery shows how residual humanist mind-body splits persist even in a posthuman story.
- ch. 12 "Mind" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Is Everybody Disembodied (or, Have We Always Been Posthuman Beings )" **·** René Descartes, John Searle, Alan Turing, Aristotle, Plutarch, Heraclitus, Augustine of Hippo, Daniel Dennett — Personal Identity; The Cogito; Mind–Body Dualism; Ship of Theseus — Stevens uses the film's closing voice-over 'I am Tetsuo' in Otomo's Akira to run the classic identity-and-mind problems: Heraclitus's river and Plutarch's Ship of Theseus ask whether the grotesquely transformed, Akira-absorbed Tetsuo is still the same person, while Descartes's cogito — riffed as 'cogito ergo Tetsuo' — Turing's Test, and Searle's Chinese Room test whether a voice that seems to think proves a thinker behind it (syntax is not yet shown sufficient for semantics, though Edwin Hutchins's distributed cognition reads the Room from the inside out). Weighing embodiment (Pinker's brain-makes-mind, Dennett's self as a 'center of narrative gravity') against disembodiment (Akira levelling Neo-Tokyo from nothing but cryogenically stored remains; Tetsuo's matter teleportation), he concludes through the problem of representation — every utterance is re-presented, as in Augustine's guilty tears over Dido — that we always treat semblances of thinkers as thinkers, so we may have always been decentered, re-presented, posthuman beings no different from Tetsuo and Akira.
- ch. 14 "Questioning the Question" **·** William James, Blaise Pascal — The Will to Believe; Pascal's Wager; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Hartung takes Ross's snapping reply to Armitage's repeated 'Why are you helping me?' in Armitage the Third: Poly-Matrix as a misreading: she wants a practical reason for his choice, not theoretical evidence, and since no evidence can prove an android is a person, William James's 'Will to Believe' applies — Ross's belief in her moral standing is a live, forced, and momentous genuine option (suspending judgment means she is scrapped, and losing her means losing a possible life together), so his passions may legitimately decide. He contrasts this with evidentialist cases where suspending judgment is right (the shuttle company skipping inspection; Dr. Asakura's silicon-based-life hypothesis) and with Pascal's Wager, reading Mars's church-empty, hedonistic culture of sex-tourism Seconds as one that denies genuine options and so kills Thirds. The attached original screenplay 'Eye Am,' a sequel to Magnetic Rose, extends the theme as salvage officer Nadine Beckner is lured by the AI Eva/Sam impersonating her dead husband Heintz, with Schrodinger-style observer-affected reality deciding which version becomes real.
- ch. 15 "Spirit" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "In the Palm of Your Hand" *(no index entry yet)* — Callis reads Spirited Away as Miyazaki's bid to make audiences feel rather than doctrinally learn Shinto, deploying Thomas Kasulis's distinction between existential Shinto (awe before kami: Chihiro's soot-sprite rescue, cleansing the trash-choked River God, makoto no kokoro purity) and essentialist Shinto (State Shinto's war-era nationalism, visually evoked by the abandoned 1940s train station and the train that haunts the whole film). She argues the drive into the spirit world is a compressed visual history of Shinto — folk shrines, Buddhist guardian statues, torii — and that Chihiro's stolen and recovered name enacts Japan's need to remember its traditions without forgetting how they were abused, supported by Miyazaki's own quotes on rural solstice bath rituals and Tarkovsky-style nostalgia. The three endings (Zeniba's 'Nothing that happens is ever forgotten' vs the Disney dub's memory-will-return line, and Miyazaki's preferred solo train ride) all say the same: the kami spark, like Thich Nhat Hanh's ancestors in the palm of the hand, is carried forward.
- ch. 17 "Gay Angels, Female Cardinals, and Bishōnen Popes" **·** Immanuel Kant, Augustine of Hippo — Phenomena and Noumena; Moral Relativism; The Two Cities — Barkman argues that anime's Christian imagery — the legendary Santa on a cross, Earthian's gay angel lovers, Trinity Blood's bishonen pope and female cardinal, Evangelion's hostile Angels — encodes Japanese religious pluralism rather than orthodox Christianity. He lays out the pluralist position (following Kant's phenomena/noumena distinction and John Hick: Ultimate Reality is only equivocally describable, so doctrine matters less than spiritual mood), contrasts it with exclusivism and inclusivism, and charges pluralism with self-undermining intolerance, then traces Japan's historical demonization of Christianity from the 1637 Shimabara massacre (Ninja Resurrection, Samurai Champloo) to Eva's alien invaders, with Armageddon imagery fueled by the atomic bombs. Because Shinto-Buddhist-Daoist anti-realism dissolves fixed categories, anime dissolves good and evil (Chrno Crusade's I Ching balance) and gender itself, gravitating to the symbol-rich 'jungle' of Catholicism (C.S. Lewis's phrase) — and where Trinity Blood lets some vampires be righteous, Augustine's City of God marks the one nearly orthodox note.
- ch. 18 "The Legend Has Come True. The Wind Has Come Back" **·** Immanuel Kant — Consequentialism; Humanity as an End in Itself; Anthropocentrism — Haas opposes two princesses of Nausicaa of the Valley of the Wind: Kushana of Tolmekia, whose 'whatever it takes' survivalism (invading Pejite, resurrecting a God-Warrior) is consequentialism pursued to its limit, and Nausicaa, whose attention to each act — befriending the biting fox-squirrel, staying her sword over her father's killer because vengeance would doom the Valley — restricts means, not just ends. The reductio comes from Pejite, whose torture of a baby Ohmu to bait a stampede on the Valley is exactly what strict outcome-maximization requires; against this, Kant's demand to treat beings as ends in themselves explains Nausicaa's 'mysterious power' of empathy. Because she respects the jungle and the sentient Ohmu rather than viewing nature anthropocentrically as a plague to burn, she discovers the plants are purifying the war-poisoned earth — the knowledge that saves everyone and proves Kushana's conquering approach self-defeating even on its own terms.
- ch. 19 "Conflict" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "Freeing Clausewitz’s Soul from Gravity" **·** Michael Walzer — Just War Theory — Melancon tests Just War Theory against Gundam's One Year War: Gihren Zabi's 'Sieg Zeon' funeral oration and Char Aznable's rhetoric show how the vague criterion of comparative justice lets a militarist regime dress aggression — the Three Seconds of Declaration gassing of colonies, Operation British's colony drop on Earth — in just-war language. On the conduct side, Ramba Ral's troops spare civilians and differentiate child targets on White Base, yet Zeon's still-unjust ad bellum position cannot be rehabilitated; and asking whether WMDs are inherently immoral, he uses Anavel Gato's nuclear strike on the Federation fleet review (Operation Stardust) — discriminating, proportional, militarily necessary — to argue weapons are amoral tools judged only by Walzer-style jus in bello criteria. Clausewitz resolves the theory's usefulness: war's inner tendency toward the absolute must stay nested within political goals, and Zeon's drift from fighting for independence into conquering and governing Earth shows exactly how a war that abandons just-war limits loses sight of what victory even is.
- ch. 21 "Redemption and Hope for the Future" **·** Aristotle — The Golden Mean; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Peters applies Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics account of courage — the mean between confidence and fear, shown only in facing an honorable death in war for a noble motive — to Colin MacLeod's two-thousand-year hunt for Marcus Octavius, the Immortal Roman who killed his wife Moya, in Highlander: The Search for Vengeance. Because MacLeod fights from pain and rage, 'like wild beasts,' and secretly seeks death as refuge from grief ('to seek death as a refuge from poverty, or love... is not the act of a brave man'), Aristotle would judge him a coward despite his fearlessness — Amergan the spirit guide diagnoses him the same way. Redemption comes only when Dahlia and the villagers threatened by Marcus's new Rome give him a noble cause and hopefulness: he becomes their general, fights with discipline instead of rage, and turns Marcus's own quickening to destroying the virus, illustrating the chapter's closing thought that for an Immortal dying is easy and living courageously is hard.
- ch. 22 "Don’t Be a Vegeta" **·** Adam Smith — The Invisible Hand; Social Darwinism — Argues that shonen fighting anime stage a choice between Adam Smith's and Darwin's models of competition: Zoro hunting Mihawk in One Piece, Goku's technique-by-technique training, and Rock Lee's specialization show competition improving people within the limits of a human excellence, while Cell's absorbing evolutions, the Super Saiyan transformations, and Ichigo's risky hollowification represent limitless 'leveling up' that changes what you are. The chapter concludes that the Darwinian win-at-all-costs model produces the perpetually dissatisfied Vegeta, and that framing competition around being a certain kind of person (a great swordsman, not just a winner) gives life a completable aim — while conceding that Darwinians rightly warn our picture of the 'normal human' has unjustly cast off jinchūriki like Naruto and Gaara and Devil Fruit users like Luffy.
- ch. 23 "Heroes" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "Alchemic Heroism" **·** Yin and Yang; Buddhism — Reads Edward and Alphonse Elric as a single composite 'alchemic hero' who fuses Japanese heroic archetypes: the dual-hero rooted in the Izanami-Izanagi creation myth and yin-yang complementarity (brash yang Ed, calm yin Al whose armored body actually fights better), the student hero forged by Izumi Curtis's brutal island training, and the wandering redemption seeker atoning for the forbidden attempt to resurrect their mother. It argues that the sacrifice built into this fusion — Al's lost senses, puberty, and body — mirrors Japan's own cultural alchemy of absorbing Buddhism, Confucianism, and yin-yang thought while retaining a coherent national identity.
- ch. 25 "The Sacrificial Lamb" **·** Martin Heidegger, Theodor Adorno, Plato — The Question Concerning Technology; Theory of Forms — Argues Astro Boy re-enacts the sacrificial logic of the atomic age: Atomu is the nuclear-powered copy of the dead Tobio, born from Dr. Tenma's refusal to accept mortality, and his repeated self-sacrifice for humans who discard him (as in 'Save the Classmate,' where the rescued boys' parents still want him removed) shows him functioning as Heidegger's 'standing-reserve' — an object valued only as stored power, with humans themselves next in line. Drawing on Plato's Republic anxiety over originals and copies, Adorno and Horkheimer's dialectic of enlightenment, and Rey Chow's account of mimesis as the sign left by sacrifice, the chapter reads the series as mourning an innocence that became impossible after Hiroshima and Nagasaki.
- ch. 26 "The Aftermath of Tragedy" **·** Aristotle — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Potentiality and Actuality — Argues that Grave of the Fireflies works as a critique of anime's child-hero convention: Seita acts exactly like a plucky anime protagonist when he defies his not-actually-evil aunt, strikes out on his own with Setsuko, ignores the farmer's advice to apologize and return, and demands a quick fix from the doctor — but these 'heroic' choices kill them both, because he lacks the developed capacity to reason. Against the medieval 'child as little adult' picture that most anime (Dragon Ball, Pokémon, Yu-Gi-Oh!) romanticizes, the chapter deploys Aristotle's view of children as unfinished beings who cannot yet reason or achieve happiness, concluding the film is a cautionary tale about the years of nurture and guidance real children need.
- ch. 27 "Devils" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "Equivalent Exchange" **·** John Locke, Karl Marx, Aristotle, Thomas Aquinas — Labor Theory of Property; Alienation; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Reads alchemy's law of equivalent exchange as a parable of capitalism: Locke's labor-mixing argument explains how hours of a life become property and why money is 'distilled life'; Aristotle's attack on limitless wealth-getting (the Midas problem) and Aquinas's capital vices explain how pursuing money for its own sake brings the homunculi — Greed, Pride, and the Sloth the Elric brothers' forbidden act produced — into the world. Marx's 1844 Manuscripts supply the punchline that money performs 'human alchemy' on people, buying their time, esteem, and bodies the way red stones are made from sacrificed humans, so the chapter concludes that capital, like the Philosopher's Stone, profits only by consuming lives and that markets must be held to a promise of fairness and care beyond mere exchange.
- ch. 29 "Textually Transmitted Disease" **·** Aristotle, Plato — Objectification — Traces hentai from Hokusai's 'Diver and Two Octopi' through the 1920s eroguro journals to the Lolita Anime and Cream Lemon OVAs, then asks whether even this extreme material has value as fantasy. The chapter frames the media-effects debate with Aristotle's Poetics catharsis (revived by Feshbach and tentatively supported by Milton Diamond's finding that Japanese rape rates fell as pornography spread) against Plato's Republic worry that sensational works license irrationality (updated by the Disinhibition, Desensitization, and Cultivation theories), and concludes that hentai's real harm is attitudinal rather than behavioral: its ritual 'breaking' of strong women and sex-as-something-done-to-passive-girls cultivates misogyny and a dehumanizing view of sex.
- ch. 30 "18 - The Devil Within Sara Livingston" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "Future Perfect" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "Who Wants to Be Human Anyway" **·** Gilbert Ryle, René Descartes — The Ghost in the Machine; Atman and Brahman; Mind–Body Dualism — Reads Kenji Kawai's thrice-repeated minyo 'Ghost theme' as the key to Major Kusanagi's quest in Ghost in the Shell and Innocence: each richer orchestration tracks her progress from manufactured shell, through the diving-scene worry that only others' treatment makes her human, to merging with the Puppet Master. Using Koestler's holons — individuals preserved while functioning within an evolving whole — and his view of consciousness as emergent (itself a rejection of both Descartes's immaterial soul and Ryle's dismissal of the 'ghost in the machine'), the chapter argues Oshii first celebrates the Major's Atman-like self-transcendence, then subverts Koestler in Innocence by making machines human: the ghost-dubbed gynoids act as a voiceless pack while the Major, still recognizably herself, aids Batou from the net — and Kawai's music literally gives the dolls a voice.
- ch. 33 "Cyborg Goddess" **·** Gilbert Ryle, René Descartes, Plato — The Ghost in the Machine; Mind–Body Dualism; Allegory of the Cave — Reads Ghost in the Shell as Donna Haraway's 'Cyborg Manifesto' made flesh: Major Kusanagi is built not born, has no sexuality or romance, and relegates male partners to sidekicks, enacting Haraway's post-gender cyborg that breaks the dualisms binding women to body, nature, and reproduction. The chapter traces the 'ghost' back through Koestler to Ryle's mockery of Descartes's mind-body dualism, reads the genderless, net-born Puppet Master as the Singularity of transhumanist philosophy, and interprets the Major–Puppet Master fusion — urged to 'come out of the shadows and into the light' in an explicit Plato's-cave gesture — as a posthuman, techno-spiritual 'Cyborg Goddess' that renders both gender and humanity obsolete.
- ch. 34 "One Last Anguished Existential Scream" **·** Martin Heidegger — The They-Self (das Man); Authenticity; Free Will vs Determinism — Argues Neon Genesis Evangelion and Akira dramatize a justified anxiety about humanity's evolutionary future: SEELE's Human Instrumentality Project — dissolving everyone into the Sea of LCL because separate, mutually harming individuals have 'run the course' of their evolution — is Heideggerian anxiety (the objectless unease that breaks us out of 'the They') curdled, via Mary Midgley's 'fatalism,' into the belief that genes, Moore's Law, or 'the Market' leave no room for human choice. Akira's Numbered Children, who sacrifice themselves to remove Tetsuo and save Kaneda, model the responsible alternative: accepting that the power is coming, screaming about it, and still choosing how humanity faces it rather than abdicating to fate.
- ch. 35 "Anime and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 36 "ALTERNATE ENDING" *(no index entry yet)* — A closing personal essay arguing that anime's defining gift is the genuinely unexpected — a robot sprouting from Naota's forehead bump in Fooly Cooly, Berserk's catastrophic finale, Samurai Champloo's Edo-meets-hip-hop fusion, Paprika's dream parade — which jolts viewers into noticing and rethinking their expectations, the very opening that philosophic inquiry requires. It also credits anime's sincere, two-way engagement with its audience (the cast-and-crew interviews on DVD extras that won the author over) for the genre's ongoing, unpredictable evolution.
- ch. 37 "Credits" *(notes)*
- ch. 38 "Subtitles" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "Making Of . ." *(notes)*
- ch. 40 "Easter Eggs" *(notes)*
- ch. 41 "Deleted Scenes" *(notes)*
- ch. 42 "Scene Selections" *(notes)*
- ch. 43 "Anime and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 44 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Anthony Bourdain and Philosophy - An Appetite for Life

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Start to Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "The Menu" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "The Last Supper Game" **·** Socrates — The Unexamined Life; Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know) — This editor's introduction frames Bourdain as a modern philosopher by playing a variation on his 'Last Meal' game and choosing Bourdain as the ideal dinner companion. It argues that Bourdain's professed humility about how little he knows ('realizing how small I am, and unwise') echoes Socratic ignorance and the love of wisdom, and previews the volume's questions about happiness, authenticity, taste, and freedom.
- ch. 8 "I Taste" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "1. A Man for All Seasonings" **·** Plato, Socrates, David Hume — Allegory of the Cave; Man Is the Measure (sophistic relativism) — Shaffer uses Brillat-Savarin's distinction between direct and reflective taste and Hume's 'Of the Standard of Taste' to argue gastronomic expertise is only the acquired ability to describe tastes richly, not a superior sense. He then casts Bourdain as a culinary Socrates who, like the freed prisoner in Plato's allegory of the cave, returns to lead provincial eaters out of the darkness of narrow experience toward cultural tolerance.
- ch. 10 "2. To Eat or Not to Eat" **·** Jeremy Bentham, Epicurus — The Hedonic Calculus; Utilitarianism; Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia) — Barta and Miranda read Bourdain's 'good eating is all about risk' credo through Bentham's cost-benefit hedonic calculus, sorting risk/reward into Ascetic, Epicurean, Cyrenaic, and Utilitarian quadrants. They classify Bourdain's fugu-eating and drunk cliff-driving as failed calculations, his comfort-food returns as Epicurean, and his gluttonous adventures (French Laundry, Russian vodka) as Cyrenaic high-risk/high-reward living.
- ch. 11 "3. The Nasty Bits" **·** Aristotle — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime; The Absurd — Calef asks how Bourdain could find disgusting foods (cobra heart, blood soup, warthog anus) delicious, using Korsmeyer's taxonomy of disgust and the 'paradox of aversion.' He argues their appeal comes from Aristotelian learning-through-representation, Burke's and Bullough's sublime experienced at psychical distance, and Kristeva's 'abject' — foods that remind us of death and mortality let Bourdain transcend and disarm it by eating them appreciatively.
- ch. 12 "4. How Anthony Bourdain United the Local and the Universal" **·** Immanuel Kant, Karl Marx, Diogenes the Cynic — Stoic Cosmopolitanism (citizens of the world); Perpetual Peace; Commodity Fetishism — Barta and Miranda contrast Kant's 'universally communicable' taste with Bourdieu's view that taste is a social construct shaped by field, habitus, and cultural capital that reinforces class distinctions. Using Bourdain's episodes (Portuguese bacalao, New York hot dogs, the French Laundry), they argue he synthesizes both: acknowledging taste's locality while democratizing it as a cosmopolitan 'citizen of the world' in the tradition of Diogenes and Kant's perpetual peace.
- ch. 13 "II Life" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "5. Friendship, Fun, Freedom, Food, and the Future" **·** Plato, Socrates, Aristotle, Epictetus — The Philosopher King; Stoicism; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Le Gall and Taliaferro read Bourdain's Libya and Iran episodes of Parts Unknown through Pierre Hadot's 'philosophy as a way of life,' arguing that Gaddafi and Khomeini were failed Platonic philosopher-kings. They interpret ordinary Libyans and Iranians eating Uncle Kentucky chicken, playing pinball, or hosting feasts as popular 'spiritual exercises' — tatonnement — showing how the personal becomes political and how friendship, fun, freedom, and food nourish the Socratic care of the soul.
- ch. 15 "6. Authenticity in a World of Bullshido" **·** Aristotle, Martin Heidegger — The Golden Mean; Authenticity; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Falcioni uses Aristotle's doctrine of the mean and Gillian Russell's 'epistemic viciousness in the martial arts' to read Bourdain's obsession with Brazilian jiu-jitsu as a struggle to cultivate the virtues of humility, courage, and authenticity against the vices of 'bullshido' (fraudulent martial arts). He connects Bourdain's craving for BJJ's brutal, ego-slaying honesty to a Heideggerian authenticity, while noting Bourdain's return to old vices near the end.
- ch. 16 "7. Anthony Bourdain’s Extremely Objectionable Stories" **·** Plato, Aristotle, Thomas Hobbes — The Noble Lie; Euthyphro Dilemma — Holmes examines Bourdain's horror graphic novel Hungry Ghosts as cautionary folklore in the tradition of Aesop and EC Comics. He stages a debate between Aristotle, who in the Rhetoric endorses fables as accessible moral tools, and Plato, who in the Republic wants to censor frightening tales for children — yet Holmes shows Plato himself (Euthyphro, Laws) praised fear as 'modesty,' and notes Bourdain used cautionary tales (Ronald McDonald 'has cooties') to steer his own daughter from fast food.
- ch. 17 "8. Why Do We Care Who Bourdain Really Was" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Friedrich Nietzsche, Søren Kierkegaard — Bad Faith; The Apollonian and the Dionysian; Anxiety (the dizziness of freedom); Authenticity — Finn asks why fans cared about Bourdain's 'authenticity,' arguing his persona of freedom from bourgeois convention embodied Sartre's rejection of 'bad faith' while his meticulous control made him, per Keefe, 'Apollo in drag as Dionysus.' Reading the Hanoi/Obama episode as proof that even the President wanted to be Bourdain, she suggests his freedom (Kierkegaard's 'dizziness') may have become a trap, and proposes 'integrity' rather than authenticity as the word for his consistency.
- ch. 18 "9. Anthony Bourdain’s Inquiring Mind" **·** Charles Sanders Peirce — Pragmatism; Truth as What Works; The Pragmatic Method — Anderson and Mauro trace Bourdain's growth from the dogmatic provocateur of Kitchen Confidential to a Peircean inquirer, using Peirce's 'The Fixation of Belief' and its four methods (tenacity, authority, a priori, inquiry). They argue Bourdain's tattooed motto 'I am certain of nothing,' his surprise and self-correction in Cambodia, Beirut, Saudi Arabia, and Haiti, and his pragmatic theory of evolving truth make him a model of the self-correcting inquiring life.
- ch. 19 "III Death" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "10. Some Dark Genie" **·** John Stuart Mill, Sigmund Freud, Arthur Schopenhauer — The Death Drive (Thanatos); The Harm Principle; On Liberty — Calef asks whether Bourdain's choice to become a heroin addict could be rational and responsible, using Mill's On Liberty and Harm Principle (including the voluntary-slavery exception) to argue addiction is reversible and can minimize suffering when alternatives are bleak. He then reads Bourdain's self-destructive drives — the drunk cliff-driving 'death trip' — through Freud's death drive (Thanatos) and Schopenhauer's pessimism, concluding the 'dark genie' was partly disease-like and thus not wholly blameworthy.
- ch. 21 "11. The One Truly Serious Philosophical Problem" **·** Albert Camus, Plato, Aristotle, Seneca — The Myth of Sisyphus; Stoicism; Death Is Nothing to Us — Doll surveys philosophical views on suicide to make sense of Bourdain's death, opening with Camus's claim that suicide is the one serious philosophical problem. She weighs Plato's Phaedo prohibition (we are the gods' possessions) against his allowance for extreme misfortune, Aristotle's view that suicide violates one's duty to self and community, and Seneca's Stoic position that a wise man 'lives as long as he ought, not as long as he can' — framing Bourdain's death as an autonomous choice.
- ch. 22 "12. Bourdain’s Taste for Freedom" **·** Aristotle, Socrates, The Buddha — Virtue Ethics; Two Concepts of Liberty; Compassion (karuna) — Porot and Simon argue Bourdain's goodness is best captured by virtue ethics rather than deontology or consequentialism, and read his life through Isaiah Berlin's positive/negative liberty (via MacCallum and Carter). They contend Bourdain's indulgence of his 'lower self' through addiction and excess was paradoxically the path by which his 'higher self' of empathy and compassion grew — echoing Hesse's Siddhartha, the Buddhist Middle Way, and Aristotle's mean — recasting his suicide in mystical rather than cynical terms.
- ch. 23 "IV The Good, the Bad, and the Ugly" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "13. Anthony Bourdain’s Antisocial Ethics" **·** Søren Kierkegaard — Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal); Communitarianism — Kitchen and Scalambrino build Bourdain's 'ethics for travelers' around his tagline 'Be a traveler, not a tourist,' arguing he preferred authentic community over abstract, Capitalist-conditioned society. Using Guy Debord's Society of the Spectacle to characterize the tourist's 'corporate commodified darkness' and Kierkegaard's dictum about contemplating death daily, they show through the Bhutan and Lebanon episodes how Bourdain's food-based humanism values lived communal reality over consuming others' experiences as banal spectacle.
- ch. 25 "14. Did Bourdain Go #MeToo Far" **·** Catharine MacKinnon — Dominance Approach (pornography and gender); Objectification; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Finn tests Catharine MacKinnon's 'Rape Redefined' — rape as sex compelled by inequality rather than lack of consent — against Bourdain's #MeToo allyship and his payment to Asia Argento's teenage accuser Jimmy Bennett. She argues MacKinnon's power-based standard could recognize Argento's act as assault, and reframes Bourdain's hush-money payment, via the Wendat/Huron compensation practices in Graeber and Wengrow, as a compassionate vengeance-staying reparation consistent with his empathy.
- ch. 26 "15.
