- ch. 29 "PART IV FIRE" *(front)*
- ch. 30 "Every Soul Experiences the Battle Within" **·** Confucius, Xunzi, Mencius — Ren (benevolence/humaneness); Human Nature Is Bad (and needs ritual); Human Nature Is Good; Xiao (filial piety) — Reads the Fire Nation royal family as a philosophical battle between Legalism (Han Fei, taking Xunzi's dictum that human nature is evil, so order requires terrifying sanctions: Ozai burning Zuko in the agni kai so 'suffering will be your teacher', Azulon's death sentence on Zuko, the Fire Nation Oath recited in schools) and Confucianism (Iroh teaching ren, that true honor cannot be had apart from humaneness, preferring honorable begging over theft, and talking a would-be mugger into tea). Zuko's 'battle within' is the legacy of his two great-grandfathers, Sozin's Legalism versus Roku's line, and it is resolved when he disowns Ozai, calls Iroh his 'real father', and is adopted into Team Avatar, whose filial bonds (Katara's care, Hakoda's sacrifice) contrast with the scheming royal family. The chapter closes with Solzhenitsyn's image of the line between good and evil passing through every human heart to explain why Zuko's inner war resonates.
- ch. 31 "Opening Up Possibilities" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Forgiveness; Personal Identity — Argues, through Hilde Lindemann's relational account of personhood, that Zuko genuinely is all of the identities others hold him in (disappointing son, villain, ally) because being a person is a social practice of expression, recognition, and response. Aang's decision to hear Zuko out at the Western Air Temple, Iroh's stubborn refusal to accept Ozai's script for him (his anger itself recognizing Zuko as an agent capable of choosing otherwise), and Katara's historically-sensitive slow trust open up the possible future in which Zuko can become trustworthy, while the Earth Kingdom villagers' rejection of him in 'Zuko Alone' shows how weight of history and stereotype can shut possibility down. The chapter insists this is not letting wrongdoers off the hook: genuine apology, repair, and moral anger remain required (contrasting the Brock Turner case), but only a moral community that holds people in personhood makes redemption possible, so society should be arranged to give everyone the Iros and Aangs that Zuko was lucky to have.
- ch. 32 "A Zhuangzian Sage All Along" **·** Zhuangzi, Confucius — Wu Wei (effortless action); Taoism (Daoism) — Argues that Iroh's Book One laziness and foolishness is a Zhuangzian pose rather than genuine folly: like Zhuangzi's fictional Confucius counseling Yan Hui not to lecture the young, willful lord of Wei, Iroh never pressures Zuko directly, keeps himself unthreatening by playing the fool over tea and Pai Sho, and practices the 'use of uselessness' so he is never a tool of the prince's quest, 'singing' his wisdom only when Zuko is ready to hear it. The couple's falling out in 'Avatar Day', triggered precisely when Iroh offers explicit moral advice, confirms the strategy, since Zuko rejects both the advice and the uncle. The authors add an empirical study (Prolific participants rating clips): even viewers totally unfamiliar with the series rate Book One Iroh as wise and suspect his foolishness is strategic, supporting the interpretation that he is a Zhuangzian sage all along, and that ATLA in turn illuminates Zhuangzi's cryptic political advice.
- ch. 33 "Becoming Good at Being Good" **·** Martin Heidegger, Christine Korsgaard — Dasein (being-there); Kantian Constructivism; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Argues that Zuko's cry 'Why am I so bad at being good?' after burning Toph shows that changing one's explicit self-conception (Korsgaard's practical identity, the description under which you value yourself) does not by itself change one's being-in-the-world (Heidegger): Zuko's perception, dispositions, and rage-fueled firebending still run on the residual identity of the banished prince seeking his father's approval. The chapter traces this friction through the series: releasing Appa triggers a metamorphosis fever, but he still sides with Azula at the Crossroads of Destiny; the regained 'honor' of Book Three leaves him empty ('I should be happy now, but I'm not'); and only after renouncing Ozai, apologizing to Toph, and re-learning fire from the Sun Warriors without rage do his conscious commitments and existential engagement begin to align, culminating in taking Azula's lightning for Katara. Aspiration, in Agnes Callard's sense, is thus a deep change in how one sees and feels, requiring retraining of bodily skills and practical perception, not just new stated values.
- ch. 34 "Sometimes We Can Let Go" **·** Seneca, Martha Nussbaum — Forgiveness; Stoicism; Buddhism; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Sets the intuition that angry blame is how we hold people responsible (Strawson; Katara's useful fury at Pakku's sexism, Iroh's 'whatever you do to that spirit, I will unleash on you tenfold') against the Senecan and Buddhist verdict that anger is greedy for revenge, and reads ATLA as modeling ways to hold people responsible without toxic anger. In 'The Southern Raiders' Katara neither forgives Yon Rha nor kills him, showing that blame can be set down when expressing it is pointless, while she forgives Zuko in Lucy Allais's 'wiping the slate clean' sense because his past betrayal no longer speaks to his present character. Iroh's handling of the mugger in Ba Sing Se ('I was never angry; I was afraid that you had lost your way') models responding to the suffering behind wrongdoing with compassion instead of blame, and Zuko's self-anger is diagnosed as hypocritical self-blame whose antidote is self-compassion: acknowledging how he himself was hurt is what finally lets him take ownership of his wrongs.
- ch. 35 "PART V AIR" *(front)*
- ch. 36 "Silent Wounds" **·** Power/Knowledge — Argues that the Fire Nation's empire, like the United States, was founded on genocide that national narratives erase: Sozin's extermination of the Air Nomads to remove the one check on his ambitions parallels manifest destiny and massacres of Indigenous peoples such as Sand Creek and the Wiyot killings at Tuluwat. The chapter shows erasure at work inside ATLA itself, as Gran Gran repeats Fire Nation propaganda by calling the Air Nomads 'extinct' rather than victims of genocide, and the refugees of the Northern Air Temple appropriate the annihilated people's home, identity, and sacred murals for a bathhouse. Aang's discovery of Gyatso's skeleton and his grief-block on the Avatar State are read as the 'silent wounds' of genocidal trauma, with the caveat that Guru Pathik's chakra cure is far simpler than the real, ongoing healing Indigenous communities require.
- ch. 37 "The Anarchist You Are" **·** Anarchism; The State of Nature — Argues that anarchism, understood through David Graeber's consensus-based action, Noam Chomsky's demand that hierarchies justify themselves, and Kropotkin's mutual aid, runs all through ATLA even though no character calls themselves an anarchist. The Dai Li are an unjustified hierarchy that delivers Ba Sing Se to the very conquest it claims to prevent, High Monk Pasang's arbitrary rank authority over Gyatso and Aang causes the century-long catastrophe, and Zhao embodies domination justified by 'fire is the superior element', whereas justified power is self-effacing (Iroh mentoring Zuko without coercion, Katara teaching Aang waterbending). Toph's insistence that she can 'carry my own weight' in 'The Chase' dramatizes the mutual-aid lesson that letting those who love you help you is part of the cooperative human nature Kropotkin opposed to the Hobbesian view, and Zaheer in Korra, who rules by fear, shows that a chaos-bringer is no anarchist at all, while playful, communal Aang is.
- ch. 38 "Fearlessness and Power" **·** The Buddha — Buddhism; Non-Self (anatman); Compassion (karuna) — Argues that Aang's energybending victory over Ozai vindicates a Buddhist conception of power against Ozai's equation of power with domination: the Lion Turtle's 'unbendable spirit' is not resistance but thorough vulnerability, achieved through the true heart (overcoming the five hindrances mapped onto Aang's sensory desire, ill will, dullness, restlessness, and doubt across the series) and the true mind (overcoming the fundamental delusion of a self separate from others, learned from Huu in 'The Swamp' and Guru Pathik). The merging of Aang's and Ozai's spirits is pictured by the metaphor of Indra's net, and Indra's own Jataka victory, halting his assault to protect eagle hatchlings and disorienting the demons, parallels Aang's refusal to win by killing. Drawing on a Zen story of the abbot who could 'be run through by a sword without batting an eye', the chapter concludes that Aang's fearlessness flows from compassion, so that weakness by Ozai's standard is precisely the power that defeats him.
- ch. 39 "Overcoming Dilemmas" **·** The Buddha, Mahatma Gandhi — Ahimsa (nonviolence); Buddhism; Yin and Yang; Compassion (karuna) — Frames Aang's conflict between his Avataric duty to restore balance even by killing Ozai and his Air Nomad duty of ahimsa as a genuine dilemma only if both obligations are non-negotiable (Tessman's negotiable and non-negotiable duties), then argues the show resolves it because Sublime Ahimsic Practice, the Four Sublime States joined to non-harm, reshapes the very conditions the dilemma rests on. Aang's cultivation of loving-kindness and his grasp of dependent origination (Huu's banyan-grove insight that everything is interconnected) keep him open to the world, which is what leads him to the Lion Turtle; energybending then lets him stop Ozai without killing, dissolving the kill-or-be-killed framework rather than choosing a horn. The Avataric role itself is read as Daoist yin-yang balance-keeping, and the chapter links Aang's triumph to the lessons of Gandhi, Martin Luther King Jr., and nonviolent movements: becoming different people changes which options the world affords.
- ch. 40 "Journey’s End and a Vision of a New Community" **·** The Buddha — Buddhism; Compassion (karuna); Non-Self (anatman) — Reads Aang's quest through the Karmapa Ogyen Trinley Dorje's book Interconnected: like the seventeenth Karmapa, identified at age seven and taken from his family, Aang learns that an exalted title complicates friendship and that freedom is self-control rather than the rugged individualism of the American hero's quest. Training episodes (Katara's jealous competition in 'The Waterbending Scroll', Jeong Jeong's warnings, the Toph-versus-Katara sifu debate in 'Bitter Work') show moral training as communal, widening from Team Avatar as adoptive family toward universal compassion, with Aang's refusal to open the seventh chakra illustrating how local attachment can obstruct universal love. Zuko's counter-arc from competition, isolation, and betrayal to humility, forgiveness, and contentment shows compassion turning an enemy into a friend, and the finale's simultaneous battles (White Lotus liberating Ba Sing Se, Sokka and Toph destroying the fleet) affirm that Aang wins as an 'interdependent individual', never alone.
- ch. 41 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 42 "End User License Agreement" *(front)*

## Avengers Infinity Saga and Philosophy - Go for the Head

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Avengers Infinity Saga and Philosophy Go for the Head" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Avengers Infinity Saga and Philosophy Go for the Head" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "A Seriously Mind-Blowing Book about Seriously Mind-Blowing Movies" **·** Utilitarianism; Humanity as an End in Itself — The introduction argues that all villains, real or fictional, share one trait: they treat people as mere objects to be used, which is why Thanos's Snap feels monstrous. It then springs the book's central provocation: since Thanos kills half of all life to save the rest of the universe from overpopulation, resource exhaustion, and destruction, he is 'actually a strictly moral being' from a utilitarian perspective. It frames the tension between using persons and respecting them (the grenade-jumping soldier who gives himself willingly versus those sacrificed without consent) and previews the volume's chapters on Thanos's morality and mind, time travel, and death.
- ch. 8 "I The Snap" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "1. Rights and Wrongs of Reversing the Snap" **·** Consequentialism; Deontology; Humanity as an End in Itself — Philip Mouch weighs consequences against rights to ask whether the Avengers were wrong to undo the Snap in Endgame. He imagines a survivor whose returned spouse finds her remarried, cataloguing displaced families, food shortages, and political turmoil, and argues these harms, however real, are outweighed by restoring the lives Thanos took. The deeper problem is Bruce Banner's wager with the Ancient One: removing the Time Stone dooms a branch reality on a 1-in-14,000,605 chance, gambling innocent lives without their consent, which resembles Thanos's non-consensual sacrifice of Gamora rather than the noble self-sacrifice of Hawkeye and Black Widow on Vormir. He concludes that two wrongs do not make a right: even for a noble end, risking countless lives tramples the rights of others.
- ch. 10 "2. What Does Life Mean after the Snap" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Albert Camus, Emmanuel Levinas, Friedrich Nietzsche — Existence Precedes Essence; The Myth of Sisyphus; The Face of the Other; The Death of God — This chapter reads four post-Snap Avengers as existentialist answers to how life keeps meaning after catastrophe. Tony Stark embodies Sartre's existence precedes essence, defining himself through free choice up to his final 'I am Iron Man' snap that erases Thanos and his army; the merged Professor Hulk lives Camus's Sisyphus, embracing the absurd and concluding 'all is well' after watching his own past rage; Captain America lives Levinas's ethics of the Other, from refusing to abandon brainwashed Bucky in Civil War to wielding Mjolnir to save Thor; and Ronin-era Hawkeye enacts Nietzschean nihilism until Black Widow's Vormir sacrifice, read through Zarathustra's burning in one's own flame, restores his meaning. The authors conclude Marvel's answer is affirmation: courage to affirm freedom, the Other, and life itself against Thanos as personification of genocide, climate change, and war.
- ch. 11 "3. Confronting Grief in the Post-Snap World" **·** Judith Butler — Ethical Egoism; Virtue Ethics — Benjamin Carpenter applies Judith Butler's idea of grievability from Precarious Life and Frames of War to Endgame: lives matter insofar as they can be mourned, and Thanos renders his billions of victims ungrievable by scale, turning deaths into statistics beside white stone monuments Scott finds his own name on. Most Avengers turn away (Tony retreats, Thor kills Thanos and sinks into depression, Clint goes on a murderous rampage), but Natasha stays at her post, keeping agency and hope alive, which is what lets Scott find her and the team reassemble. Her willing self-sacrifice on Vormir makes her the moral opposite of Thanos, 'the ultimate egoist' who wraps genocide in the language of balance and kills only others, and the chapter asks why Tony's funeral gets a long communal mourning while Natasha is mostly forgotten.
- ch. 12 "4. Why Does Grief Hurt So Much" **·** Martin Heidegger, Gilbert Ryle — Dasein (being-there) — J. Keeping argues grief hurts because the griever still inhabits the past world of meaning where the loved one lived, drawing on Heidegger's 'referential totality of significance' (the hammer's for-structure) and Ryle's knowing-how versus knowledge-that to show our emotional lives are skills learned by doing, not facts we can update at will. Thanos is the pathological case: emotionally stuck in Titan's collapse, he applies his genocidal 'solution' to every planet, even ones that are not overpopulated, while Tony models healthy grieving by trading the superhero life for a different one built around Pepper and Morgan and finding something to live for. Because our identities are constituted by our actions, meanings, and interconnections with others, losing loved ones is 'the affective equivalent of amputation,' which is why Cap's support group and Tony's funeral matter.
- ch. 13 "II Time Travel" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "5. Getting Time Travel Right" **·** David Lewis — The Metaphysics of Time Travel; Possible Worlds; Utilitarianism — Duncan Gale asks whether Endgame 'gets time travel right,' using David Lewis's 'The Paradoxes of Time Travel' to explain personal time versus external time (Steve and Nebula meeting their past selves), causal loops (Tony quoting the saying about money and time back to Howard, who seemingly hears it first), and the grandfather paradox (Rhodey's baby-Thanos plan, which the existence of Nebula proves the Avengers would always fail to carry out). He traces Marvel's branching-timeline rule from Marvel Two-In-One #50 through the Ancient One's warning that removing a stone splits reality, and argues the film's moral is that the Avengers refuse quick resets and dutifully return the stones, while Thanos would shred the universe and rebuild a 'grateful' one. The chapter closes with Ivan Karamazov's challenge about building the edifice of human happiness on a tortured child's tears, which Thanos would accept and the Avengers refuse.
- ch. 15 "6. Nebula’s Time Travel Paradox" **·** Aristotle — The Metaphysics of Time Travel; Free Will vs Determinism; The A-Series and B-Series of Time — Charlene Esby defends Endgame against the charge that Nebula killing her past self is a plot hole. She reconstructs the grandfather paradox from Nathaniel Schachner's 'Ancestral Voices,' explains how conceiving time as a fourth dimension makes travel conceivable, and deploys Aristotle's Physics (time as the number of change and rest) and the De Interpretatione sea-battle problem, where the past is necessary but the future contingent on present choices, to argue the Avengers' victory depends on free choices made now, which is why the Ancient One releases the Time Stone only on Banner's promise. The resolution is Hulk's subjective-timeline point: once traveled to, the past becomes the traveler's present, so present Nebula kills past Nebula in a shared present rather than negating her own future, just as Thanos from the past arrives alive in a present where he already died.
- ch. 16 "7. Something Strange about Time Travel" **·** David Lewis — The Metaphysics of Time Travel; Possible Worlds — Rhys Woodward asks how MCU time travel can be coherent when future Captain America fights past Captain America and future Nebula kills past Nebula, apparent contradictions a frozen Steve Rogers by contrast does not even count as time travel. He lays out David Lewis's view that a time traveler simply cannot do what would generate a logical contradiction, then argues the MCU is more permissive and proposes that Avengers time travel jumps not through one timeline but to alternate worlds, reading the Ancient One's 'branching' when a stone is removed as an allusion to the many-worlds interpretation of quantum mechanics cued by the quantum realm of the Ant-Man films. On this multiverse reading past Nebula belongs to a different world history than future Nebula, so the murder does not erase the killer, and the apparent plot holes dissolve into deliberate clues.
- ch. 17 "8. Time Travel and the Problem of Evil" **·** Boethius — The Problem of Evil; Fate and Foreknowledge; The Consolation of Philosophy — Ray Bossert reads Endgame through Boethius's Consolation of Philosophy, arguing that Doctor Strange and Tony Stark, who permit the Snap and five years of suffering for the sake of the one winning outcome, run 'uncannily parallel' to Thanos, who decided for Gamora that her unwilling death served the greater good. Strange's viewing of 14,000,605 futures illustrates Boethius's distinction between an infinite being riding along the timeline and an eternal one seeing all moments at once, and the refusal to tell Stark his role mirrors why God's foreknowledge sees our choices without compelling them. Boethius's providence weaving free choices into justice, the Avengers' passion-driven failures on Titan (Star-Lord and Thor avenging lost lovers), justice as the wicked's own punishment (Thanos dying wretched twice in one film), and Umberto Eco's 'Myth of Superman' all frame a universe where suffering is allowed because it is woven into a pattern that ends in justice.
- ch. 18 "III Ethics" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "9. With Infinite Power Comes No Responsibility" **·** Nick Bostrom, Blaise Pascal, Frank Jackson — Consequentialism; Pascal's Wager; Deontology — James Hart casts Tony Stark as a consequentialist and Steve Rogers as a deontologist, then argues Endgame's infinite stakes destroy Tony's morality through Nick Bostrom's 'infinitarian paralysis': in an infinite multiverse every act leaves the balance of happy and sad lives unchanged, as an infinite Stark orphanage rearranged Hilbert's-Hotel style shows, and even genocide becomes permissible so long as infinitely many remain alive. Tony's attempted fixes each fail: the causal approach collapses because reversing the Snap has infinite butterfly effects, expected value (credited to Frank Jackson) runs into Pascal's Mugging and an 'infinitarian trance' demanding suicidal bets on any chance of infinite value, and Bostrom's 'infinity shades' that filter tiny probabilities would blind Tony to the one-in-a-trillion time-heist chance he should take. The chapter concludes there is no consequentialist way to say when wagering lives on infinite stakes is ethical, and Iron Man should let Captain America lead.
- ch. 20 "10. Natasha, Gamora, and the Soul Stone" **·** John Stuart Mill, Plato — The Trolley Problem; The Greatest Happiness Principle; Platonic Love (the ladder of love) — T.J. McSherry frames the two Vormir deaths as 'a futuristic, utilitarian trolley problem,' using Mill's greatest-happiness principle to argue Natasha's martyrdom was the more utilitarian choice (only the Avengers mourn her, while Clint's children need a father), even as Thanos's tossing of Gamora shows the same sacrifice-of-few arithmetic the Avengers rely on. Because the amoral Soul Stone rewards good and evil deaths identically, the chapter turns to Plato's Symposium, casting Red Skull as a Diotiman spirit-mediator of love and ranking each relationship on Diotima's ladder: Thanos is 'stuck at level one' in his stunted love for Gamora, kept alive only as a useful sacrifice, while Clint and Natasha's non-romantic love reaches level four through shared history, empathy, and their fight over who gets to die for the other.
- ch. 21 "11. Population Ethics for Mad Gods" **·** Derek Parfit — Utilitarianism — Leonard Kahn takes Thanos seriously as a participant in population ethics, asking 'how many people should there be?' after Titan's collapse, and tests the Snap against three schools. Totalism condemns it (random killing just cuts total happiness in half while traumatizing survivors like Steve's support-group members) but falls to Parfit's Repugnant Conclusion: it must prefer a universe of trillions barely worth living to one of ecstatic individuals. Averagism also condemns the Snap, since random removal leaves the average untouched, yet would obligate Thanos to kill the least happy half, and the person-affecting view condemns the murder but gives the Avengers no reason to snap anyone back, rendering Natasha's sacrifice pointless, so Kahn concludes Thanos should have used the Stones to reconsider the Population Question before acting, since humanity's own flourishing depends on answering it.
- ch. 22 "12. Why Blood Sacrifice" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Immanuel Kant — Teleological Suspension of the Ethical; The Leap of Faith — Philip Wilson defines sacrifice as gift-giving with payback ('I give x in order to get y') and reads Thanos's murder of Gamora through Agamemnon's sacrifice of Iphigenia at Aulis, while Black Widow's willing leap from the same cliff parallels Christ's atoning self-sacrifice. The core is Kierkegaard's Fear and Trembling: Abraham's binding of Isaac as the 'teleological suspension of the ethical,' where obeying God suspends universal morality, sharpened by Kant's argument that Abraham should have told the supposed divine voice he could never be certain it came from God. Wilson argues the films restore rather than suspend ethics, Gamora was murdered rather than sacrificed, Natasha died willingly with no collateral harm, and Tony's final 'I am Iron Man' self-sacrifice completes the tragic arc, leaving readers with Kierkegaard's sleepless questions about a moral order that demands blood.
- ch. 23 "13. Jumping to Conclusions" **·** Charles Sanders Peirce — Tuomas Manninen uses Endgame's detective work to teach abductive reasoning, inference to the best explanation, as introduced by Charles Peirce, distinguishing it from deduction (Tony Stark is mortal) and induction (Mjolnir-worthiness generalizing to Stormbreaker). He reconstructs Tony's false conclusion that Strange 'gave up the store,' War Machine's wrong guess that Thanos hid the Stones, Cap-2012's mistaken Loki hypothesis upon meeting his future self, and the Ancient One's revised reasoning about Strange surrendering the Time Stone, applying Peter Lipton's actual-versus-potential and likeliest-versus-loveliest distinctions plus five criteria of adequacy (testability, fruitfulness, scope, simplicity, conservatism). Doctor Strange's fourteen-million-futures gambit turns out to combine abduction and deduction into a fallible but insightful plan that only Tony's survival and final snap could fulfill, illustrating Peirce's point that abduction is an instinctive yet rational act of insight.
- ch. 24 "IV Death" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "14. When the Stakes Are Highest" *(no index entry yet)* — John Karavitis argues the Infinity War-Endgame arc only makes sense through the alien lens of feudal Japan's warrior code as codified in Yamamoto Tsunetomo's Hagakure, where 'the way of the warrior is to be found in dying' and success is measured in death, the enemy's and one's own. He reads Thanos's sacrifice of Gamora, his calm acceptance of death in the Garden, Black Widow's and Tony's willing sacrifices, and Thor's failure to 'go for the head' at Wakanda through Olivier Ansart's concepts of ichinen (single-minded resolve) and shini-gurui (the frenzy to die), with the code's roots in Buddhism, Shinto, and Confucianism. The provocation is that Thanos, not the fractious Avengers, is the true samurai, since his voluntary destruction of the Infinity Stones shows the self-discipline the code demands.
- ch. 26 "15. Life Is a Laugh and Death Is a Joke" **·** Sigmund Freud, Thomas Hobbes — The Unconscious — Jarno Hietalahti applies the three classic theories of humor to Endgame: incongruity theory explains trickster Loki, 'an epitome or embodiment of humor' who cheats death and escapes with the Tesseract; Hobbes's superiority theory from Leviathan, laughter as 'sudden glory' at others' weakness (extended by Gruner's game of humor with winners and losers), explains Thanos's self-satisfied smirks as he shrugs off the heroes' combined assault and snaps half of life away; and Freud's relief theory, in which jokes express the unconscious and release inner tension, explains fat Thor's terror-ridden laughter as escape from his failure, grief, and lost control. The chapter concludes that humor is neither good nor bad in itself but part of a worldview, and that death as the horizon giving life meaning is precisely what makes laughter possible for beings like us and like the superheroes.
- ch. 27 "16. The Defeat of Death" **·** Plato, Arthur Schopenhauer — Immortality and the Soul; The Meaning of Life — JD Lyonhart asks which of four philosophies of death Endgame adopts: embracing death as natural balance (Thanos's view, where the Snap keeps life in check), denying it, grimly accepting it, or defeating it. Opening with Schopenhauer's 'Death is the muse of philosophy,' he sketches Plato's Meno argument that the slave boy's innate knowledge requires a pre-existing, hence immortal, soul, and then argues the film starts with Tony's acceptance of death as final before completing a trajectory that runs from Yinsen's sacrificial death in the cave, through Tony's cruciform fall through the wormhole in The Avengers, to a Christ-like defeat of death in Endgame where Tony dies facing death head-on so others may live. The film avoids cheap denial by sitting in five years of grief before the triumph, mirroring the Christian pattern in which Easter cannot be celebrated without Good Friday.
- ch. 28 "17. Life Unchecked Will Cease to Exist" **·** Martin Heidegger, René Descartes — The Question Concerning Technology; Anthropocentrism — Diego Perez Lasserre argues Thanos is not an external villain but a personification of what Heidegger and Carl Schmitt call the 'metaphysics of technology': once modernity banished transcendence from the metaphysical realm, mundane concepts like economic growth and pleasure occupied it, turning nature into mere resource (a tree becomes furniture or paper) until, as Descartes's mastering stance predicts, devouring the world leaves 'life unchecked' facing extinction. Read this way, the Snap is the inevitable consequence of our own predatory interpretation of the world, and the Avengers only prevail in Endgame once their existential crises evict growth-and-wealth concepts from their metaphysical realm, replacing them with family (Tony), friendship (Natasha), justice (Hawkeye), self-knowledge (Banner), love (Vision and Wanda), and transcendence (Rogers). The closing exchange, 'the arrogant never do' suspect a thing, is read as a warning about our own climate crisis.
- ch. 29 "18. Iron Man’s Choice to Die" **·** Sigmund Freud, Albert Camus — The Death Drive (Thanatos); Consequentialism; The Absurd; Utilitarianism — Argues Tony's death in Endgame is best explained three ways: via Freud's Eros/Thanatos drives (Tony oscillates between the cabin family-life drive and the risk-seeking death drive that takes him aboard the donut ship in Infinity War and has him lay down to die with Nebula), via Camus's absurd (like Sisyphus, Tony creates subjective meaning by choosing — Pepper, Morgan, and finally the snap itself, 'Part of the journey is the end'), and via consequentialism (the snap as a desirable consequence, contrasting Infinity War's deontological 'We don't trade lives' with Endgame's 'whatever it takes'). It contends that because the time heist rewrites history, deaths in both films — Vision's, Natasha's, Tony's — risk becoming meaningless, so Tony dies on his terms to make his death meaningful.
- ch. 30 "V Thanos" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "19. Thanos Is No Abraham" **·** Søren Kierkegaard — The Stages on Life's Way; Teleological Suspension of the Ethical; Subjectivity Is Truth; The Leap of Faith — Uses Kierkegaard's Fear and Trembling to compare Thanos's killing of Gamora on Mount Vormir with Abraham's binding of Isaac, laying out the aesthetic, ethical, and religious stages. It argues Thanos initially looks 'religious' — his sacrifice expresses a particular, personal encounter with the absolute balance of all things, neither an aesthetic whim nor an ethical calculation — but that his Endgame slip ('what I'm about to do to your stubborn, annoying little planet… I'm gonna enjoy it. Very, very much') exposes him as a mere aesthete dressing bloodlust in metaphysics. Hence Thanos is no Abraham, and the truly religious type remains a frightening but unrealized possibility.
- ch. 32 "20. Thanos and the (Un)grateful Universe" **·** Utilitarianism; The Trolley Problem — Argues that Infinity War only has the structure of a superhero movie when told from Thanos's perspective, and that he passes a three-part 'superhero test' — beneficent goal (saving the universe from overpopulation), self-sacrifice (killing Gamora, destroying the stones, 'I lost more than you can know'), and willingness to make the tough decisions — while the Avengers fail it, staying split after Civil War and acting from selfish motives (Ant-Man wants Hope back, Black Widow says 'we owe it to everyone who's not in this room'). It then applies utilitarianism — Titan's collapse, Gamora's planet turned 'paradise,' the whales in the Hudson, the Snap as a trolley-problem lever Thanos alone will pull — to conclude Thanos is the true hero of the saga and the Avengers the unintended villains.
- ch. 33 "21. I Plucked My Soul Out of Its Secret Place" **·** Immortality and the Soul; Fate and Foreknowledge — Traces the Soul Stone across comics and film — Thanos courting Death, the Vormir 'a soul for a soul' trade of Gamora, and Natasha's parallel sacrifice for Clint in Endgame — reading it through sacrifice and resurrection motifs from the Binding of Isaac and asking why the film's Thanos must complete the offering Abraham was spared. It contrasts Thanos, who styles himself a god but is a 'false god,' an idol beside the sentient being that existed before all realities and became the six Infinity Gems, with Adam Warlock, who dwells in the Soul World and 'knows his soul' (the chapter's title borrows Claude McKay's poem), while Thanos is driven by destiny — 'I ignored my destiny once. I cannot do that again.'
- ch. 34 "22. The Mad Titan and the Mother of Dragons" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli — The Ends Justify the Means; Consequentialism; Utopia and Dystopia — Pairs Thanos with Game of Thrones' Daenerys as twin Machiavellian utopians acting on 'the ends justify the means' (quoting The Prince and the Discourses): Daenerys burns King's Landing to 'break the wheel,' Thanos snaps half of life away 'at random, dispassionate, fair to rich and poor alike' as 'a small price to pay for salvation.' It roots both villains in cruel childhoods and a nature-versus-nurture question (with historical analogies to Hitler and Stalin), and in messiah complexes built on improbable success — 'Dread it, run from it, destiny arrives all the same' — while noting the key inversion: Thanos refuses to rule and dies content watching the sunrise, whereas the breaker of chains claims a new throne.
- ch. 35 "23. Where Thanos Got Lost" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Language Games; Virtue Ethics (arete); Utilitarianism — Diagnoses where Thanos 'got lost' via Wittgenstein's aspect perception (the duck-rabbit, the Thanos-chin-as-panini memes): morality concerns not just choosing but seeing, and Thanos's impoverished worldview — shaped by a lifetime of rejection, drawn from the novel Thanos: Titan Consumed — lets him see life only as 'the condition of living things,' never as 'the existence of individuals,' so erasing half of all individuals strikes him as a moral act. His single-minded reading of Titan's collapse becomes confirmation bias ('I'm the only one who knows that'), and his final plan to erase everything proves he never saw the individual-existence aspect at all: his starting point, not his reasoning, is missing information.
- ch. 36 "24. The Left Hand of Thanos" **·** Forgiveness; Fate and Foreknowledge — Reads the Endgame 'I am' exchange through Martin Luther's two hands of God: Thanos's left-handed gauntlet enacts God's 'alien work' of law, judgment and destruction (his 'inevitability,' seen in all 14,000,605 of Strange's futures), while Tony's right-handed snap enacts the 'proper work' of grace, where freedom means relying on a promise — Strange's gift of the Time Stone, the Ancient One resting reality on Banner's promise. It applies the law/grace contrast to Thor's reconciliation with Frigga ('you're going to be the man you're meant to be'), Tony's with Howard, and Steve's gift of the shield to Sam, arguing the heroes' failures at who they were 'supposed to be' are the law's condemnation, while the promise delivers a freedom that turns Thanos's inevitability into impossibility.
- ch. 37 "25. Reasonable Genocide" **·** Hannah Arendt, Herbert Spencer — Utilitarianism; Totalitarianism; Social Darwinism — Uses Horkheimer's Eclipse of Reason to dissolve the puzzle of Thanos's unsettling sanity: the Snap is subjectively rational — pure means–ends efficiency in service of a Malthusian 'simple calculus' whose end only the subject chooses — so Thanos 'is mad because he is rational,' with the murder of Gamora as the final severing of his last bond to the world. Captain America's refusal to trade Vision ('We don't trade lives') is read as a stand for ends valuable in themselves, while Ultron embodies the totalitarian variant (via Arendt and Spencer's social Darwinism) that declares humanity 'obsolete': Ultron wants to accelerate nature, Thanos to correct it, both absolutizing the subject against objective reason.
- ch. 38 "26. Thanos’s Mistake" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Master and Slave Morality; Ressentiment; Climate Ethics — Argues Thanos embodies Nietzschean master morality — his will as its own justification, 'the hardest choices require the strongest wills,' echoing the Melian dialogue's 'the strong do what they will, the weak suffer what they must,' with Doctor Strange answering in kind ('you'll find our will equal to yours') — while the Avengers speak from slave morality, judging by intentions and branding genocide evil. Thanos's real error, though, is a categorical one: imposing a technical quantity fix (a one-time 50 percent cull) on a social relationship problem, since Hardin's carrying-capacity logic and the St. Matthew Island reindeer show populations must self-limit like apex predators, making his correction a mere stopgap that confuses quantity with relationship.
- ch. 39 "27. I Am Inevitable" **·** Martin Heidegger, Jacques Lacan, Gilles Deleuze, Friedrich Nietzsche, Sigmund Freud — Being-Toward-Death; The Death Drive (Thanatos); Eternal Recurrence; Dasein (being-there) — Reads Thanos's 'I am inevitable' (his name echoing thanatos) as death personified, then asks 'whose death?' through Heidegger — 'people die' is the evasive 'one dies,' and Tony's final snap and 'I am Iron Man' figure an authentic appropriation of being-toward-death that redeems his guilt — before Blanchot's reversal and Lacan's symbolic death dissolve that authenticity into impersonal, language-borne dying (Loki's 'No resurrections this time' as the meta-textual murder of comic-book death). It ends with Deleuze's Nietzschean eternal return as the empty form of time that the time heist and quantum multiverse enact, concluding the real inevitability 'engraved in the very fabric of reality' belongs to Steve Rogers and Peggy Carter's dance, not to Thanos or Iron Man.
- ch. 40 "VI Character" *(front)*
- ch. 41 "28. How Captain Rogers Became a Good Man" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Golden Mean; Consequentialism — Traces Steve Rogers's arc from Erskine's charge — 'Not a perfect soldier, but a good man' — through Aristotle's virtue ethics (virtues are learned, none innate, each a mean between vices like courage between cowardice and rashness) to argue that the perfect soldier's obedience is not itself a virtue, so saving Bucky in Winter Soldier, outlaw leadership in Civil War, and gracious command in Endgame move Cap from black-and-white moral absolutism into the grey area where real virtue lives. The payoff is worthiness: only after pain has refined him can he lift Mjölnir, and the chapter warns that a 'perfect soldier' Cap — Thanos's ideological twin — might have heard the Mad Titan out.
- ch. 42 "29. I Am Iron Man" **·** Emmanuel Levinas — The Meaning of Life; Philosophy of Love — Reads Tony Stark's fifteen-year transformation — from Yinsen's 'Is this the last act of defiance of the great Tony Stark?' in the cave to the final snap — through Maurice Blondel's philosophy of action, where meaning comes from what we will and do rather than what we know, each willed action building toward an ultimate end. It frames the journey as taking on an infinite responsibility for the other (Levinas's register): the wormhole flight in The Avengers, the armor-around-the-world obsession after the Scarlet Witch's vision, Ultron, Civil War, and finally the snap as the pure act of radical love that answers Yinsen's 'Don't waste your life.'
- ch. 43 "30. Socratic Superheroism" **·** Socrates — Socratic Method (elenchus); Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); The Unexamined Life; Theory of Forms — Stages a dialogue between Socrates and Nebula to build a four-part scorecard of 'Socratic Heroism' — question all assumptions, intellectual humility (Socrates before the Delphic oracle: 'I am very conscious that I am not wise at all'), improve society without ever doing injustice, and courage unto death (the gadfly of the Apology) — then scores Thanos 0 for 4: 'I am incapable of error' and 'reality can be whatever I want' are the very opposites of humility and of objective, heavenly truth. It nominates Doctor Strange as the MCU's Socratic hero, whose humbling at Kamar-Taj, willingness to hand over the Time Stone against the obvious choice, and trust in the one winning future among 14,000,605 pass the tests.
- ch. 44 "31. Does the Hulk Have a Soul" **·** George Berkeley, Alvin Plantinga — The Hard Problem of Consciousness; Mind–Body Dualism; Subjective Idealism (esse est percipi) — Weighs whether the Hulk has a soul by running philosophy of mind's three positions: substance dualism (qualia and the hard problem, Plantinga's modal argument via Leibniz's Law — Peter Parker really is Spider-Man — but an unresolved mind–body interaction problem), physicalism (causal closure and conservation of energy, rebutted through objective truths and Einstein's dynamic spacetime), and idealism (Berkeley's master argument plus the holographic principle and quantum observation). It concludes idealism best explains Banner: Hulk and Banner share one mind/soul whose information is projected as two different 3D bodies, with the Ancient One's 'source code that shapes reality' speech in Doctor Strange as idealism's on-screen statement.
- ch. 45 "Bibliography" *(notes)*
- ch. 46 "Guardians of Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 47 "Index" *(notes)*

## Baseball and Philosophy - Thinking Outside the Batter's Box

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Baseball and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Popular Culture and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Foreword" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Acknowledgments" *(notes)*
- ch. 8 "Pre-Game Warm-up" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "First Inning - Hometown Heroes" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Rounding Third and Heading Home" *(no index entry yet)* — Joe Kraus uses Gaston Bachelard's Poetics of Space to read baseball's 'home': the batter begins at home yet must leave it, face the pitcher's threat, and return to make scoring meaningful, which is Bachelard's dialectic of shelter versus threat (with Joseph Campbell's monomyth alongside). Freud's uncanny (unheimlich, un-home-like) explains why home plate is both inviting and demoralizing. Bachelard's 'well-determined centers of revery' and Benedict Anderson's shared-news account of nationhood then explain ballparks as communal homes, from Wrigleyville to 'Cardinal Nation' in Quincy, Illinois, and why the Dodgers leaving Brooklyn felt like losing a home.
- ch. 11 "The Obligation to Play Baseball" **·** Jeremy Bentham, John Stuart Mill — Utilitarianism; The Greatest Happiness Principle — Horan and Solomon examine the 2001-02 contraction fight in which Judge Harry Crump ordered the Minnesota Twins to honor their Metrodome lease and play the 2002 season. They argue the Minnesota courts smuggled Bentham-Mill utilitarian reasoning about the public good into contract law, which normally awards only money damages, because baseball's antitrust exemption (from Justice Holmes's 1922 ruling through Justice Blackmun's 1972 opinion) had always treated the game as more than a business. Having long profited from special treatment, the owners lost when Bud Selig's plan to eliminate the Twins collided with the same 'greatest good for the greatest number' logic.
- ch. 12 "Second Inning - You Gotta Believe!" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Who Should Be Committed" **·** Blaise Pascal, William James — Pascal's Wager; Faith and Reason; The Will to Believe — Thomas Senor argues that rooting for the perennially losing Cubs is rationally and morally on a par with religious commitment: genuine fandom, like faith, is loyalty that survives repeated disappointment (distinguished from the zealot's 'my team right or wrong' via the Portland Trailblazers' 'Jailblazers' era). Since beliefs and fan loyalties are not under direct voluntary control, he rereads Pascal's wager not as choosing to believe but as cultivating belief indirectly: attending Mass the way moving to Chicago and following the Cubs daily can make you a fan. The believer's epistemic optimism toward religious experience and the devout's second-order hope in dark times mirror the Cubs fan's springtime optimism, so neither Cubs fans nor the religious should be committed.
- ch. 14 "Kierkegaard Lays It Down" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Aristotle, G.W.F. Hegel, Seneca — Teleological Suspension of the Ethical; Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Stoicism; Geist (World Spirit) — Willie Young uses the sacrifice bunt (Derek Jeter's perfect one versus Placido Polanco's failed one) to test philosophical accounts of sacrifice. Aristotle treats bunting for teammates as friendship and civic virtue in the polis, while Seneca's Stoic sacrifices only to exercise virtue itself, detached from particular friends and refusing retaliation for hit batters. Hegel's modern ideal of self-renunciation makes the player a cog in the team machine (the Big Red Machine), obeying the manager's calculations; against this, Kierkegaard's Abraham, whose absolute duty to God is 'higher than the universal' through infinite resignation, exposes baseball's mechanical, manager-ordered sacrifice and asks whether the game trains obedient citizens who sacrifice as they are told.
- ch. 15 "Third Inning - The Umpire’s New Clothes" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "The Ambiguous Consolations of Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Ted Cohen shows that 'ties go to the runner' is no convention but follows from Rule 6.05(j) (the runner is out only if tagged before he touches first), then discovers that Rule 7.08(e) contradicts 6.05(j) once 6.09(a) makes a batter who hits a fair ball a runner: on a tie at first the rules say both out and safe. His letters to the National League rules committee get a polite brush-off ('there never are any ties'), and the essay meditates on how players barely know the rules (the 2000 World Series third-strike misplay, cricket's time-wasting law, the spitball rule's gaps) and how rules written 'by journeymen lawyers' charm yet fail. Philosophy's consolation is ambiguous: knowing the rules cost him his teammates' respect, who 'knew the game in some other way.'
- ch. 17 "The Integrity of the Game" *(no index entry yet)* — J.S. Russell argues umpires have far more discretion than 'rules are all an umpire has to work with' allows. Via J.L. Austin's performative utterances, a call both creates the out and reports a witnessed event, so it can be false; hence unwitnessed or deceived calls (Ken Burkhart's 1970 World Series missed tag, phantom tags, Bernardo Carbo never touching the plate) should be reviewable to prevent an 'anti-game.' Via Ronald Dworkin's law-as-integrity he then defends Lee MacPhail's reinstatement of George Brett's pine-tar home run and umpire Wes Curry's 1887 interference ruling through a 'first principle of games adjudication': interpret rules to preserve the lusory goal and the excellences the game tests, as umpires' de facto strike-zone management has held league batting averages near .260 for a century.
- ch. 18 "Fourth Inning - Fair or Foul" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "Kant, Aristotle, and the Cheater" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle, Socrates — The Categorical Imperative; Virtue Ethics (arete); The Golden Mean; Socratic Method (elenchus) — Answering Cal Ripken's Socratic question 'What is cheating?' (raised by Sammy Sosa's corked bat and Gaylord Perry's spitball), Randolph Feezell stages a dialogue between Abbey's Kantian absolutism, Trev's traditionalism, and Ron's win-at-all-costs realism. Kant's categorical imperative grounds the absolutist: intending to break a rule to gain an edge is making yourself an arbitrary exception to rules that bind all players equally. Aristotle's virtue ethics then lets the traditionalist say that some strategic rule-breaking belongs to a sport's 'prescriptive atmosphere' (stealing signs, the balk pickoff, catching framing), since honesty and courage are means between excess and defect guided by judgment rather than exceptionless rules, while corking a bat stays cheating because it defies the tradition and hides from umpires.
- ch. 20 "Five Questions about Good Sportsmanship" **·** Immanuel Kant, Thomas Aquinas, Augustine of Hippo — The Categorical Imperative — Mark Hamilton asks whether there is lying in baseball (riffing on Jimmy Dugan's 'There's no crying in baseball') and sorts plays of deception by their target. Kant's absolute duty of truthfulness proves too broad for the hidden ball trick, Omar Vizquel's decoys, and faking a tag to fool Manny Ramirez: Aquinas's taxonomy of officious, jocose, and mischievous lies (with Bonhoeffer's situational truth-telling) shows these are jocose falsehoods in an agreed artificial contest, not immoral lies. But deception aimed at umpires is mischievous and immoral, as when Barry Bonds sells a trapped ball as a catch or runners cut bases in the illegal double squeeze; his five questions conclude that tricking opponents is strategy while tricking the officials destroys character and sport.
- ch. 21 "Fifth Inning - Baseball and America" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "Our Big, Fat, Greek Democracy" **·** Socrates, Adam Smith, Friedrich Nietzsche — Democracy; The Invisible Hand; The Unexamined Life — Eric Bronson reads the brawling 1977-78 'Bronx Zoo' Yankees (Steinbrenner firing Billy Martin five times, Martin dragging Reggie Jackson off the field in Fenway, Jackson's dugout charge and defiant bunt) as an argument that dissent is good for democracy. Through Alexis de Tocqueville's warnings about majority conformity and democratic leveling (Nietzsche's herd mentality), he argues America needs gadflies like Socrates condemned by Athens for 'creating tension' and MLK's nonviolent troublemakers, while Adam Smith's invisible hand shows the Yankees' internal self-interest and conflict still produced championships, unlike the harmonious 1979 'We Are Family' Pirates. Dysfunctional discord, like free speech, keeps teams and democracies from going stagnant.
- ch. 23 "America’s Game" **·** Richard Rorty, Alasdair MacIntyre, Bernard Williams — Virtues, Practices and Traditions; Communitarianism — William Morgan answers Richard Rorty's charge that American pop culture breeds only militaristic chauvinism by pointing to baseball's moral self-story: played for love of the game (Whitman) rather than market value. Using MacIntyre's notion of a practice's internal goods, he argues baseball's excellences and fair play are shared, socially constructed goods that resist commodification; through Annette Baier's ethics of trust and Bernard Williams's 'one thought too many' (saving your wife because she is your wife), the home team extends familial trust into a public moral community. Hence the moral outrage of 'Say it ain't so, Joe' after the 1919 Black Sox scandal and 'Say It Ain't So Sammy' after Sosa's corked bat: fans demand that players justify themselves to the community whose common good the game embodies.
- ch. 24 "Sixth Inning - Where Have You Gone, Jackie Robinson" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 25 "Crossing the Color Line" **·** Herbert Spencer — Social Darwinism — The Rucks argue that Herbert Spencer's Social Darwinism supplied the ideological justification for baseball's color line — blacks were deemed genetically unfit for the game — and that the Negro Leagues disproved it. Rube Foster's Negro National League, and especially Pittsburgh's Homestead Grays and Pittsburgh Crawfords (Cum Posey, Josh Gibson, Satchel Paige, Cool Papa Bell), displayed black organizational skill and on-field excellence, with Negro League teams beating white major leaguers in barnstorming play. Drawing on Darwin's actual account of cultural evolution and Franz Boas's environmental anthropology, the chapter holds that institutions, training infrastructure, and environment — not genes — explain athletic success, as Jackie Robinson's 1947 crossing of the color line and the later rise of Latin stars like Roberto Clemente confirm.
- ch. 26 "The Gift of Baseball" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Cornel West, bell hooks — Ressentiment; Cultural Hegemony — McDaniels reads the Negro Leagues through Nietzsche's account of resentment as revolt and Cornel West's tragic philosophy of resistance: excluded from the white majors after Cap Anson's 1887 stand against George Stovey, black players converted resentment into counter-narratives of excellence, with Satchel Paige, Josh Gibson, and Buck O'Neil showboating and clowning in the tradition of plantation dance, gallows humor, and the blues. The call-and-response sermon scene from The Bingo Long Traveling All-Stars & Motor Kings — Bingo Long on the mound answered by the crowd like a black church congregation — shows performance as ritual that sustains community, while bell hooks's 'absence of presence' frames remembering as resistance to hegemonic definitions of race. The chapter closes with the author's grandfather, for whom Robinson, Aaron, Mays, and Paige proved with every pitch 'We're American Too.'
- ch. 27 "Seventh Inning - The Japanese National Pastime" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "Ballparks, Dojos, and Other Places of Enlightenment" **·** The Buddha — Zen Buddhism; Buddhism — Bassham argues that the core advice of hitting instructors like Ted Williams ('get a good ball to hit') and Charley Lau ('tension is the enemy') echoes Zen Buddhist principles, walking through patience, relaxation, self-knowledge, visualization (Reggie Jackson, Willie Stargell, Barry Bonds's on-deck imagery), practice, Bruce Lee-style kime concentration, and mushin or no-mind. Mickey Mantle going 0-for-30 after over-thinking Ted Williams's hitting tips, and Tony Gwynn's 'see the ball and react,' show how conscious analysis interferes with the muscle memory a half-second fastball demands. If kendo is practiced in a dojo — a 'place of enlightenment' — he concludes, ballparks and batting cages may be dojos too, with the caveat that Cobb, Ruth, and Mantle hardly model egoless humility.
- ch. 29 "What’s Wrong with Generalization" **·** The Buddha, Confucius — Zen Buddhism; Buddhism — Brannigan uses Japanese baseball to ask what is wrong with cultural generalization. He presents the Bushido case — samurai-style self-denial, absolute loyalty (Kawakami's Yomiuri Giants), Suisha Tobita's 'training of death,' resolve (konjo), team harmony (wa), and a relational self grounded in Buddhism's no-self and Confucian identity-through-relationships — then tests it against counter-evidence: Sadaharu Oh's flamingo swing owed as much to individual talent and Hiroshi Arakawa's unconventional kendo-and-aikido coaching as to sensei imitation, the 'new breed' (Ochiai, Egawa) flouts the old ways, and William Kelly's 'Whiting problem' warns against polarizing Japanese and American baseball. The chapter's 'three strikes' against the label 'Japanese baseball' conclude that generalizations become dangerous when treated as monolithic nets cast over an entire culture.
- ch. 30 "Eighth Inning - Behind in the Count" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "Numbers as Players" **·** Thomas Kuhn, Michel Foucault — Power/Knowledge — Bennett and Martin argue that the statistics baseball fans treat as objective truth embody far more chance and convention than fans realize. Statistical theory shows luck explains roughly half the spread in 2002 batting averages (Bonds's .370 versus Larry Walker; the 2003 Ramirez–Mueller race decided by one at bat), and Jim Albert's simulation of 1,000 seasons shows about four of five World Series are not won by the most talented team. Drawing on Thomas Kuhn's history of scientific quantification and Theodore Porter's Foucauldian point that numbers 'have agency' and exercise power, they show statistics also act on the game — shaping drafting, salaries, and Hall of Fame election — so Schroeder's stats to Charlie Brown are both indispensable and misleading.
- ch. 32 "Softball The Good Is the Enemy of the Best" **·** Iris Marion Young, Simone de Beauvoir — Feminism; Woman as the Other; Embodiment (the lived body) — Heaphy asks whether softball limits women's potential to be equals on the baseball diamond, using feminist philosophy to explain why women are steered to the 'soft' game. Judith Williamson's analysis of women in mass culture and Simone de Beauvoir's woman-as-invisible-Other explain why female players — the AAGPBL's skirts and charm school, Toni Stone and Mamie Johnson in the Negro Leagues, Ila Borders in the minors — are covered as women first and ballplayers second. Iris Marion Young's 'Throwing Like a Girl' supplies the phenomenology: women throw differently not from anatomy but from experiencing the body as a burden, a socially produced self-fulfilling prophecy. Softball, 'the good,' becomes the enemy of the best — women playing hardball — by letting society postpone hard questions about gender.
- ch. 33 "Ninth Inning - Under Pressure" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "The Moral and Aesthetic Superiority of Baseball" **·** John Searle — Kretchmar asks whether the intentional walk of sluggers like Barry Bonds (or Casey at the Bat) is an unfair cop-out. Building an account of games as 'artificial tests' (via Bernard Suits and John Searle's treatment of conventions like 'hello'), he tests the analogy with basketball stalling and finds it fails: walking Bonds shifts rather than avoids the defensive test (the fielding team still owes all its outs), team games legitimately run away from stars as basketball double-teams Shaq, and fans gain new drama — Jeff Kent batting with the tying run after Bonds's walk. He concludes that event-regulated games like baseball, which cannot be shortened, enjoy a moral and aesthetic superiority over time-regulated games, whose endings breed stalling and clock-killing fouls.
- ch. 35 "Last Pitch" **·** Socrates, Plato — Socratic Method (elenchus); Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); Allegory of the Cave; The Unexamined Life — Reid argues that baseball and Socratic elenchus are both constructed to cultivate aretē, excellence. She maps four parallels onto concrete moments: both live off uncertainty (pre-game jitters, the 2001 Rivera–Gonzalez Series finish), both require risking failure (Ted Williams playing a doubleheader to protect his .400, Kirk Gibson's hobbling 1988 homer off Dennis Eckersley, Billy Beane's fear beside Lenny Dykstra), both demand active self-testing (Lou Gehrig retiring when complimented on a routine play), and both impose an obligation to return and challenge others — Plato's cave allegory, Socrates the gadfly, the aging champion who applauds the record-breaker. The Delphic oracle's riddle, that Socrates is wisest in knowing his ignorance, is the batter's-box humility every at-bat demands.
- ch. 36 "Post-Game - Press Conference" *(front)*
- ch. 37 "19b" **·** Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Utilitarianism — A paired debate on whether Pete Rose belongs in the Hall of Fame despite his lifetime ban for betting on the Reds, framed by retributive versus utilitarian theories of punishment. Aeon Skoble argues yes: betting on one's own team to win commits no fraud like throwing games, and Cooperstown honors baseball excellence, not moral perfection — the Hall already enshrines racists, adulterers, and cheats. Mark Hamilton answers no with the Dowd Report's evidence, Rule 21(d), and Giamatti's banishment statement: a managing gambler may sacrifice the season to settle tonight's debt, and reinstatement without remorse would declare the game's rules toothless.
- ch. 38 "20b" **·** Alasdair MacIntyre, Adam Smith — Virtues, Practices and Traditions; The Invisible Hand — A paired political-philosophy debate over Alex Rodriguez's $252 million contract. Albert Duncan defends the salary as the just outcome of market forces: stars like Bonds, McGwire, and Sosa maximize fan satisfaction, pack stadiums (600,000 extra Cardinals tickets in 1998), and generate hundreds of millions for local economies. Joel Shuman, a self-described socialist, replies with Alasdair MacIntyre's notion of baseball as a 'practice' whose intrinsic goods belong to a traditioned community of players, coaches, and fans: when market ideology alienates fans, unbalances competition, and prices out small markets, money has stopped serving the human activity it was meant to be part of.
- ch. 39 "21b" **·** William James — The Will to Believe — A paired metaphysics debate on whether superstition helps performance. Steven Streeter argues yes: baseball's mystery-rich outcomes breed ritual (Sparky Anderson refusing to step on foul lines, Wade Boggs's chicken, Nomar Garciaparra's glove taps), whose invisible forces — like black holes — can be inferred from their measurable effects on attention, preparation, and focus, with William James's belief-makes-reality as the capstone. Neil Feit counters no: on the counterfactual account of helping, a player cured of superstition would perform the same since his athletic ability is untouched, routines that aid concentration need involve no magical belief, and the burden of proof lies with the believer.
- ch. 40 "22b" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Categorical Imperative; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — A paired legal-philosophy debate on whether steroids should be banned. Michael McGrath argues yes from Kant: PED use cannot be universalized and 'the ends do not justify the means,' so the Ken Caminiti, Mark McGwire, and José Canseco confessions stain America's pastime, whose stars are role models and whose users risk devastating health effects — down to wrestler Davey Boy Smith's fatal steroid-related heart attack. Simon Eassom replies no by reframing drugs as Performance Enhancing Technologies: rules merely define the game, enhancers (unlike bat-cannons or netted catcher's gloves) do not de-skill baseball, and banning them because we dislike them is a matter of taste — of aesthetics, not ethics.
- ch. 41 "23b" *(no index entry yet)* — A paired aesthetics debate over the best baseball movie. Vincent Toscano picks The Natural: Robert Redford's Roy Hobbs enacts fall and redemption, Barry Levinson's halo-lit amber cinematography gives the film its mythic beauty, and its Reagan-era warmth answered America's post-sixties malaise. Larry Raful counters with It Happens Every Spring: the best baseball film lets the common man — Ray Milland's chemistry teacher armed with a wood-repelling mystery juice — dream himself into the Show, and he notes we cheer fictional cheating on screen while demanding that the real game stay pure.
- ch. 42 "24b" **·** Socrates — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — A paired philosophy-of-education debate on whether baseball players were better role models then or now. Edward Sullivan answers 'then' from lived memory: Ted Williams's Korean War service and his 1966 Hall of Fame induction speech demanding Satchel Paige and Josh Gibson be enshrined, and Jackie Robinson enduring Jim Crow spring training under Branch Rickey's pact of 'guts enough not to fight back.' Graham Harman answers 'now,' turning Socrates's Apology complaint that his most dangerous accusers are those safely dead into a diagnosis of nostalgia: the past buried Ty Cobb's and Cap Anson's racism, the Black Sox, and Hal Chase, while today's game is more racially open, physically dedicated, and union-protected.
- ch. 43 "The Team" *(notes)*
- ch. 44 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 45 "Baseball and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 46 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Basketball and Philosophy - Thinking Outside the Paint

- ch. 1 "The Philosophy of Popular Culture" *(front)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Copyright Page" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Dedication Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Power Foreword" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Acknowledgments" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Tip-Off" *(no index entry yet)* — The editors' introduction surveys how basketball is woven into American pop culture (Jordan, Shaq, films like Hoosiers and White Men Can't Jump, March Madness) and previews the volume's questions: how to measure greatness, whether intentional fouling is unethical, whether Zen improves a jump shot. It frames fans' MVP arguments (citing Ric Bucher's joke acronyms) as implicitly philosophical in their demand for defined terms, and notes Naismith was himself a philosophy major, making hoops 'the thinking person's game.' It applies no single philosophical doctrine, serving instead as a roadmap to the essays that follow.
- ch. 9 "First Quarter Baseline Values, Enduring Lessons" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Building Communities One Gym at a Time" **·** Alasdair MacIntyre, Michael Sandel, Michael Walzer, Thomas Hobbes — Communitarianism; Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — Webb uses Indiana high school basketball to argue the communitarian case against liberal individualism: drawing on MacIntyre, Sandel, and Walzer, he contends communities (not rights-bearing individuals) are the source of meaning, and that small-town gyms like the New Castle Fieldhouse were public spaces that built genuine community. He reads Rick Mount's choice of hometown fame over the pros, the Milan miracle, and the abolition of Indiana's single-class tournament as evidence that mass media and national sport replace real 'face-to-face' communities with 'virtual' ones, using Conseco Fieldhouse's nostalgic theming as his prime example of that loss.
- ch. 11 "To Hack or Not to Hack" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — Virtue Ethics (arete); Deontology; Utilitarianism; Practical Wisdom (phronesis) — Kennedy asks how a great player like Kareem (the NBA's career foul leader) can be excellent while fouling so much, and answers that deontology (Kant's categorical imperative) and utilitarianism (Mill's greatest-happiness principle) can condemn dangerous 'goon' fouling but cannot explain why the legal Hack-a-Shaq strategy still feels 'clownish.' Invoking Shaq's self-nickname 'the Big Aristotle,' he argues Aristotelian virtue ethics fits better: the excellent player, like the person of good character, perceives the situation rightly and fouls (or risks a foul) at the right time in the right way, so excellence is a habit of seeing well rather than rule-following.
- ch. 12 "Basketball Purists" **·** John Searle, Alasdair MacIntyre, John Stuart Mill — Communitarianism; Virtues, Practices and Traditions; On Liberty — Kretchmar defends a 'modified purist' view that team-oriented, fundamentals-based basketball is generally better than the individualist modernist game, offering three arguments. The functionalist argument borrows Searle's account of constitutive rules to say ten-person interactive complexity makes a richer, more durable 'test' (like chess vs. tic-tac-toe); the variety argument uses Mill's On Liberty to warn that market pressures homogenize basketball toward football; and the communitarian argument uses MacIntyre's notion of a 'practice' with internal goods to show ESPN Streetball's isolated one-on-one feats erode basketball as a cooperative practice.
- ch. 13 "Hardwood Dojos" **·** Aristotle, Seneca, Epictetus, Marcus Aurelius — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Stoicism; The Dichotomy of Control; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Bassham and Hamilton distill six success principles from coaches Smith, Pitino, Summitt, and Krzyzewski and show each echoes great philosophers, treating the court as a 'dojo' or place of enlightenment. Setting goals and pursuing excellence through habit map to Aristotle's eudaimonia and doctrine of habituation (Shaq the 'Big Aristotle' given the Nicomachean Ethics by Phil Jackson); the emphasis on outworking adversity and controlling only one's own effort maps to Stoics like Seneca, Epictetus, and Marcus Aurelius, as when Summitt and Dean Smith tell players to control what they can and accept the rest.
- ch. 14 "What Would Machiavelli Do" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli, John Locke, Thomas Hobbes, Immanuel Kant — The State of Nature; The Ends Justify the Means; The Social Contract — Reitsma treats the refereeless pickup game as a Lockean state of nature and asks how to beat the 'strategic ticky-tacker' who calls phantom fouls to win. He condemns first-strike cheating as wrong on Kantian grounds (the cheat 'makes an exception of himself') and by an ideal of Homeric athletic honor, then asks whether retaliatory ticky-tacking is justified. Reading Machiavelli's 'dirty hands' argument alongside Locke's claim that in a state of nature each person may punish transgressors, he concludes proportionate tit-for-tat cheating can make one a legitimate 'agent of justice' when no referee exists.
- ch. 15 "Basketball, Violence, Forgiveness, and Healing" **·** Forgiveness — Witte recounts the 1972 Ohio State-Minnesota brawl in which he was beaten unconscious, and reflects on his decades-long path from 'moral hatred' (a concept he draws from Murphy and Hampton) to forgiveness. Using Anne Minas's four senses of 'forgive,' he identifies his task as the fourth: giving up resentment without condoning the act, excusing punishment, or reversing his judgment. He frames forgiveness as 'setting it right,' the first step in a healing process, and narrates his eventual reconciliation with attackers Corky Taylor and Clyde Turner as evidence that no game is worth the loss of a relationship.
- ch. 16 "The Breaks of the Game" **·** Daniel Dennett, Bernard Williams — Moral Luck; Free Will vs Determinism — Davison analyzes luck and fairness in basketball, using Dennett's claim that the more skill (control) a player has, the less luck is involved, so luckiness is relative to persons and times (a fan's half-court shot versus Meadowlark Lemon's). Distinguishing fairness as equality from fairness as desert, and invoking Bernard Williams's 'constitutive luck,' he argues that the game's mechanisms (spherical ball, jump ball, many possessions, referees) minimize luck, and that famous lucky plays like Robert Horry's shot, NC State over Houston, and the phantom foul on Scottie Pippen do not make outcomes unfair, since fallible human refs are a necessary part of the game.
- ch. 17 "The Beauty of the Game" **·** David Hume, Plato, Aristotle — Theory of Forms; Judgment of Taste and the Sublime; Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — The Brands stage a broadcast-booth debate about whether a championship game was 'beautiful,' with Dickie V defending Hume's subjectivism ('beauty is in the eye of the beholder' and the expert's delicacy of taste) and Billy defending Plato's objective Form of Beauty. Jim exposes each side's flaw (Hume's circular appeal to experts, Plato's third-man regress from the Parmenides/Aristotle) and proposes a synthesis: games have 'outer beauty' (universally observable fluid motion, like improvised jazz or dance) and 'inner beauty' (the strategy and reason the knowledgeable fan grasps), reconciling the objective and subjective.
- ch. 18 "Second Quarter Prime-Time Players, Coaches, and Sages" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "The Zen Master and the Big Aristotle" **·** Aristotle, The Buddha, Harry Frankfurt — Zen Buddhism; Virtue Ethics (arete); The Four Noble Truths; Non-Self (anatman) — Allhoff and Vaidya argue against the skeptic that philosophy is useless 'bullshit' (nodding to Frankfurt's book) by showing how Phil Jackson's coaching applies it. Jackson's Zen Buddhism, especially zazen meditation and selfless 'triangle offense' play, teaches players to empty the ego and stay in the present, transforming Jordan from point-maker to play-maker; and Jackson's gift of Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics to Shaquille O'Neal, who then dubbed himself 'the Big Aristotle,' illustrates Aristotle's doctrine that excellence (his improved free-throw shooting, his generosity) is a habit built by repetition.
- ch. 20 "Wilt Versus Russell" **·** Aristotle, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Ralph Waldo Emerson, Niccolò Machiavelli — Man as Political Animal; Self-Reliance; Virtù and Fortuna — O'Connor frames the boyhood 'Wilt or Russell?' debate as a version of Aristotle's Politics puzzle that a man outside community is 'either a beast or a god': is dominator Wilt a basketball beast or a divine king, and is executive-team-player Russell the higher human ideal? He links Wilt's romantic individualism to Rousseau ('J.-J.') and Emerson's genius awaiting 'practical power,' and Aristotle's discussion of the natural king to the coaching dilemma of whether to make a gifted player dominate or blend. He ends by rejecting Machiavelli's 'realist' dismissal of ideals, insisting the Russellite ideal of teamwork can be higher even when domination wins more.
- ch. 21 "The Wizard versus the General" **·** Aristotle, Ralph Waldo Emerson — Practical Wisdom (phronesis); Virtue Ethics — Walls argues Bob Knight ('the General') is a greater coach than John Wooden ('the Wizard') on two criteria. First, drawing on Aristotle's remark that a good general makes the most of the troops at his disposal and Mortimer Adler's proportional standard of achievement, he says Knight won three titles with far less NBA talent while Wooden always had all-stars like Alcindor and Walton. Second, on integrity (Sophocles' 'fail with honor' over 'win by cheating'), he contrasts Knight's clean, high-graduation-rate program against widely alleged NCAA violations by UCLA booster Sam Gilbert, concluding Knight's temper flaws damage his legacy less than cheating damages Wooden's.
- ch. 22 "Third Quarter Shooting From The Perimeter" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 23 "The Dao of Hoops" **·** Laozi, Zhuangzi — Wu Wei (effortless action); Taoism (Daoism); The Tao (the Way); Yin and Yang — Dirk Dunbar, a former professional player, argues that Daoist concepts illuminate basketball excellence and that the sport can be a path to the Dao. Balancing yin and yang means blending competition with surrender and strategy with spontaneity; Zhuangzi's archer shows how craving victory drains power. Wuwei explains the 'zone' of effortless play Dunbar experienced while scoring sixty-five points in a high school game and seventy-four in Iceland, while qi grounds free-throw breathing and visualization and ziran the discipline of releasing a shot without willing it in. Drawing on Daode jing verses and Phil Jackson's Sacred Hoops, he claims basketball teaches a life of disciplined surrender beyond the ego.
- ch. 24 "Hoop Dreams, Blacktop Realities" **·** Immanuel Kant, Karl Marx, Plato — Humanity as an End in Itself; Commodity Fetishism; Allegory of the Cave — Bernard Jackson Jr. argues that basketball is an institution where black manhood is socially constructed (leaning on Naomi Zack's feminist point that gender identity is imposed by social designation), and that love among black players is essential to that construction while toughness is equivocal - the 'soft' label stuck to the gentlemanly David Robinson even as the Pistons walked off without congratulating the Bulls. Reading the documentary Hoop Dreams, he shows Arthur Agee and William Gates being used as commodities by recruiters, Coach Pingatore, and family expectations, invoking Marx on exploitation and 'use value' (Spike Lee's Nike-camp speech) and Kant's demand that persons be treated as ends, never merely as means. He likens William Gates's return to Chicago's Cabrini-Green as a minister to Plato's enlightened prisoner going back down into the cave.
- ch. 25 "She Got Game" **·** Michel Foucault — Discipline and Docile Bodies; Feminism; Objectification — The Wallaces argue that both the detractors (Stacey Pressman's claim that women's basketball 'sucks') and the admirers (John Wooden's 'purest basketball' praise) judge the women's game by patriarchal standards drawn from the men's game. Using Sandra Bartky's feminist extension of Foucault, they show how the female body was disciplined under male surveillance - early rule changes split the court and banned stealing to protect 'femininity', while players deflected the 'Amazon' charge with beauty pageants, revealing uniforms, and the Playboy offer to Lisa Harrison. The modern WNBA of Lisa Leslie and Michelle Snow, who can dunk but choose team play, points to a new game that escapes the feminine/masculine dichotomy instead of measuring women against men.
- ch. 26 "Fourth Quarter Metaphysical Madness" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "Shooting with Confidence" **·** Bernard Williams, Francis Bacon — Kevin Kinghorn asks why practice stars like Kentucky's Josh Carrier suddenly choke in games and argues, via Bernard Williams's 'Deciding to Believe', that a player cannot simply choose to believe her next shot will go in: beliefs aim at truth, so a belief known to be self-chosen would not be a belief at all, any more than a mapmaker could 'choose' where to put Florida. Instead, desire and fear shape belief indirectly - Francis Bacon's 'whatever a man wishes were true, that he more readily believes' explains Larry Bird's unshakeable confidence, while fear of being the goat (Fred Brown's 1982 pass, Chris Webber's timeout) sabotages the underconfident. The practical strategies are to stockpile evidence of success through goals and visualization, or to remove the feared consequences of missing, as Rick Pitino did by publicly promising Kenny Walker he would stay a Knick as long as he kept shooting.
- ch. 28 "The Hot Hand in Basketball" **·** Philosophical Skepticism — Steven Hales rebuts the Gilovich-Vallone-Tversky studies (and Stephen Jay Gould) claiming that hot hands in basketball are statistical illusions. He dismantles their three skeptical arguments - 'success doesn't breed success' (76ers data), the demand for statistically improbable streaks (Joe DiMaggio's 56-game hitting streak), and the chance argument about run frequencies - showing each rests on an arbitrary, unprincipled cutoff for how 'hot' a streak must be, in the classic skeptical pattern of setting knowledge standards impossibly high. He then defends a commonsense definition on which a hot hand just is any run of above-average shooting: Wilt Chamberlain's thirty-five straight makes made him genuinely hot even if no one could feel the streak ending, and fans' and players' widespread judgments are vindicated rather than error-theorized away.
- ch. 29 "Philosophers Can't Jump" **·** John Dewey, Immanuel Kant, Augustine of Hippo — Experience and Education; Phenomenology; Pragmatism — Tim Elcombe uses Dewey's pragmatist account of lived experience to read the Hoosiers scene in Butler Fieldhouse: Coach Dale's tape measure shows time and space are durable constants (the basket is ten feet in Indianapolis too), yet the awestruck Huskers will live that same space in a radically new way, and Kant and Augustine frame the gap between clock time and lived time. He argues basketball is the most 'phenomenal' game because the ticking clock creates genuine drama (Reggie Miller's 1995 buzzer three, childhood 'buzzer beater' games) and the ten-foot basket builds a vertical dimension no other sport matches, answering Scott Kretchmar's defense of event-regulated baseball.
