Our cultural obsession with dunking - Michael Jordan's Nike ads, Teen Wolf, Like Mike, White Men Can't Jump - expresses the human fascination with briefly defying gravity, a 'moving image of eternity'.
- ch. 30 "Playing for the Same Team Again" **·** Plutarch, Heraclitus, Thomas Hobbes, John Searle, Aristotle — Ship of Theseus; Personal Identity; Everything Flows / Logos — A fictional five-day phone dialogue between Michael Jordan and Phil Jackson works through the metaphysics of team identity before Jordan's 1995 comeback: qualitative versus numerical sameness, necessary and sufficient conditions ('same players, same positions'), Heraclitus's river, and whether the Bulls survive gradual turnover. The Ship of Theseus becomes the 'Boars' puzzle - the original five reassembled on an expansion team versus the continuous, degenerate Chicago roster - with Hobbes's two-ship twist, alongside the Minneapolis Lakers' move and a Bulls-Pistons gradual player swap. The resolution distinguishes the group (which persists by composition or continuity) from the team, which 'counts as' the Bulls only partly through fans' allegiance, echoing Searle's social reality; Jordan concludes he was asking the wrong question, and Aristotle's circular account of virtue supplies the model for circular criteria of loyal fandom.
- ch. 31 "Plato and Aristotle on the Role of Soul in Taking the Rock to the Hole" **·** Plato, Aristotle, Immanuel Kant — Tripartite Soul; Virtue Ethics (arete); The Golden Mean; Justice in the Republic — Daniel Gallagher reads Patrick Sparks's 2005 Elite Eight free-throw clank and miracle buzzer-beater through Plato's tripartite soul: the rational part failed at the line but the spirited part carried the off-balance game-tying three, while the Republic's three classes map onto basketball's workers (Steve Nash's passes), warriors (Dennis Rodman's rebounding), and rulers (John Stockton's leadership). Aristotle's four character types structure learning a jump shot - vicious, weak, self-controlled, and finally virtuous, as reason and inclination harmonize through repetition ('we are what we repeatedly do') - and the golden mean governs shot selection between the ball hog and the compulsive passer, with Michael Jordan as the player whose habituated instincts find the mean. Against Kantian armchair ethics, the jump shot and Kareem's sky hook were discovered by trial and error: virtuous performers set the standard, and we become good shooters by watching and imitating them.
- ch. 32 "The Basket That Never Was" **·** Philosophical Skepticism; Scientific Realism vs Anti-Realism — Thomas Flint's thought experiment - a conference-championship buzzer-beater waved off because the clock expired, with inconclusive video - asks whether South Shore 'really' scored. He rejects the 'Goofy View' that game facts are referee-independent, since it would entail that only he and his camcorder-owning boss know who won any game ever (imperiling even Michael Jordan's 1982 title and Pistol Pete's scoring record) and would poison a coach's duty to teach rule-following when officials miss traveling. He also rejects the 'Tyranny of the Zebras' that makes officials infallible like the pope, since NCAA replay rules presuppose fallibility, and settles on officials as contest judges who award points: without the verdict no points exist, yet a blown call remains a genuine mistake in failing to apply the rules properly.
- ch. 33 "Hoosiers and the Meaning of Life" **·** Aristotle, Thomas Aquinas, Hannah Arendt, Bertrand Russell, Jacques Derrida, Martin Buber — The Meaning of Life; Eudaimonia (flourishing); Forgiveness; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Michael Peterson argues that Hoosiers is a microcosm of life's meaning: Aristotle's eudaimonia frames the characters' searches (Norman Dale rebuilding his career, Shooter his self-esteem and his bond with son Everett), and Dale's 'in my book, we're going to be winners' speech shows that character, unselfish teamwork, and playing to potential - virtue learned by doing, as coaching models moral instruction - matter more than the scoreboard against South Bend Central. The film's second chances (Principal Summers's clean slate, Whit's return, Dale's hiring of the town drunk Shooter) reveal that character alone is insufficient: mercy and forgiveness, which Hannah Arendt traces to Jesus, reframe an unchangeable past and open a future. Peterson then surveys worldviews - naturalism (Bertrand Russell's 'accidental collocations of atoms'), postmodernism (Derrida's deconstruction), Hinduism's Atman-Brahman - and argues only Christian theism, in which God's nature is love (Aquinas), can ground the character and forgiveness the film dramatizes.
- ch. 34 "The Lineup" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "Index" *(notes)*

## Batman and Philosophy - The Dark Knight of the Soul

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Batman and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "The Blackwell Philosophy and Pop Culture Series" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Copyright Page" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Acknowledgements" *(notes)*
- ch. 8 "Introduction" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "PART ONE - DOES THE DARK KNIGHT ALWAYS DO RIGHT" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "So, Case Closed-Right" **·** Philippa Foot, Judith Jarvis Thomson — The Trolley Problem; Utilitarianism; Deontology — Mark D. White asks why Batman refuses to kill the Joker even after the Joker murdered Jason Todd, paralyzed Barbara Gordon, and killed Sarah Essen. Framing the refusal as deontological rather than utilitarian, he runs the case through Foot and Thomson's trolley problem and transplant case, arguing the classics disanalogize because the Joker himself creates the danger and because 'do not kill' is an agent-specific rule rather than an agent-neutral one. He extends the analysis to prepunishment via Philip K. Dick's Minority Report, and concedes that even a self-described strict deontologist can conclude Batman maybe should have killed the Joker years ago.
- ch. 11 "Sometimes Heroes Fail" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle, Plato, Alasdair MacIntyre, John Stuart Mill, Jeremy Bentham — The Categorical Imperative; Utilitarianism; Virtue Ethics — James DiGiovanna asks whether it was right for Batman to turn street kids Dick Grayson and Jason Todd into Robin. He tests the training against Kant's universalizability (including the Joker-demanding-to-know-where-Robin-is lie example), against Bentham and Mill's utilitarian appeal to Gotham's general good, and finally reads it through the virtue ethics of Plato, Aristotle, and MacIntyre's After Virtue, where Robin is trained into a role-specific character rather than taught rules. Jason's rashness and death at the Joker's hands show Batman failed to moderate his ward's courage, and the chapter concludes Batman is 'a lousy deontologist, a decent consequentialist, and some kind of virtue ethicist.'
- ch. 12 "Lacking Balance" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Golden Mean; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Stephen Kershnar argues that Batman's hatred of criminals is not a vice but a virtue, since hating what is evil is the morally appropriate attitude toward wrongdoers like the Joker or Batman: Year One's brutal Detective Flass. Against Aristotle's person-focused Virtuous-Persons Theory he defends a 'Virtuous-Thoughts-and-Actions Theory,' illustrated by Gordon's retaliatory beating of Flass and Batman elbow-smashing a pimp in Year One. The chapter closes on the golden mean of balanced love and hate: Batman's virtuous hatred leaves his life isolated and unflourishing, showing that virtue does not guarantee eudaimonia.
- ch. 13 "PART TWO - LAW, JUSTICE, AND THE SOCIAL ORDER WHERE DOES BATMAN FIT IN" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "The Thin Veil" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Niccolò Machiavelli — The State of Nature; The Social Contract — Brett Chandler Patterson reads the No Man's Land storyline, in which earthquake-isolated Gotham is carved into gang territories by Two-Face, Penguin, and the Joker, alongside Hurricane Katrina's flooding of New Orleans as twin real-and-fictional tests of Hobbes's state of nature and the social contract. He contrasts Jim Gordon's lawful, Machiavellian-seeming rebuilding of trust with Officer William Petit's slide into execution-style 'despotic' power, and highlights the pacifist witness of Father Christian and Dr. Leslie Thompkins against Hobbes's demand that churches defer to the sovereign. Batman's turn as a benevolent gang lord working to hand the city back to Gordon shows, for Patterson, that Batman's real enemy is anarchy itself.
- ch. 15 "Theorizing Government" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Friedrich Nietzsche — The State of Nature; The Social Contract; Anarchism — Tony Spanakos argues that Gotham's government, not Bruce's psyche, explains Batman: the state failed at Crime Alley, and nearly every major character reacts to a state that is either too weak or too restrictive. Working from Max Weber's definition of the state as monopoly on legitimate violence, Hobbes's Leviathan, and Nietzsche's state as 'new idol,' he reads Year One's corrupt Commissioner Loeb, Year Two's self-appointed Reaper, Anarky's failed anarchist dystopia, Two-Face's broken faith in the law, and The Dark Knight Returns' state-loyal Superman and Commissioner Yindel. The Batman-Gordon partnership emerges as a precarious unofficial order in which Gordon tolerates the one exception to the state's monopoly on violence.
- ch. 16 "Who Has the Last Laugh" **·** Harry Frankfurt — Second-Order Desires (personhood); Free Will vs Determinism; Compatibilism — Christopher Robichaud asks whether the insane Joker, who paralyzed Barbara Gordon and beat Jason Todd to death, can be held morally responsible for his crimes. After distinguishing causal from moral responsibility and using a Frankfurt-style Joker-machine case to keep free will compatible with determinism, he applies Frankfurt's second-order desires: the Joker's madness blocks the capacity to evaluate and rank his own homicidal first-order desires. But because the Red Hood freely chose a life of crime without foreseeing that a chemical plunge would make him a lunatic, the origin story does not rescue responsibility, and the chapter concludes the Joker is not morally responsible, leaving Batman's duties toward him unresolved.
- ch. 17 "PART THREE - ORIGINS AND ETHICS BECOMING THE CAPED CRUSADER" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "Batman Forever" **·** Epicurus, Aristotle, Thomas Nagel — Death Is Nothing to Us; Deontology; Consequentialism — Randall Jensen examines the boyhood promise Bruce made after his parents' murder ('I swear by the spirits of my parents... to avenge their deaths by spending the rest of my life warring on all criminals') and argues it is a backward-looking, deontological moral reason that consequentialism cannot accommodate. He then asks whether a promise to the dead can bind at all, weighing Epicurus's argument that death is nothing to us against Aristotle's and Thomas Nagel's postmortem and unexperienced harms, concluding Bruce wrongs the living people Thomas and Martha Wayne were if he breaks it. From The Dark Knight Returns to Kingdom Come, Batman emerges as a man who can never fully keep the promise yet cannot live without it.
- ch. 19 "But This Ruins Everything!" **·** Peter Singer — The Drowning Child (obligation to aid); Utilitarianism — Mahesh Ananth and Ben Dixon pit young Bruce Wayne against Peter Singer: given seven billion dollars, Singer's 'Famine, Affluence, and Morality' principle, illustrated by the drowning child, seems to require giving nearly everything to famine relief rather than dressing up as a bat. Against appeals to honoring his parents' memory and to supererogation, the authors deploy Singer's strong and moderate versions of giving and utilitarian indifference to distance, concluding that a true utilitarian would forbid becoming Batman. The irony they highlight is that the older Batman himself reasons utilitarianly when he abandons Jason Todd to intercept the Joker's poisoned convoy in A Death in the Family.
- ch. 20 "Batman Is a Moral Exemplar" **·** Virtue Ethics — Ryan Indy Rhodes and David Kyle Johnson defend Batman as a genuine moral exemplar whose justice, beneficence, generosity, and courage (from reprinting No Man's Land land deeds to sacrificing his reputation in Batman Begins) are worth emulating even though no one can copy his feats. They rebut the unrealistic objection, the language objection that 'Batman is virtuous' lacks a truthmaker (likening it to 'dragons breathe fire'), and the exaggeration objection by noting that exemplars like Buddha, Jesus, and Socrates reach us through embellished, partly fictional depictions anyway. Fiction even helps, since we can read Batman's thought balloons, and his growth into an 'icon' supplies the stable true character that emulation requires.
- ch. 21 "PART FOUR - WHO IS THE BATMAN (IS THAT A TRICK QUESTION )" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "Can You Face the Bat" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Michel Foucault — Will to Power; Power/Knowledge; Perspectivism; Eternal Recurrence — Sarah Donovan and Nicholas Richardson use Nietzsche and Foucault to present Batman as a self-constructed identity: across Batman: Year One, Arkham Asylum, and The Dark Knight Returns, Bruce consciously builds the persona ('Yes, Father, I shall become a bat') rather than revealing a deeper true self. Foucault's constructed categories of normal and abnormal surface in Arkham Asylum's 'super-sane' Joker and the Alice in Wonderland parallels that put Batman's own sanity in question alongside Amadeus Arkham's madness. Nietzsche's will to power and eternal recurrence, via the shepherd biting the snake in Zarathustra, explain why Bruce can face the bat that others flee, becoming heroic where Arkham and Dr. Cavendish go insane.
- ch. 23 "All Joking Aside, This Is a Modal Muddle" **·** Possible Worlds; Naming and Necessity (rigid designators) — Sam Cowling and Chris Ragg ask whether Batman could have been the Joker, using the Indiscernibility of Identicals and the Necessity of Identity over possible worlds to argue that if 'Batman' is a name of a merely possible individual, he could not have been identical to the Joker. The 'Robin argument' (many people have been Robin) pushes back that 'Batman' might be a disguised description, and the authors then treat Batman and the Joker as abstract fictional characters whose modal properties are stipulated by their authors. On that view the Elseworlds tale Batman: Two-Faces, where the two are literally one Jekyll-and-Hyde person, shows Batman could have been the Joker after all.
- ch. 24 "Keeping It in the Family" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Language Games — Jason Southworth deploys counterexamples, from Golden Age Batman shooting the vampire Monk with silver bullets, through Jean-Paul Valley and Dick Grayson serving as Batman during Knightfall and Prodigal, to the Frankenstein twist of Castle of the Bat and the criminal Batman of Earth-A, to show that nothing (not being Bruce Wayne, not never killing, not murdered parents) is necessary or sufficient for being Batman. Turning to Wittgenstein's Philosophical Investigations, he applies family resemblance and the game examples: 'Batman' works like 'game,' identified through overlapping similarities rather than a fixed definition. Retconned Robins and disappearing Batarangs show language works without fixed meanings, which he warns undermines using Batman as a determinate moral exemplar.
- ch. 25 "What It Is Like to Be Out of the Asylum" **·** Thomas Nagel, Simone de Beauvoir, Frank Jackson, Immanuel Kant — What Is It Like to Be a Bat?; The Knowledge Argument (Mary's Room); The Ethics of Ambiguity; Phenomena and Noumena — Ron Novy argues nobody can know what it is like to be Batman: deploying Nagel's 'What Is It Like to Be a Bat?' and a Mary's-Room variant with a color-deprived Barbara Gordon, he shows subjective experience resists even complete physical knowledge. Simone de Beauvoir's situated freedom then explains how 'one bad day' (Bruce's parents' murder, the Joker's Red Hood plunge into chemicals) threw each man into the same terrifying glimpse of Kant's noumenal world behind appearances, yet into opposite narratives of order and chaos. Even while sharing the joke that ends The Killing Joke, Batman and the Joker remain mutually unknowable, each the only one who can 'keep up' with the other.
- ch. 26 "PART FIVE - BEING THE BAT INSIGHTS FROM EXISTENTIALISM AND TAOISM" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "Paradox and Peace" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Martin Heidegger, Jean-Paul Sartre — The Leap of Faith; Despair (sickness unto death); Thrownness (Geworfenheit); Existence Precedes Essence — Christopher Drohan reads Batman and Alfred through Kierkegaard's Fear and Trembling: Batman, like Abraham, believes 'by virtue of the absurd,' resigning himself to an infinite, never-realizable justice as a knight of infinite resignation, while Alfred embodies the higher knight of faith whose justice is concrete love expressed in mending the suit, making sandwiches, and guarding the manor. Heidegger's thrownness and Sartre's condemned freedom frame both men's absurd existential condition, including Batman sparing Joe Chill and even allying with him against the Reaper. The chapter's verdict is that Alfred's faithful devotion to one person is the superior ethic, and that peace begins with him.
- ch. 28 "Conclusions, Capes, and Cowls" **·** Martin Heidegger, Jean-Paul Sartre, Albert Camus, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Authenticity; The They-Self (das Man); Being-Toward-Death; Dasein (being-there) — Jason Howard asks how Batman keeps from becoming what he fights, answering with Heidegger's authentic conscience against Rousseau's innate moral-sense view. Bruce's guilt over his parents' death is reinterpreted in a Heideggerian 'moment of vision': the bat through the window summons Dasein out of the they-self, so becoming Batman is not vengeance but the call of an authentic conscience that owns guilt, fallenness, and death. Batman's return from alcoholic retirement in The Dark Knight Returns shows conscience as 'the loyalty of existence to its own Self,' a freedom-toward-death that makes him instructive rather than merely heroic.
- ch. 29 "Determinism and the Dark Knight" **·** Martin Heidegger — Being-Toward-Death; Authenticity; Thrownness (Geworfenheit); Dasein (being-there) — David Hart uses Heidegger to dissolve the question of whether Batman is determined by childhood trauma or freely chooses his mission. Heidegger's being-in-the-world and thrown-project, illustrated by Alfred the butler for whom the clock is essentially something to dust, replace the subject-object picture that grounds both free-will and determinism talk. Read this way, the bat crashing through the window in Batman: Year One is Bruce's Angst-laden confrontation with death as 'the possibility of my own impossibility,' through which he freely takes up the project of being Batman when nothing forced him to.
- ch. 30 "PART SIX - FRIEND, FATHER, . . . RIVAL THE MANY ROLES OF THE BAT" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "To Be Continued . ." **·** Virtue Ethics — Galen Foresman arms Bat-fans with value theory: most evaluative comparisons of Batman and Superman beg the question, since Super-fans load pros-and-cons lists with superpowers and Bat-fans with intelligence without independently justifying why those traits count. He then argues that bravery is essential to superhero greatness, because the brave person knowingly confronts danger, so the powerless, bulletable Batman is systematically braver than the nearly invulnerable Superman. The conclusion inverts the fans' debate: the very superpowers Super-fans celebrate are what disqualify Superman from being the better hero.
- ch. 32 "BSFs Best Superfriends Forever" **·** Aristotle, Friedrich Nietzsche — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); The Übermensch — Daniel Malloy argues Batman and Superman are friends who disagree about friendship itself: Superman practices Aristotle's highest philia between good men who are 'another self' and true equals, symbolized by the kryptonite ring he trusts Batman to use on him should he go rogue, while Batman's friendship is Nietzschean, loving in his friend the overman 'as your cause' in the one equal he respects as a rival. Stories like Tower of Babel, Bruce Wayne: Fugitive, and the Brother Eye satellite show Batman manipulates everyone except this one peer, and that he never fights Superman fairly because he does not need to. Their fundamental, unresolvable differences, Malloy concludes, are exactly what strengthen the World's Finest friendship.
- ch. 33 "Leaving the Shadow of the Bat" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant — Virtue Ethics (arete); Autonomy of the Will — Carsten Fogh Nielsen stages Aristotle against Kant on moral education: Aristotle says we become just by doing just acts and imitating exemplary people, as Robin imitates Batman, while Kant objects that every example must first be appraised by principles of morality and that imitation breeds heteronomy rather than autonomy. The Gordon-Yindel disagreement over Batman in The Dark Knight Returns shows people can genuinely clash about who counts as morally exemplary in the first place. Dick Grayson resolves the tension by using the courage, skills, and integrity Batman instilled in him to stand up to Batman and become Nightwing, proving emulation can be the route to, not the enemy of, Kantian autonomy.
- ch. 34 "NOTES" **·** Laozi, Zhuangzi — Taoism (Daoism); Yin and Yang; The Tao (the Way); Wu Wei (effortless action) — The book's final chapter is a mock interview in which 'Bat-Tzu' applies Taoism to Bruce Wayne's life: his yang (hard, retributive crime-fighting) has dominated his yin (his tenderness toward Dick, Tim, and Helena), and Robin has always been the yin balancing the Dark Knight. Quoting Lao-Tzu's Tao Te Ching and Chuang-Tzu on duality, acceptance of death (Jason Todd's resurrection), and effortlessness, the piece casts Alfred as the Taoist sage whose help exemplifies wu-wei, like Chuang-Tzu's butcher who cuts without dulling his blade. It even reads Batman's most intimidating feature, his absent shadow, as proof that nothingness can matter more than substance.
- ch. 35 "CONTRIBUTORS" *(notes)*
- ch. 36 "INDEX" *(notes)*

## Batman, Superman, and Philosophy - Badass or Boyscout_

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "It’s About Damn Time" *(no index entry yet)* — The editor's introduction reframes the Batman-versus-Superman brawl: what fans are really arguing about is not strength but who is the better hero and "who should we try to be like." Michaud recalls learning sacrifice and persistence from Burton's Batman and total self-giving from the Death of Superman, and frames the book's choice as hope in humanity versus cynical vigilance about what people could become. He promises that the assembled philosophers flatly disagree and leaves the verdict of the fight to the reader.
- ch. 8 "Pre-Fight Warm-Up Battle of the Billennium" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "1. Where Have All the Supermen Gone" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; The Apollonian and the Dionysian; The Death of God; Will to Power — Holdier argues that neither hero is Nietzsche's Übermensch: Superman's servile mild-mannered Clark Kent guise would strike Nietzsche as slave morality, while Batman's self-loathing (his Hush confession that deep down he is not a good person) and breakable human body disqualify him too. Instead, reading them through The Birth of Tragedy and Grant Morrison's Supergods, Superman embodies the Apollonian force of order, sunlight, and rationality and Batman the Dionysian force of chaos and passion, so their battles (The Dark Knight Returns, Batman v Superman) are the inevitable but never-final friction of art's twin poles. The fight's real winner is the reader, who gets both deities at once.
- ch. 10 "Round One Decision—Superman! The Son Shines Bright" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "2. Sure We’re Criminals" **·** Socrates, Thomas Hobbes, John Locke, David Hume, Robert Nozick — The Social Contract; Natural Rights; Anarchism — Malloy asks whether the two law-breaking vigilantes are criminals and whether any duty to obey the law survives scrutiny. He runs consent theory through Superman surrendering in handcuffs in Man of Steel and the Red Son Superman's residence in the USSR, uses Darkseid's Apokolips as Hobbes's security sovereign against Locke's natural-rights limits, deploys Hume against tacit consent (Batman would never leave Gotham even in No Man's Land), and dismantles gratitude theory (the Kents' adoption involved a sacrificial choice the law cannot make) and fair play (Nozick's Batman-lottery undercuts "I'm not wearing hockey pads"). He lands near Wolff-style philosophical anarchism: Superman's constrained lawbreaking endorses the law's legitimacy while Batman treats law as one more tool in his war, making him a force of chaos.
- ch. 12 "3. Darwin in Metropolis" **·** Social Darwinism — Springett applies evolutionary biology to the matchup: comparing fitness, adaptations, and arms races (Batman's escalating gadget war with his rogues versus Superman facing no competition at all), he concludes Superman is objectively fitter, while on the technical definition of altruism Batman is more altruistic than the invulnerable Superman, who cannot lower his own fitness. Redefining a "Superhero" as an individual who effectively raises the fitness of many others, and adding Gould's catastrophist view that species extinction is the norm, he argues a planet with Superman keeps the whole species alive through catastrophes Batman could never avert. He explicitly rejects Social Darwinism - Superman is its counterexample because he saves the less fit - and, with Dawkins, credits conscious awareness with letting us choose our own goals, crowning Superman the superior Superhero.
- ch. 13 "4. A Cape and a Code" **·** Virtue Ethics — Farrell traces the heroes' knightly nicknames (Dark Knight, Caped Crusader, Man of Steel) to medieval chivalry as C.S. Lewis defines it in "The Necessity of Chivalry": the knight's double demand to be both stern and meek, each "to the nth degree," with knightly character a work of art achieved rather than a gift of nature. Both heroes show the split (playboy Bruce versus Spartan Batman in Batman Begins, meek Clark versus bold Superman), and both go to extremes - Batman's interrogation-room brutality in The Dark Knight, Superman turning back time for Lois in Superman: The Movie - but Lewis's hardest test is the mighty man who restrains himself. Superman's lifelong self-restraint, contrasted with uninhibited red-kryptonite Clark in Smallville's "Red," makes him the truer modern knight.
- ch. 14 "5. How Heroes Argue" **·** Aristotle, Friedrich Nietzsche — The Death of God; Virtue Ethics (arete) — LaBauve opens with a staged Justice League debate over rescuing the Joker's hostages, where Batman's emotional, vengeance-tinged appeal loses to Superman's trust-based plea, and reads it through Aristotle's On Rhetoric: Batman persuades almost entirely by pathos (the relatable brokenness of Nolan's trilogy) with detective's logos, while Superman balances pathos, logos, and above all ethos, since Aristotle calls the speaker's character the strongest appeal. Against Max Landis's claim that Superman is irrelevant, he pairs the Death of Superman with Nietzsche's "God is dead" parable and the Kill Bill monologue to argue that Superman is a mythology of virtue - alongside real moral heroes like Gandhi, King, and Jesus - showing humans the ideal they ought to become, so Superman not only wins but must win.
- ch. 15 "Round Two Decision—Batman! More Human than a Superman" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "6. The God We Need Now" **·** The Death of God — Ketcham stages the essay as a 1939 diary (a boyhood piece longing for a new god now that the biblical God has, he feels, abandoned the Depression-era world) followed by an old man's reread: America made Superman its messiah - born in a flash of light like the star of Bethlehem, canonized by DC's "publishing synod" much as the church redacted the New Testament - but he suspects the god will age as badly as the God of Abraham. Rereading after Korea, Vietnam, and Wall Street scandals, he argues we now need and worship a darker god modeled on Hades: the rich, hidden, vengeful billionaire Bruce Wayne, whose subterranean mansion, Cerberus-like Alfred, and tax-break tributes from citizens mirror the underworld god and Greek sacrifice. He ends imagining our grandchildren's next god as a cyber-god of AI, and declares Batman, not Superman, the god we need now.
- ch. 17 "7. How Batman Cowed a God" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Master and Slave Morality; Ressentiment; The Übermensch — Reider reads the two heroes through Nietzsche's genealogy of morals: Superman's humility, guilt, empathy, and farmer-parent values (he is Clark Kent at heart, keeping the alter ego to unload the burden of his power) are textbook slave morality, making him the unwitting pawn of the weak rather than Nietzsche's Übermensch, while Batman's pride, wrath, and vow of vengeance - he refuses to kill the Joker or Robin's attacker not from respect for life but from aristocratic pride - revive the pagan "good" of Achilles dragging Hector's body. From this he builds a deliberately provocative strategic claim: because slave morality leaves Superman secretly craving to be bested and made ordinary again, the merely human Batman can stare down and "cow" the god in their showdowns, winning the psychological war Wonder Woman's and Lois's preferences supposedly confirm.
- ch. 18 "8. Saints and Superheroes" **·** Peter Singer, Susan Wolf — The Drowning Child (obligation to aid); Utilitarianism; Deontology — Malloy uses the two heroes to explain supererogation - Urmson's "Saints and Heroes" puzzle of acts that are praiseworthy but not obligatory, like Batman rushing into burning buildings while Jimmy Olsen is under no duty to follow. Against the utilitarian and deontological denials that anything goes beyond duty (Singer's argument that Clark Kent's designer glasses are money he owes to charity, and Susan Wolf's Loving and Rational Saints whose lives, like Batman's broken retirements in The Dark Knight Returns and Batman Beyond, leave no room for anything worth living), he adopts Raz's view that a duty can be overridden by the moral reason of grave personal risk. Hence Batman's nightly risk-taking is a genuinely heroic choice, while Superman's powers make saving others morally required - so Batman is more truly a hero and Superman "just a good person with the powers of a living god."
- ch. 19 "9. Masks, Hearts, and Superheroes" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Golden Mean; Ring of Gyges (why be moral?) — Dupree and Fuš build an independent definition of the "true superhero" from identity statements (Batman = Bruce Wayne, Clark Kent = Superman): only an essentially human character who forges near-superhuman excellence through cultivated virtue can count, and Aristotle's virtue ethics supplies the standard. Batman qualifies - his refusal to kill the Joker shows the struggle for the golden mean between foolhardiness and cowardice, and Gotham Knights #31 shows how far he plans to avoid killing - whereas powers merely given, Superman's Kryptonian biology or the Green Lantern ring (their update of Gyges's Ring), bestow no virtue, as Kyle Rayner's and Guy Gardner's early careers show. Since Clark Kent is "in his heart" the alien Superman while Bruce Wayne is merely Batman's mask, Batman is a true superhero and Superman is not.
- ch. 20 "10. Gods Are Overrated" **·** David Hume, Friedrich Nietzsche — The Problem of Evil; The Death of God; Immortality and the Soul; The Meaning of Life — Michaud grants that Superman is godlike and turns Hume's problem of evil on him: if Superman is all-powerful, all-knowing, and good, why does suffering persist that he could prevent at super-speed - the desert animal dying painfully for no purpose, the crimes that happen while he picnics with Lois - and the free-will defense and "it's all part of the plan" excuses fail him even worse than they fail God. Superman then becomes our excuse for doing nothing, and like belief in immortality and the afterlife (holy wars where no one "really" dies, prayers instead of feeding the starving) he dulls our sense that this life is all we get. Reading Nietzsche's "God is dead" as the discovery that we keep gods only to feel special and saved, Michaud concludes we would live more meaningful, Batman-like self-reliant lives if the savior-Superman were dead to us.
- ch. 21 "Round Three Decision—Superman! The Best Man for Tomorrow" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "11. Of Bats and Supermen" **·** Maurice Merleau-Ponty, Thomas Nagel — Embodiment (the lived body); What Is It Like to Be a Bat?; Social Darwinism — Springett argues Superman, not Batman, is the proper goal of transhumanism - Batman's training and gadgets are only a stage toward transcending the human condition - and that the eugenics and Social Darwinism objection fails because Superman directs his superior fitness at saving the less fit. Through Merleau-Ponty's embodied cognition (Lois's churning stomach over a deadline, Alfred retracing his steps and posture to recall a thought, the "Subway Superman" Wesley Autrey's instinctive track rescue) he shows that radically different bodies mean radically different minds and moral perception. That turns Nagel's "What Is It Like to Be a Bat?" into "what is it like to be Superman?" - a problem of other minds for our transhuman future, with Watchmen's Doctor Manhattan as the warning of a transcendent being who simply stops caring about humans.
- ch. 23 "12. Vigilante Wanted" **·** Forgiveness — In a fictional Dust Bowl voice, Ketcham has a 1940 farm kid watching foreclosure bulldozers decide which single comic hero to keep as the vigilante who will burn down the bank - weighing Superman, who has gone over to the big-city bankers as reporter Clark Kent, against Batman, whose riches resemble the bankers' but whose hatred of his parents' killer matches his own. He tests his candidates against Hamlet's fatal indecision, Henry V at Agincourt, a Lex Luthor who is evil exactly like the bank president, and Tom Joad of The Grapes of Wrath, the real "I'll be ever'where in the dark" vigilante. In the end the boy burns the Batman box, reads Superman's restraint as the preacher's forgiveness, and lets his anger - and the need for any vigilante - go.
- ch. 24 "13. Time to Choose" **·** John Stuart Mill, Immanuel Kant — Utilitarianism; Deontology; Moral Luck; The Categorical Imperative — Goldberg and Gavaler read the heroes' first-year comics (The Superman Chronicles, The Batman Chronicles) as rival moral codes: Superman is a "super consequentialist" out to benefit mankind - reforming the Blakely Mine owner, demolishing slums, buying back swindled stock - while Batman is a "dark deontologist" whose vengeance oath makes consequences irrelevant, waiting out the Joker's murders of Claridge, Wilde, and Judge Drake because catching the villain, not saving victims, is his duty. Mill's utilitarianism frames Superman's consequentialism and Kant's Groundwork - intentions over outcomes, the batarang blocked by invisible glass leaving Batman's morality untouched - frames Batman's, with moral luck the battleground: deontology shrugs it off, but Superman's powers let him smooth luck over too. On the opening dilemma, Superman defuses the bomb while Batman chases the villain, so the authors judge Superman's code superior because Batman's can let hundreds die without moral failure.
- ch. 25 "14. Who Watches the Justice League" **·** David Hume, Karl Popper — Consequentialism — Judged by negative consequentialism - the view the chapter attributes to Hume and Popper, that ethics means minimizing future suffering - Superman is the more ethical hero because he plans failsafes against himself: the gold Kryptonite vault he enters after killing Mr. Mxyzptlk in 'Whatever Happened to the Man of Tomorrow?', the de-powering device he entrusts to Batman in Injustice, even his own death as harm prevention. Batman's countermeasures against the League (Tower of Babel, Justice League: Doom) fall short because he builds no failsafe for himself, hiding behind 'My failsafe is the Justice League.' The chapter reads Superman's killing of Zod in Man of Steel and Bruce's compulsive detective work through Hume's claim that reason is the slave of the passions, concluding that Clark's willingness to enable his own defeat wins the ethical contest.
- ch. 26 "15. Infinite Environmental Crisis" **·** Karl Marx — Anthropocentrism — Through a mock Lois Lane interview the chapter argues that Batman's endless Bat-vehicle upgrades and Wayne Enterprises' worldwide production repeat the 'metabolic rift' John Bellamy Foster developed from Marx: we take materials from the earth faster than we return them in a form it can process, generating an infinite chain of environmental crises. Superman looks like the winner because he runs on sunlight, yet Krypton died of its own ecological abuse, making him an 'environmental refugee' rather than a model. The real point is that 'we are all Bruce Wayne': humans cannot photosynthesize, so the choice is not Batman versus Superman but infinite consumption versus a balanced relationship with nature.
- ch. 27 "16. You Made Me First" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer, William James — Free Will vs Determinism; The Will to Believe — The chapter argues that origin stories fix character: Batman, forged by watching his parents murdered (and in Burton's film by the Joker himself - 'You made me first'), is trapped by a past that sets his trajectory, since as Schopenhauer said we can do what we will but cannot will what we will. Kal-El's origin is instead an act of hope - his parents launch him into the universe believing the wildly unlikely - which the chapter reads through William James's will-to-believe, the same faith Bruce needs to jump the chasm unroped in The Dark Knight Rises. Because the past causes the future and 'I can do whatever I want' is not free will, Bruce can never escape his tragedy, while Superman's hopeful origin lets him believe, and make us believe, that we might yet fly.
- ch. 28 "Round Four Decision—Batman! It’s Always Darkest Before the Knight" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "17. Batman’s Mask in Aristotle’s Drama" **·** Aristotle, Plato — Reading Batman through Aristotle's Poetics, the chapter argues the bat-mask is mimesis - a theatrical imitation that makes criminals imagine worse than he is, as when Batman bluffs the germophobic goon with a jar of 'crimson fever' in 'Feat of Clay,' and when his staged heart-attack death in The Dark Knight Returns lets him beat Superman with 'fists and thespianism.' Unmasked, Platonic Superman presents his true self - the Mirror of Truth cannot show Clark Kent - and wins trust, but Aristotle's pity, fear, and catharsis favor Batman, whose human limits create suspense about whether he will finally cross the line. Because the Batman is a repeatable performance - copied by the Sons of Batman, franchised in Batman Incorporated - he converts gang members and changes hearts and minds, an immortality the inimitable alien Superman can never achieve.
- ch. 30 "18. Superman the Super Dick" **·** Peter Singer, Harry Frankfurt — The Drowning Child (obligation to aid); On Bullshit — Modeled on Harry Frankfurt's analysis of 'bullshit,' the chapter defines a 'dick' as someone who willfully disregards others' needs, and argues Superman qualifies: he lets crimes proceed while he works at the Daily Planet or hunts vending-machine change, although the SuperBat storyline (Superman/Batman #53-56) shows a briefly super-powered Batman clearing every city's crime in a single night. Applying Peter Singer's famine-and-affluence argument - the penny that saves a life, the ten-cent polio vaccine - the chapter contends that saving at little or no cost to oneself is obligatory rather than supererogatory, so Superman's convenience-first choices make him a 'Super Dick.' Batman, who risks his mortal life seeking out strangers to save, does what he can and therefore escapes the charge.
- ch. 31 "19. Overpower versus Empowerment" *(no index entry yet)* — The chapter argues from game design that Superman is a poor video game hero because his built-in, all-or-nothing powers are too rigid: an overpowered character destroys the balance of challenge and skill that Csikszentmihaly calls flow, Kryptonite strips every ability at once rather than letting powers be lost and regained one by one as in Metroid, and no controller input can test the force of will - the 'heart of a hero' - that lets Superman unleash his strength. Batman's trainable skills, swappable equipment, and player-solvable investigations (with in-fiction help such as a Gordon tip-off) give designers flexible ways to empower the player, which is why the Arkham series thrives while good Superman games barely exist. A playable Clark Kent journalist mode is floated as the way designers could finally make an almighty hero worth playing.
- ch. 32 "20. What It Takes" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau — The Problem of Evil — The chapter argues Batman is the more heroic superhero because his heroism is supererogatory and replicable: like Wesley Autrey the Subway Samaritan rather than the duty-bound Captain Sullenberger, he acts beyond obligation with human abilities we could imitate, while Superman's alien powers make his help barely optional and himself impossible to emulate. It frames Batman as Bruce's answer to the problem of evil in Susan Neiman's secular version - Rachel Dawes's challenge that good people must 'do something' about Falcone's Gotham - siding with the Rousseau line that reason must make evil intelligible against Voltaire's refusal. Batman earns his 'super' by marshaling reason as well as wealth, like the cell-phone sonar he submits to Lucius Fox's veto in The Dark Knight, and his parting 'anyone can be a hero' to Gordon shows heroes begetting heroes, which Superman's otherworldliness never manages.
- ch. 33 "21. Batman Is Superman" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; The Apollonian and the Dionysian; Eternal Recurrence; Will to Power — The chapter argues that Bale's Batman, not the alien Superman, is Nietzsche's Übermensch: a mortal whose identity is forged in the unreconciled struggle between Dionysian emotion and Apollonian reason - vengeance against justice in Batman Begins - and who relives his traumas as Nietzschean eternal recurrence, from the bat-filled well to the prison leap of The Dark Knight Rises. The Joker is his dark double, whose will to power and ferry-boat experiment nearly extinguish Gotham's hope, but Batman absorbs the chaos and sacrifices his heroic name, whereas Superman in Injustice, losing Lois, falls into a vengeful Dionysian abyss he cannot master. Only a mortal who extends his limits without succumbing to the herd's 'gravity of madness' can be Nietzsche's superman, so the mortal, conflicted, imitable Batman is the true superman.
- ch. 34 "Post-Fight Analysis Superhero Truthers Speak Out!" *(front)*
- ch. 35 "22. Unmasking the World and Its Heroes" **·** Plato, Socrates — Allegory of the Cave; Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know) — Mapping both heroes onto Plato's cave, the chapter argues each must first see past shadows: Bruce abandons revenge on Joe Chill to change Gotham's whole idea of justice, its citizens climb out of apathy when the Dark Knight ferries refuse the Joker's detonators, and Clark needs Jonathan Kent ('It's not to score touchdowns') and Jor-El to learn what his powers are for. Socrates' Apology supplies the mask's rationale - the just man must lead a private life to survive, so secret identities protect loved ones from Luthor's kryptonite stabbing and FBI kidnapping - and Plato's warning that the returned cave-escapee would be killed explains why the heroes must lead by hope rather than force. The oracle story's lesson that wisdom is knowing that you know nothing grounds the close: anyone can be a hero, as Batman tells Gordon about the coat around a boy's shoulders.
- ch. 36 "23. I Fight Crime in My Sleep" **·** The Buddha, Mahatma Gandhi — The Four Noble Truths; Non-Self (anatman); Impermanence (anicca); Karma and Rebirth — Staged as a Fight Club parody in which a Buddhist psychiatrist, Dr. Quinzel, evaluates a costumed narrator sent to Arkham, the chapter argues neither hero is close to awakening: Batman's crime-fighting is craving that perpetuates dukkha, his secret identities a twice-constructed self that the Buddha's no-self teaching dissolves, and his violence fails the test that 'hostility is never appeased by hostility.' Superman fares no better - his super-intellect imposes illusory order on impermanence, his flight and X-ray vision are distractions beside the Buddha-eye and tapas, and as one of the titans of the six realms he is further from Buddhahood than a human - while the doctor defines justice as karma rather than retaliation and finds Batman one step from American Psycho's Bateman. Only conquering Mara, one's own inner deceiver, is 'the only superpower worth having,' and the closing reveal that the doctor has a plan of her own for 'Mr. Kent' caps the diagnosis.
- ch. 37 "24. The Fifty-Third Card" **·** The Drowning Child (obligation to aid); Moral Luck — Writing as the Joker playing 'the fifty-third card,' the chapter trashes both heroes at once: Superman is an attention-starved show-off who could save people invisibly but wants worship, and Batman is a billionaire who pours his fortune into toys and beatings while thousands of children die for want of the charity he could give. The real punchline is our hypocrisy: we are only as moral as our circumstances allow (born in the ghetto we would steal too), we mourn pets while eating smarter pigs and buying chocolate picked by child slaves, and in The Dark Knight's ferry scene everyone would really have pushed the button - since choosing not to help kills as surely as detonating, 'we kill every day.' The only honest hero would drop moral pretense, do what he genuinely thinks right, and remind us that under the skin we are all clowns laughing as the world burns.
- ch. 38 "References" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "Rogues Gallery" *(notes)*
- ch. 40 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 41 "290" *(front)*
- ch. 42 "291" *(front)*

## Battlestar Galactica and Philosophy - Knowledge Here Begins Out There

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Battlestar Galactica and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Popular Culture and Philosophy® Series Editor George A. Reisch" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Foreword" *(no index entry yet)* — Show writer David Weddle recounts how Ronald Moore pushed the 'Act of Contrition' script beyond Star Trek-style expository dialogue into interleaved flashback snippets dramatizing Starbuck's guilt over passing Zak Adama's flight test. He argues this stream-of-consciousness technique, letting memories, dreams and hallucinations flow out of characters' inner emotional lives (as later in 'Scar' and 'Maelstrom'), is what made the series groundbreaking, echoing Faulkner's 'the past isn't over. It isn't even past.' The foreword frames the book's philosophical interest as rooted in the show's narrative form itself.
- ch. 6 "“I Dare You to Watch This Show!”" **·** The Meaning of Life — The introduction argues, via Edward James Olmos's campus talks, that Battlestar Galactica is 'truth' television that dares viewers to think about war, insurgency, torture, abortion, genocide, race and identity. It traces the franchise from Glen Larson's 1978 original through Galactica 1980 to the Moore/Eick re-imagining, insisting the show is 'a cultural discussion' rather than a cultural artifact. Borrowing Orson Scott Card's claim that real science fiction works like true religious literature, it frames the contributors' essays as probing who we are, our place in the universe, and how we find or make meaning, and explains the book's conventions for naming the Sixes, Eights, Sharons and its own anonymous 'Final Five' chapters.
- ch. 7 "Acknowledgements" *(notes)*
- ch. 8 "MODEL ONE - Some Are Programmed to Think They Are Human" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Retelling Our Stories" **·** Alasdair MacIntyre — Personal Identity — Milsky applies the narrative theory of personal identity drawn from Paul Ricoeur and Alasdair MacIntyre (we are the story we emplot about ourselves) to the two Sharons: Boomer's discovery that she is a sleeper Cylon is a 'factual disruption' of her self-story (she shoots Adama because her human narrative cannot cohere), while Athena's love for Helo is a 'disruption of belief' that rewrites her values about humans. He extends the analysis to humans: Starbuck's repressed childhood abuse, Roslin's Pythian-prophecy conversion, and Helo's love for a Cylon, plus Six expelling Athena and Tyrol refusing to acknowledge Boomer, show identity is also socially constructed and can become self-fulfilling prophecy. The chapter concludes disruptions are not identity-altering: like the Final Five in 'Crossroads, Part 2' (Tigh's 'My name is Saul Tigh, I am an officer in the Colonial Fleet'), integration and retelling preserve the continuity of self.
- ch. 10 "Frak-tured Finality" **·** Michel Foucault, Gilles Deleuze — Personal Identity — Booth argues the 'Resistance' webisodes fracture BSG's narrative across media, so viewers must actively construct the story (which is why Jammer's execution in 'Collaborators' only makes sense if you watched them), and that this viewer-side fragmentation mirrors the characters' fractured identities. Drawing on Foucault's constructed self, Deleuze and Guattari's 'schizophrenia,' Goffman's performative roles, and Haraway's cyborg, he reads Baltar's 'Am I a Cylon?', Boomer's wet-floor amnesia in 'Water,' and Hera's human-Cylon hybridity as postmodern identity-in-process. Against Baltar, who waits for D'Anna to assign him an identity, Tigh actively constructs his ('that's the man I want to be'), and the chapter ends suggesting viewers too become human-machine hybrids by constructing themselves from the show's fragments.
- ch. 11 "The Avatar Unparalleled Opportunities" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Sigmund Freud — Bad Faith; The Unconscious; Existence Precedes Essence — Cuddy argues the gamer's bond with the avatar is an extension of the ego: Freud's id/ego/superego model explains why playing a Cylon Raider or flying the 'Bastille Day' mission lets gamers reconcile unconscious desires with the superego's demands in ways reality forbids. But when we lose we disconnect from the avatar and blame the game or 'cheating' opponents, which Sartre diagnoses as bad faith, a denial of freedom, contrasted with Adama's 'a man takes responsibility for his actions' in '33' and Baltar's vacillation over the vice-presidency. Through facticity (the unforgiving no-save 2003 game) and existence preceding essence, the chapter concludes that videogames offer unparalleled opportunities to practice Sartrean responsibility and achieve Freudian reconciliation through the right avatar.
- ch. 12 "Becoming One" **·** Aristotle, Plato — The Golden Mean; Platonic Love (the ladder of love) — The chapter reads Baltar's head-Six and Caprica Six's head-Baltar through the gothic Double and Doppelganger tradition and Dissociative Identity Disorder: Number Six is Baltar's repressed guilt over betraying humanity ('I'm your subconscious frakking with your mind'), letting him escape responsibility for signing execution orders in 'Precipice,' while Gina Six serves as a tangible Double. Plato's Symposium myth of the split self frames Baltar and Six as two halves of one whole, since he cannot function without some version of Six present, physical or hallucinated. The resolution is Aristotle's golden mean from Book Two of the Nicomachean Ethics: like integration therapy for DID, virtue is a balance between extremes rather than eliminating one side, so Baltar and Six's child Hera may embody the balanced union of human and Cylon.
- ch. 13 "No Reason to Get Excited" **·** René Descartes, David Hume, G.E. Moore — Philosophical Skepticism; Methodological Doubt (evil demon); The Cogito — The chapter turns the Final Five reveal (Tigh, Tyrol, Anders, Foster) and Boomer's sleeper activation into a skeptical problem about self-knowledge: Descartes's dreaming doubt (Roslin's Opera House dream) and the cogito show we can know that we exist but not what we are, and Baltar's rigged 'bright green' Cylon test destroys testimonial assurance. G.E. Moore's common-sense realism fails to engage the skeptic's special standards, and contextualism and Dretske's relevant-alternatives theory cannot help because in a world of sleeper Cylons the possibility 'I might be one' is always relevant. The answer is Hume's: like Tigh declaring 'I am an officer in the Colonial Fleet' and Anders repeating 'I am Samuel T. Anders' before his first combat mission, we banish skeptical paralysis by carrying on with life, dining and playing backgammon.
- ch. 14 "MODEL TWO - They Look Like Us Now" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "This Has All Happened Before and It Will All Happen Again" **·** Sigmund Freud — Rennes reads the miniseries' opening kiss of death through Verges's 'colonial family romance' (built on Freud's family romance): humans created Cylon 'children' to serve them while fixing categories of racial difference (toaster, skin job, 'it') to keep them in their place. Using Homi Bhabha's ambivalence and mimicry ('almost the same, but not quite') and the history of miscegenation panic and blood taxonomies, she traces how human-Cylon intimacy (Gina's rape aboard Pegasus, Helo and Athena, Starbuck at the breeding Farm) makes difference collapse into sameness. Hera, the mixed-blood child whose fetal blood cures Roslin's cancer, is Bhabha's 'resemblance and menace': acknowledging her would force humans to admit they created Cylon slaves, so Roslin socially reconstructs Hera's difference and the romance ends happily never after.
- ch. 16 "Who They Are or Who They Want to Be" **·** Judith Butler, René Descartes — Gender Performativity; Personal Identity — Harwood-Smith argues, against Descartes's pre-given 'I think therefore I am,' that Judith Butler's performativity explains the Cylons: Six is female because she performs femininity, no copy performs identically, and Cylon society fractures into hybrid identities with an 'embarrassed etc.' Sexual contact with humans is the catalyst that makes performance constitute reality, producing Athena's defection, Caprica Six's celebrity status, and D'Anna's religious fervor, while Cylons who never slept with humans stay apart. The same dynamic destabilizes humans who sleep with Cylons: Baltar's 'I must be a Cylon because I slept with one,' Tyrol's fear, and Helo's 'toaster lover' stigma, with Tigh's chosen stability ('that's the man I want to be') as the counterexample of performing who you want to be.
- ch. 17 "The Secret Plot of ‘Baby Boomers’" **·** Judith Butler — Heteronormativity — Chess argues Battlestar Galactica is 'a narrative about queer reproduction': via Haraway's Cyborg Manifesto and Butler's account of heteronormativity, the Cylons are 'technoqueer,' embodying how fears of technology overlap with fears of non-heterosexual reproduction. Their download-based 'photocopying' reproduction and their obsessive pursuit of legitimate biological babies (the Farm's breeding facility, Athena's 'unnatural' pregnancy that Roslin calls a 'thing') express the anxiety that new reproductive technologies make heterosexuality obsolete, which the chapter pairs scene-for-scene with The L Word's Bette and Tina. Helo's 'I'm in love with a woman I know isn't a woman' and the Pegasus crew's 'you cannot rape a machine' debate show homophobia folding into technophobia, with Hera as the illegitimate queer child both societies fear.
- ch. 18 "Stable Bodies" *(no index entry yet)* — Heinricy argues the one clear human-Cylon difference is the body, analyzed through Mary Douglas's account of bodies as culturally invented boundaries: Cylons are feared as 'contamination' of the fleet (Boomer's body, segregated like Jim Crow bodies), and Gina Six's torture and rape mark her body as out-of-place other even as her human-looking blood and wounds undermine the boundary. She contrasts messy, changing human bodies (Apollo's weight fluctuation, aging, scars as records of experience) with stable, disposable Cylon bodies that never mark learning, arguing human change yields potential and hope while Cylon bodily stability may cost mental stability (Tigh killing Ellen, Boomer attacking Hera). Cyborg theory then collapses the boundary (we are all cyborgs via vaccines and prosthetics, Cylons merely more integrated, the Hybrid a warning of over-integration), leaving Hera and Nicky as the 'ultimate contamination' that blends the two.
- ch. 19 "Into the Future" **·** John Locke, Peter van Inwagen — Personal Identity (memory theory); Immortality and the Soul; Personal Identity — Hanley dismantles the four alleged human-Cylon differences: humans are also 'made' (by God), contain non-organic parts, come as identical twins, and expect to survive death, so religious believers already believe they are Cylons. He defends reproductive cloning as reproduction not replication (a clone is your child, not your copy), models Cylon resurrection as scanning-death-transmission-replication, and shows van Inwagen's replica-swap account of bodily resurrection faces the same worries about blank bodies and which body returns. Following Locke's consciousness criterion, that a soul independent of body and memory explains none of our identity judgments, he rejects karma-based reincarnation without memory, proposes a fission-style plot-fix for Athena's impossible memories of Tyrol, and ends with Cylons settling ancient Earth: 'I am a Cylon, and so are you.'
- ch. 20 "MODEL THREE - We Became What We Beheld" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "Hedging Our Bets" **·** Peter Singer, Tom Regan, Blaise Pascal, Alan Turing, René Descartes — Speciesism; Animal Rights (subjects-of-a-life); Pascal's Wager; The Turing Test — Kind asks whether the Fleet's treatment of Cylons (Roslin airlocking Leoben, Fisk's 'you can't rape a machine,' Cally's killing of Boomer charged as mere discharging of a firearm) presupposes that Cylons lack the moral status Mary Anne Warren defines against the stone-versus-child contrast. Against species membership as a criterion she deploys Singer's anti-speciesism (sentience grounds equal consideration: Gina's torture, the Raider Scar) and Regan's subject-of-a-life, and against the 'no souls' argument she cites Descartes's animal automata and Turing's reply that an omnipotent God could give machines souls, as Leoben himself suggests to Adama. Since no criterion rules Cylons out with certainty, the chapter closes with a Pascal's Wager structure: under uncertainty, treat plausible moral-status-holders as having it, because denying it costs us our moral compass, answering Adama's 'why are we worth saving?'.
- ch. 22 "Learning from Our Mistakes" **·** Aristotle, Plato — Democracy; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Tamplin uses Baltar's landslide win on the New Caprica issue and Roslin's attempted election theft to argue democracy's guarantee of bad decisions is its saving feature: Plato and Aristotle ranked democracy among bad regimes because the many rule for private advantage rather than the common good, and Baltar's campaign of 'hope rather than truth' exploits exactly that weakness. A thought experiment about an oracle computer that always selects the best option shows that following it would abolish self-determination, the freedom to 'live as one wants' that democracy values above results. Only by choosing and frakking up (settling New Caprica, suffering the occupation, Apollo's shame speech at Baltar's trial) can citizens confront their role, learn from mistakes, and move toward Aristotle's polity aiming at virtue, making self-determination the mediating value between freedom and virtue.
- ch. 23 "History Will Judge" **·** Objectification — Livingston reads Razor through George Lakoff's nation-as-family metaphor: Cain's Pegasus is the Strict Father family (the world is dangerous, Belzen shot for disobedience, Gina tortured for intelligence, Kendra's humiliating 'birth slap'), while Adama's Galactica is the Nurturant Parent (empathy, questioning authority, 'it's not enough to survive; one has to be worthy of surviving'). She traces how military kill-conditioning, desensitization plus objectifying 'toaster' and 'it' language, turns Kendra into 'a razor' and a PTSD case, while Apollo and Kendra hybridize the two family models on Pegasus. Adama's closing 'history will have to make its judgments' frames the chapter's claim that understanding Cain's context matters more than villainizing, and that humanity's worthiness depends on the balance struck between our idealized parents.
- ch. 24 "It’s the Disappointing Stories that Sound True to Life" **·** Karl Marx — Class Struggle; Social Darwinism — Shipman argues the series first builds viewer sympathy for Cylons as rebel slaves (their monotheism and their New Caprica role as US-style occupiers make them 'more like the viewers than the humans'), then undercuts it: the Numbers rebuilt Cylon society as oppressors, breeding the voices out of Centurions, dismissing the Hybrids as mad, and breeding the sentient Raiders (Starbuck's discovery in 'Scar') into functional animals. The opening narration's claim that Cylons 'evolved' is disingenuous, since the model hierarchy was deliberately engineered, 'not evolution, but eugenics,' with Marx's 'from each according to his ability' division of labor as thin cover for literal class construction. The former slaves became slave-owners, fulfilling 'all of this has happened before': heroes of one story become villains of the next, inviting the muted models to rebel against the Numbers in turn.
- ch. 25 "Seeing Ourselves Reflected Back" **·** Immanuel Kant, Peter Singer — Humanity as an End in Itself; Speciesism — Gray asks whether the Cylons deserve moral status, testing them against Kant's formula of humanity as an end in itself: the resistance's suicide bombings on New Caprica and Adama's plan to weaponize virus-infected Cylons against the resurrection ship in 'A Measure of Salvation' use beings as mere means, while Athena's defection and D'Anna's dissent (and her line's boxing) display the rational autonomy Kant requires of persons. Singer's sentience principle (Gina's catatonia after torture, Leoben's suffering under interrogation) and Mary Anne Warren's multi-criterial personhood (Boomer's 'I have a conscience' in 'Downloaded') independently support Cylon personhood. The chapter concludes that the show's double standards, accepting Athena while drawing guns on Caprica Six, mirror our own confusion as cloning and artificial intelligence advance.
- ch. 26 "MODEL FOUR - Battlestar Iraqtica" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "Fire Without Burn" **·** The Ethics of Political Violence — Pinedo argues the re-imagined series restages 'blowback,' the CIA-coined term for retaliation against covert intervention that public discourse avoids, in science-fiction guise: Adama's 'you cannot play God, then wash your hands of the things you've created' speech, and the episode 'Hero,' where Adama confesses his black-ops crossing of the Armistice Line ('I started it') before Roslin reframes his guilt into 'a thousand things' the Colonials did over forty years. The chapter shows how the sci-fi 'smokescreen' (Moore and Eick's own word) lets the show do what news coverage cannot: sympathetically depict suicide bombing on New Caprica and torture by the 'good guys' (Starbuck's waterboarding of Leoben, Cain's ordered rape of Gina), dismantling the Manichean myth of American innocence after 9/11 while avoiding the network wrath that naming blowback would bring.
- ch. 28 "The Future" **·** Frantz Fanon, Jean-Paul Sartre, Karl Marx, Herbert Marcuse — The Ethics of Political Violence — Dinello argues the New Caprica occupation arc deliberately dramatizes Fanon's The Wretched of the Earth applied to Iraq: Cylon colonization (Cavil as Cheney-Rumsfeld bringing 'the love of god,' Baltar as puppet leader, Colonial One as Green Zone, hooded detainees) enacts Fanon's colonial violence and psychological colonization of the mind, seen in Leoben's manipulation of Starbuck and Jammer's recruitment into the New Caprica Police. The insurgency enacts Fanon's violence-as-cleansing-force: Tigh's 'blood for blood,' Duck's suicide bombing of the NCP graduation, and Tyrol's Mario Savio-inspired 'throw your body on the gears' speech, which the chapter links to Marx's seizure of the means of production and Marcuse on technology's repressiveness, while Sartre's preface licenses anticolonial violence against liberal moralizing. It concludes that occupation breeds the terrorists it fights, insurgents win by not losing, and the show is subversive because it makes viewers sympathize with 'our real-world enemies.'
- ch. 29 "Living with Our Choices" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Radical Freedom and Responsibility; Existence Precedes Essence — Peters reads 'The Resistance' webisodes through Sartre's occupied-France dilemma from Existentialism Is a Humanism (resist, collaborate, or do nothing, modeled on his student's choice between joining the Free French and caring for his mother): Tyrol joins the resistance for his family's future, Jammer joins the New Caprica Police to save lives and pays with execution by his former friends in 'Collaborators,' and Duck's choice to do nothing ends with wife Nora's death in the Temple massacre and his revenge suicide bombing. Since 'man is nothing other than what he makes of himself,' the chapter argues morality lies in the freedom to choose and in living with the consequences: Duck's refusal to accept his choice, not the choice itself, is the failure, and the Final Five revelation cannot retroactively alter choices made in good faith by Tigh, Tyrol, Anders and Foster. Which side holds the ethical high ground may simply be whichever side won and gets to write history.
- ch. 30 "Walking the Tightrope" **·** Consequentialism; Deontology; The Social Contract; Democracy — Melancon weighs the Fleet leadership's secrets and lies: Adama's false claim to know the way to Earth, concealing that Cylons look human, Roslin faking Hera's death and hiding the baby, and Zarek's secret collaboration courts on New Caprica. He argues that keeping secrets from the Cylons can be justified by the state's duty to protect, but the leadership repeatedly deceives the governed themselves, discarding the transparency democratic legitimacy requires. Deontological duty to the governed keeps losing to consequentialist reasoning, and the chapter asks what happens to Colonial democracy's 'soul' when trust between government and governed erodes.
- ch. 31 "Damned If You Do, and Damned If You Don’t" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli, Michel Foucault — The Ends Justify the Means; Power/Knowledge; Faith and Reason — McHenry reads the re-imagined series as a holy-war allegory in which both sides weaponize religion, then asks whether that manipulation is ever moral. Adama's lie about Earth is Machiavellian statecraft (with Foucault's point that privileged knowledge holds power), yet defended as serving the greater good, while Roslin's Pythia-based campaign contrasts with Bush's faith-based fear politics. The Cylons emerge as evangelical fundamentalists whose conversion of Baltar through inner Number Six runs on fear, exactly what Madison's case for religious liberty forbids. The chapter concludes the show's own gods have sided with the Colonials.
- ch. 32 "Misunderestimated" **·** Democracy — Muir argues the 1978 original was serious political allegory rather than Moore's 'popcorn': Adar's armistice with the Cylons stands in for Carter-era detente and the SALT treaties, and the sneak attack dramatizes hard-right fears of unilateral disarmament. Adama's 'preparedness' and Sire Uri's disarmament proposal map onto the 'Peace through Strength' doctrine that carried Reagan to power, and 'Experiment in Terra' repeats the lesson as Apollo tells Terra's politicians the opposite of war is slavery, not peace. The chapter notes Soviet Izvestia read it the same way, and that the original thereby fulfilled Moore's own criterion of relevant science fiction from the opposite end of the political spectrum, including Apollo's wartime argument that dissent weakens Colonial resolve.
- ch. 33 "MODEL FIVE - Finding Purpose in the Void" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "Speed and Destination" **·** Jacques Derrida, Martin Heidegger — Being-Toward-Death — Martinon reads '33' as a phenomenology of temporality under exile: with sunrise, history, and open future gone, the fleet's existence becomes eschatological 'bare life' (following Agamben), sandwiched between the death of the Colonies and death at Cylon hands every thirty-three minutes. The FTL jump figures Derrida's messianic 'to-come' - an undecidable advent that may be salvation or destruction - while the Vipers' obsession with speed narrows life to the next possibility, in a Heideggerian shift from cultural to ontological questioning of death. The episode stays undecidable between Number Six's fatalism ('God is watching out for you, Gaius') and Baltar's contingency, with a birth aboard the fleet - Roslin's smile - as the one open future.
- ch. 35 "Finding Our Way" **·** John Dewey — Pragmatism; Experience and Education; The Pragmatic Method — Milburn uses Dewey's account of habit, impulse, and inquiry to explain how the humans navigate: Adama's blocked plans (the decommissioning) are reoriented into a new 'end-in-view' (Earth), and the fleet continuously reformulates its route from scriptures, star charts, pulsars, and the Tomb of Athena constellations as interchangeable signs. The Cylons by contrast cannot revise - Boomer's peace initiative gets her model boxed, and even Cavil's admissions of error lead back to the same path - because they locate their plan in God rather than in constructed, revisable inquiry, needing Baltar even to interpret the signs to Earth. On Deweyan grounds the chapter concludes the humans are never lost while the Cylons are.
- ch. 36 "Free to Be You and Me" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer — Free Will vs Determinism; Compatibilism; The World as Will — Barker reconciles Cylon programming with freedom through compatibilism and Schopenhauer's 'intelligible character': Boomer's sabotage and shooting of Adama and her betrayal of the Cylons to save Tyrol both flow from one innate, conflicted character, just as Baltar's cowardly 'conversion' to the Cylon god expresses his opportunism. Tigh's decision to keep serving after learning he is a Cylon ('My name is Saul Tigh; I am an officer in the colonial fleet') shows character unchanged by revelation. Starbuck's 'special destiny' in 'Maelstrom' is reread not as fate but as her own character - mother-induced self-doubt at war with pilot confidence - which she finally accepts in the vortex; freedom is not freedom from programming but the necessity of manifesting who one already is.
- ch. 37 "Taking in the View from the Paranoid Position" **·** Sigmund Freud — The Death Drive (Thanatos) — Christian reads the fleet through Freud's death instinct and Melanie Klein's paranoid position: Starbuck's self-destruction, Tigh and Ellen's sadomasochism, and Baltar's relationship with Six fuse Eros and Thanatos, and 'Maelstrom' returns Starbuck to the abusive mother whose rejection of her love taught her that love itself is poisonous. Humans project all aggression and badness onto the Cylons they created - 'toaster' talk, Cain's torture of Gina - to keep a split between good self and evil other. The price is permanent vigilance in the watchtower: since anyone might be a Cylon (the Chief's fear, Baltar's test), the crew can never be sure the projected badness is not their own, which is why the machines must keep 'coming back.'
- ch. 38 "Embracing Death" **·** Socrates, Plato, Albert Camus — The Meaning of Life; Immortality and the Soul — Vessey asks whether the humanoid Cylons' resurrection makes meaningful life impossible. Humans can aim at a 'successful death' - Socrates drinking the hemlock, the Greek warrior accepting his moira - in which dying completes a life understood as a whole oriented to something larger; a Cylon's death is a mere reset (download), and the exceptions available, dying out of range of a resurrection ship or D'Anna's boxing, occur only through failure or shame and so cannot contribute to meaning. Plato's Symposium link of children to immortality is inverted, since Cylons can only have mortal human-Cylon babies; what separates the Cylons from Sisyphus is not purpose but the option to opt out, which mortality forces on humans and resurrection denies the Cylons.
- ch. 39 "MODEL SIX - Near the End of Our Journey" *(front)*
- ch. 40 "To the Third Power . ." **·** Fate and Foreknowledge; Immortality and the Soul — Wolfe argues the original series is 'deeply Mormon' in ontology though not in epistemology: Kobol anagrams Kolob, the Quorum of Twelve and 'sealing' marriages mirror LDS structure, and the Beings of Light's 'as you are now, we once were; as we are now, you may become' restates Lorenzo Snow's deification couplet, while Count Iblis, like the Mormon Satan, can only rule those who freely yield - agency is supreme. Because none of this is required to follow the show, later licensees could rebuild the metaphysics: the Maximum Press comics incoherently (free will violated when inconvenient), Richard Hatch's novels with a new pure-blood-of-Kobol cosmology, and Ronald Moore with a circular 'all this has happened before' replacing eternal progression.
- ch. 41 "So Say We All" **·** Faith and Reason — Berger deploys Bakhtin's dialogism to answer the 'GINO' charge: the re-imagined series is not a betrayal of Larson's show but a heteroglossic utterance in an ongoing conversation that already included the Book of Mormon, von Daniken's Chariots of the Gods, Greco-Roman myth, and later fanfic and blogs, with each version sourcing its sources' sources. The 2003 series is read as polyphonic about belief: skeptic Adama, devout Number Six, the messianic convert Baltar, and Dawkins/Hitchens-style atheist critiques of fundamentalism are all given equal voice. The show's value lies in inviting debate across the whole continuum of belief rather than preaching a position.
- ch. 42 "The Cosmic Divide" **·** Protagoras — Moral Relativism — The chapter locates the real fan schism in worldview: the original series is Manichaean moral absolutism - Count Iblis and the Ships of Light wage a cosmic Light/Darkness war in which Evil is one force (Iblis sharing the Imperious Leader's voice) and humanity is the battleground - while Moore's re-imagining is Protagorean relativism ('man is the measure'), where right depends entirely on context. Evidence: Roslin abandons the FTL-less ships as 'a matter of numbers' yet burns forty-three percent of fuel reserves to find Starbuck; Leoben is airlocked after Starbuck's interrogation while Thorne is killed for torturing Athena; producer Eick even defends Caprica Six's killing of the baby as possibly a mercy. The two shows thus embody absolutist nostalgia for moral clarity versus post-9/11 moral ambiguity.
- ch. 43 "With or Without Starbuck" **·** Feminism; The Male Gaze; Objectification — Kirkland argues BSG imagines gender-free institutions (unisex heads and quarters, Starbuck never having to justify fighting) while installing a default masculinity under Adama, so that feminine-coded women die: Ellen the femme fatale is poisoned, pilots Kat and Kendra earn redemption only through death, and Cain's female command is written as militarist nightmare aligned with Gina's sexual abuse. Starbuck confounds the sexualizing male gaze and reverses rescue narratives, and 'The Farm' plus her captivity with Leoben on New Caprica literalize feminist critiques of patriarchal medicine and compulsory heterosexuality. Yet she is masculinized, pathologized via her abusive mother, brought to submit to Leoben as spiritual guide, and killed in 'Maelstrom' - containing the transgressive heroine within the show's patriarchy.
- ch. 44 "Human, Machine, or Something In-between" **·** Feminism — Rolufs maps Genesis' Eve onto Boomer (who shoots Adama without understanding her act, as Eve could not know betrayal before eating the fruit) and onto Caprica Six (whose fall required Baltar's Adam-like blame-shifting), and the Zohar's Lilith - seductress, child-killer, seeker of equality - onto Six's red dresses and neck-snapping and Sharon's sexual dominance of Helo. Both Cylon women are compromised figures who love the humans they betrayed, and their ambiguity between human and machine mirrors the ambiguity between woman and demon in the myths. Through them the chapter argues pop culture redeems Eve and Lilith from misogynistic readings: Six's killing of the baby becomes possibly merciful, and the first women get the chance at redemption the myths denied them.
- ch. 45 "32 - Dreamers in the Night [ ]" **·** Theodor Adorno, Walter Benjamin — The Culture Industry; The Aura of the Artwork — This fan-fiction interlude dramatizes the fandom debate aboard Galactica: Roslin voices the Frankfurt School view (a 'Scorpion School' of Adorno and Benjamin - mass culture as mindless absorption, reproduction destroying the artwork's aura), while aide Billy cites Henry Jenkins and Textual Poachers to defend fanzines as participatory culture repairing corporate ownership of myth, and a fan meeting wins the President over as community-building. Baltar and inner Number Six extend the argument: Six demands 'the singular truth of the show' against fan rewriting, and Baltar answers that the Cylons are themselves a fanfiction of the human brain - twelve unoriginal faces. Starbuck is revealed as the anonymous fanfic writer.
- ch. 46 "Case in Point" *(no index entry yet)* — The chapter introduces Wayne Booth's 'coduction' - ethical criticism of fiction validated by comparing one's reasoned judgments with other qualified readers in ongoing, open-minded conversation - and argues Battlestar is a highly ethical show not because its characters behave well but because it invites coduction: the abortion-under-extinction, torture, and New Caprica suicide-bombing plots (Baltar cornering Roslin, who cannot justify the bombings) open discussion instead of preaching, the mark of art rather than propaganda. Rules for genuine coduction follow - treat Iraq as one applicability among several occupations, stay informed, remain open to changing your mind - with Ron Moore's blog exchange on the absence of gay characters as the model case, and the book itself offered as one contribution to the continuing conversation.
- ch. 47 "Dossier" *(front)*
- ch. 48 "Callsigns" *(notes)*
- ch. 49 "Decodings" *(notes)*
- ch. 50 "Lexicon" *(notes)*
- ch. 51 "Intel" *(notes)*
- ch. 52 "Other Campaigns" *(notes)*
- ch. 53 "War Journal" *(notes)*
- ch. 54 "The Universe is a vast and complex system coincidental, serendipitous events" *(notes)*
- ch. 55 "Battlestar Galactica and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 56 "Battlestar Galactica and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 57 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Battlestar Galactica and Philosophy - Mission Accomplished or Mission Frakked Up_

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Battlestar Galactica and Philosophy" *(front)*
