- ch. 3 "Popular Culture and Philosophy® Series Editor George A. Reisch" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Foreword" **·** Stream of Consciousness — Show writer David Weddle recounts how Ron Moore pushed him and Brad Thompson to abandon Star Trek-style expository dialogue and tell 'Act of Contrition' through flashbacks interleaved with the present, dramatizing Starbuck's guilt over passing Zak Adama on his flight test. He argues this unlocked a stream-of-consciousness mode — dreams, hallucinations, and memories flowing out of characters' inner emotional lives — that the series extended in episodes like 'Scar,' 'Maelstrom,' and 'Unfinished Business,' echoing Faulkner's 'the past isn't even past.' The foreword is a creator's testimony that BSG's scrambled-chronology storytelling is what made the show worthy of a book of philosophical readings.
- ch. 6 "“I Dare You to Watch This Show!”" **·** The Meaning of Life — Editor Josef Steiff's introduction traces Galactica from Glen Larson's 1978 series and Galactica 1980 through the Moore/Eick re-imagining, and frames the show through Edward James Olmos's campus talks, where he 'dares' audiences to watch a series he calls truth about identity, race, culture, and purpose. It endorses Orson Scott Card's claim that true science fiction, like religious literature, asks who we are, what our place in the universe is, and how we find or make meaning. It closes by explaining the book's Cylon naming conventions (Sixes, Eights, Boomers, Athenas) and its gimmick of five unattributed 'Final Five' chapters.
- ch. 7 "Acknowledgements" *(notes)*
- ch. 8 "MODEL ONE - Some Are Programmed to Think They Are Human" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Retelling Our Stories" **·** Alasdair MacIntyre — Personal Identity — Milsky uses Ricoeur and MacIntyre's narrative theory of personal identity to read the two Sharons: Boomer's fabricated human backstory suffers a 'factual disruption' when she shoots Adama, while Athena's 'disruption of belief' comes from falling in love with Helo. He argues such disruptions — also suffered by humans like Starbuck (recovered memories of abuse in 'Maelstrom'), Roslin (coming to believe she is Pythia's dying leader), and Helo — need not destroy identity, since narratives can be retold, as the Final Four do in 'Crossroads, Part 2' with Tigh's 'My name is Saul Tigh' speech. Third-party narratives (Six disowning Athena, the crew refusing to see Boomer as Boomer) show identity is partly socially constructed and can become self-fulfilling prophecy, with Boomer's blackouts resembling dissociative identity disorder.
- ch. 10 "Frak-tured Finality" **·** Michel Foucault, Gilles Deleuze — Personal Identity; Power/Knowledge — Booth argues that the 'Resistance' webisodes fracture BSG's narrative across television and Internet, forcing viewers to actively construct the story — Jammer's execution in 'Collaborators' and Duck's suicide bombing only make full sense if you pieced the webisodes together — and that this viewer-side fragmentation mirrors the characters' fractured identities. Drawing on Foucault's constructed self, Deleuze and Guattari's 'schizophrenia,' Goffman's performed roles, and Haraway's cyborg, he reads Baltar's 'Am I a Cylon?', Boomer's amnesia in 'Water,' Hera's human-Cylon hybridity, and Tigh's choice in 'Crossroads' to actively construct who he will be. The payoff: there is no central location for the self, the narrative, or Cylonhood — each becomes whole only through active construction.
- ch. 11 "The Avatar Unparalleled Opportunities" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Existence Precedes Essence; Bad Faith; Radical Freedom and Responsibility — Cuddy asks how gamers relate to BSG videogame avatars — as an extension of the ego or a disposable other — using Freud's id/ego/superego to cast the avatar as a socially safe venue for reconciling forbidden desires (piloting a Cylon Raider) with the superego's demands. Against Rehak's claim that gamers simply drop the avatar when losing, he enlists Sartre: blaming the game or 'cheating' opponents is bad faith, a denial of the freedom to create one's own essence, with Lee's '33' debate about responsibility for the Olympic Carrier and Baltar's vacillation as in-show parallels. Sartre's facticity bounds the freedom — hard missions are fixed facts to deal with — so the gamer, like Adama's fleet, must take responsibility within limits.
- ch. 12 "Becoming One" **·** Aristotle, Plato — The Golden Mean; Platonic Love (the ladder of love) — The chapter reads Baltar's Head-Six (and Caprica Six's Head-Baltar) through the gothic Double and Doppelgänger tradition, re-imagined via dissociative identity disorder: Baltar splits off his guilt over the genocide into a female doppelgänger who lets him evade responsibility, as in 'Precipice' where he finds the execution order signed without any memory of signing it. It then links this doubling to Plato's Symposium myth of lovers as split halves seeking their other half, since Baltar cannot function without some version of Six. Its resolution is Aristotle's golden mean: virtue is not choosing one self over another but balancing extremes, for individuals and for humanity and its Double, the Cylons — with Hera as the embodied balance.
- ch. 13 "No Reason to Get Excited" **·** René Descartes, David Hume, G.E. Moore — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); The Cogito; Philosophical Skepticism — After 'Crossroads, Part 2' revealed Anders, Foster, Tigh, and Tyrol as Cylons, the chapter argues we can know that we exist (Descartes' cogito) without knowing what we are, since even Boomer's Cylon-detection result — Baltar's lie about the green light — could itself be a dream, returning us to Descartes' whirlpool. Moore's common-sense realism fails against the skeptic's special standards, and contextualism / relevant alternatives (Dretske) lets Starbuck dismiss dreaming-hypotheses about a Viper but cannot dismiss the possibility that you are a sleeper Cylon in a world where anyone might be one. Following Hume, who escaped skeptical paralysis by dining and playing backgammon, Tigh's 'report to your stations' shows the only live option: carry on being the person you want to be.
- ch. 14 "MODEL TWO - They Look Like Us Now" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "This Has All Happened Before and It Will All Happen Again" **·** Objectification — Rennes reads the miniseries opening — Six's kiss of death with the Armistice officer — through postcolonial theory: Vergès's 'colonial family romance,' in which the human metropole adopts Cylons as perpetually subordinate children, and Bhabha's ambivalence and mimicry ('almost the same, but not quite'). The humans police difference first by appearance (toaster, skin-job slurs; the Centurion reflected in Helo's toaster) and then by blood, but métissage — Hera, whom Roslin tries to abort and then hides — exposes the categories as social constructions, since as D'Anna says, 'This stuff all looks the same.' Hera's menace is that acknowledging her means acknowledging human complicity in creating Cylon slaves, collapsing the colonial romance ('happily never after').
- ch. 16 "Who They Are or Who They Want to Be" **·** Judith Butler, René Descartes — Gender Performativity; The Cogito — Harwood-Smith argues via Judith Butler's performativity that Cylons have no pre-cultural 'I': Six is female because she performs femininity, and performing humanity increasingly makes it real — sex being the catalyst that destabilized Athena, who switches sides, while Boomer never takes responsibility for shooting Adama and so remains a slave to her programming. The performance runs backward too: humans who sleep with Cylons (Baltar, Tyrol, Helo treated as a 'toaster lover') absorb self-doubt about their own humanity, against the Cartesian inner 'I' that Baltar, who remade himself from farm-boy Aerelon, clings to. Butler's thesis is vindicated: if preprogrammed machines can be changed by the roles they play, anybody could.
- ch. 17 "The Secret Plot of ‘Baby Boomers’" **·** Judith Butler — Heteronormativity; Objectification — Chess proposes that BSG is 'a narrative about queer reproduction,' combining Haraway's cyborg with Butler's heteronormativity to coin 'technoqueer': Cylons embody fears that technology makes heterosexual reproduction unnecessary. Helo's 'I'm in love with a woman I know isn't a woman,' the 'you can't rape a machine' debate after the Pegasus assault, and download-resurrection as asexual reproduction all mark Cylons as queer others — yet the Cylons' obsession with conceiving Hera, paralleled with The L Word's Bette and Tina, shows them trapped between rejecting and emulating heteronormativity, farming human women at the Cylon 'Farm' while craving legitimate babies. Hera's birth terrifies Roslin because it legitimizes the technoqueer and with it human reproductive obsolescence.
- ch. 18 "Stable Bodies" **·** Embodiment (the lived body) — Heinricy argues the human/Cylon line is drawn on the body: following Mary Douglas, bodies function as cultural boundaries, so Cylons figure as contamination to be hunted (Boomer in the fleet) and marked as other by violence (Gina's torture), though Athena's loyal service and Tigh's exposure show the fear is socially constructed rather than natural. Cylon insides are known only through technology (Caprica Six's glowing spine, Athena's blood interface in 'Flight of the Phoenix'), and their bodies' stability — no scars, aging, or change — is paid for in unstable minds (Tigh killing Ellen, Boomer strangling Hera), whereas messy human bodies carry potential, hope, and change, as Apollo's weight fluctuation shows. Since we are all already cyborgs (vaccines, pills, GMOs), the Numbers are a further human-machine integration, and Hera and Nicky's blending is the ultimate 'contamination' and a new hope.
- ch. 19 "Into the Future" **·** John Locke, Peter van Inwagen — Personal Identity (memory theory); Teleporter Paradox (personal fission); Immortality and the Soul; Karma and Rebirth — Hanley dismantles the four claimed human/Cylon differences: artificial reproduction is in principle possible for us; prosthetics already blur what we are made of; model copies differ like identical twins (Boomer is not Athena); and resurrection has human analogues — he proposes an SDTR (scanning, death, transmission, replication) model and uses van Inwagen's replica-switch account of bodily resurrection while rejecting soul-based reincarnation on Lockean grounds, since consciousness rather than soul is the criterion of identity. He defends reproductive cloning as reproduction, not replication (a clone is your child, not your copy), and reads Athena's memories of Tyrol as evidence she is a replicated Boomer. The upshot: being a Cylon is nothing to fear — 'I am a Cylon, and so are you.'
- ch. 20 "MODEL THREE - We Became What We Beheld" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "Hedging Our Bets" **·** Peter Singer, Tom Regan, Blaise Pascal, Alan Turing, René Descartes — Speciesism; Animal Rights (subjects-of-a-life); Pascal's Wager — Kind asks whether humanoid Cylons have moral status, using Warren's stone-versus-child contrast to show that the Fleet's blender-and-toaster attitude (Roslin airlocking Leoben, Fisk's 'you can't rape a machine') rests on the unargued assumption that Cylons are mere machines. Singer's anti-speciesism grounds status in sentience (Gina's suffering, even Scar the Raider), while Regan's subject-of-a-life criteria are met by rational, self-aware Cylons; Helo's 'no human could do the things they've done' argument fails because humans commit equal evils, as the legal limits on punishing criminals show. Since we cannot rule Cylon status out, she closes with Pascal's Wager: treat them as morally considerable, because the asymmetry of error costs us our moral compass.
- ch. 22 "Learning from Our Mistakes" **·** Aristotle, Plato — Democracy; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Tamplin defends democracy through the Baltar election: Roslin's attempted theft failed, the voters' bad choice produced the New Caprica occupation, and this is exactly what Plato and Aristotle predicted of democracy as the deviant rule of the many, contrasted with Aristotle's polity aimed at the common advantage and a virtue 'every city' must care for. A thought experiment — an infallible computer advising citizens on every choice — shows that following it would abolish democracy, whose value lies not in results but in Aristotle's freedom, 'living as one wants.' Only by being allowed to frak things up can citizens learn from their mistakes, the road Apollo points to in his 'Crossroads' trial speech about collective shame, which is why Baltar's acquittal is a good sign.
- ch. 23 "History Will Judge" **·** The Ethics of Care; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Livingston reads the Razor movie through George Lakoff's Strict Father and Nurturant Parent models: Cain's Pegasus assumes a dangerous world and enforces obedience (shooting Belzen for refusing orders, the torture of Gina), while Adama's Galactica assumes empathy and open questioning (asking Boomer 'Why do you hate us so much?', sparing Starbuck). Kendra Shaw is the 'child' conditioned by desensitization and objectifying language into a killing razor and a PTSD victim, whose recovery under Apollo shows the two family models being blended — boldness with communication. Adama's verdict that 'history will have to make its judgments' frames the chapter's claim that understanding Cain requires seeing the family culture that produced her.
- ch. 24 "It’s the Disappointing Stories that Sound True to Life" **·** Karl Marx — Class Struggle; Objectification — Shipman argues that the re-imagined Cylons, having rebelled as slaves, recreated oppression inside their own society: the human-form Numbers rule over mute Centurions (voices literally bred out, like banning slave literacy), animalized Raiders, and dismissed 'insane' Hybrids, whose sentience ('Scar' shows strategy and self-preservation) the Numbers willfully deny. The division of labor echoes Marx's 'from each according to his ability,' but assignments are engineered by their makers, and the model-making program is eugenics rather than evolution — the series' own word 'evolved' is, he says, disingenuous. Hence the Numbers lose the moral superiority their victimhood earned, and the scriptural 'all of this has happened before' implies the subaltern models may rebel next.
- ch. 25 "Seeing Ourselves Reflected Back" **·** Immanuel Kant, Peter Singer — Humanity as an End in Itself; Speciesism; Autonomy of the Will — Gray asks whether Cylons deserve moral consideration, starting from Kant's demand to treat humanity never merely as a means: the New Caprica suicide bombings and Adama's plan to weaponize a Cylon virus treat captives merely as means, while Helo's sabotage of 'A Measure of Salvation' acknowledges their intrinsic value. D'Anna's dissent before being boxed and Athena's defection for Helo show the rational autonomy Kant's personhood requires; if that is dismissed as programming, Singer's sentience criterion (Leoben tortured, Gina catatonic) and Warren's multi-criterial personhood — including Boomer's 'I have a conscience' in 'Downloaded' — still open personhood to Cylons. The chapter ends on the humans' double standard (Athena accepted, guns drawn on Caprica Six) as a mirror for our own treatment of uncertain others.
- ch. 26 "MODEL FOUR - Battlestar Iraqtica" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "Fire Without Burn" **·** The Ethics of Political Violence — Pinedo argues the series restages 'blowback' — the CIA term for covert intervention rebounding on the intervener — in science-fiction guise: Adama's 'playing God' speech, the 'Hero' revelation that his Armistice Line black op (abandoning Bulldog Novacek) may have triggered the Cylon attack, and his confession 'I started it,' complicated by Roslin's warning against simple explanations. The sci-fi 'smokescreen' lets Moore and Eick critique the war on terror — torture, the Pegasus rapes, sympathetic suicide bombers — without naming blowback in a corporate media environment, as the producers' interviews and podcast commentaries show. The show refuses Manichean demonology: the Cylons are made in our image, and the capacity for evil 'is part of what we do in the name of war.'
- ch. 28 "The Future" **·** Frantz Fanon, Jean-Paul Sartre, Karl Marx, Herbert Marcuse — The Ethics of Political Violence; The Ends Justify the Means — Dinello reads the New Caprica occupation as an Iraq allegory interpreted through Fanon's The Wretched of the Earth: occupation is violence personified (Cavil's 'saving them from damnation'), psychological violence colonizes minds (Leoben's manipulation of Starbuck, Jammer's recruitment into the New Caprica Police), and Tyrol's 'throw your body on the gears' strike speech channels Savio, Marx's seizing the means of production, and Marcuse on technology's repression. Fanon's thesis that anticolonial violence is a cleansing, liberating force is dramatized in Tigh's 'blood for blood,' the Temple massacre that recruits a thousand fighters, and Duck's suicide bombing — with Sartre's preface endorsing killing the oppressor and the ends-justify-means dispute between Tigh and Tyrol. The insurgency wins by not losing, as in Algeria and Vietnam, and the show is subversive because it makes viewers sympathize with the occupied.
- ch. 29 "Living with Our Choices" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Radical Freedom and Responsibility; Existentialism — Peters applies Sartre's occupied-France analysis from Existentialism Is a Humanism to 'The Resistance' webisodes: on New Caprica one can resist (Tyrol, Tigh), collaborate (Jammer joining the NCP), or do nothing (Duck staying home with Nora), each path a Sartrean project whose morality lies in choosing freely and living with the consequences. Jammer's execution for treason in 'Collaborators' and Duck's suicide bombing after Nora's death dramatize the stakes — for Sartre, Duck's immorality lies in refusing to own the choice he made at the crossroads, and the Final Four revelation changes nothing since they chose as they believed themselves human. The chapter leaves open the Sartrean worry that wartime morality may simply be written by the winners.
- ch. 30 "Walking the Tightrope" **·** The Social Contract; Consequentialism; Deontology; Democracy — Louis Melancon weighs the Fleet leadership's secrets and lies: Adama's 'blind squirrel' lie about Earth, classifying the fact that Cylons look human, Roslin's faked death of the baby Hera, and Tom Zarek's secret collaboration courts on New Caprica. Democratic duty is framed as a deontological obligation of transparency grounded in the governed's trust, while the leadership repeatedly defaults to consequentialist wartime exceptions. The chapter proposes a rule-of-thumb: secrecy is justified only to hide specific information from an external threat like the Cylons, never to protect the government from its own citizens, and each violation erodes the trust on which Colonial democracy's 'soul' depends.
- ch. 31 "Damned If You Do, and Damned If You Don’t" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli — The Ends Justify the Means; Faith and Reason — Bryan McHenry asks whether manipulating religious belief for political ends is ever moral, using Adama's motivational lie about Earth, Roslin's Pythia-driven 'dying leader' campaign (compared with George W. Bush's faith-based politics), and the Cylons' evangelical monotheism aimed at scaring Baltar into conversion. Machiavelli's Prince licenses rulers who use a religion they do not share, and the chapter judges Adama's hope-based use of the Earth myth and Roslin's uniting religiosity as essentially moral while the Cylons wield faith as a coercive weapon. Madison's Enlightenment demand that religion be directed by reason and conviction rather than force supplies the standard the fear-based Cylon conversion of Baltar fails.
- ch. 32 "Misunderestimated" *(no index entry yet)* — John Kenneth Muir argues the 1978 original series was serious Cold War allegory, not the 'popcorn' Ron Moore dismissed: President Adar's armistice with the Cylons mirrors Carter-era detente and the SALT treaties, Adama's 'preparedness' embodies the American Security Council's 'Peace through Strength' doctrine, and Sire Uri's disarmament bid plays the naive appeaser. In 'Experiment in Terra' Apollo delivers a Goldwater-Reagan-style speech to a Western Alliance menaced by an Eastern Bloc surrogate whose sneak nuclear attack vindicates him. The chapter concludes the original's moral absolutism was genuine political commentary, just as serious as the re-imagined series' post-9/11 allegory.
- ch. 33 "MODEL FIVE - Finding Purpose in the Void" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "Speed and Destination" **·** Jacques Derrida, Martin Heidegger — Being-Toward-Death — Jean-Paul Martinon reads the episode '33', where the fleet jumps every thirty-three minutes for over 130 hours, as a study in a temporality 'of the limit': an interrupted life sandwiched between the death of the Colonies and death at Cylon hands. The survivors' existence becomes eschatological and 'bare' (following Agamben), stripped of history and future with death as the only horizon, and the FTL jump becomes a metaphor for Derrida's messianic to-come, the sliver of undecidable hope between promise and danger. Baltar's line that the universe is a serendipitous system versus Number Six's 'God is watching out for you' marks the fatality-contingency undecidability that structures Colonial time.
- ch. 35 "Finding Our Way" **·** John Dewey — Pragmatism; The Pragmatic Method; Experience and Education — Trudy Milburn applies Dewey's account of habit, impulse and inquiry to navigation in the series: Adama's 'I know the way to Earth' declaration starts an inquiry whose end-in-view is continuously reformulated as humans creatively combine scriptures, pulsars and constellations as signs. Roslin uses scripture at face value and other people's belief in it, which on Dewey's anti-foundationalist terms is legitimate map-making rather than piety. The Cylons, by contrast, treat their plan as God-given, cannot reinterpret signs for themselves (needing Baltar to locate Earth) and fall back on inherited habit, which is why, on Dewey's view, they are the ones who are lost.
- ch. 36 "Free to Be You and Me" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer — Free Will vs Determinism; Compatibilism; Fate and Foreknowledge — Bryan Barker reconciles Cylon programming and human 'destiny' with freedom through compatibilism and Schopenhauer's intelligible character: Boomer's sabotage and shooting of Adama under activation, Baltar's cowardly conversion to the Cylon God, Tigh's decision to stay at his post upon learning he is a Cylon, and Starbuck's flight into the storm in 'Maelstrom' all express innate, unchanging character rather than external compulsion. Even destiny, as in Leoben's prophecies to Starbuck, works like determinism unless reframed as character. On this view we are free because everything we do manifests who we immutably are, whether that character is programmed or born.
- ch. 37 "Taking in the View from the Paranoid Position" *(no index entry yet)* — Psychoanalyst Christopher Christian reads the series through Freud's death instinct and Melanie Klein's paranoid position: Starbuck's recklessness, Tigh's alcoholism and poisoning of Ellen, and Apollo's and Starbuck's peaceful death-hallucinations fuse Eros and Thanatos, while the Chief's sleepwalking attack on Cally shows the death drive deflected outward as aggression. Because humans project their own destructiveness onto the Cylons ('toasters', 'it'), they must keep the machines perpetually in view, leaving the whole fleet stuck in Klein's paranoid position. Cain's torture of Gina in Razor thus becomes a splitting operation meant to keep evil safely outside the self.
- ch. 38 "Embracing Death" **·** Socrates, Albert Camus, Plato — The Meaning of Life; Immortality and the Soul; The Myth of Sisyphus — David Vessey argues the humanoid Cylons' resurrection denies them the 'successful death' that makes a life meaningful: like Greek heroes governed by moira or Socrates drinking the hemlock, mortals must be able to see life as a completed whole that death can crown, with how one lived shaping what death means. Cylon deaths (dying out of range of a resurrection ship, D'Anna being boxed) are always failures or shames, and their religion carries no after-death stakes, unlike Socrates' philosophical preparation for death. Camus's Sisyphus shows agency and the option of suicide remain necessary for meaning, while Plato's Symposium model of immortality through children is the only one left to the Cylons, via mortal human-Cylon babies like Hera.
- ch. 39 "MODEL SIX - Near the End of Our Journey" *(front)*
- ch. 40 "To the Third Power . ." **·** Free Will vs Determinism — Ivan Wolfe shows that Glen Larson's original series is 'ontologically Mormon': Kobol anagrammatizes Kolob, the Quorum of Twelve mirrors the LDS hierarchy, marriage is a 'sealing' for eternity, the Beings of Light's 'as you are now, we once were' restates Lorenzo Snow's couplet on human deification, and Count Iblis can rule only those who freely submit, since agency is supreme in Mormon theology. Because none of this is epistemologically required viewing knowledge, later licensees could rewrite the metaphysics: the Maximum Press comics became an incoherent muddle by abandoning the inviolability of free will, while Hatch's novels and Moore's re-imagining built new ontologies, Moore replacing progressive godhood with the circular 'all this has happened before.'
- ch. 41 "So Say We All" **·** Faith and Reason — Richard Berger reads the 're-imagined' series through Bakhtin's dialogism: Moore's show is not a GINO remake but a polyphonic dialogue with the 1978 series and its own sources, including the Book of Mormon, von Daniken's Chariots of the Gods, Greek myth and fan fiction. That polyphony appears on screen as a spectrum of belief, from Baltar's militant atheism argued over with Number Six's fundamentalism, a staging of the Dawkins-Hitchens debate in which the two extremes prove close enough for conversion, to Roslin's prophetic politics. The chapter argues the reboot is 'more Battlestar Galactica' because it makes the original's latent religious debates explicit.
- ch. 42 "The Cosmic Divide" **·** Protagoras — Moral Relativism; Utilitarianism — The chapter casts the two series as opposed worldviews: the original is Manichaean moral absolutism, with 'War of the Gods' staging Count Iblis and the Ships of Light as cosmic Darkness and Light battling over humanity, while Moore's re-imagining is Protagorean moral relativism where context decides right and wrong. Its clearest cases are comparative: Roslin abandons the FTL-less ships as 'a matter of numbers' yet burns forty-three percent of the fleet's fuel reserves to rescue Starbuck, and Leoben is airlocked after interrogation while the tortured Athena and Gina are treated with mercy. The fan schism between the shows, it concludes, is really the centuries-old quarrel between absolutism and relativism.
- ch. 43 "With or Without Starbuck" **·** Feminism; The Male Gaze; Objectification — Ewan Kirkland argues BSG's gender-free surface, unisex bunks and a Starbuck who never has to defend her right to fly, masks a default masculinity under Adama's patriarchal regime: Ellen is killed off as the negative femme fatale, Kat and Cain die or turn monstrous, and 'The Farm' literalizes feminist fears of patriarchal medicine turning women into breeders. Starbuck confounds the sexualizing male gaze, never objectified, repeatedly rescuing men and reversing damsel-in-distress plots, yet her success is entirely masculinized, bought by becoming like a man. Her New Caprica captivity as Leoben's simulated wife literalizes the feminist critique of compulsory heterosexuality, and her death in 'Maelstrom', submitting to her former kidnapper, suggests the series contains its most transgressive heroine.
- ch. 44 "Human, Machine, or Something In-between" **·** Free Will vs Determinism; Feminism — Heather Rolufs maps Boomer Sharon and Caprica Six onto Eve and Lilith: Boomer's unwitting shooting of Adama parallels Eve eating the fruit before she could understand disobedience, raising whether her free will was ever real, while Baltar repeats Adam's blame-shifting and Six gains the rebuttal Eve never had. The Lilith material from the Alphabet of Ben Sira and the Zohar explains Six's red-dressed seduction of Baltar and her ambivalent violence, snapping a baby's neck as a mercy before the attack yet later risking herself to return Hera. The chapter argues the show lets these demonized first women of myth be reread as neither machines nor demons but human.
- ch. 45 "32 - Dreamers in the Night [ ]" **·** Theodor Adorno, Walter Benjamin — The Culture Industry; The Aura of the Artwork — This fan story stages the fanfiction debate inside the fleet: Roslin recites the 'Scorpion School' line, Adorno's mass culture mindlessly absorbed and Benjamin's fear that reproduction destroys an artwork's aura, while aide Billy Keikeya counters with Henry Jenkins, arguing fanfiction is culture repairing myths owned by corporations and that the zine writers build real community. Baltar, secretly a fan himself, needles Number Six by calling the Cylons 'a fanfiction of the human brain,' and her insistence that there should be one singular truth of the show mirrors her fundamentalist monotheism. Adama and Roslin conclude the fan writers are harmless, even healing, leaving creativity and communal play on the human side of the human-Cylon divide.
- ch. 46 "Case in Point" **·** The Ethics of Political Violence — Using Wayne Booth's coduction, the ethical evaluation of fiction refined through open, informed conversation, this chapter argues BSG is a highly ethical show precisely because it keeps moral debate alive instead of preaching: the unresolved New Caprica suicide-bombing storyline, where an unsympathetic Baltar confronts a saintly Roslin in jail and she still cannot justify the bombings, invites the audience to do the ethical work. The chapter distinguishes Tolkien's allegory from applicability to block a one-to-one Iraq reading, and holds up Ronald Moore's candid blog exchange about the absence of gay characters as coduction in action. Sustained conversation, it concludes, is both the best defense against censorship and the best way to judge art.

## Better Call Saul and Philosophy - I Think Therefore I Scam

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 4 "Copyright" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 5 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "It’s Showtime, Folks!" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "I Slippin’ Jimmy with a law degree is like a chimp with a machine gun" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "1. Scamming for Fun" **·** Immanuel Kant — Humanity as an End in Itself; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Deontology — Clipsham distinguishes Jimmy and Kim's 'scamming for fun' (deceiving Ken over rare tequila in 'Switch', or a montage of pointless cons in 'Marco') from the 'scheming for benefits' of Mike, Gus, and Chuck, who lie only to secure a specific end. Using Kant's prohibition on treating people merely as means, he argues that scheming for a benefit is more disrespectful than scamming for its own sake, because the latter treats a mark as an unwitting player in a game rather than a tool. He also rejects the worry that fun-scamming corrupts character, noting Jimmy was already dishonest and that Gus and Lalo's worst vices have no root in playful deception.
- ch. 9 "2. Don’t Go to Lawyers for Moral Guidance" **·** John Dewey, Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); The Categorical Imperative — Ralston reads Jimmy McGill's slide into Saul Goodman as a study in moral decline, using Dewey's (and Bourdieu's) theory of habit to explain why a person capable of right action repeatedly chooses the wrong path—the pressures of criminal clients, Chuck's contempt, and HHM's rejection wear down his will as an ingrained disposition. He contrasts Kant's categorical imperative with mere 'imperatives of prudence' (rules of thumb for achieving any end), arguing Jimmy consistently follows the latter, as when he unhinges Chuck at the disbarment hearing to win. Invoking Hart's separability of law and morality and Singer on the ethicist's training, he concludes lawyers like Saul make poor moral guides.
- ch. 10 "3. Can It Be Right to Defend the Guilty" **·** Immanuel Kant, David Hume, John Stuart Mill, Jeremy Bentham — The Categorical Imperative; Consequentialism; Utilitarianism; Virtue Ethics — Cook tests whether lawyers may defend the legally and morally guilty against several ethical theories, anchored by Saul's contemplated lie at Lalo Salamanca's bail hearing (knowing Lalo murdered Fred Whalen). Kant's contradiction-in-conception makes lying to the court a perfect duty to refrain from, but Cook shows this looks too rigid via the invented 'Jemma' surgeon case; act and rule consequentialism (Bentham, Mill, Hooker) permit honest defenses while diverging on lies and mitigation. He argues Hume's sentimentalist virtue ethics best fits intuition: Saul's bail lie is wrong because motivated by self-interest rather than the procedural justice a virtuous advocate should feel.
- ch. 11 "4. Better Call the DEA" **·** John Stuart Mill — The Harm Principle; On Liberty; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — White uses Better Call Saul's cartel world—the 'Bagman' shootout, Mike's account of Philadelphia police corruption, Tuco's meth-driven violence—to frame the ethics of the U.S. War on Drugs, tracing its racist origins and drawing a sustained analogy to alcohol Prohibition. She marshals Mill's harm principle from On Liberty to argue adults may choose their own risks absent harm to others, making criminalization inconsistent given legal alcohol. She then fairly lays out the pro-War case (Bennett on deterrence, the Robins Vietnam-heroin study, Mill's own bar on selling oneself into slavery applied to addiction) before favoring decriminalization on the Portugal model.
- ch. 12 "II You can be on one side of the law or the other" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "5. The Sovereign State of Salamanca" **·** Thomas Hobbes — The State of Nature; The Social Contract — Barta and Barnes read the show's Court and Cartel as parallel Hobbesian sovereigns. The New Mexico desert and the criminalized drug trade function as Hobbes's state of nature—lawless, where Nacho robs thieves who 'have no recourse' and Mike sticks up a mule with a garden hose—illustrating natural equality (a small man can kill a big one) and the war of all against all. From mutual fear, deals emerge (Mike and Gus's desert standoff, Mike keeping his word over twenty dollars), but 'covenants without the sword' need a Common Power: Tuco, Hector, Lalo, and ultimately Don Eladio form a feudal hierarchy enforcing loyalty by terror, just as the state's police monopoly on force sustains the Law.
- ch. 14 "6. The Morality of Mike’s Manifesto" **·** Aristotle, Marcus Aurelius, Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — Stoicism; Virtue Ethics (arete); The Golden Mean — The Gibsons analyze Mike Ehrmantraut's code—'if you make a deal with somebody, you keep your word'—through virtue ethics and Stoicism. Aristotle's function argument and Golden Mean partly vindicate Mike's counsel toward temperance and restraint (not carrying a gun, sparing the Salamanca driver), yet Aristotle's claim that 'bad people are full of regrets' marks him as a flawed 'wicked' man given his inner turmoil over breaking his son. They read his calm as Stoic (Marcus Aurelius's Meditations on controlling the mind, Spartan simplicity), and weigh Kant's intent-based defense of his killing the dirty cops Hoffman and Fensky against Mill's social-rule view of sanctionable wrongs.
- ch. 15 "7. Saul Goodman Stands Before the Law" **·** Jacques Derrida — Deconstruction — Haven reads Jimmy's transformation into Saul through Kafka's parable 'Before the Law' as interpreted by Derrida. Like Kafka's man from the country waiting at a gate made only for him, Jimmy stands before 'the Law' as an inaccessible, self-deferring promise guarded by doorkeepers—Mike at the parking booth, Howard, and truly Chuck, who secretly blocks his job. Derrida's analysis of the secret as an unfulfillable promise explains the Law's contradictions (sacred yet petty, present yet silent). Haven argues Jimmy grasps the Law's dirty secret—that it 'doesn't exist' apart from its agents—and, unlike Kafka's man, chooses the liminal outlaw space of Saul Goodman rather than waiting for admittance.
- ch. 16 "III Put on your big boy pants and face reality" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "8. Self-Hatred as Identity" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Bad Faith; Personal Identity; The Look (hell is other people) — Cash argues that Jimmy and Mike become their Breaking Bad selves through a 'negative self-relation' (a term from Martin Hägglund) rather than Walt's outward resentment. Mike's 'I broke my boy' speech in 'Five-O' shows him internalizing guilt for making Matty 'debase' himself, leaving nothing to live for but stashing money for Kaylee; Jimmy internalizes Chuck's verdict that he is 'Slippin' Jimmy with a law degree, a chimp with a machine gun,' so each success as Saul only confirms the worst image of himself. Cash uses Sartre's Being and Nothingness—that one cannot truly be evil 'for myself' without judging oneself as an other—to argue Jimmy's self-hatred must become unconscious for the Saul mask to become his face.
- ch. 18 "9. The Prequel versus Free Will" **·** Gottfried Leibniz, Socrates — Free Will vs Determinism; Principle of Sufficient Reason — Frim argues that Better Call Saul, precisely because it is a prequel, illustrates Leibniz's principle of sufficient reason—every event, including Walt's meth empire or Jimmy's burner-phone hustle, has a causal 'because' stretching infinitely back—and thus a deterministic universe. He dismantles the appeal to Heisenberg/quantum indeterminacy (randomness is not free choice) and refutes three myths: that determinism is boring (the causal 'Story One' is far more gripping than the free-will 'Story Two'), amoral (murder is wrong for its effects; we can keep 'suffering' and drop 'guilt'), and mindless (Nacho's ordered mind versus Tuco's drug-addled one shows minds function well or badly). He enlists Socrates' 'no one goes willingly toward the bad' to recast wrongdoing as disordered reasoning.
- ch. 19 "10. Slippin’ Identity" **·** John Locke — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory) — Šekrst uses Jimmy's many personas—Slippin' Jimmy, James McGill, Saul Goodman, Viktor St. Clair, Gene Takavic—to survey theories of personal identity. She shows psychological continuity (Locke's memory-based view) falters on sleep, comas, and Chuck's apparent dementia, and the brute-physical/bodily view falters on brain-transplant thought experiments and the Phineas Gage case ('no longer Gage'). She defends a narrativist account with a reality constraint: identity is the story one is disposed to tell about oneself, so Jimmy can disown Slippin' Jimmy and reidentify as Saul, and Gene can recall his Saul self—narrative being sufficient though perhaps not necessary for persistence, with others' stories contributing but the protagonist's being authoritative.
- ch. 20 "11. The Self-Deception Road" **·** Adam Smith — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Bad Faith — Doll analyzes self-deception across the series using Gregg Ten Elshof's four mechanisms—attention management, procrastination, perspective shifting, and rationalization. Chuck's electromagnetic hypersensitivity is a real psychological condition about which he deceives himself as to its physical cause, reinforced by everyone accommodating him; his refusal to see Jimmy as anything but 'Slippin' Jimmy' is rationalized as protecting others while sabotaging his brother. Jimmy, via perspective-shifting through Chuck's eyes, comes to believe he can only be Slippin' Jimmy, then rationalizes the Saul persona. Drawing on Joseph Butler and Adam Smith, Doll argues self-deception blinds moral judgment and is ultimately Saul's undoing, leaving him alone in hiding.
- ch. 21 "IV Confidence is good—facts on your side, better" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "12. A Cave of His Own Making" **·** Plato, Aristotle, Søren Kierkegaard — Allegory of the Cave; The Golden Mean; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Golden reinterprets Plato's Allegory of the Cave to argue Chuck enslaves himself in a 'cave of his own making'—his hypochondriacal electromagnetic hypersensitivity—to keep Jimmy accountable and to exact vengeance for Jimmy's unpunished childhood thefts. Using Aristotle's De Anima psychology and the function argument, he places Chuck's imagination (phantasia) below reason: Chuck retains the rationality to serve clients like Mesa Verde and to detect Jimmy's document fraud, but sinks below the Golden Mean into the vices of meanness (short of kindness) and cowardice (short of courage) toward his brother. He adds Kierkegaard's Repetition: Chuck is stuck in backward-looking 'recollection' of vengeance and cannot make the forward, committed 'repetition' of simply being Jimmy's brother.
- ch. 23 "13. Chuckrates v. The Saulphists" **·** Plato, Socrates, Protagoras — Socratic Method (elenchus); Man Is the Measure (sophistic relativism); The Unexamined Life — Staged as a mock disciplinary-board trial, the chapter casts Chuck McGill as Socrates and Jimmy as a Sophist to replay Plato's dialectic-versus-rhetoric quarrel. Chuck prosecutes Jimmy as a mere rhetorician who 'makes the weaker argument defeat the stronger,' quoting the Gorgias, Apology, and Republic, while Jimmy counters that Socrates was himself a self-righteous show-off and that the legal system runs on Sophistic persuasion (juries, opening statements) as much as on truth. The Board's verdict finds Chuck blind to his own rhetoric and Jimmy a quintessential but empathetic Sophist, using the McGill brothers to argue that law fuses dialectic and rhetoric just as ancient Athens did.
- ch. 24 "14. Salvaging Sunk Costs" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Using Jimmy's wish to quit law despite years of study, and Kim's objection, the chapter examines whether the 'sunk cost fallacy' is always a fallacy. It grants that unrecoverable past investments are irrelevant to a narrow cost-benefit goal, but argues via Nozick's opera-ticket case and Thomas Kelly that reflecting on sunk costs can be rational for habit formation, regret avoidance, and signaling resolve to opponents (illustrated by Mesa Verde's land, Jimmy and Kim's office lease, and Jimmy re-scamming Irene). It concludes that a complex career choice legitimately weighs a wider range of psychological, social, and moral costs than the relentless gambler's simple case.
- ch. 25 "15. Saul’s Bullshit’s Not All Good, Man" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Applying Harry Frankfurt's account of bullshit as speech that lacks proper concern for truth, the chapter reads Jimmy's reinstatement-hearing speech about Chuck as pure bullshit: every word he says is true, yet he says it only to win the panel over, as his 'Did you see those suckers?' aside reveals. It then argues Jimmy and Saul's bullshit is harmful on three fronts: it undermines the epistemic demands we owe to what we care about (Kim's ability to know Jimmy's real state), it corrupts the epistemic duties tied to social roles (the panelists as quasi-jurors), and it erodes general epistemic trust across society.
- ch. 26 "16. Better Call Saul Because Chuck’s Condition Is Real" **·** René Descartes — Mind–Body Dualism; Phenomenology; Scientific Realism vs Anti-Realism — The chapter treats Chuck McGill's electromagnetic hypersensitivity as a case study in the metaphysics and phenomenology of medicine, asking whether an unmeasurable condition is 'real.' It contrasts a realist demand for a biomarker with an anti-realist, phenomenological stance that privileges Chuck's first-person lived experience, and blames Cartesian mind-body dualism for medicine's split between neurology and psychiatry that leaves Chuck uncared for. Reading his blackouts, space blanket, and nocebo-driven coma as psychosomatic, it argues that stigmatizing 'crazy' media portrayals of Chuck worsened his isolation and that a whole-person, first-person-narrative approach was needed.
- ch. 27 "V See, that’s your problem—thinking the ends justify the means" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "17. Is Morality for Suckers" **·** Plato — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Tripartite Soul; Justice in the Republic — The chapter reads Jimmy through Plato's Republic, likening his resolve to stop letting conscience stop him to Glaucon's Ring of Gyges thesis that people act immorally whenever they can escape detection, as when Jimmy dodges 'bullets' in his Matrix-like winning speech. Using Plato's tripartite soul, it diagnoses Jimmy as ruled by his appetitive and spirited parts rather than reason, hence lacking self-control and unable to find morality pleasant in itself. It then tests and finds wanting Plato's arguments (contemplative pleasure, connectedness, the immortality of the rational part) that a healthy, reason-ruled soul is both moral and happiest, granting Jimmy's intense scamming pleasures their pull.
- ch. 29 "18. Dissenting Opinions" **·** Moral Relativism; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Framing Kim and Jimmy's clashes as a problem in the epistemology of peer disagreement, the chapter asks whether learning an epistemic peer disagrees should make you suspend judgment (the conciliatory view) or stay steadfast, using a tip-calculation dispute and their office-versus-practice compromise. It argues moral disagreements differ because we rarely count someone who disagrees on one moral question as a genuine peer, and rejects 'no fact of the matter' relativism for implying slavery could be permissible. It then reads Kim's arc, including 'Wouldn't I?', as her moral beliefs genuinely shifting toward Jimmy's, complicated by self-interested and self-deceiving reasons for holding beliefs.
- ch. 30 "19. Can We Blame Jimmy for Being Jimmy" **·** Thomas Nagel, Immanuel Kant — Moral Luck; Free Will vs Determinism; Virtue Ethics — The chapter runs Thomas Nagel's 'Moral Luck' argument on Jimmy, cataloguing his constitutive, circumstantial, causal-deterministic, and outcome luck (the con-man 'wolves and sheep' encounter, the Chicago sunroof children, the missed 1.6 million) to suggest we cannot fairly blame him. It then rebuts Nagel with a poker-and-wager analogy: since 'wagering on freedom weakly dominates' wagering on fatalism, we should treat character as cultivable through virtue theory's habits. It concludes that because nobody prevented Jimmy from changing and he has the resourcefulness to do so, his moral decline was unnecessary and blameworthy.
- ch. 31 "20. Why Is Breaking Skateboarders’ Legs Wrong" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill, Bernard Williams — Consequentialism; The Categorical Imperative; Deontology — The chapter uses three characters to test three single-value moral theories: Chuck as a Kantian deontologist whose rigid loyalty to the categorical imperative and the 'sacred' law destroys his bond with Jimmy; Jimmy as a Mill-style consequentialist whose ends-justify-means scams (Sandpiper, the squat-cobbler film) let him decide what counts as good outcomes and cost him integrity, invoking Bernard Williams; and Mike as a Scanlonian contractualist who honors even immoral agreements like the Tuco hit. Through cases like the 'murderer at the door,' it argues each theory alone leads people to do wrong, so a moral life must combine outcomes, rules, and contracts.
- ch. 32 "VI Never make the same mistake twice" *(front)*
- ch. 33 "21. Finding the Good in Nacho" **·** Immanuel Kant — Deontology; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — The chapter uses Kant to argue that Nacho Varga, though a genuinely bad drug dealer, can perform morally worthy acts in isolated moments. Because Kant locates morality in the rational will and intention rather than accomplishment, it treats Nacho's attempts on the Salamancas and his desire to leave the game as evidence of active moral reasoning, while conceding his motives are muddied by self-interest and love for his father (the shopkeeper example warns against acts done for loved ones). Its clean case is the phone call in 'Something Unforgivable,' where Nacho risks everything to plead for Lalo's near-innocent staff, doing the right thing purely because it is right.
- ch. 34 "22. Breaking Bad Promises" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — The first half of this chapter lays out the moral paradox of 'bad' promises through Better Call Saul: Jimmy's payment to the Lindholm twins for the staged accident, the Kettlemans' bribe for silence, Nacho and Mike's hit contract on Tuco, Gus and Mike's criminal alliance, and Jimmy and Ira's Hummel heist. It stresses Mike's code ('if you make a deal, you keep your word') and his refund to Nacho when the hit fails, then builds a taxonomy of illicit promises as legal or illegal crossed with moral or immoral, setting up the question of whether we may break promises that are wrongful.
- ch. 35 "22. Breaking Bad Promises" **·** David Hume — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — The second half surveys and rejects existing solutions to bad promises: Seana Shiffrin's claim that an immoral promise is no promise and David Owens's 'transfer of authority' theory both fail Hume's objection that words alone change nothing and beg the question of who has authority, while Altham and Gilbert's 'not binding because wicked' is circular. Drawing on Saul/Jimmy's common-law training, the author distinguishes void (malum in se) from voidable (malum prohibitum) promises and proposes a harm-based framework keyed to the location of harm, arguing third-party harms void a promise while purely internal harms grant the harmed party a 'moral veto.'
- ch. 36 "23. Why Does Jimmy Get to Determine Chuck’s Healthcare" **·** Isaiah Berlin, Simone de Beauvoir, John Stuart Mill, Friedrich Nietzsche — Two Concepts of Liberty; The Harm Principle — The chapter uses Chuck's EHS to probe when paternalistic intervention against a competent person's will is justified. It argues Chuck is epistemically irrational (the hidden battery and electrified bed disprove his causal beliefs) yet notes, via Sharon Street and Nietzsche, that we all act on 'illogical' but coherent evaluative attitudes, so absurdity alone cannot license Dr. Cruz's push to commit him. Invoking Isaiah Berlin's warning against oppression in the name of someone's 'real self' and Mill-style concern for self-regarding choices, it finally uses Simone de Beauvoir to distinguish Jimmy's genuine devotion to Chuck from Chuck's controlling 'tyranny,' which shaped Jimmy's turn into Saul.
- ch. 37 "Bibliography" *(notes)*
- ch. 38 "The Hostile Witnesses" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 40 "Back Cover" *(front)*

## Bioshock and Philosophy - Irrational Game, Rational Book

- ch. 1 "Hacking into This Book (Introduction)" *(no index entry yet)* — Luke Cuddy's introduction tours the philosophical riches of the Shock series: the Little Sister's idealized vision of Rapture in BioShock 2, Andrew Ryan as a male counterpart of Ayn Rand, the Motorized Patriots of Columbia, SHODAN's god complex, Elizabeth's tears, and Comstock's prophecy. It frames the volume's guiding questions, such as whether BioShock legitimately critiques Rand's philosophy and whether Booker ever had free will, and closes by invoking the 'false philosopher' mark (knowledge without justification) before inviting the reader to turn the page.
- ch. 2 "Part I LEVEL 1 RESEARCH BONUS" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Gaming Freedom Choosing or Obeying" **·** Hans-Georg Gadamer, Andy Clark, David Chalmers — The Extended Mind; Philosophical Hermeneutics (fusion of horizons); Free Will vs Determinism — Pointon argues the 'would you kindly' twist in Andrew Ryan's office works on two levels at once: it reveals Jack's conditioning within the game and simultaneously falsifies the player's meta-narrative of playing a free agent. He uses Clark and Chalmers's extended mind to explain why games literally extend cognition and body (mirror-neuron empathy with Little Sisters, craning your neck while peeking around corners), and Gadamer's hermeneutic horizons and fusion of horizons to explain how BioShock deliberately builds and then shatters player expectations. The three alternative endings keyed to saving or harvesting Little Sisters then restore a third horizon in which the player, like Jack fighting Fontaine, is a 'man' who chooses rather than a slave who obeys, a critique of linear RPGs that only feign autonomy.
- ch. 4 "How I Learned to Stop Worrying and Love the ADAM" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Ethical Egoism — Rose argues BioShock is not a cheap strawman of Ayn Rand but a use of her own philosophy-through-fiction method (Atlas Shrugged as the hidden utopia Rapture continues) to show what happens when an ideologue divides reason from emotion. Ryan, unlike the flawless John Galt, is a flawed human destroyed by his refusal to question and by sentiment (killed by the son he cannot shoot), while Fontaine wins by manipulating feelings and Tenenbaum's motherly attachment to the Little Sisters becomes her salvation. Drawing on Antonio Damasio's point that emotion filters options for practical reasoning, the chapter reads the save-or-harvest dilemma as proof that moral choices must include emotional response, concluding Randians should celebrate a game that warns against being blinded by ideology rather than against Objectivism itself.
- ch. 5 "“Your Flesh, Too, is Weak. But You Have… Potential”" **·** John Locke, Derek Parfit, Thomas Reid — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Teleporter Paradox (personal fission) — Mentyka maps the two antagonists of System Shock 2 onto the two classical criteria of personal identity: the parasitic Many, one flesh persisting through wildly different bodies, embody the bodily criterion, while the rogue AI SHODAN, pure data abandoning first Citadel then the Von Braun, embodies psychological continuity from Locke's memory theory. He runs the standard objections (Reid and Butler on memory's circularity, cell replacement, cybernetic implants, sleep and infancy) against both sides, then turns to Parfit's claim that identity is not what matters, only relations of degree, likening the reused 'Soldier #G65434-2' avatar to a person surviving through successors. The player character, physical like the Many yet armed with SHODAN's cybernetic knowledge, suggests identity needs both body and mind.
- ch. 6 "“Smother Him in the Crib”—Booker" **·** Simone de Beauvoir, Marilyn Frye — Oppression as a Birdcage; Woman as the Other; Objectification — Origitano reads Elizabeth through Beauvoir and Frye: Columbia's propaganda infantilizes and objectifies her ('the Seed of the Prophet', 'Lamb', 'Specimen', always defined relative to a man), the tower literalizes Frye's birdcage of systematic oppression, and her confinement for an uncontrollable power mirrors women caged by a supposed natural inferiority. Booker's rescue only grants freedom, echoing Beauvoir's point that women have 'only received' what men are willing to give, so Elizabeth remains caged as his poker chip, her lock-picking skills formed by captivity and her tears dismissed as foreign. Her emancipation comes through action and collective solidarity, allying with Lady Comstock's ghost, taking control of Songbird, and finally drowning Booker/Comstock as equals, which enacts Beauvoir's claim that liberation must be won, not granted.
- ch. 7 "Part II TEARS, TIME, AND REALITY" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "The Last Bit" **·** David Chalmers — Philosophical Zombies; The Hard Problem of Consciousness — Cook asks whether Rapture's Plasmids and ghosts refute physicalism and answers no: Plasmid powers are genetically engineered via ADAM and thus describable in physical vocabulary, while contractor Bill McDonagh's audio diary explains the ghosts of Rapture as leaked memories passed through gene-swapping, so all their facts supervene on physical facts in Chalmers's sense. The classic anti-physicalist pressures, Chalmers's zombie argument and the Nagel-style gap between objective physical description and subjective qualia ('the smell of poo'), do not apply because Rapture's phenomena carry no extra subjective residue. Invoking Clarke's third law that advanced technology is indistinguishable from magic, he concludes Andrew Ryan did not choose the impossible.
- ch. 9 "“One Can’t Believe Impossible Things”" **·** Harry Frankfurt, William James, Immanuel Kant — Compatibilism; Free Will vs Determinism; The Metaphysics of Time Travel — Laas runs BioShock Infinite through the free will debate: since the game posits Everett's many-worlds, every Booker is pinned by constants (the coin always landing heads, the always-recruited Bookers) and lacks alternate possibilities, which the incompatibilist would read as destroying responsibility. He answers with Frankfurt's counterexamples, modeling Jack's Little Sister choice under the hidden 'would you kindly' trigger with Atlas's prior-sign intervention as a Frankfurt case where one is responsible though one could not have done otherwise. The drowning ending then implies a 'transworld moral responsibility' in which every counterpart Booker is answerable for all Bookers, a secular massa damnata that the chapter finally challenges with Kant's 'ought implies can'.
- ch. 10 "Pretending to Talk about It" **·** Plato — Allegory of the Cave; Theory of Forms — Travis claims everyone misses the point of both Plato's cave and BioShock, and reads the post-assassination self-destruct sequence as the game's cave moment: though you may roam Ryan's office shooting walls ('active inaction', like the escaped prisoner contemplating the form of the Good), the sequence will not resolve until you insert the item, exposing 'a man chooses, a slave obeys' as hollow. The harvest-versus-save equivalence (Tenenbaum's gifts cancel the ADAM loss) is likewise a stroke of genius, not a flaw, because it shows in-game 'ethical choices' are as meaningless as the prisoners' shadow-naming contests. Since both cave and game run on mimesis, playing pretend, the way forward is to 'pretend to talk about it': performing philosophy through play as Plato's dialogues stage Socrates.
- ch. 11 "The Plurality of Worlds to Come" **·** Gottfried Leibniz — Possible Worlds — Horn tests BioShock Infinite's lighthouse multiverse against the metaphysics of modality: actualism versus possibilism, transworld identity versus counterpart theory, and the Arnauld-Leibniz dispute over whether punishment for Wounded Knee requires that the Booker who did it be identical to the Booker who could have refrained. Applying the essentialism menu (anti-, super-, moderate) he argues superessentialism forces world-boundedness, so the game, whose worlds are concrete yet traversable through Elizabeth's tears, best fits a possibilist counterpart-theoretic model: Booker/Comstock split at the baptism, Anna/Elizabeth across the severed finger, and the Luteces as counterparts existing in every world. He ends by suggesting the always-a-lighthouse-man-city refrain makes the series' recurring figures necessary beings, a structural echo of God.
- ch. 12 "Final Thoughts" **·** William James — Fate and Foreknowledge; Free Will vs Determinism; Possible Worlds — Squires and McBain argue the ending wrongly necessitates that Booker's baptism must produce Comstock in every possible world, cashing out the necessity in possible-world terms and quoting William James on determinism's closed future. Against this 'God of necessity' they defend a traditional omniscient, omnipresent (panentheist) God whose foreknowledge does not determine choices, citing the justice requirement for free will and the biblical case of Hezekiah's prayer adding twenty years as proof the future stays open. Read this way, Booker never had to become a racist zealot, and the game's shockingly limited ending unfairly reinforces the stereotype that deep religious devotion necessarily leads to extremism.
- ch. 13 "Part III THE “UNION” AND THE SODOM BELOW" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "Bird or Cage" **·** Democracy; The Ethics of Political Violence — Elmore reads Columbia's founding through Carl Schmitt: Comstock's baptism Voxophone ('who is that man who lies submerged?') dramatizes how every legal order is founded from outside the law, and the city's identity is built on friend/enemy antagonisms, racial, class, and theocratic, against the 'Sodom below'. The Vox Populi's collapse into tyranny (Daisy Fitzroy threatening Fink's son) follows logically from an identity defined purely by opposition, while Columbia's dependence on Georgia's penal labor shows the friend group materially needs its enemy, making politics an endless war like the War on Terror. Schmitt's sovereign ('he who decides on the exception') and the tears' endless splitting of Booker and Elizabeth mirror the infinite regress of legitimate decision, leaving 'you can't lose if you don't play' as the bleak lesson, softened only by old Elizabeth's tear that lets Booker try again.
- ch. 15 "Noble Lies, Noble Propaganda" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — McKinnon distinguishes lying (asserting what you believe false) from Frankfurtian bullshit (indifference to truth) and argues Andrew Ryan's propaganda, from 'Is a man not entitled to the sweat of his brow?' to the smuggler scare, is closer to bullshit than lying: it aims at controlling citizens' behavior, not at fooling them about facts. Frankfurt's warning that habitual bullshitters lose the ability to discern truth is borne out by Ryan's own audio diary ('have I become so convinced by my own beliefs that I have stopped seeing the truth?'). Yet the chapter defends 'noble' warranted assertions: Ryan's nostalgia lie to Jack about family and friends is told so Jack will discover the truth of what he is, showing that some propaganda aimed at the hearer's truth is not morally problematic.
- ch. 16 "Symbolism How We Create Meaning" **·** Karl Marx, John Rawls, Herbert Spencer — Class Struggle; Ideology and False Consciousness; The Difference Principle — DeHaven and Hendrickson read Columbia through Marx's class struggle: Comstock and Fink form the bourgeoisie controlling the means of production (Finkton, Handyman conversions of injured workers), while religion as 'opiate of the masses', the deified Founders, kinetoscope propaganda, and the Raffle keep the Shantytown proletariat in check. The Vox Populi's revolt instantiates conflict theory's inevitable collapse, but the revolution loses its ideals, turns anarchistic, and becomes the new oppressor burning Columbia. Against Marx the chapter sets Spencer-style structural functionalism (Fink's lions-and-oxen ecology justifying the status quo) and Rawls's difference principle, asking whether Columbia's inequalities ever benefited the least advantaged; Booker's rejection of baptism-as-erasure of his past contrasts with Comstock's laundered sins.
- ch. 17 "Part IV THE CIRCUS OF VALUES" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "The Illusion of Choice" **·** John Dewey, Carl Jung, Plato — Experience and Education; Archetypes; The Collective Unconscious — Kajtár argues BioShock Infinite is meta-fictional art: applying Dewey's Art as Experience, the whole journey from lighthouse to drowning is 'an experience', a unified aesthetic whole you live as Booker rather than a mere object of contemplation. The recurring lighthouse-man-city figures work as Jungian archetypes of the collective unconscious (extended to Campbell's hero's journey structuring Booker's departure, initiation, and return), and the Rapture cameo plus the ocean of lighthouses make the game a story about video game storytelling itself, where the constants are the fictional worlds players enter and the variables are sky or sea. The bird-or-cage choice that changes nothing, like Booker forgetting his 122 previous attempts, shows that rail-bounded interactivity does not disqualify the game from being art, and Levine's 'narrative legos' may one day unite story with real choice.
- ch. 19 "Is a Man Not Entitled to the Sweat of His Brow" **·** Maurice Merleau-Ponty, Martin Heidegger, Thomas Nagel, Edmund Husserl — Phenomenology; Embodiment (the lived body); What Is It Like to Be a Bat? — Schevelier uses the Andrew Ryan assassination to teach phenomenology: the moment the controller stops responding, the 'mineness' of the experience collapses and the story stops being yours, revealing that Jack's (and the player's) freedom depended on a thin self-narrative the mind 'desperately struggles to create', per Lutece's epigraph. Nagel's bat shows why no brain scan captures what it is like to play, while Husserl, Heidegger, and Merleau-Ponty supply the toolkit; Heidegger's hammer, which shows itself in use or in breakdown, explains both immersive scenes like the Kashmir restaurant and what breaks them (bugs, wrong display, a talking protagonist). Following Merleau-Ponty's claim that art records subjective experience, the chapter treats Levine's team as artistic phenomenologists whose design question 'What is this experience we are trying to make?' turned Columbia and Elizabeth into phenomenological analyses to be read.
- ch. 20 "Would You Kindly Evolve Today" **·** Jürgen Habermas — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Philosophy of Disability — Ledder reads Plasmids and ADAM as human enhancement technology and stages the real bioethics debate: therapy versus enhancement, transhumanist enthusiasm (whole-brain emulation like Eleanor's ADAM-collecting, Sandberg's morphological freedom) against fears of Splicer-like superhumans and eugenics. The Rapture arms race, where your Electric Bolt Plasmid goes obsolete and forces further splicing, dramatizes the coercive spiral of competitive enhancement, while Habermas's objection that genetically designing children turns them into projections grounds the reading of Jack, Fontaine's purchased and conditioned embryo triggered by 'would you kindly', as a metaphor for engineered autonomy destroyed. The chapter closes with disability critiques: maybe the problem is not the body but the social barriers that 'disable' it, so changing society might beat changing flesh.
- ch. 21 "This Is the End" **·** Plato, Aristotle, John Dewey — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Muniz uses Dr. Steinman's surgical madness in the Medical Pavilion to test whether beauty and morality are entangled: Plato's Symposium makes beauty the value binding truth and goodness (Steinman's 'Goddess' of symmetry), Aristotle names order, symmetry, and definiteness the chief forms of beauty, and Keats's 'Beauty is truth' frames Steinman's descent into asymmetry-hating madness. Dewey's point that the young let the beautiful govern their behavior, plus the author's puppy-versus-rat classroom experiment, shows how the ugly get treated as morally worse, as in Steinman's 'moral obligation' to beautify. The Circus of Values vending machines frame the intrinsic-versus-extrinsic value distinction behind the Little Sister save-or-harvest dilemma, and the chapter concludes players must disentangle beauty from goodness and choose values for themselves.
- ch. 22 "Notes on Contributors" *(notes)*
- ch. 23 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 24 "End User License Agreement" *(front)*

## Black Mirror and Philosophy - Dark Reflections

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "part0001" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "part0002" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "part0003" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "part0004" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "part0005" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Contributors" *(notes)*
- ch. 8 "The Black Mirror Multiverse An Editor’s Note" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "INTRODUCTION" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Black Mirror" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Jean-Paul Sartre — The book's introduction argues that Black Mirror does philosophy the way Sartre's No Exit and earlier philosophical fictions (Lucian, Ibn al-Nafis, More, Bacon, Defoe) do: its near-future scenarios — the pig spectacle of The National Anthem, Nosedive's star ratings — work as thought experiments and social criticism rather than simple 'warnings about technology,' since Brooker insists the show is about the human condition, not gadgets. It applies Wittgenstein's duck–rabbit 'seeing as' distinction to explain why two viewers of the same episode may legitimately see different philosophical arguments in it, which is exactly what the book's chapters then do. It closes by presenting each episode (and each chapter) as a self-contained philosophical prompt.
- ch. 11 "SEASON 1" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "Our First Gaze into the Black Mirror" **·** Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham, Aristotle, Martha Nussbaum — Consequentialism; The Categorical Imperative; Virtue Ethics; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Collins and Boesch use Prime Minister Callow's coerced live sex act with a pig in The National Anthem to weigh conflicting moral obligations: the public's initial 72% Kantian refusal flips to an 86% utilitarian capitulation after the kidnapper's finger video, and the withheld revelation that Susannah was released half an hour early undermines any consequentialist justification of the act. A virtue-theory reading faults Callow's character for faking the rescue video and putting self-interest first, and the UKN media's Kantian compliance collapses once ratings are at stake. The closing section applies Martha Nussbaum's theory of disgust (contamination transferring disgust from blood and vomit to the spectacle-watchers themselves) to argue the episode's graphic imagery functions as a moral emotion condemning the audience's own obsession with spectacle.
- ch. 13 "Resistance" **·** Karl Marx, Theodor Adorno, Herbert Marcuse — Commodity Fetishism; The Culture Industry; One-Dimensional Man — Reading Fifteen Million Merits through Marx, the chapter treats Bing's world of endless pedaling, Wraith Babes, and Hot Shot as a commodity society in which people themselves carry use and exchange values — Oliver the 'lemon,' Glee deemed 'quite worthless,' Abi's unsellable papier-mache penguin discarded as detritus. Marx's commodity fetish explains the universalized pricing in merits, and Lukacs-style reification shows things commanding people (Cuppliance compelling compliance). Adorno's culture industry explains why all the shows share one form and why Bing's glass-shard rebellion is absorbed as just another commodity, while Marcuse's One-Dimensional Man supplies the hope that reason, love (Bing's love for Abi as something 'real'), and switching off the media can still seed resistance.
- ch. 14 "The Grain Can Lead Us to Know Too Much" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Michel Foucault — The Look (hell is other people) — The chapter argues that the Grain in The Entire History of You ruins Liam and Ffion's marriage not because the truth is bad but because total transparency destroys trust (via Luhmann), feeds a paranoid cycle where any deleted footage looks like guilt, and strips Fi of authority over her own life story. Goffman, Plessner, and Simmel ground the claim that social masks, tact, and privacy are preconditions of selfhood, while Sartre's 'look' explains Fi's shame when Liam forces her to replay her infidelity and Rousseau explains how dependence on others' gaze makes us unfree. Framed by Foucault's reading of Oedipus as a victim of his own knowledge, it concludes that Liam should have let the past lie — and ends with Byung-Chul Han's transparency society as our own already-arrived trajectory.
- ch. 15 "SEASON 2" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "Taking the Leap (It’s Not Fair)" **·** Robert Nozick, Derek Parfit — Personal Identity; Philosophy of Love; Teleporter Paradox (personal fission) — Against the natural reading that Be Right Back fails because AshBot is a poor copy, the chapter argues that even a perfect duplicate, AshBot+, would not be Ash: like a perfect forgery of a twenty-dollar bill, it would share all intrinsic but none of Ash's relational properties (being Martha's beloved, the coming father of her child), which also exposes a fission paradox for psychological-continuity theories. Nozick's account of love as a 'we' — shared memory, autonomy, and well-being — explains why Martha cannot trade up or replace a dead partner without destroying the self constituted by the relationship. Parfit's view that only psychological continuity matters is then rejected: because Martha's relationship survives only if Ash does, survival itself matters, and Martha must mourn rather than cling to a specter.
- ch. 17 "“Don’t forget, she’s a dangerous individual”" **·** Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham, Friedrich Nietzsche, John Locke, G.W.F. Hegel, Niccolò Machiavelli, Judith Jarvis Thomson, John Stuart Mill, Derek Parfit — Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; The Categorical Imperative; Personal Identity (memory theory) — The chapter tests the White Bear Justice Park's daily torture of Victoria Skillane against every major theory of punishment: Kant and Hegel's retributivism makes her deserve punishment (her crime could not be universalized and annulled justice) but cannot justify a punishment disproportionate to the crime. Locke's memory criterion suggests Punished Victoria is not the person who did the crime, though Parfit's psychological continuity and Thomson's bodily continuity restore the identity. Bentham and Mill's utilitarianism partially vindicates the park as deterrence and incapacitation, but cannot explain the laughing crowds — which Machiavelli's civic spectacle and Nietzsche's genealogy of punishment (Schadenfreude as moralized creditor-debtor pleasure) explain: the crowd is gleefully reenacting Victoria's own crime and calling it justice.
- ch. 18 "After the Waldo Moment" **·** Plato, John Stuart Mill — On Liberty; Democracy — Littmann reads The Waldo Moment through Donald Trump — Waldo's 'knobber' and 'dickhead' taunts anticipate 'Little Marco' and 'Crooked Hillary' — to ask what is wrong with mockery and disrespect in democratic politics. Plato's Meno shows why true beliefs acquired by mockery rather than understanding are unstable and steerable toward any content, and ad hominem attacks substitute ridicule for engagement with arguments. Mill's On Liberty arguments against censorship and orthodoxy — we might be wrong, collision with error clarifies truth — show that refusing to engage opponents (as Waldo refuses Monroe) locks everyone in 'reality bubbles,' and the chapter concludes that letting anger and mockery replace reasoned engagement, as when Waldo's followers riot, sabotages democracy.
- ch. 19 "THE CHRISTMAS SPECIAL" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "A White Christmas with Dark Reflections" **·** Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham, Robert Nozick, Hannah Arendt, Peter Singer — Humanity as an End in Itself; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Utilitarianism; The Minimal State — The chapter follows White Christmas's three escalating uses of the Z-eye digital block — Beth blocking Joe (whose information deprivation leads to two deaths), court-ordered blocks extending to children, and Matthew's society-wide red-blur sex-offender block — as a conflict between privacy and access to information. Utilitarian and Kantian frameworks (autonomy, persons as ends) leave the trade-offs genuinely unsettled, while Nozick's minimal state shows the automatic, loophole-free blocking regime is an indefensibly paternalistic state violating proportionality. Arendt's account of meaningfulness through speech grounds the conclusion that erasing someone's face and voice violates their humanity, and the chapter's final move is defamiliarization: if digital blocks are wrong, we should re-examine the social-media blocking, restraining orders, prisons, and sex-offender registries they mirror.
- ch. 21 "SEASON 3" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "A Black Mirror of Our Anxieties" **·** Michel Foucault, Jean-Paul Sartre, John Stuart Mill, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Plato — The Panopticon (surveillance); The Look (hell is other people); The Harm Principle; The General Will — Nosedive's star ratings are read as a digital panopticon — Bentham's prison design generalized by Foucault's Discipline and Punish — with China's Zhima Credit and Social Credit Systems showing the 'future is already here.' Rousseau's general will and Plato's ship-of-state frame the tyranny-of-the-majority worry (Chester destroyed by an office mob, Lacie jailed for a wedding toast, post-truth tribalism), which Mill's harm principle rebuts: Lacie's rant harms no one and should not be punished. Sartre's 'look' and No Exit explain Lacie's staged cookie photo ('Heaven!') and her final liberating exchange of insults with her cellmate, while Goffman's masks ground the epistemological worry that digital profiles are performances we nonetheless trust, and Bourdieu's symbolic capital names what the scores actually measure.
- ch. 23 "Forget Cooper. Are We in a Simulation" **·** Nick Bostrom — The Simulation Hypothesis; Personal Identity — The chapter argues the horror of Playtest lies not in novelty but in immersion erasing the reality/fiction line: the rubber-hand and Muller-Lyer illusions, the paradox of fiction, and 'aliefs' show that knowing something is fake does not stop it feeling real, and for incorrigible experiences like pain the distinction collapses entirely. The AI's stripping of Cooper's memories and preferences is framed as loss of psychological continuity — a living death echoing his father's Alzheimer's — since with no memories Cooper cannot even answer who he is. The final move turns Bostrom's simulation argument on the viewer: because Cooper 'woke up' twice into deeper simulation and died 0.04 seconds in, we too may have no test that certifies we have ever left the simulation.
- ch. 24 "Do We Attain Justice by Punishment" **·** Jeremy Bentham, John Stuart Mill, Marcus Aurelius — Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; The Harm Principle; Utilitarianism — Kenny's blackmail arc in Shut Up and Dance — the twist that reclassifies the sympathetic protagonist as a child-pornography offender — anchors an argument that the hackers' vigilantism cannot attain justice: their punishments are unannounced, disproportionate, aimed at merely moral wrongs (infidelity, racist emails), and exempt from the presumption of innocence and nulla poena sine lege that states replaced vendetta with. A distinction between natural-law and legal-positivist conceptions of justice shows why punishment belongs to the state alone. Against retribution ('a mere prison sentence is too good for Kenny'), Bentham's and Mill's utilitarianism — deterrence, incapacitation, and above all rehabilitation, capped by the harm principle — shows punishment should be no more than deterrence requires, making the hackers sadists rather than agents of justice.
