- ch. 25 "The Value of Finitude" **·** Robert Nozick, Martha Nussbaum, Epicurus, Thomas Nagel, Bernard Williams, Lucretius, Immanuel Kant — The Experience Machine; Death Is Nothing to Us; Immortality and the Soul; The Meaning of Life — The chapter tests each motive for uploading to San Junipero and finds it wanting: Kantian cosmic justice fails because the resort neither rewards the good nor can punish the wicked; Tolstoy's cosmic-insignificance dread fails because a finite life can still be meaningful, and even Epicurus and Lucretius deflate the fear of death (while Nagel notes death's evil lies in foreclosed possibilities, and Williams's Makropulos case warns eternal life breeds boredom). Nozick's experience machine is the closest analogy, but the chapter argues San Junipero differs — residents know it is digital and share it with other conscious people — before Nussbaum's argument lands: without finitude there is no risk, so no courage, sacrifice, or deep love, which is why Kelly's forty-nine real years with Richard outweigh forever with Yorkie.
- ch. 26 "The Future of Our “MASS Implant”" **·** Emmanuel Levinas, Ludwig Wittgenstein — The Face of the Other; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Stripe's MASS implant in Men Against Fire, which makes targeted humans look like monstrous 'roaches,' literalizes the chapter's claim that dehumanizing language — chosen by Brooker after columnist Katie Hopkins called migrants 'cockroaches' — is the real-world technology that enables genocide, from the Armenian and Holocaust cases through Rwanda's Tutsis to the Rohingya. Wittgenstein's 'the limits of my language mean the limits of my world' grounds how propaganda deadens empathy, and Kelman-style accounts of excluded identity explain why the villagers hate but cannot kill while the implanted soldiers kill gleefully. Levinas's face of the Other explains the turn: once Stripe sees Catarina and Alec's faces, he cannot pull the trigger, and the chapter closes on our own MASS implant — Trump's 'animals,' the ascent-of-man studies, and the temptation to reset and forget.
- ch. 27 "#WithGreatPowerComesGreatResponsibility" **·** Hannah Arendt, John Rawls — The Banality of Evil; Justice as Fairness — The chapter weighs the #dangers of trial by Twitter — Tess's suicide attempt after a mob turned on her, Clara Meades killed over a war-memorial photo, the fundamental attribution error and echo chambers convicting the innocent, and Rawls's point that unpredictable, lawless punishment destroys liberty — against the #benefits, above all #MeToo as 'the beautiful flip side of #DeathTo' that finally held powerful abusers accountable when courts would not. Arendt's banality of evil explains both Garrett Scholes's bee-drone scheme and the ordinariness of the shamers: Parke's 'He's ordinary. That is the type,' and Liza Bahar's crowdsourced 'Fucking Bitch' cake show diffused responsibility enabling cruelty. The moral drawn is that anonymous power over others' lives demands conscious, individual responsibility for every click.
- ch. 28 "SEASON 4" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "“King of space, right here!”" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant, Maurice Merleau-Ponty — Virtue Ethics; Deontology; Intentionality — The chapter asks whether Robert Daly wrongs the USS Callister NPCs even if they are 'merely' sentient code: his theft of co-workers' DNA violates a fundamental right to privacy, and Kant's focus on the will shows his in-game intentions aim at real people — he logs in solely to strangle James's NPC and make him confess to being pathetic, wanting to harm the actual James. Aristotle's virtue ethics supplies the second indictment: because hyperrealistic VR blurs game and life, Daly's daily practice of cruelty habituates vice and makes cruelty part of who he is, unlike clearly fictional violence such as Mortal Kombat. Merleau-Ponty's intentionality links avatar actions to the self, so the chapter ends by asking what our own intentions in games like The Sims or Grand Theft Auto reveal about our character.
- ch. 30 "What Not to Do as a Parent" **·** Aristotle — The Golden Mean; The Ethics of Care; Virtue Ethics (arete) — The chapter pinpoints Marie's failure in Arkangel using Feinberg's parental-rights framework: by filtering out every fright (the neighbor's dog, her father's stroke) she violated Sara's right to an open future, misconstrued her welfare and protection rights, and stunted the empathy that makes a child a fully functioning human being. Aristotle's golden mean frames over-parenting as a vice of excess — as damaging as neglect — and Ruddick's maternal ethics of care also convicts Marie, since mothering requires fostering growth and social acceptability, not just preserving life. The Philip K. Dick comparison lands the conclusion: an android is distinguished from a human only by lacking empathy, so Marie has manufactured a partial android rather than raised a child.
- ch. 31 "Learning from Mia’s Mistakes" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle — The Categorical Imperative; Practical Wisdom (phronesis); The Ethics of Care; Virtue Ethics — The chapter grades each of Mia's escalations in Crocodile — helping Rob hide the cyclist's body, killing Rob fifteen years later, then killing Shazia, her husband, and baby Ali to keep the secret — under Kant's categorical imperative (the cover-up cannot be universalized and treats persons as means), the utilitarian calculus (which also condemns every killing once the widow's suffering and Mia's guilt are counted), and feminist care ethics, which rejects her defense that she killed to protect her family because care obligations never cancel proportionality and others' rights. Aristotle's virtue ethics supplies the diagnosis: Mia's undoing is lack of practical wisdom — every split-second choice (which she never had time to deliberate) went wrong because she lacked virtuous habits. The chapter urges practicing everyday virtue so we never take the first step down her path.
- ch. 32 "Two Philosophies, Two Compasses to Navigate Life" **·** Epictetus, Jean-Paul Sartre, Marcus Aurelius, Albert Camus — The Dichotomy of Control; Bad Faith; Authenticity; The Absurd — Against 'Hang the DJ,' where Frank and Amy's coaching system assigns partners and expiration dates inside a simulated dating park, the chapter argues a Stoic would refuse to sign up: delegating judgments to the coach violates Epictetus's dichotomy of control, and the endless date-cycle fails all four virtues, treating dates (like Amy's bored lovemaking) as means rather than inherently worthy persons. An existentialist reading via Sartre says nearly every system-arranged relationship is bad faith - couples act as if compatible against the expiry they were given, as Frank and Nicola do - and only Frank and Amy's open-ended, freely chosen relationship escapes it, so their 'fuck off' to the system is a leap of faith that 998 of 1000 simulated couples make, which is how the app computes its 99.8% match. The authors close with Camus's absurd, doubting that the real Frank and Amy, who cannot rebel against an app that tells them they belong together, will fare as well as their simulations.
- ch. 33 "Reflections on Technophobia" **·** René Descartes, Karl Marx, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Thomas Aquinas — Mind–Body Dualism; Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; Alienation; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — The chapter asks whether the technophobia of 'Metalhead' - robo-dogs hunting Bella and her friends across a wasteland - is justified, testing Daniel Dinello's list of fears (identity, values, emotion, freedom, life). Descartes's and Aquinas's human-uniqueness over the mechanical fails because the robo-dogs, like DeepMind, learn and problem-solve, while the episode's Rousseau-style romanticism (only the non-technological cliff, tree, sunlight and stream aid Bella) and the teddy-bear mission show technology amplifying compassion rather than threatening it. The real dangers are robots as Latourian 'black boxes': Asimov's Three Laws cannot be operationalized, and if we cannot know how robo-dogs decide, our frameworks for blame and punishment break down (who answers for a killing - robot, programmer, or, on Marx's analysis of capitalist technology as an instrument of oppression, the economic system behind it?). The chapter concludes that since we already share the world with robots, learning to live responsibly alongside them must replace simple fear.
- ch. 34 "Happily, Ever After" **·** Martha Nussbaum, The Buddha — Forgiveness; The Four Noble Truths; Karma and Rebirth; Compassion (karuna) — The chapter asks whether Nish's revenge in 'Black Museum' - poisoning Rolo and downloading his digital self into her father Clayton's eternal-electrocution exhibit - is morally justified. Nussbaum's Anger and Forgiveness condemns payback as sadism plus 'magical thinking' and prescribes transition-anger, then unconditional forgiveness, then best of all unconditional love (the prodigal son); Christian agape commands loving enemies and leaving wrath to God; and the Buddhist analysis of dukkha, karma and non-self shows Nish's hateful revenge generates worse karma than Rolo's greed and deepens the very suffering it means to end. The verdict: revenge, however emotionally satisfying, is never justified - even the tourists zapping Clayton and Nish zapping Rolo fall into the same trap.
- ch. 35 "BANDERSNATCH" *(front)*
- ch. 36 "The Real World" **·** Aristotle, Theodor Adorno, Jean-Paul Sartre, Søren Kierkegaard, Harry Frankfurt, David Lewis — Free Will vs Determinism; Compatibilism; The Culture Industry; The Metaphysics of Time Travel — Written as a choose-your-own-adventure whose branches mirror Bandersnatch's, the chapter treats you, the Netflix viewer, as the external force Stefan suspects: Libet-style readiness-potential neuroscience suggests conscious choice is an after-the-fact story, and compatibilist rescues (Frankfurt's second-order desires, Fischer's guidance control) founder on the 'BundyBot' argument that programmed desires are not freedom. Everett's many-worlds interpretation supplies the multiverse of 'one reality for each possible course of action,' and David Lewis's fixed-past block universe answers the grandfather paradox raised by Stefan's mirror-trip to die on the 8:45 train with his mother. Aristotle does double duty: the Poetics explains why Bandersnatch rightly limits your options (Sartrean absolute freedom would make a crappy story), and the Nicomachean Ethics on voluntary action pins moral responsibility for Stefan's murders on the viewer rather than the coerced Stefan. Adorno's culture industry asks whether Tuckersoft's 'hit factory' - and Black Mirror itself - can produce genuine art, while Kierkegaard's dread of irreversible choice is overturned by an episode that always lets you go back and try again.
- ch. 37 "SEASON 5" *(front)*
- ch. 38 "“Exit Game”" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Plato, John Locke, John Stuart Mill, Arthur Schopenhauer — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Personal Identity (memory theory); Higher and Lower Pleasures; Master and Slave Morality — Reading 'Striking Vipers' through its closed- and open-door imagery, the chapter asks whether Danny and Karl's virtual sex as Lance and Roxette is adultery, testing the question with Plato's Ring of Gyges (what people do when no one can see them), Locke's memory theory (if Danny remembers Roxette's sex acts, is Roxette Danny?), and behaviorist replies that only physical penetration counts. Nietzsche supplies the chapter's frame: his master/slave morality and his view of life as self-created representational art explain how the characters' hidden, mind-only moral inversion creates real existential meaning in virtual sex. The episode's fusion of bodily and mental pleasure is said to break down Mill's higher/lower pleasures distinction - 'lower' physical virtual pleasures yield a 'higher' sense of wholeness - and Schopenhauer's insatiable striving plus Husserl's meaning-conferring perception underwrite the conclusion that virtual love can be real, with Danny and Theo's once-a-year open marriage endorsed as an act-consequentialist balance between monotonous fidelity and escapist fantasy.
- ch. 39 "“He’s done tech detox weekends here and there, but ten days ”" **·** Karl Marx — Alienation; Commodity Fetishism — Reading Smithereens as today's reality rather than a dystopia, the chapter explains the crash that killed Chris's fiancée through Herbert Simon's attention economy: platforms like Smithereen are free because they harvest and sell attention, and Billy Bauer admits the app was deliberately tuned 'like a crack pipe' with dopamine targets. Using Marx's analysis of capitalist surplus, the chapter argues (with Zuboff's 'behavioral surplus' and Crary's 24/7 capitalism) that users are wageless laborers generating value for free, and that compulsive checking is Gerald Moore's 'dopamining' - brain plasticity exploited by a hostile attention environment - rather than free choice. It rejects individual tech-detox consumerism as a false solution and closes with Simondon: knowing our machines is the beginning of the political reflection needed to dissolve the alienating 'fetishism of the tool.'
- ch. 40 "“Oh, honey, I’ll do anything for you”" **·** Martin Heidegger, Aristotle — The Question Concerning Technology; Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Virtue Ethics (arete) — Using Rachel's Ashley Too doll, the chapter distinguishes robots as instruments (Kevin's MAUZR mouse) from social robots designed to be perceived 'as being present in the way a person is,' and invokes Heidegger's 'Question Concerning Technology' to argue that social robots break the instrumentalist picture: it is our empathy - triggered even by the insubstantial hologram Ashley Eternal - that they exploit. The Aristotelian test of friendship (we emulate those we admire, and friendship forms virtuous character) shows Ashley Too fails: her flattery installs false confidence, and because she never requires Rachel to attend to another's needs, she models no real friendship. Girard's mimetic desire explains Rachel's borrowed aspiration to become Ashley O and Jack's emulation of their dead mother, so the chapter concludes robot 'friends' are poor friends - not because they are machines, but because a real friendship is 'an education in the social virtues.'
- ch. 41 "BLACK MIRROR REFLECTIONS" *(front)*
- ch. 42 "Ethical Issues" **·** Alan Turing — The Turing Test; Philosophical Zombies; Mind–Body Dualism — Focusing on White Christmas's cookies and Ashley Too's synaptic snapshot, the chapter argues behavior can never settle whether an artificial system is conscious: the Turing test fails against Geminoid-robot-style cases where recorded signals produce human-like behavior, and philosophical-zombie possibilities plus the point that computations and information patterns are subjective interpretations (not objective physical properties) mean only discovering the physical neural correlates of consciousness could tell us whether a cookie feels pain. It then separates copying from transferring: with souls and substance dualism rejected, Black Museum's 'transfers' are really copies, and San Junipero's upload would house a copy whose future experiences are as alien to Yorkie as a plaster cast of her feet. Ethically, if cookies are conscious, then Matt's breaking of Cookie-Greta, Daly's captives, and Clayton's keychain are torture of persons, and it is the very uncertainty about cookie consciousness that lets characters rationalize the abuse - which is why the in-world legislation granting cookies human rights matters.
- ch. 43 "A Dilemma" **·** John Locke, Derek Parfit — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Teleporter Paradox (personal fission); Bundle Theory of Self — The chapter asks 'Is your cookie you?', running theories of personal identity through Black Mirror's copies: the soul theory surfaces when Clayton signs away his 'digital self'; Locke's memory theory implies Cookie-Greta is Greta - a reductio, since one person cannot be wholly in two places at once (the same problem for Ashley Too and the thousand Hang the DJ simulations) - and the perdurantist patch recasts Greta and her cookie as time-slices of one four-dimensional person. Physical theories (Olson's animalism, Unger's brain-essentialism) rule out survival by upload, so Yorkie dies when her body dies even though digital Yorkie continues in San Junipero; Parfit's no-self bundle view and the teletransporter case then dissolve the fear, since there is no ego to lose and ordinary survival is 'about as bad' as being destroyed and replaced by a replica. The upshot: the punishment and immortality plots (Joe's million-year Christmas loop, San Junipero) require cookies to be both conscious and psychologically connected in the right way, and the episodes' horror depends on leaving that genuinely uncertain.
- ch. 44 "The Death of Death" **·** Martin Heidegger, Jacques Derrida, Albert Camus, Sigmund Freud, Epicurus — Deconstruction; The Myth of Sisyphus; Death Is Nothing to Us; The Death Drive (Thanatos) — Reading Black Mirror's deaths through Bandersnatch's train ending, Be Right Back's AshBot, and San Junipero, the chapter uses Heidegger's presence-at-hand (Stefan's absent mother and rabbit remain present as possibilities he can act on) and Derrida's différance (the meaning of life and death is endlessly deferred as technology scrambles the presence/absence binary - Martha's simulated Ash, Liam's Grain deletions, Chris's guilt-haunted fiancée). A central section frames the whole debate as transhumanism - Kurzweil's three bridges to the posthuman against Fukuyama's warning that self-directed evolution replaces God - before Camus resolves the question of meaning: Kelly and Yorkie, like Sisyphus, create meaning by choosing their fate in a VR heaven that objectively matters nothing. Freud's Eros/Thanatos drives and Epicurus's 'death is nothing to us' close the chapter's question of whether to extend, hasten, or simply accept mortality.
- ch. 45 "Anti‐love Shows What Love Is" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Aristotle — Philosophy of Love; Cardinal and Theological Virtues — The chapter takes Irma Thomas's 'Anyone Who Knows What Love Is (Will Understand)?' - the song Brooker plants across five episodes - as the key to the series, defining love with Aquinas (to love is to wish the good by which the beloved flourishes, rooted in proper self-love, on an Aristotelian base) and evil as the void left where that good is withheld. Each episode that plays the song features characters who mishear it: Hot Shot's judges take Abi's sacrificial love song as an audition for submission to WraithBabes, Joe misreads Beth's song as meant for him and kills Beth's father in the resulting void, and Mia in Crocodile refuses the self-sacrificing loving act of confessing, murdering to fill the gap. Callow's pig-for-a-princess self-sacrifice, Theo's speech about commitment, and digital Walton dying to protect a copy of his son Tommy are the exceptions that know what love is, so the chapter concludes Black Mirror is as much about a world emptied of love as about dangerous technology.
- ch. 46 "Heaven is a Place on Earth" **·** René Descartes, Michel Foucault, Robert Nozick, Jeremy Bentham — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); The Experience Machine; The Panopticon (surveillance); Philosophical Skepticism — The chapter argues Black Mirror's perception tech works because the brain constructs experience top-down (predictive coding), so implants like Playtest's mushroom, Arkangel's filter, and Men Against Fire's MASS can inject perceptions and suppress error signals - and Cooper's total VR illusion defeats every one of Descartes's strategies (memory continuity, cross-checking the senses, reason) for telling dream from reality, killing him seconds after implantation. Nozick's experience machine illuminates Danny's preference for Striking Vipers X over his marriage and Arkangel's lesson that filtering Sara's childhood perceptions left her unable to read danger or emotion. Foucault's panopticon (via Bentham) frames White Christmas's cookie interrogation modeled on real police deception (Frazier v. Cupp), the MASS implant's manufacture of 'roach' targets for killing, and Nosedive's self-imposed social ratings as modern perceptual discipline - with only trusted others (Catarina, Susan, Kelly) punching through the manipulation.
- ch. 47 "29 The Dangers of Technology in Black Mirror" **·** Martin Heidegger, Francis Bacon, Walter Benjamin, Hannah Arendt, Emmanuel Levinas — The Question Concerning Technology; The Aura of the Artwork; The Face of the Other — Staged as a chatroom of uploaded dead philosophers (itself a Black Museum-style cookie gimmick), the chapter stages the technology debate as Francis Bacon's optimism - technology is neutral, humans mess up, the Grain could educate and MASS could treat PTSD - against Heidegger's claim that technology is a way of thinking that turns everything, including the human mind kept on call behind the Grain and Nosedive's rated behaviors, into a resource. McLuhan's 'the medium is the message,' Benjamin's aura (he recoils at being a reproduction without aura, then a 'limited' Benjamin 2.0 embraces exhibition value and Netflix's reach), Levinas's ethics of the Other against Heidegger's ontology, Arendt's ethic of responsibility born of Nazism, and Ellul's technique - the autonomous totality of efficiency-seeking methods that enslaves us - collide over episodes from The National Anthem to Hated in the Nation, until uploading all the Greek philosophers to recover techne crashes the computer.
- ch. 48 "CONCLUSION" *(front)*
- ch. 49 "30 Black Mirror in the Future" *(no index entry yet)* — The closing essay asks whether Black Mirror will still be watched in fifty years, applying Howard Gardner's three marks of artistic beauty: interestingness (Hated in the Nation's twists), memorable form (White Bear's editing and pacing), and the invitation to revisit (Bandersnatch, which embodies it literally). Even if its 2010s gadgets come to look dated like duck-and-cover films, the chapter argues the show endures twice over - through timeliness, capturing our technological zeitgeist as Gatsby captured the Jazz Age and Guernica the Spanish Civil War, and through timelessness, since its real subject is glacially unchanging human nature grappling with change, making 'How is technology changing us?' a perennial question.
- ch. 50 "Appendix How to Use This Book in the Classroom" *(no index entry yet)* — The editor's appendix advises instructors on teaching philosophy with Black Mirror: either watch an episode, discuss its puzzles, then read the matching chapter; or use chapters as springboards into primary literature, for example pairing Bandersnatch with van Inwagen's incompatibilism and Dennett's compatibilism on free will. Its practical heuristic - ask 'what's the technology and what's the human foible?' (the Grain magnifies Liam's paranoia, Arkangel magnifies Marie's over-parenting, Smithereens magnifies distractibility) - frames each episode as amplifying a human weakness rather than condemning technology itself.
- ch. 51 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 52 "End User License Agreement" *(front)*

## Black Panther and Philosophy - What Can Wakanda Offer the World_

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Series page" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Title page" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "vii" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Contributors One Single Tribe" *(notes)*
- ch. 8 "Introduction A Few Words from the Wakandan International Outreach Centre" *(no index entry yet)* — The editors frame the volume: Stan Lee and Jack Kirby created the first Black superhero in Fantastic Four no. 52 (1966) during the Civil Rights era, when an advanced African nation or a Black president seemed unthinkable to many Americans, and Stan's 1968 'Stan's Soapbox' column called for destroying bigotry by exposing it rather than punching it. They present Wakanda as significant but imperfect, and position the contributors' philosophical essays on racism, colonialism, feminism, spirituality, and Afrofuturism as continuing Lee and Kirby's tradition, with Coogler's 'one single tribe' message as the through-line.
- ch. 9 "Part I Yibambe!" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "1 Challenge Day Tradition and Revolution in Wakanda" *(no index entry yet)* — Boudreaux reads Wakanda's Challenge Day and Killmonger's coup through Edmund Burke's critique of the French Revolution, arguing that pure reason divorced from tradition cannot supply social cohesion: M'Baku's reverence for the ritual combat allows a peaceful transfer of power, and the ceremony itself checks arbitrary rule by making T'Challa fight stripped of Panther powers. Killmonger's disregard for custom ('Weeks? I don't need weeks') and burning of the Heart-Shaped Herb garden show revolution destroying what it claims to save, splitting Okoye's throne-loyalty against Nakia's 'I save my country,' while M'Baku sheltering T'Challa and T'Challa's incremental outreach centers embody the organic, gradual reform Burke endorsed over rationally designed reinvention.
- ch. 11 "2 Transforming Wakanda Justice (or Not ) in Black Panther" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Locke — Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Humanity as an End in Itself; Natural Rights — Bein and Lewis imagine Killmonger surviving his duel to face Wakandan justice, and sort what justice would demand into retributive justice (Kant: punishment only what the offender deserves, never using him merely as a means to public safety, though they note Kant's own racist views), reparatory justice (Locke's natural rights and what Wakanda owes N'Jadaka for T'Chaka killing N'Jobu and abandoning him in Oakland), and the restorative-to-transformative justice of Changamire's gatherings in Coates's 'A Nation Under Our Feet,' modeled on South Africa's Truth and Reconciliation Commission. They argue a restorative Wakanda would seat Killmonger with his victims and even the king at the table to repair harm rather than imprison him, and sketch a prison-abolitionist Wakanda meeting Thanos with 'Wakandan justice is more civilized.'
- ch. 12 "3 Sins of the Fathers Historical Injustice and Its Repair in lack Panther" **·** Charles W. Mills, John Rawls, Aristotle — Justice as Fairness; The Veil of Ignorance; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Almassi contrasts Rawlsian ideal theory (the veil of ignorance, justice as fairness) with Charles Mills's critique that idealization marginalizes actual historic oppression, arguing the film's characters seek Aristotle's corrective justice in a world already steeped in wrongdoing: the 1991 deaths of W'Kabi's parents at Klaue's hands and of N'Jobu at T'Chaka's. He works through the Museum of Great Britain axe scene as restitution, W'Kabi's 'you kill him where he stands' and Killmonger's 'I'mma make sure we're even' as retribution (tested against Walen's proportionality principles, with vengeance untethered from legitimate punishment), and Nakia's and T'Challa's outreach centers as Margaret Urban Walker-style relational repair, reading T'Chaka's concealment of N'Jobu's death as actively constructed ignorance in service of isolationism.
- ch. 13 "4 “What Would You Have Wakanda Do about It ” Black Panther, Global Justice, and African Philosophy" **·** Miranda Fricker — Epistemic Injustice; Cosmopolitanism — Hobden argues Wakanda's isolationist 'us vs. them' politics is Western rather than African in spirit: Ramonda's branding of Killmonger as a 'charlatan' enacts Fricker's testimonial injustice and echoes the colonial epistemicide that dismissed African knowledge, while African philosophers (Etieyibo, Eze, Menkiti) derive cosmopolitan duties from ubuntu ('I am because we are') and Igbo proverbs like 'the right hand washes the left,' which welcome the stranger as a bearer of knowledge. She finds it striking that an isiXhosa-speaking Wakanda shows no trace of ubuntu, and reads T'Challa's UN speech as a Western-style aid relationship ('what would you like us to do about it?') rather than genuine recognition of shared humanity.
- ch. 14 "5 T’Challa’s Liberalism and Killmonger’s Pan-Africanism" **·** W.E.B. Du Bois — The Ethics of Political Violence — Graves sets T'Challa's liberal internationalism — UN sovereign equality and collective security, which he traces through the organization's Charter limits and its record of preserving the status quo — against Killmonger's Pan-Africanism, the liberation lineage of the 1900 Pan-African Conference, Du Bois's Pan-African Congresses, Garvey's UNIA, and Nkrumah's 'Africa Must Unite,' which N'Jobu and Killmonger carry forward with vibranium weapons after centuries of Wakandan indifference to slavery, colonialism, and apartheid. He argues collective security has repeatedly failed oppressed Black populations, making Killmonger's realist 'Africans must save themselves' position understandable, though he questions whether arms rather than aid can liberate and calls T'Challa's single Oakland outreach center inadequate to Wakanda's wealth.
- ch. 15 "Part II Wakanda Forever!" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "6 Panther Virtue The Many Roles of T’Challa" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Practical Wisdom (phronesis); The Golden Mean; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — White applies Aristotle's virtue ethics to T'Challa's many roles — Panther Tribe chieftain, king, Avenger, ambassador, brother, husband — arguing that virtues like the honesty and courage his fellow heroes praise need practical wisdom (phronesis) to guide action when roles conflict, as when he chooses country over rescuing Storm from trial, spies on the Avengers as king, or abducts a businessman as a matter of national security. Through tragic dilemmas like destroying Wakanda's vibranium to keep it from Doctor Doom ('a king must make terrible choices'), he shows such choices haunt without dirtying character, and that over-adopting roles is itself a failure of the golden mean, evidenced by T'Challa feeling 'imprisoned' by a kingship he never chose.
- ch. 17 "7 Should Wakanda Take Over the World The Ethics of International Power" **·** Plato, John Stuart Mill, Immanuel Kant, Jean-Paul Sartre — The Philosopher King; Perpetual Peace; Utilitarianism; Cosmopolitanism — Littmann asks whether Wakanda should take over the world, staging N'Jobu's and Killmonger's 'Wakanda could rule them all... the right way' plan against the history of benevolently justified empire: Roman civilization of barbarians, Kipling's white man's burden, and Mill's trusteeship argument for educating non-Europeans toward democracy. Plato's Republic case for rule by the wisest initially favors Wakandan conquest, but Kant's Perpetual Peace condemnation of conquering 'European savages,' Mill's own four reasons imperial rule goes wrong (ignorance of local conditions, lack of sympathy, partiality, exploitation), and Diderot's claim that distance from home erodes moral restraint convince him domination breeds abuse; he endorses Nakia's aid-and-refuge outreach and Mill's hope that the 'aristocracy of nationality' will one day look as wrong as racism.
- ch. 18 "8 T’Challa, the Revolutionary King Legitimation Crises in Wakanda" **·** Jürgen Habermas, John Locke — The Social Contract — Porter uses Habermas's legitimation crisis to read Killmonger's reign: by governing flagrantly against Wakandan tradition — burning the Heart-Shaped Herb, waving off the unfinished challenge ritual with 'All that challenge shit is over with. I'm the king now' — he erodes not just his own standing but acceptance of kingship itself, moving Okoye from procedural objection to 'you are not fit to be a king.' He then argues the film inoculates monarchy against viewer critique through Shuri's permissible teasing and Wakanda's harmonizing of opposites, and shows that T'Challa's UN speech, matched line-by-line to Bossuet's defense of the divine right of kings, quietly reaches toward absolutism — leaving audiences cheering a hereditary, divinely sanctioned throne that Locke's consent of the governed would never legitimate in their own world.
- ch. 19 "9 T’Challa’s Machiavellian Methods" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli — Virtù and Fortuna; The Ends Justify the Means — Drake and Lloyd argue the comic-book T'Challa of 'Panther's Rage' is classically Machiavellian in a way the film's earnest king is not: he rules through fear and strategic violence against Killmonger's uprising, cultivates the appearance of The Prince's five qualities (mercy, faith, honesty, humanity, religion), and exploits Wakandan religion — letting subjects believe the Black Panther is divine, never doffing the sacred garb even when swimming with Monica — as an instrument for preserving power. Advisers like Taku ask whether T'Challa differs from Killmonger, and the chapter concludes that, sharing Machiavelli's low view of human nature, T'Challa never considers democracy and keeps his people quiescent through a divinity he knows is performance.
- ch. 20 "10 Understanding the Reigns of T’Challa and Killmonger through Hannah Arendt" **·** Hannah Arendt — Totalitarianism; The Ethics of Political Violence — Sinanan reads both reigns through Arendt's account of collective responsibility: T'Challa moves from conscience-based 'I am not king of all people' to owning what Wakanda did in its name ('He was the truth I chose to omit') and finally to political action in Oakland and at the UN, while Killmonger — an MIT-educated Navy SEAL embodying the collective that formed him — becomes the imperialist he opposed, his 'burn it all' revolution matching Arendt's warning about totalitarian domination from within. She compares Killmonger's council speech and herb-burning directly to Hitler's 1924 Munich trial speech, reads his manipulation of the challenge ritual as totalitarian propaganda that organizes rather than persuades, and credits Nakia, Okoye, Shuri, and Ramonda with refusing Arendt's ultimate danger, indifference.
- ch. 21 "Part III The Heart-Shaped Herb" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "11 Beastly Boys The Racial-Sexual Politics of Meat" **·** bell hooks, Val Plumwood — Intersectionality; Ecofeminism — Huerter reads M'Baku's 'I'm kidding. We are vegetarians' punchline through Carol Adams's sexual politics of meat and bell hooks's plantation patriarchy: since colonial meat-eating signified masculine mastery over nature (Plumwood's reason/nature dualism) while enslaved people were fed plant foods and cast as edible themselves, audiences primed by the racist cannibal trope expect M'Baku to be a savage, so his refusal of flesh turns the colonizer's gaze back on itself. Killmonger, by contrast, embodies the Black patriarchy produced by the American military-industrial complex ('He's one of ours'), a plantation patriarch whose hypermasculine conquest — killing Linda, burning the Herb garden, his defiant 'death was better than bondage' exit — replays colonial violence even in liberation's name, against which M'Baku's warrior-but-vegetarian politics marks a moral asymmetry.
- ch. 23 "12 Panther Mystique Wakandan Feminism Demystified" **·** Simone de Beauvoir, Betty Friedan, Judith Butler — Gender Performativity; The Feminine Mystique (the problem with no name); One Is Not Born a Woman; Woman as the Other — Wright and Pérez ask whether Wakanda's powerful women are feminists, arguing M'Baku's dismissal of Shuri as a child trades on Friedan's feminine mystique while Wakandan women escape Beauvoir's woman as the Other because their society, lacking any history of female oppression, never inscribed the second sex. Drawing on Lorde, Davis, Collins, and Walker's womanism, they read Shuri's rejection of romance, Okoye's throne-loyalty, and Nakia's stubbornness as self-definition and interdependency across difference, then use Butler's gender performativity to explain why Okoye squirms in a wig while Nakia breezes through Seoul: womanhood performed in womanist (community-centered) versus feminist (individual-transcendence) modes, with Wakanda modeling the collective call for solidarity the authors hope the future inherits.
- ch. 24 "13 The Ancestral Plane Metaphysical Mystery or Meaningful Metaphor" **·** René Descartes — Mind–Body Dualism; Immortality and the Soul — Kowalski treats the Heart-Shaped Herb's Ancestral Plane visits as a test case for Cartesian substance dualism: if T'Challa truly embraces T'Chaka's soul, the film commits to bodies and souls as distinct substances, with the film's own analogy — Shuri remotely driving T'Challa's car from her lab — standing in for soul-body interactionism. But Princess Elisabeth's interaction problem (how a non-physical soul could be embraced, seen, or chemically affected) makes dualism implausible, so he argues the second and third visits work better as metaphor: Killmonger's audience with N'Jobu and T'Challa's 'You were wrong! All of you!' confrontation with his ancestors dramatize taking stock of how one's father shaped who one is, with T'Challa's sunlit third visit symbolizing his choice not to be bound by that past.
- ch. 25 "14 The Afterlife of Erik Killmonger in African Philosophy" **·** René Descartes — Personal Identity; Mind–Body Dualism; Immortality and the Soul — Paul Dottin argues that Black Panther lore inherited the racist 'Hamitic Hypothesis' (crediting Egypt rather than sub-Saharan Africa for Wakanda's civilization) and that Ta-Nehisi Coates and Ryan Coogler begin a Wiredu-style conceptual decolonization of it. Taking Killmonger's dying wish to be buried with the ancestors who jumped from slave ships literally, the chapter asks which African metaphysics could make his postmortem survival of substance, subjectivity, and setting rationally intelligible. It rejects Cartesian substance dualism (illustrated by T'Challa defeating the demon Mephisto only after trapping his spirit in a body) in favor of Wiredu's Akan quasi-physicalism and Adeofe's Yoruba theory of destiny (ipin) and inner head (ori-inu), then ranks Killmonger's afterlife options from limbo through the Ancestral Plane to deification as the ocean deity Olokun.
- ch. 26 "15 Wakandan Resources The Epistemological Reality of Black Panther’s Fiction" **·** Miranda Fricker, bell hooks — Epistemic Injustice — Ruby Komic treats Black Panther as an 'epistemic resource' in Gaile Pohlhaus's sense: because of its majority-Black creative team the film supplies audiences with narrative tools that answer to real Black experience, against Anthony Mackie's charge that the film's Black-only hiring is itself racist. The chapter reads the film's plural representations - the T'Challa/Killmonger ideological tension, Nakia's 'No, I save my country' defiance of Okoye's institutional loyalty, Okoye throwing away her wig, Black viewers rooting for the 'villain' - as the kind of nuanced material that remedies decades of stereotypical representation. Following Miranda Fricker, it concludes that the historic scarcity of such resources is an epistemic injustice against Black knowers, and that Black Panther is a partial act of epistemic justice.
- ch. 27 "Part IV Vibranium" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "16 When Tech Meets Tradition How Wakandan Technology Transcends Anti-Blackness" **·** Nick Bostrom — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Timothy Brown argues that transhumanist futures - Bostrom's fable of the Dragon Tyrant, Savulescu's moral bioenhancement, AI built by homogeneous teams - exclude Black people because technologies embed anti-Blackness, from Ruha Benjamin's New Jim Code to facial recognition failing Black women. Black Panther counters this by rooting its future in traumatic memory: Killmonger's museum scene shows how whitewashed 'aesthetic Africanism' licenses incredulity about African technological achievement, while Shuri's comics arc (named 'Ancient Future' by her ancestors in D'Jalia) shows that Wakandan technology flourishes only through the traditions it is tempted to scoff at. Africanfuturism, on this reading, treats Blackness itself as a 'prosthesis' for stress-testing whether the futures we build will include everyone.
- ch. 29 "17 Vibranium Dreams and Afrofuturist Visions Technology, Nature, and Culture" **·** Utopia and Dystopia; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Alessio Gerola reads vibranium as an instrumentalist prop: the film treats it as a neutral means whose value depends only on the ends served, staged through three disputes - M'Baku's tradition versus Shuri's innovation, Nakia's humanitarian aid versus Killmonger's armed revolution, T'Chaka's isolationism versus T'Challa's openness. Drawing on Langdon Winner's politics of artifacts, the chapter argues that real technology and culture co-shape each other, so vibranium alone cannot explain why Wakanda stayed peaceful instead of becoming the empire Killmonger promises. The film's genuine lesson is Afrofuturist: the harmonious relation among technology, nature, and culture visible in biomimetic ships, kimoyo beads, traditional aesthetics, and the Jabari's principled refusal of vibranium.
- ch. 30 "18 Black Panther’s Afrofuturism Reconnecting Neural and Cultural Pathways" *(no index entry yet)* — Traces Afrofuturism from Flying African folklore through Delany, Butler, and Okorafor, then reads three motifs of the film as figures of diasporic disconnection and reconnection: T’Challa’s Royal Talon Flyer reimagines the Flying African tale, Shuri’s healing of Agent Ross’s spinal cord injury (contrasted with Rhodey’s fragile Stark exoskeleton in Civil War and Endgame) models rebuilding severed connections, and water marks the divide — the quelled falls of ritual combat versus the oceanic erasure where Killmonger chooses burial. It argues Wakanda’s blend of ancient ritual and futuristic technology shows a tradition’s essence surviving modernization, and closes with Nnedi Okorafor — paralyzed by scoliosis, walking again, and writing Shuri as a literal Flying African — as the “singularity” where the chapter’s themes converge. Leaves slugs empty because Afrofuturism is a literary-cultural genre, not an entry in the taxonomy (logged as a hole under ch29).
- ch. 31 "19 Wakanda and the Dilemma of Racial Utopianism" **·** Utopia and Dystopia; Cosmopolitanism — Juan Floyd-Thomas defends Wakanda as 'racial utopianism' - an Afrofuturist promised land answering Toni Morrison's point that utopias are defined by who is kept out - while pressing its dilemma: the utopia flourishes only through isolation from the diaspora it could uplift, which is the root of Killmonger's grievance. Killmonger embodies the counter-vision, a Paul Gilroy 'Black Atlantic' landless nationalism whose plan to arm the world's oppressed and let 'the sun never set on the Wakandan Empire' turns the utopia into a dystopian empire rooted in vengeance rather than equity. The chapter reads T'Challa's UN speech against Trump's 'shithole countries' remark and Terry Gilliam's dismissal, as a case for a liberal world order of global solidarity over walls.
- ch. 32 "20 The Value of Vibranium" **·** Karl Marx, Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham — Commodity Fetishism; Utilitarianism; The Categorical Imperative — Edwardo Pérez asks what makes vibranium 'the most valuable metal known to man' through Marx's distinction between use value and exchange value: Klaue prizes exchange value (money, power, a black-market arm), while Wakandans structure their whole society around use value and never trade the metal. The museum scene raises cultural property - does vibranium belong to Wakanda, as the cultural nationalist holds, or to humanity, as the cultural internationalist holds? - and T'Challa's shift on sharing it is analyzed through Kant's categorical imperative versus Bentham's utilitarian calculus, with his saving of Agent Ross and sparing of M'Baku vindicated by their consequences. Vibranium's greatest value, the chapter concludes, is atonement: redeeming T'Chaka's crime and uniting humanity as 'one single tribe.'
- ch. 33 "Part V Black Lives Matter" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "21 Dismantling the Master’s House with the Master’s Tools" **·** bell hooks — Feminism; The Ethics of Political Violence — Thanayi Jackson reads Killmonger through Audre Lorde's 'the master's tools will never dismantle the master's house,' asking whether a CIA-trained operative wielding the colonizer's own weapons can end colonialism. Comparing Killmonger to Wallace from The Wire - both orphans of Africa reared by America's ghettos - she concedes the appeal of his Oakland-forged mastery ('I know how colonizers think') but argues through bell hooks's gloss of Lorde that one cannot destroy imperialist white-supremacist capitalist patriarchy by creating one's own version of it: Killmonger's plan to kill oppressors 'and their children' makes him a new master, not a liberator. T'Challa's Oakland outreach center answers the challenge only partially, leaving the chapter asking whether the master's house was dismantled or merely refurbished.
- ch. 35 "22 An Impossible Return (Anti)Colonialism in of Black Panther" **·** Frantz Fanon — Black Skin, White Masks; The Ethics of Political Violence — Julio Covarrubias-Cabeza argues that Black Panther is a supremely colonial film: its narrative coherence depends on the history of slavery, franchise colonialism, and neocolonial domination, yet it buries that history in the pathologized figure of Killmonger. Drawing on Fanon's account of the European unconscious projecting its base instincts onto Black bodies, on Tommy Curry's 'man-not' and anti-Black misandry, and on Quijano's coloniality of power, the chapter contends the film devalues Black American men and discredits radical anti-colonial politics while letting Wakanda ally with the CIA against its own. Killmonger's 'impossible return' to a pre-colonial homeland - his body scarred by each kill - is the site where the film's repressed colonial violence ruptures to the surface.
- ch. 36 "23 T’Challa’s Dream and Killmonger’s Means Echoes of MLK and Malcolm X" **·** The Ethics of Political Violence — Gerald Browning maps T'Challa onto Martin Luther King Jr. and Killmonger onto Malcolm X: T'Challa's UN speech about building bridges 'as if we were one single tribe' echoes 'I Have a Dream,' while Killmonger's vibranium insurrection enacts Malcolm's language-of-the-shotgun rhetoric and 'by any means necessary' motto. The comparison is deliberately qualified: Killmonger lacks Malcolm's 'intelligently directed' extremism and his post-Mecca evolution toward a universal brotherhood, never reconsidering even when T'Challa offers to heal him, and he turns his aggression on Wakandans themselves in a way Malcolm never did. The chapter concludes that the T'Challa-Killmonger foil extends the King-Malcolm conversation into the Black Lives Matter era.
- ch. 37 "24 “It’s Time They Knew the Truth about Us! We’re Warriors!” Black Panther and the Black Panther Party" **·** The Ethics of Political Violence — Karen Kohoutek places N'Jobu's and Killmonger's radicalization in the lineage of the Black Panther Party, founded in Oakland months after the comic debuted in 1966: both cite the same police brutality and impoverished communities, and both answer with arming the oppressed. Through Robert F. Williams's Negroes with Guns and Max Weber's state monopoly on legitimate violence, the chapter shows how Black armed self-defense keeps getting recoded as aggression - from the Panthers' patrol of Oakland to the Mulford Act passed to disarm them - so that Killmonger's plan reads as genocidal while CIA destabilization, his actual training, passes as legitimate. Wakanda's closing pivot to outreach centers mirrors the Party's Free Breakfast and health-clinic programs, making T'Challa's UN speech an imagined corrective to failed American idealism.
- ch. 38 "25 Fear of a Black Museum Black Existentialism" **·** W.E.B. Du Bois, G.W.F. Hegel, Plato — Double Consciousness; Allegory of the Cave; Struggle for Recognition — Charles Peterson reads the museum scene through Black existentialism and Africana phenomenology: colonial museums freeze African existence in an 'archaic amber' of aesthetic purity, and Killmonger's reclaiming of the mask and questioning of the curator perform a truth-telling that breaks the museum's performance of dominance. Du Bois's double-consciousness explains Killmonger's inseparable Wakandan-and-American two-ness (extended to Nakia and the War Dogs), while Paget Henry's contrast of the DuBoisian subject who oscillates between two 'We's' with Hegel's recognition-seeking subject explains why T'Chaka chooses Wakanda and abandons N'Jobu, and Plato's cave frames Killmonger as dragging Wakanda's comfortable council toward truths 'some truths are too much to bear.' The chapter concludes that Killmonger fails because wearing the mask he embraced negrification rather than shedding it, whereas T'Challa matures from a Hegelian into a DuBoisian subject by opening Wakanda to the world.
- ch. 39 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 40 "End User License Agreement" *(front)*

## Black Sabbath and Philosophy - Mastering Reality

- ch. 1 "Globalization and Contemporary Art" *(front)*
- ch. 2 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — William Irwin's editor introduction tells how a tape of "N.I.B." stopping at "My name is Lucifer, please take my hand" hooked him at thirteen, and argues Sabbath deserve serious philosophy: their riffs and lyrics confront existential despair, war, drugs, and the nature of evil in shades of gray rather than Satanism. It frames the book's guiding questions (what makes Sabbath sound evil, is evil in the ear of the beholder, is it still Sabbath without Ozzy) and promises that philosophers can deepen our listening to Ozzy, Tony, Geezer, and Bill without recovering authorial intent.
- ch. 7 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 8 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Albert Camus, Friedrich Nietzsche — The Absurd; Bad Faith; The Death of God; The Myth of Sisyphus — William Irwin reads the Ozzy-era catalog as unwitting British existentialism: "Johnny Blade", "Paranoid", and "Wheels of Confusion" voice Camus's absurd, the mismatch between our desires and a world where life has no meaning and "hell is other people" explains why Iron Man, saved yet treated as a mindless thing, plans vengeance. The chapter argues the band moves from despair toward freedom and self-creation: "Supernaut" and "Under the Sun" reject religion in the spirit of Nietzsche's death of God, "Cornucopia" depicts bad-faith conformity to plastic suburban lives, and "Children of the Grave" plus Sabbath's own music-making exemplify the claim that artistic creativity is the proper response to pain, with the fan invited to sculpt himself into an authentic individual Sisyphus-style.
- ch. 10 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Bertrand Russell — The Problem of Evil — Liz Stillwaggon Swan surveys Sabbath's pictures of moral evil (nuclear annihilation in "Electric Funeral", heroin's "deadly pin" in "Hand of Doom", social oppression allegorized as the Satan of "Black Sabbath") and argues the band poses the problem of evil without solving it theologically. Against the view that invoking evil presupposes God, she aligns Sabbath with Augustine's rejection of Manichaean evil-as-a-force, reading evil as a privation traceable to free human choices, and with Bertrand Russell's "A Free Man's Worship": "Children of the Grave", "Into the Void", "Spiral Architect", and "Symptom of the Universe" urge that we defeat evil by imagining and building a better possible reality through love, vegan-pacifist Geezer Butler being the living proof behind the stage persona.
- ch. 11 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Brian Froese argues Black Sabbath's occult imagery expresses an eschatological imagination forged in the rubble of working-class, postwar Birmingham as a rebuke to hippie utopianism. Songs like "Black Sabbath", "War Pigs" (originally "Walpurgis"), and "Electric Funeral" borrow the literary devices of the Book of Revelation, Satan, final judgment, cosmic journeys, as what Elaine Pagels calls war literature for a Cold War and Vietnam world, while "Iron Man" transvalues the Christian savior into a resurrected anti-hero and "After Forever" and "Lord of this World" both embrace and resent the faith. He concludes the band built a sacralized, contradictory, fatalistic cosmology in which "the chosen one" cannot escape his fate, reworking Christian apocalypse for the industrial marginalized.
- ch. 12 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer — Joel McIver, drawing on years of interviewing the band, argues the sinister sound is psychoacoustics rather than Satan: Iommi's lower tuning adopted after the sheet-metal press took his fingertips, the tritone or diabolus in musica that anchors "Black Sabbath", horror-film tremolo, minor keys, Ozzy's untrained monotone, and Bill Ward's slightly dragged, jazzy beats, artistic imperfections that keep the music lifelike. Against Schopenhauer's view that music directly expresses universal emotions, so the tritone would naturally sound like discord to anyone, McIver contends the devil's interval is a Western convention that only became inseparable from evil once Sabbath fused it with Geezer's occult lyrics and nightmare cover art.
- ch. 14 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer, Pythagoras — The World as Will; Philosophical Pessimism; Pythagorean Harmony of the Spheres — James Heathers asks why the most improbable band became the one true originator of heavy metal and answers with Schopenhauer: industrial Aston, Birmingham was a monument to the ceaseless, unsatisfied striving of the Will, and music, which maps directly onto the Will itself, gave that world its voice. The tritone's numerically hideous ratios, a point traced through Rameau back to Pythagoras, make it the natural interval of morbidity, which is why "Black Sabbath" and everything from Liszt's Dante Sonata to death metal is marinated in it, and why doom was not a predictable development like Deep Purple or Led Zeppelin. He closes on the irony that Schopenhauer, who preached ascetic denial of the Will, would have loathed a band that answered factory-town despair by smothering the Will in lager, hash, cocaine, and women, only to breed more want in the worst of all possible worlds.
- ch. 15 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Maurice Merleau-Ponty, Jean-Paul Sartre, René Descartes — Embodiment (the lived body); Radical Freedom and Responsibility; Mind–Body Dualism — Ken Pepper uses Merleau-Ponty's Phenomenology of Perception to explain how Tony Iommi's homemade plastic fingertips work: like the blind man's cane, they are incorporated into his body schema so that he perceives the fretboard through them, refuting the Cartesian "objective body" picture on which playing would be inner plans causing finger movements. On creative freedom, the chapter stages Merleau-Ponty against Sartre's radical freedom, Iommi did choose to relearn guitar after hearing two-fingered Django Reinhardt, and his restricted two-finger chords, lighter strings, and drop tunings birthed heavy metal, but Sartre is wrong that attitude alone transcends disability, since some possibilities (playing at all without the tips) were simply not live. Only Iommi, taking up the possibilities his body-in-his-situation afforded, could have invented metal.
- ch. 16 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Aristotle — The Golden Mean — Greg Littmann imagines Aristotle auditioning for Black Sabbath in 1970 to argue the band satisfies the Poetics and Politics: music that gives pleasure, even the "perverted modes" working-class audiences enjoy, is legitimate art, and Sabbath's darkness works as catharsis, purging fear and pity as Greek tragedy did through Oedipus, the doomed figure in black of "Black Sabbath", the betrayed and ignored Iron Man, and the hopeless narrator of "Paranoid". Songs like "War Pigs", "Electric Funeral", and "Children of the Grave" also do what Aristotle says poetry does better than history, imagining alternative futures that spur citizens to political thought, while the band's drug lore maps onto the Aristotelian mean: beer and a little weed yes, heroin and cocaine never, and Ozzy's firing shows the price of intemperance.
- ch. 18 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Aristotle — Language Games — Søren Frimodt-Møller argues that heavy metal cannot be defined by necessary and sufficient conditions, down-tuning, distortion, growls, and double-kick each fail as criteria, and that genres instead work like Wittgenstein's family-resemblance concepts, learned by actual cases the way words are learned in language games. The family of metal begins with Black Sabbath as prototype: he traces doom to "Black Sabbath" and "War Pigs", thrash to "Paranoid" and "Symptom of the Universe" (compare "Master of Puppets"), progressive and power metal to the Dio-era records that influenced Iron Maiden, and grunge to Soundgarden and Alice in Chains, while standards of heaviness shift historically so that former metal like stoner rock now counts as plain rock.
- ch. 19 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Plato, Daniel Dennett — Manuel Bremer and Daniel Cohnitz run "St. Vitus Dance" and Sabbath's doom through the failure-prone history of definitions of art: Plato's imitation requirement cannot cover the Technical Ecstasy robots or Pollock, Tolstoy's expression theory falters because Iommi's sampled cough on "Sweet Leaf" was not felt emotion, and the institutional theory merely says art is what the artworld accepts. They adopt Levinson's historical definition, an artwork is intended to be regarded as past artworks are regarded, and argue via Dennett's "forced moves" that, unlike relativity theory, Sabbath's 1968 leap in artistic space was radically unpredictable yet became the objective standard of doom that Saint Vitus and Sunn O))) later extended.
- ch. 20 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Apollonian and the Dionysian — Dennis Knepp finds Nietzsche's Birth of Tragedy gods at the birth of heavy metal: the Dionysian is the intoxication Nietzsche says makes art possible, realized in Sabbath's Hamburg residencies, Bill Ward's banana peels, the cough that opens "Sweet Leaf", and the mosh-pit loss of self that cures loneliness through communal revel, while the Apollonian is the god of terrifying dreams and visions seen in Geezer Butler's black apparition at the foot of his bed, the origin of "Black Sabbath", and Iommi's ghosts and angels. He argues Sabbath only looks Satanic because Christianity turned the satyrs and old gods into devils, and he rewrites Nietzsche's aphorisms Sabbath-style ("Iron Man is dead, and we have killed him") to drive the identification home.
- ch. 21 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Plutarch, Thomas Hobbes — Ship of Theseus; Personal Identity — James Bondarchuk tests purist principles about which line-ups are really Black Sabbath with thought experiments: the Ship of Theseus, from Plutarch plus Hobbes's reassembled planks, fits poorly because Geezer Butler is no plank, musicians contribute irreplaceable style, and "original members only" absurdly entails a Bieber piano-cover band would still be Sabbath, while the legal-ownership reading collapses once fans admit Bieber buying the name changes nothing. He concludes the whole "is it still Sabbath without Ozzy?" debate is not a metaphysical question with a fact of the matter but disguised aesthetic judgment, he remains a purist because Bill Ward's brooding drumming on "Black Sabbath" and "Hand of Doom" are integral to the Sabbath Sound, while conceding no objective answer exists.
- ch. 23 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Personal Identity — Wesley Cray distinguishes fragile line-ups from bands that persist through them and proposes three criteria for a band's identity over time, continuity (successive line-ups share at least one member, chaining across gaps), intention (the line-up directs its intentions toward being that band), and uniqueness (only one line-up per band at a time, as the Gorgoroth lawsuit shows). Applying them, Tony Iommi is Sabbath's only essential member since "Ozzy was fired" makes sense while "Tony was fired" would not, so most post-Ozzy line-ups really were Black Sabbath, though Seventh Star, Born Again, and Cross Purposes fail the intention criterion, and the 2006 Heaven and Hell reunion was Sabbath continuing under another name.
- ch. 24 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself — Mark D. White shifts the is-it-still-Sabbath question from metaphysics to ethics, asking whether bands owe fans a stable line-up when they keep the name. Kant's Formula of Universal Law permits the maxim "change members, keep the name" without contradiction, and the Formula of Respect forbids only coercion or deceit, using fans as mere means, so the real wrong is deceptive marketing, as when record-company pressure billed Iommi's solo project Seventh Star as "Black Sabbath featuring Tony Iommi" and sent it out playing "War Pigs" and "Iron Man". The Dio reunion's honest rebranding as Heaven & Hell passes the test, while fans, symmetrically, owe bands no loyalty and may legitimately love only one era.
- ch. 25 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Karl Marx — Historical Materialism; Ideology and False Consciousness — Erich Christiansen reads the supernatural imagery of the first two albums as the worldview of powerless working-class Birmingham men, applying Marx's claims that social being determines consciousness, that capitalists are "privileged and idle gods" over the worker, and that religion is simultaneously an expression of real suffering and its opiate. On this reading the figure in black of "Black Sabbath" personifies foremen and unseen bosses looming like factory smoke, "The Wizard" and the Lucifer-seducer of "N.I.B." answer the desperate worker's need for hands-on power when prayer fails, and "War Pigs"' Day of Judgment for the sorcerer-generals is itself the opium fantasy of workers who cannot deliver justice themselves, with "Wicked World" naming the whole system.
- ch. 27 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** René Descartes — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); Philosophical Skepticism — Kevin McCain turns Ozzy's "Am I Going Insane?" into a lived version of Descartes' skeptical scenarios, the madman who cannot trust his senses, the dream argument, the deceiving demon, and asks whether Ozzy can know anything at all. Using inference to the best explanation, he argues the commonsense external-world view beats skeptical scenarios because it predicts experiences (drop the book on your toe) and raises fewer unanswerable questions, so Ozzy keeps knowledge in the no-answer and "no" cases, but if he has reason to think he is insane and everyone tells him "yes", his reasons are defeated and he cannot even know their answers, leaving him, like the rest of us, one testimonial crisis from the void.
- ch. 28 "Black sabbath & philosophy" **·** Michael Walzer — Just War Theory; The Ethics of Political Violence — Jacob Held uses "War Pigs", "Electric Funeral", and "Children of the Grave" to teach just war theory's two halves, jus ad bellum (just cause, last resort, proportion) and jus in bello (proportion and discrimination toward non-combatants), reading Sabbath as condemning unjust wars like Vietnam and atomic overkill rather than all force, with Ozzy's "Thank God for the Bomb" voicing deterrence. Citing Walzer on "even in hell, it is possible to be more or less humane", he argues pacifism functions as a vital moral check, and that the war on terror fails just war standards outright: it cannot be won, so any means, assassination, torture, reclassifying terrorists outside both soldier and criminal law, are disproportionate and morally bankrupt, exactly the war-pig behavior the song's judgment day condemns.
- ch. 29 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Robert Arp dismantles the caveman stereotype of metalheads, stupid, simplistic misogynists who club women, by identifying it as the fallacy of hasty generalization, while conceding kernels of truth: groupie lines in Bel Air, studies linking metal to reckless behavior, and male sexual predation grounded (he suggests) in evolutionary psychology. Against the "numskull" charge he points to Geezer's clever anti-war lyrics in "War Pigs", Sabbath's complex song structures, the Wagner connection via Iommi's tritone, and classical-trained shredders, notes Sabbath's songs about women mourn loss ("Solitude", "Changes") rather than boast of conquest, and closes with Rob Halford's point that metal relieves the pain of dysfunctional homes rather than causing it.
- ch. 30 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 31 "Black sabbath & philosophy" *(notes)*

## Blade Runner 2049 and Philosophy - This Breaks the World

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Blade Runner 2049 and Philosophy This Breaks the World" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Blade Runner 2049 and Philosophy This Breaks the World" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Thanks" *(notes)*
- ch. 8 "Philosophy and Reality in Blade Runner 2049" **·** Jean Baudrillard — Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal) — Robin Bunce's introduction shows that the word 'real' is used in at least six different senses in 2049: Joshi's authentic, K's genuine (unimplanted) memories, Joi's natural-born 'real boy', Stelline's naturalistic 'real human responses', Mariette's physical, and Deckard's true. It argues the film makes reality an essentially contested concept, then maps how each contributor deploys a different sense of 'real', from Baudrillard's simulacra in the chapter on women and birth to socialist realism in the Wallace chapter.
- ch. 9 "I What Makes Us Human" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "1. Do You Long for Having Your Heart Interlinked" **·** John Locke, Plato, Immanuel Kant — Philosophy of Love; Free Will vs Determinism; Personal Identity (memory theory) — Timothy Shanahan asks whether K and Joi's relationship counts as romance, sex, and love, using Locke's definition of a person as a thinking, reflective being to ask whether a programmed AI can love, and weighing Joi's programming against the determinism that may bind human lovers too. He reads the Joi-Mariette-K sex scene through Plato's Symposium myth of severed halves seeking union, substance dualism, and a Kant-inspired 'controlled hallucination' view of perception, concluding all sex is a person-to-person conscious connection through bodily intermediaries. Deckard's rejection of Wallace's replica Rachael is explained with a Mona Lisa forgery analogy: an indistinguishable copy still lacks the irreplaceable original person. The baseline question 'Do you long for having your heart interlinked?' frames love as the longing of lonely cells of awareness to connect.
- ch. 11 "2. Is Joi a Person" **·** John Locke, John Searle, René Descartes — Personal Identity (memory theory); The Chinese Room; Mind–Body Dualism — Chris Lay argues Joi is a person. He raises Searle's objection that programs only simulate understanding and need the right biological 'stuff', then answers it: Joi apparently violates her 'everything you want her to be' programming, so her affection for K is authentic either way. Testing Joi against Locke's personhood criteria — rationality, sentience, and self-consciousness grounded in memory and in her self-sacrifice before Luv — he adds a Cartesian reading in which her transfers between console and emanator make her a disembodied soul, closer to Descartes's essence of the human than embodied replicants are. If a digital person like Joi would also fail the baseline, the wall separating kind would need a full societal reset.
- ch. 12 "3. Are You for Real" **·** Being-Toward-Death; The Meaning of Life — Aron Ericson reads being 'real' in 2049 as being special: having an existence that is your own. Borrowing Stephen Mulhall's reading of the first film — life gets its meaning in relation to death, so what matters is burning brightly, like Tyrell's 'he who burns twice as brightly burns half as long' — he argues Joi becomes real by deleting her backup to live unguarded, 'like a real girl', and Deckard's 'I know what's real' names the responsibility and love for the daughter he has never met. K's arc, from Sapper's 'you've never seen a miracle' through Freysa's miracle birth to his mortal rescue of Deckard, shows realness is adopted through what one loves and dies for rather than conferred by origin.
- ch. 13 "4. The Aesthetics of Being a Person" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle, Plato — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — S. Evan Kreider argues that aesthetic capacity evidences personhood in replicants: Kant's beautiful and the mathematically and dynamically sublime explain both K's cowed passage through Los Angeles and his storm-soaked defeat of Luv through strength of will and moral conviction. After Plato's and Aristotle's arousal and catharsis theories of art, he turns to Croce, Collingwood, and Suzanne Langer's expression theory and the 'virtual', reading Sapper's home-grown garlic, Joi's holographic dinner and rain scene, the Mariette-proxy sex as expressive symbol, and Stelline's memory-making ('we recall with our feelings') as aesthetic-emotional communication only persons can sustain. Even K's baseline judges personhood by the aesthetics of how he responds, not what he says.
- ch. 14 "5. A Replicant’s Guide to Becoming Human" **·** Seneca — Stoic Cosmopolitanism (citizens of the world); Stoicism — Justin Kitchen uses the Stoic 'cradle argument' — Chrysippus on self-preservation, echoed in Wallace's 'the first thought tends to fear, to preserve the clay' — to deny that replicant slavery is natural, noting Seneca's point that rage like Batty's derails reason. Mapping the Stoic process of oikeiosis through Hierocles's concentric circles onto the films, he traces four stages: the Nexus-6's fight for survival, Rachael identifying with Deckard in love, the Nexus-8s' revolutionary community under Freysa, and finally K, who disobeys Freysa's order and sacrifices himself for Deckard as a Stoic cosmopolitan, a fellow citizen of the world. Replicants can become human because they develop through the same expanding circles of concern.
- ch. 15 "6. You Don’t Need to Be Human to Have a Human Hand" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer, Aristotle, Hannah Arendt, René Descartes — The World as Will; Embodiment (the lived body) — Sue Zemka contrasts the film's opening eye — reason and consciousness, in a tradition Arendt summarizes as thinking figured as seeing — with its closing image of Deckard's hand reaching toward Ana's through glass, the sense of touch leading out toward others. Schopenhauer explains K's wondering gaze at his own hands in the snow as the human predicament of being both will and representation, subject and object at once, while Condillac's and Maine de Biran's statue thought experiments ('I feel, therefore I am' against Descartes's cogito) are charged with imagining humanness in isolated figures. The film's answer is relational and political: humanness is made in relationships and resistance, from the Joi-Mariette hand-synced sex scene to the final father-daughter touch.
- ch. 16 "7. More Human than Humans" **·** Personal Identity — Rob O'Connor reads replicants through N. Katherine Hayles's posthumanism — persons as amalgams of biological and informational-technological systems — arguing Rachael's procreation shows the posthuman body bypassing its designed limits, while Joi is a posthuman of the opposite construction, a hologram transcending her limits through emotion. Memory and experience, even implanted ones (Batty's 'tears in rain', Deckard's unicorn dream, K's wooden horse, 'all the best memories are hers'), supply the existential context that defines the posthuman self. K's epiphany before the giant Joi advertisement reveals he is a tool of the capitalist system, and his choice to die for the right cause is how he takes charge of his own identity.
- ch. 17 "8. Ode to Joi" **·** The Buddha, Nagarjuna — Non-Self (anatman); Karma and Rebirth; Emptiness (sunyata) — Steve Bein first tests Joi against Mary Anne Warren's five functionalist personhood criteria — consciousness, reason, self-motivated activity, communication, self-awareness — and finds she passes, which makes Luv's stomping of the emanator a murder. The Buddha's no-self doctrine then denies that anyone, Joi included, is an enduring person: we are bundles of fleeting psychophysical processes, 'like tears in rain', which threatens to let Luv plead that the Luv who killed Joi no longer exists. Nagarjuna's two truths resolve the courtroom problem: ultimately there are no persons, but conventionally 'Luv killed Joi' is perfectly true, and the karma ripples through the whole network.
- ch. 18 "II A Miracle Delivered" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "9. Replicant Birth, Moral Miscarriage" **·** Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham — Anthropocentrism; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — L. Brooke Rudow argues 2049's 'biological turn' — the miracle of Rachael giving birth — quietly reverses the first film's ethical innovation that artificial beings matter whether or not they are natural. She assembles the classic grounds for replicant moral status: Kant on cruelty to animals breeding cruelty to humans, Bentham's 'can they suffer?', and the rationality criterion of Aristotle, Descartes, and Kant. Because the resistance and the plot now treat the born child as the only proof replicants are persons, she contends the sequel becomes anthropocentric where its predecessor was liberating, shutting the door it had opened for animals, machines, and anything that thinks and feels.
- ch. 20 "10. Should Humans Dream of Designer Babies" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Samantha Noll and Laci Hubbard-Mattix treat replicants as designer babies and grade the Wallace Corporation against the Belmont Report's four principles of biomedical ethics: autonomy, beneficence, non-maleficence, and justice. Wallace's killing of the 'barren' newborn and Luv's advice not to 'waste' money on attachment or intelligence violate non-maleficence and beneficence; replicants' engineered lack of choice ('I wasn't aware that was an option, Madam') violates autonomy; and Joshi's wall that 'separates kind' institutionalizes distributive injustice, with replicants bearing all society's risk for none of its reward. The film serves as a cautionary foil for our own approaching genetic-engineering choices.
- ch. 21 "11. Conceiving AI" **·** The Turing Test — Beth Singler reads 2049 as a creation story, tracing the born/made tension from Frankenstein and Pinocchio through Genesis: Wallace speaks in Abrahamic language of angels, Eden, and 'God remembered Rachael', yet fails where Tyrell succeeded, his blades slipping into the newborns he calls barren. She widens the lens to the 'concept of the child' — Ariès's socially constructed childhood, chosen children from Joseph and Jesus to the Indigo Children, and the orphanage where Mister Cotton molds children 'worth buying' — to show the mystery child reopening what children are for. The baseline's 'interlinked', she argues, names the healthy separateness-with-connection Deckard models by becoming a stranger to protect his daughter; the Voight-Kampff is described as a Turing test for this neon world.
- ch. 22 "III Cells Interlinked" *(front)*
- ch. 23 "12. The Riddle of Niander Wallace" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Four contributors unravel Wallace: his name honors Alfred Russel Wallace of speciation and the 'Wallace effect', while 'Niander' echoes Neanderthal — the 'new man' he is trying to breed through artificial speciation. He is read through the Old Testament God whose angels do the dirty work, the Tower of Babel, and the devil sweetly tempting Christ, and his technobabble-free, poetic speech is explained by Wittgenstein's Tractatus picture theory: his ideas are so far beyond us that language can only paint possibilities like 'storm Eden and retake her'. Joi's Prokofiev chime finally links him to Socialist Realism's 'engineers of the human soul' — Tyrell engineered bodies, Wallace engineers souls — making him a false god who saves without redeeming.
- ch. 24 "13. Flow My Tears, Rick Deckard Said" **·** Jeremy Bentham — Speciesism — M. Blake Wilson, a criminal-justice scholar, reads K as the latest of Philip K. Dick's humanized policemen, whose grief and empathy — beginning with Rachael's corpse and peaking when he believes he is the child he was ordered to kill — qualify him for the moral community. Applying Bentham's 'can they suffer?' test, he notes Dick's novelistic androids fail it while the films' replicants pass, so the movies make empathy, not birth or reason, the entrance criterion. He then presses the allegory: 'skinjob' slurs, shoot-on-sight orders, slave patrols and fugitive-slave laws, and the Replicant Freedom Movement as a replicant Black Lives Matter fighting police violence and the 'new Jim Crow' of constructed illegality.
- ch. 25 "14. After the Blackout" **·** Alasdair MacIntyre, The Buddha, Immanuel Kant — Virtues, Practices and Traditions; Non-Self (anatman); The Death of God; Impermanence (anicca) — Martin Muchall treats the offscreen Blackout as an emblem of the moral catastrophe diagnosed in MacIntyre's After Virtue: our moral vocabulary has shattered into emotivist hissing and clapping, with Roy's murder of Tyrell, his 'God of biomechanics', staging the death of God and the 'everything is permitted' that follows. Utilitarian pleasure and Kantian cold reason (Spock, who must tell the truth to the murderer at the door) have both failed, leaving neoliberal hypercapitalism — corporate psychopathy, the orphanage sweatshop, the Baseline as workplace performance review — as the real villain. Against this, K's ending models the Buddha: everything is impermanent, there is no self to defend, and his selfless, empathetic death points to a morality grounded in evolved and cultivable altruism.
- ch. 26 "15. The Phenomenology of Replicant Life and Death" **·** Maurice Merleau-Ponty — Phenomenology; Embodiment (the lived body) — Zachary Sheldon analyzes 2049's style of violence through Merleau-Ponty's account of film as perceived conduct that shows consciousness thrown into the world, plus the defamiliarizing power of camera choices. In the killings of Sapper and Joshi and the underwater K-Luv fight, the fatal blows are elided, glimpsed only in reaction shots, or distanced through high angles and blurred bodies, so the audience's embodied, empathetic response makes a replicant's death feel exactly as real as a human's. If we feel Sapper die as we would feel a man die, the film's stylistic answer goes, the divide between kinds was never really there; K dies on the stairs because he has truly lived.
- ch. 27 "16.
