Who Am I to You" **·** Immanuel Kant, Nel Noddings, Carol Gilligan — Caring as the Ground of Ethics; Autonomy of the Will; The Categorical Imperative — James Okapal sets up the Kantian view — moral agency, shown by passing the contradiction-in-conception test (which Wallace's 'all should be masters of slaves' fails), is the precondition of moral status, so non-agents like replicants, on Tibor Machan's extension of Kant to animals, matter no more than toasters — against the ethics of care of Nel Noddings and Carol Gilligan. On the care reading, K's and Batty's final acts of ethical caring, like Joi's risky choices for K, confer moral status on another and thereby constitute moral agency, reversing the Kantian order. Empathy, not universalizable reason, is what removes bricks from Joshi's wall.
- ch. 28 "IV The Kingdom of God" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "17. You’ve Never Seen a Miracle" **·** Paradigm Shifts — Andrew Kuzma reads the film's religious texture from Sapper's Graham Greene novel to Wallace as a Gnostic Demiurge — a lesser creator-god — whose 'angels', like biblical ones, possess the free will to rebel, which is exactly what Luv does when she lies to Wallace about Joshi's death. The mystery child imports the whole miraculous-birth and chosen-one tradition, from Rachel's opened womb to Joi's 'born, not made' echoing the Nicene Creed's 'begotten'. But K is not the chosen one, and via 'Galatians Syndrome' the chapter applies Paul's paradigm shift in Galatians — faith, not flesh, makes one Abraham's heir; 'no longer Jew or Greek, slave or free' — to show birth never mattered: K, Joi, and Sapper become human by believing, choosing, and dying for the right cause.
- ch. 30 "18. K’s Most Excellent Kierkegaardian Pilgrimage" **·** Søren Kierkegaard — Teleological Suspension of the Ethical; The Stages on Life's Way — Ben Franz runs K and Luv through Kierkegaard's Either/Or and Fear and Trembling. Luv is the Knight of Faith, an Abraham whose total devotion to her god Wallace involves the teleological suspension of the ethical — killing the Rachael copy, torturing Joshi — and who dies, film-noir fashion, for failing her creator's command. K is the Knight of Infinite Resignation, repeatedly raised to believe he is special and repeatedly deflated — the wooden horse date, the nursemaid's denial, Joi's death — until he resigns himself to faking Deckard's death and reuniting father and daughter; in the noir inversion, the villain has perfect faith and the hero never attains it.
- ch. 31 "19. And God Created Replicants in His Own Image" **·** The Banality of Evil — Jerry Piven argues both films are dark mirrors of human inhumanity: we create beings who feel, then declare them soulless 'skin jobs' so we can enslave and slaughter them without guilt, disavowing and projecting our own evil onto our creations. Roy's nail-pierced hand, his saving of Deckard, and his 'tears in rain' lament make him a Christlike figure whose grace shames the humans, while 2049 replays Exodus — Rachael with the matriarch Rachel, K renamed Joe the liberator — only to reveal the true messiah is the concealed daughter Ana. Beneath it all lies the banality of evil and an existential dread of death that drives men like Wallace to play God and murder what they cannot gestate.
- ch. 32 "20. Sympathy for the Existential Devils" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Existence Precedes Essence; Radical Freedom and Responsibility — James McLachlan reads the films through Sartre: replicants are made like penknives, with an essence before existence, and their drama is going 'off baseline' — the freedom of nothingness by which Batty murders his maker-gods (Tyrell the Father, Chew and Sebastian the trinity) and K disobeys everyone. Sartre's Satan from his Baudelaire biography, Blake's Orc and the Marriage of Heaven and Hell's contraries, and the Great Chain of Being's contradiction between filling one's place and moral freedom frame Luv as a perfect penknife, a 'good angel' who fulfills her vocation, while Batty and K are redeemed by the love that breaks their programming. 'More human than human' turns out to mean: existence precedes essence.
- ch. 33 "V A Real Girl Now" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "21. The Trouble with Joi" **·** Judith Butler — Gender Performativity; Feminism — M.J. Ryder uses Judith Butler's Gender Trouble to argue that Joi's womanhood is a performance, and so is her humanity: an 'it' playing a woman playing a human, in nested layers from Ana de Armas through the hologram to the 1950s housewife act. The chapter situates this in the real economy of female-voiced assistants (Siri, Cortana) scripted by human writers to seem real and trustworthy, and rereads the awkward Mariette threesome as Joi acting out what she believes human sex is, the two bodies never quite coinciding. Joi's final choice of mortal peril — 'like a real girl' — completes the performance, which is why the film leaves us unsure whether performing humanity differs from having it.
- ch. 35 "22. Rachael, Weeping for Her Children" **·** Jean Baudrillard — Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal); Objectification; Feminism — Bonnie McLean maps Baudrillard's four-stage precession of simulacra onto the women of 2049: Joshi anchors reality, implanted memories distort it, Joi is representation masking the absence of reality, and the eye-color-wrong reconstructed Rachael is a simulacrum of a simulacrum bearing no relation to reality. Read this way, the film lionizes birth — Wallace kissing the replicant he has just disemboweled, her blood running down her legs — while stripping every woman of agency in service of K's quest, a dynamic the chapter links to pro-life politics and DACA-era savior narratives. Only by removing K from the narrative, she shows, does the women's erasure become visible.
- ch. 36 "23. The Future Is Female … Robots!" **·** Gilles Deleuze — Woman as the Other; The Male Gaze; Objectification — Emily Cox-Palmer-White applies Deleuze and Guattari's 'becoming-woman' to the mechanical women of both films: the girl as universal other, a vacuum or absence at the center of femininity, is exactly what lets Rachael, Pris, Luv, and Joi destabilize the patriarchal world that manufactured them. Wallace's drones undressing the barren replicant with their gaze before he slashes her womb gives the male gaze a literal, lethal form, while the Joi-Mariette sync scene stages the frightening interchangeability of women under that gaze. Deckard's 'Her eyes were green' — a lie, since Rachael's eyes were brown — refuses the premise that women are replaceable products.
- ch. 37 "VI The World Is Built on a Wall" *(front)*
- ch. 38 "24. A Modern utopia" **·** Robert Nozick, Isaiah Berlin — The Minimal State; Two Concepts of Liberty; Utopia and Dystopia — Robin Bunce reads 2049 as a libertarian utopia realized: a Los Angeles without welfare, planning, or federal power, where Hayek's market knowledge and Nozick's minimal state have won and everything from water to children to replicants is voluntarily bought and sold. Yet the result is a slave state, and Nozick's freedom-as-non-coercion — the negative liberty Berlin also held — cannot explain Luv, the film's most privileged character, who is nonetheless a slave because her existence hangs wholly on Wallace's will. Quentin Skinner's and Algernon Sidney's neo-Roman liberty, independence from another's will, explains her fear, and the Baseline, like Orwell's 'Thou Art', reveals an ambition to enslave not actions but souls; Freysa's 'we are our own masters' is a republican declaration of independence.
- ch. 39 "25. Less Human than Human" **·** Objectification; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Leah Schade and Emily Askew read replicants as undocumented Latinx immigrants: Sapper's grub farm parallels an ICE raid on a meatpacking plant and the deportation cell of 'Justo', blade runners mirror enforcement agents hunting 'illegals', and the miracle child maps onto DACA Dreamers whom well-meaning liberals recast as superhuman saviors at the cost of their ordinary personhood. Using Mary Midgley's personhood criteria — the ability to suffer, emotional fellowship, minding what happens to one — they argue replicants, like migrants, plainly qualify, and that 'illegality' is a constructed status, not a moral fact. The chapter warns that messianic sacrifice narratives, however sympathetic, re-objectify the very people they mean to defend.
- ch. 40 "26. Do Replicants Have Nightmares of Ethnic Discrimination" *(no index entry yet)* — Dominic Nardi asks whether replicants constitute an ethnic group and answers with social-identity theory: they self-identify as distinct ('I've seen things you people wouldn't believe'), inherit their traits through manufacture rather than birth, and treat the Tyrell tower as their homeland. He sets primordialist 'ancient hatreds' against constructivist accounts in which demagogues activate identity — Milosevic's Serbia, post-1979 Sunni-Shia polarization — and shows Blade Runner is purely constructivist while 2049 first insists on biological difference (the eye scan, barrenness), then makes the wall porous as Rachael's birth reinterprets replicant biology. K's journey from accepting his designations to dying for what is right, not because of whose son he is, delivers the constructivist moral: identity follows belief, not blood.
- ch. 41 "27. Who Keeps a Dead Tree" **·** Utopia and Dystopia — Alî Riza Taşkale and Reşat Volkan Günel argue 2049 is trapped in Mark Fisher's capitalist realism: it can dystopianize the present but cannot imagine a future beyond corporate capitalism, with Joshi as the hollowed-out state unable to protect even herself and Wallace as capitalism without limits, monopolizing the food supply. Wallace figures capitalism's fantasy of creative destruction and technological determinism — the future belongs to billionaire engineers — while K's lone sacrifice, however moving, never becomes collective resistance and so mirrors the neoliberal individualism he defies. The chapter closes by demanding new emancipatory future narratives that can make post-capitalist worlds thinkable again.
- ch. 42 "28. Replicants of California, Unite!" **·** Karl Marx — Class Struggle; Historical Materialism; Alienation — Christopher Innes gives 2049 a Marxist class analysis: the replicants are an enslaved working class, the Wallace Corporation the ruling class owning the means of production, and the LAPD a state apparatus with 'relative autonomy' that really upholds the system — K is a slave who kills his own kind without seeing his class position. Applying the materialist (not idealist) dialectic and the Eighteenth Brumaire's lesson about conflicting ruling-class interests, he reads Wallace, Atari, and even Ana as factions with divergent interests. Freysa's 'we are our own masters' and 'we are building our own army' mark the replicants as class conscious, aware of their Marxian 'species being', with nothing to lose but their chains.
- ch. 43 "I Know What’s Real" **·** The Problem of Evil — Trip McCrossin's afterword works out what Deckard can mean by 'I know what's real': given the green eyes in the Voight-Kampff playback versus the brown eyes in Wallace's staged memory, Deckard is claiming the overwhelming authority of his own lived experience — of Rachael's eye color, their love, her personhood — over any recording or replica. He folds this into the book's larger move from the problem of personhood to the problem of evil, why bad things happen to good people, and the secular task of justifying the ways of Wallace, Tyrell, and the other ersatz gods. With Mr. Cotton's 'raining fire' echoing the Book of Job, the afterword presents the volume as an invitation to save ourselves by saving one another.
- ch. 44 "References" *(notes)*
- ch. 45 "Those Who Hunt Them Still Go by the Name …" *(notes)*
- ch. 46 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 47 "Blade Runner 2049 and Philosophy This Breaks the World" *(front)*

## Blues – Philosophy for Everyone - Thinking Deep About Feeling Low

- ch. 1 "BLUES" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Globalization and Contemporary Art" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "BLUES" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "BLUES" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "BLUES" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "BLUES" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "BLUES" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "BLUES" *(no index entry yet)* — In this foreword, Alligator Records founder Bruce Iglauer argues that the blues is 'magic' rooted in the specific 300-year African-American experience of slavery, sharecropping, and Jim Crow, yet reaches a worldwide audience. He dissects the 'tension and release' at the music's core — delayed vocals, notes bent up to pitch, Muddy Waters' slide, Albert King's guitar — as the mechanism of its emotional effect, and contrasts the communal call-and-response of black juke joints with the more passive reception of white festival audiences. The piece is a practitioner's testimony rather than a sustained philosophical argument, so no taxonomy philosopher's doctrine is genuinely applied.
- ch. 9 "BLUES" **·** Plato, Socrates, Friedrich Nietzsche, Jeremy Bentham — Tripartite Soul; The Unexamined Life — The editors' introduction argues blues and philosophy are close kin in confronting the struggling soul. It reads Lightnin' Hopkins's 'Lonesome Dog' through Plato's tripartite soul from Republic IX — the howling 'dog' is the appetitive part — but claims the blues achieves a 'blues harmony' rather than Plato's reason-ruled harmony. It casts Socrates, calm before execution in the Phaedo, as having a 'bluesy sensibility' that rises above worry through philosophizing, invokes Bentham's claim that the capacity to suffer is what matters morally to gloss the blues as knowledge of suffering, and likens the blues' triumphant affirmation to Nietzsche's positivity.
- ch. 10 "BLUES" *(notes)*
- ch. 11 "BLUES" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "BLUES" **·** Heraclitus — Everything Flows / Logos — Joel Rudinow stages a three-way internal dialogue ('Me,' 'Myself,' and 'I') debating whether the commercialized, white-inflected blues — the House of Blues chain, the Blues Brothers, Cyndi Lauper's Memphis Blues, Clapton's Cream 'Crossroads' — is still really the blues. 'I' introduces Heraclitus's doctrine that everything flows and you cannot step twice into the same river, using the Heraclitean flux of culture to argue the blues is a living, ever-changing tradition whose 'canon' cannot be pinned to a fixed time, place, or racial purity, dissolving the survive/thrive/arrive question into one about how a canon forms and evolves.
- ch. 13 "BLUES" **·** Walter Benjamin — The Aura of the Artwork — Ken Ueno recounts a B. B. King lecture where King played the 'right' notes, then 'turned it on' with identical notes made electric by personality, to argue that the blues locates a song's identity in the performer's aura rather than a written score. He appropriates Walter Benjamin's 'The Work of Art in the Age of Mechanical Reproduction' but inverts it: where Benjamin held that mechanical reproduction withers the aura, Ueno claims recordings actually preserve and transmit the aura of blues performers (bent notes, B. B.'s voice) that classical notation cannot capture, making recordings a more democratic notation for an aural tradition.
- ch. 14 "BLUES" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Socrates — Language Games — Wade Fox and Richard Greene argue that no set of necessary and sufficient conditions — twelve-bar structure, AAB lyrics, mournful content — defines the blues, since paradigm cases violate each (Charley Patton's one-chord songs, cheerful jug band tunes, instrumentals). Invoking the 'Socratic fallacy,' they deny that failure to define a term means failure to grasp it, then apply Wittgenstein's Philosophical Investigations notion of family resemblance so that blues songs share overlapping features without a common essence. They extend this to argue contemporary blues (Stevie Ray Vaughan, Robert Cray, Jonny Lang) are 'twelve-bar zombies' — resembling the blues but lacking the lived authenticity that gives it a soul.
- ch. 15 "BLUES" *(no index entry yet)* — Philip Jenkins distinguishes the blues as musical form from the blues as cultural expression, defending a version of Amiri Baraka's expressivist claim that white musicians (imagining Barry Manilow) cannot authentically play the blues. Against the formalism of Rudinow and James O. Young, he theorizes that one's expressive 'options' are internalized from one's social group, so authentic blues requires the lived, embodied experience of African-American oppression that a cultural outsider lacks. He roots the disagreement in a 'too-loose' definition of culture that ignores how involuntary, systematic oppression makes African-American culture more experientially cohesive than voluntary subcultures.
- ch. 16 "BLUES" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "BLUES" **·** G.W.F. Hegel — A trauma-psychology team (Steinberg, Pynoos, Abramovitz) argues that the blues functions like psychotherapy for trauma, loss, and adversity rooted in slavery, sharecropping, and disasters such as the 1927 Mississippi flood (Charley Patton, Bessie Smith). They map blues techniques onto clinical findings: the repeated AAB line mirrors the traumatized need to retell; verbal labeling of emotion dampens amygdala activity; delayed notes and tension-release (Muddy Waters, Sonny Boy Williamson II) recreate and discharge distress. They cite Hegel's Aesthetics on music's capacity to absorb the listener into inner life, and Robert Stoller on aesthetic excitement, to explain how the blues transforms raw trauma into bearable, shareable art.
- ch. 18 "BLUES" **·** Immanuel Kant, Plato, Aristotle, Arthur Schopenhauer, Jean-François Lyotard — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime; The World as Will — David C. Drake argues that the blues' distinctive achievement is presenting 'sadness as beauty,' which lets performers and listeners find their own sorrow beautiful and thus therapeutic. He surveys theories of beauty — Plato's proportion/harmony/unity and Aristotle's order/symmetry (a beautiful life needs some sorrow), Kant's claim that beauty is a subjective-yet-universal judgment requiring reflection, Lyotard's sublime that 'pleases only by causing pain,' and Schopenhauer's view that aesthetic contemplation temporarily frees us from the striving Will — and links beauty to truth and goodness (Keats, Nietzsche's 'whoever despises himself respects himself as one who despises') to explain why blues sorrow feels good.
- ch. 19 "BLUES" **·** Aristotle, Sigmund Freud, Albert Camus — The Myth of Sisyphus; Civilization and Its Discontents — Ben and Owen Flanagan ask why people seek out 'anguished art,' comparing the blues to Aristotelian tragedy. They argue the blues protagonist, unlike the doomed tragic hero, survives — and his downfall comes from ordinary personal choices (wanderlust, whiskey, the wrong woman) rather than fated conflicts of duty, making imaginative identification easier for a plebeian audience. Aristotle's catharsis of pity and fear is broadened by Freud's account of sublimation and release of repressed sexual and aggressive drives; Robert Johnson's 'Love in Vain' and Muddy Waters' 'Five Long Years' are read as Sisyphean (Camus) images of loving and losing, so the blues offers beauty, release, and shared humanity by being more 'real' than happy pop.
- ch. 20 "BLUES" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Roopen Majithia asks why a music born of African-American suffering became universally beloved and answers with Aristotle's account of musical catharsis in the Politics. Drawing on the Nicomachean Ethics link between habituated action, emotion, and disposition (as courage is built by acting courageously), he argues music images real emotions and thereby purges excess feeling. Modern electric urban blues (T-Bone Walker's 'Stormy Monday,' Muddy Waters, Howlin' Wolf), with its danceable, sexually charged rhythm, recreates the emotional landscape of repetitive urban work and, aided by dancing, effects a broad catharsis — which also explains why non-black communal performers can play it and why intimate bars beat arenas.
- ch. 21 "BLUES" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "BLUES" **·** Epictetus, Plato, Friedrich Nietzsche, Aristotle — Stoicism; The Apollonian and the Dionysian — Brian Domino frames a choice between the reasoned Stoic life and the emotional life through the Rolling Stones and the blues. He presents Epictetus's Stoicism — apatheia, the dichotomy of control, and the Enchiridion's 'it was given back' — applied to blues themes of floods, death, and cheating women ('When the Levee Breaks,' Robert Johnson's 'Terraplane Blues'), arguing emotions stem from false beliefs and akrasia. Against this he sets 'Jumping Jack Flash,' 'Monkey Man,' and '(I Can't Get No) Satisfaction' as celebrations of raw emotional existence, invoking Plato's tension of reason and passion and Nietzsche's image of the 'music-making Socrates' to leave the choice deliberately unresolved.
- ch. 23 "BLUES" **·** René Descartes, Robert Nozick, Socrates — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); The Experience Machine; Philosophical Skepticism — Jesse Steinberg argues that doubt — a pervasive blues theme (B. B. King's 'nobody loves me but my momma,' Otis Spann's 'another mule kickin' in my stall,' Buddy Guy's 'I Smell a Rat') — supports Descartes's external-world skepticism, since knowledge would require ruling out the dreaming hypothesis. Against the 'I don't care' hedonistic reply he deploys Nozick's experience machine to show we value authentic reality over mere pleasant appearance, and he rejects contextualism as a solution. Endorsing Socrates' examined life, he settles for strong justification rather than certainty, reading Charley Patton's 'can't tell my future, can't tell my past' as the blues' honest coping with irreducible doubt.
- ch. 24 "BLUES" **·** Martin Heidegger, Friedrich Nietzsche — Being-Toward-Death; Authenticity — Robert and Benjamin Stolorow argue the blues is musical therapy that lets us face universally traumatizing dimensions of existence. Drawing on Robert Stolorow's Trauma and Human Existence, they hold that emotional pain becomes traumatic only when it finds no 'relational home,' and that trauma shatters the 'absolutisms of everyday life,' exposing the existential vulnerability of finite, mortal beings — an explicitly Heideggerian being-toward-death and authenticity of owning rather than evading vulnerability. Blues lyrics (Louisiana Red's 'Too Poor to Die,' Willie Dixon's 'One More Mile to Go') voice tragic-ironic mortality, while pitch-bending and major/minor ambiguity viscerally hold the pain in a shared, therapeutic conversation.
- ch. 25 "BLUES" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Karl Marx, The Buddha, David Hume — The Leap of Faith; Alienation; The Four Noble Truths — Joseph Lynch uses Son House's 'Preachin' the Blues' — a fallen preacher who preaches the blues, not the gospel — to explore the tension between blues sensuality and religion, arguing the expression of suffering is itself liberation from it. He reads Marx's 'religion is the sigh of the oppressed creature' as making the blues a heart of a heartless world that voices and eases alienated labor; the Buddha's Four Noble Truths and impermanence as embracing suffering to be freed from craving; and Kierkegaard's passionate leap of faith, born of doubt and despair and contrasted with Hume, as the model for how blues 'preachers' like Son House and Robert Johnson embrace the absurd contradiction of embodied spirituality.
- ch. 26 "BLUES" **·** W.E.B. Du Bois, Cornel West — Double Consciousness — Kimberly Connor reads the blues as story, song, and prayer — a secularized descendant of the spirituals and an African-American philosophical guide to making meaning from suffering, exemplified by the Robert Johnson crossroads myth. Using Albert Murray's idea of 'worrying the line' (breaking a phrase to emphasize or subvert) as both technique and survival philosophy, she casts the bluesman as an 'Omni-American' whose art dissolves black/white division, invoking Du Bois's double consciousness and Cornel West's claim that the blues chronicles catastrophe 'with grace and dignity.' She argues Martin Luther King, Jr.'s nonviolent resistance and Brother Will Hairston's 'Alabama Bus' show the blues functioning as prayer and an agent of social change.
- ch. 27 "BLUES" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "BLUES" **·** Iris Marion Young — Five Faces of Oppression; Objectification — Meghan Winsby answers George Carlin's gibe that white people have nothing to be blue about by arguing that women — black or white — legitimately sing the blues because they share an experience of oppression. She applies Iris Marion Young's 'five faces of oppression' (exploitation, marginalization, powerlessness, cultural imperialism, violence) to women's silencing, sexual objectification, and gender exploitation, showing that early blueswomen (Ma Rainey, Bessie Smith, Memphis Minnie) and later ones (Bonnie Raitt) asserted sexuality and independence against a patriarchal order. She then weighs, without fully endorsing, the moral cultural-appropriation and audience-appropriation objections (via James O. Young) to white men playing the blues.
- ch. 29 "BLUES" **·** Alasdair MacIntyre, Aristotle, Augustine of Hippo — Douglas and Nathaniel Langston argue that even white folks 'get' the blues, citing B. B. King and Howlin' Wolf treating the white Jimmie Rogers as a bluesman and looking to hard-life experience rather than color. They distinguish the experiential 'get' from two comprehension senses — understanding the lyrics and understanding the music — detailing twelve-bar form, slide, pitch-bending, and finger-picking to show any skilled musician can 'get' the music. To test whether black experience is required to grasp the lyrics they engage MacIntyre's thesis of incommensurable conceptual schemes (Aristotle vs. Augustine) and Davidson's reply, concluding that the common human hurt Howlin' Wolf sings about is accessible to anyone regardless of race.
- ch. 30 "BLUES" *(no index entry yet)* — Michael Neumann attacks the 'white rip-off' account of rock and roll as a species of 'distributive history' that redistributes reputation from white to black artists but relies on racial stereotypes. He marshals musical history — the banjo's black origin, Jimmie Rogers's blues, Chuck Berry and Howlin' Wolf borrowing from country, color-blind commercial exploitation by black label owner Don Robey — to argue black and white music were always intertwined, so cultural (as opposed to commercial) theft was impossible. He contends the account slanders black music by reducing it to 'jungle sex and violence,' ignoring doo-wop, sophisticated pianists, and the aesthetic range black audiences actually preferred. The argument is historical and aesthetic, applying no taxonomy philosopher's doctrine.
- ch. 31 "BLUES" **·** Aristotle, Plato, Socrates — Theory of Forms — Ron Bombardi argues there are two incompatible histories of the blues, tracing the split to Plato's and Aristotle's opposed views of difference. Using Ian Hacking's 'making up people,' he says the 'orthodox' tale treats the blues as the product of a distinct kind of people (Platonic kinds generating individuals), while the 'unorthodox' tale — the hybrid, cosmopolitan music of the Mississippi Sheiks — fits Aristotle's nominalism, where only individuals exist and forms are things 'in name only.' Invoking the Socratic 'know thyself,' he concludes the blues has no essence and that the 'pay your dues' authenticity maxim reduces persons to social kinds and perpetuates the very oppression the blues protests; the blues, he says, is finally 'ours.'
- ch. 32 "BLUES" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "BLUES" *(notes)*

## Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy - Bootleg This Book

- ch. 1 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The editors' introduction argues that Boardwalk Empire's Prohibition setting - bootleggers, corrupt politicians, G-men, and ordinary citizens at one table against the Volstead Act - puts its characters in philosophically loaded situations that bear directly on real life. It then maps the volume: Littmann reads Nucky as a Machiavellian Prince, Greene casts him as Nietzsche's Superman, Archibald reads Gillian through Plato, Brace and Kingsbury test Aristotle's happiness against Nucky, Chalky, and Margaret, and Robison-Greene treats Jimmy and Richard as war veterans facing existential problems. It closes by previewing chapters on Van Alden's God, the ethics of lying (Fallis), storytelling (Carveth) and deception (Cobb), the body politic (Schwanebeck) and the social contract (Fitzpatrick), causing versus allowing harm (Da Silva), violence (Mellamphy), and a Freudian reading of Jimmy and Gillian (Hirschbein).
- ch. 11 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli — Virtù and Fortuna; The Ends Justify the Means — Littmann tests The Prince against the real bosses behind the show - Nucky Johnson, Capone, Luciano, Rothstein, Torrio - and argues that Prohibition-era history bears out Machiavelli's rules of power. Seizing new opportunities pays (Nucky turning Atlantic City into the World's Playground, Colosimo gunned down for refusing bootlegging, Luciano eliminating Masseria and Maranzano to modernize the Mafia), and harming a rival without crushing him invites revenge (Nucky's fatal leniency toward Jimmy, Maranzano leaving the scarred Luciano alive). A ruler needs good arms, must tailor appearances to each audience (Capone's banquet murder of Anselmi and Scalise, Nucky's respectability and staged generosity), and above all must avoid hatred - the downfall of Nucky, Capone, and Luciano, and of the needlessly arrogant Rothstein, shot over a gambling debt he could easily have paid.
- ch. 13 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; Eternal Recurrence; The Death of God; The Last Man — Greene asks whether Nucky is Nietzsche's Ubermensch and works through each criterion. Nucky, Gyp Rosetti, and the other mobsters are passionate, active shapers of their world (Van Alden fails here), and Nucky is a genuine creator of values after the death of God - his whopper to the Temperance League, his flip-flopping sermons to black and white congregations, and his 'I. Am. Not. Seeking. Forgiveness.' over Jimmy's body reject slave morality where the other gangsters merely follow the objective moral code of omerta. He sacrifices lesser lives (Hans Schroeder, Gillian) and has the required dark side, but he fails the final test of teaching the eternal recurrence, so he remains only 'well on his way' to Superman - a status the show's women (Margaret, Lucy, Gillian) never approach, hovering closer to the last man.
- ch. 14 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Thomas Hobbes — Tripartite Soul — Hirschbein has Freud analyze the audience first: like Van Alden, the repressed hypocrite whose true self erupts in drowning Sebso and burning his workplace tormentor, we watch Nucky and Jimmy as avatars of our own repressed aggression and lust. Freud's contest of Eros and Thanatos - his reframing of Plato's tripartite soul as id, ego, and superego - explains why civilization demands renunciation while spectacle like Boardwalk supplies vicarious gratification, with Milgram's and Zimbardo's experiments showing how thin the civilized veneer is. Jimmy's seduction by his mother and killing of the Commodore enact the Oedipus complex writ large, Freud's primal-horde story of the sons' parridical revolt against the father, and the show's catharsis is what lets us live with the sin.
- ch. 15 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Virtue Ethics (arete); Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — Brace and Kingsbury use the Nicomachean Ethics to argue that Nucky, Chalky, and Margaret mistake pleasure and the mere instruments of happiness - tailored suits, money, useful connections, the appearance of virtue - for Aristotle's stable, self-sustaining virtuous activity, so their lives stay unstable and empty. Nucky 'buys' even Jimmy and Margaret, Chalky's jail-cell staging with David Copperfield shows how fragile his facade of cultivation is, and Margaret's acquisitions end in blackmail and in bribing God over Emily's polio. Only Richard Harrow, the Tin Man whose collage-clippings represent a happiness he believes he can never have, moves toward real Aristotelian friendship through Jimmy's 'Of course I would; right down to the last bullet' - the philia Aristotle calls most necessary for a happy life.
- ch. 16 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Carveth argues that the show's historical license is ethically troubling because docudrama viewers - especially those who know nothing of 1920s Atlantic City - absorb 'reel history' as fact. He documents the blend of real and invented: Buscemi's slight Nucky Thompson against the imposing real Nucky Johnson, a fictional military past for Capone, Torrio stripped of his mentor role, and the McGarrigle episodes' misrepresentation of de Valera, Collins, and the Anglo-Irish Treaty. The deepest distortion is violence - the real Nucky ruled through jobs, ostracism, and economic coercion rather than murder, but that would not make good television - and given research showing viewers 'learn' from docudramas they know least about, Terence Winter's ninety-percent-accuracy claim amounts to an unethical misleading of the audience.
- ch. 18 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Thomas Aquinas — The Problem of Evil; Fate and Foreknowledge — Sirvent and Baker take Van Alden's 'strong providential theology' - 'his fate rests in God's hands' at the burned Agent Clarkson's bedside - as a live version of the problem of evil, traced through Augustine and Aquinas to Calvin's all-determining divine omnipotence. The Arminian free-will escape leaves God like a parent watching a child run into traffic, and the 'mysterious ways' reply makes God's moral vocabulary so alien that a God who burns his beloved is 'one-sided' and unworshipable - the same theology that lets Van Alden shift blame for Lucy's pregnancy onto a 'charge from God' before drowning Sebso as 'the judgment of the wicked.' They close with Nucky's God, an inner impetus toward good that asks rather than coerces, and with process theology's persuading God, while Gyp Rosetti's Easter rant at the crucifix shows what believing in the omnipotent alternative costs.
- ch. 19 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Derek Parfit — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Da Silva asks what Nucky deserves for bringing the thirteen-year-old Gillian to the Commodore and failing to stop the rape, and whether Jimmy - whose very existence depends on that wrong - is entitled to blame him and to ally with the worse man. Working through the doing, enabling, and allowing harm distinction with euthanasia and Good Samaritan cases, degrees of blame, and Parfit's point about collective action, he argues Nucky did wrong but likely less wrong than the Commodore, while Jimmy can blame on his mother's behalf and need not regret a birth that was still good for him - against Benatar's antinatalism. Jimmy's coup fails him, but the moral reason behind it, Nucky's contribution to the rape, was genuine.
- ch. 20 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Augustine of Hippo — The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself — Fallis sorts Atlantic City's lies to show which are wrong and why. Kant's and Augustine's absolute bans go too far and Grotius's life-saving exception does not apply - most of these lies get people killed - yet even altruistic lies (Margaret for Madame Jeunet and the ledger), lies told to those who want to be deceived, lies to liars, and Sorensen's bald-faced lies that no one believes (Jimmy's flat movie alibi, Rothstein's 'skilled player' whopper) remain wrong: they take informed choice out of the victim's hands, violating the Kantian autonomy that grounds the wrong of lying, and they corrode the trust on which even criminals' co-operation depends.
- ch. 21 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Seneca, Niccolò Machiavelli, Thomas Hobbes, Cicero — Stoicism — Schwanebeck reads Nucky's fastidious grooming and Prohibition's purity crusade through the body politic metaphor: from Cicero and Seneca's De Clementia, where head and body of the state depend on each other, through Hobbes's bearded Leviathan frontispiece, to temperance propaganda that framed alcohol as poison entering the national bloodstream and wartime posters demanding 'a clean America.' The show exposes the purity ideal as illusion - the true purists are the Klan massacring Chalky's men and the hand-washing Van Alden shouting 'Let these waters wash you clean!' over Sebso's drowning body. Nucky rules less like Seneca's genuinely clement emperor than like Machiavelli's prince who merely appears virtuous, and the gangster genre's kingly tropes ('Et tu, Eli?') crown him head of Atlantic City's body politic.
- ch. 23 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Albert Camus, Jean-Paul Sartre — The Absurd; Bad Faith; Existence Precedes Essence; The Look (hell is other people) — Robison-Greene reads the returning soldiers through Camus and Sartre. War reveals the absurd - Jimmy's Argonne story of the German soldier who would not die even in a hopeless situation strips life of inherent value - and Van Alden's fervent Christianity is the escapist response to absurdity, bad faith that denies his own desires. Jimmy and Richard are trapped in bad faith over facticity and transcendence, Richard pinned by 'the look' of a community that only sees his ruined face ('Hell is other people'); neither fully becomes an absurd hero like Meursault, since Richard still craves meaningful ties, but Jimmy finally embraces the absurd at the War Memorial, arranging his own death with the calm of a man who 'died in the trenches, years back.'
- ch. 24 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, John Locke, John Stuart Mill, Karl Marx — The State of Nature; The Social Contract; Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; Ethical Egoism — Fitzpatrick uses the social contract tradition to explain Prohibition-era Atlantic City. Hobbes's state of nature fits the gang warfare left where no Leviathan is strong enough to enforce federal law against Nucky; Locke's fits a night-watchman government overreaching into Prohibition and thereby breaking the contract, as with Nucky's illegitimate protection 'taxes'; Rousseau's noble savage corrupted by commercial society fits the Klan's pleasure in cruelty and innocent Tommy set against scheming adults. Ethical egoism frames Nucky's framing of Hans Schroeder, consequentialism the Temperance Union's cost-benefit failure, and deontology Margaret's lying campaign speech, while Marx's and Mill's view that human nature is malleable explains the season-spanning transformation of Nucky from half-gangster into 'a gangster and a half.'
- ch. 25 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Simone de Beauvoir, Jean-Paul Sartre — Woman as the Other; One Is Not Born a Woman; The Ethics of Ambiguity; Bad Faith — Robison-Greene applies Beauvoir to the show's women. Powerful men are the One and women (like Chalky's community under the Klan) are made the inessential Other, and Beauvoir's apparatus - bad faith, the 'appeal' free beings must make to one another, and the happiness that lets women be complicit in their own oppression - frames Lucy, Gillian, and Margaret. Lucy happily performs the role her men write for her, Gillian is so consumed by the fact of her rape that even her apparent freedom keeps her the object of male desire ('just the loneliest person on earth'), while Margaret - Temperance Leaguer, advocate for women's reproductive health, and finally the woman who shuts the door on Nucky's cash - comes closest to making the appeal and to freedom.
- ch. 27 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Theory of Forms; Immortality and the Soul — Archibald reads Gillian through Plato's Phaedo. The Argument of Opposites - cyclical deaths and rebirths that the immortal soul survives - maps onto the rape at thirteen that killed the child pageant girl and birthed a survivor, her shift into caretaker to control the stroked-out Commodore, and her final drugged regression before Nucky ('Nucky he did something very bad to me'). The Theory of Forms explains her treatment of roles as interchangeable: Jimmy is son, lover, and husband-Form rather than a person to mourn, Angela's death is merely an opportunity for rebirth, and the Jimmy-lookalike Roger can be dressed with his dog tags as 'James' - Forms changing shape while remaining, to Gillian, undamaged.
- ch. 28 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Karl Marx — Ideology and False Consciousness — Mellamphy uses Zizek's Violence to divide the show's bloodshed - Jimmy's hijacking shootout, Eli's wrench murder of O'Neill, Rosetti burning Sheriff Sickels alive - as subjective violence whose spectacle distracts from objective violence: symbolic (the Klan's racism, the language of the Muhammad caricature controversy) and systemic (the capitalism that keeps Nucky untouchable). Zizek's 'chocolate laxative' logic - ruthless exploitation masked by charity - captures 'I'm a philanthropist now' answered by 'You're a gangster. Plain and simple,' and the opening credits' shifting sea shows Nucky's dual roles merging. Critics who complain about gore see only subjective violence and miss the system that produces it, exactly as Zizek predicts, with Marx's self-enhancing circulation of capital as the unseen engine.
- ch. 30 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Socrates, Immanuel Kant — Socratic Method (elenchus) — Cobb treats the show's storytellers as narrative pedagogues. Nucky's wharf-rat speech is story truth rather than happening truth - Plato's Republic question of true and false tales - and because it allows no dialogue or questioning it fails as full pedagogy; Chalky's story of his father's lynching ties past to present in the interrogation of the Klansman; Rothstein's billiard-ball anecdote for Frankie Yale is episodic memory shaped into identity and threat, a coerced but genuine reasoning exchange ('Do you know what the moral is of this tale, Mr. Yale?'). Measured against Socrates' question-posing inquiry in the Euthyphro, Kant's On Education, and Freire's dialogical 'critical co-investigators,' Nucky, Chalky, and Rothstein all emerge as reflective but deficient pedagogues at large.
- ch. 31 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 32 "Boardwalk Empire and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Bob Dylan and Philosophy - It's Alright Ma (I'm Only Thinking)

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Bob Dylan and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Bob Dylan and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Popular Culture and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Liner Notes" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Acknowledgements" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "Side 1" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Love Minus Zero, No Limit or Going, Going Gone" **·** Plato, Socrates — Platonic Love (the ladder of love); Philosophy of Love — Doug Anderson reads Dylan's Planet Waves as a modern Symposium: each song stages a rung on Diotima's ladder, from the puppy love of "You Angel You" through the physical eros of "Tough Mama" and "Hazel," the agape of "Forever Young," and the complementary true love of "Wedding Song." Like Socrates refusing Alcibiades in "Going, Going Gone," Dylan abandons finite beloveds because his highest eros is the creative act itself - poets achieve the immortality Diotima demands by giving birth to immortal works rather than children. The chapter argues both Plato and Dylan finally define love as desire for truth and beauty that outlasts any particular relationship.
- ch. 11 "The Ends of Despair From “Things Have Changed” to “Love and Theft”" **·** Albert Camus, Søren Kierkegaard, Martin Heidegger — The Absurd; Despair (sickness unto death); The Leap of Faith; Authenticity — Rick Furtak traces an existential arc through Dylan's later albums, reading Blood on the Tracks ("Tangled Up in Blue," "You're a Big Girl Now") as Camus's absurd divorce between a mind that cares and a world that has gone silent, and Heideggerian care as what the narrator loses in "Simple Twist of Fate." "Brownsville Girl" and "Most of the Time" embody Kierkegaard's knight of infinite resignation - hope abandoned but the precious past never forgotten - while "Not Dark Yet" and "Things Have Changed" mark the nadir of despair. On Love and Theft ("Mississippi," "Sugar Baby") the singer turns toward an absurd, Kierkegaardian faith that keeps caring and even loves without demanding that life make sense.
- ch. 12 "The Mystery Solved" **·** Aristotle, Bernard Williams, Thomas Hobbes — Moral Luck; The State of Nature; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Avery Kolers uses "Only a Pawn in Their Game" and its companion "Who Killed Davey Moore?" to argue that Dylan treats Byron de la Beckwith as a pawn: a poor white duped by divide-and-rule tactics, the psychological wage of whiteness, shame, and lynch-mob indoctrination into fearing a Hobbesian war of all against all. Against Aristotle's account of acting in ignorance, the chapter asks whether Beckwith's ignorance of his real enemies was culpable, concluding his bad moral luck (in Bernard Williams's sense) was manufactured by white supremacy and so reflects back on all white people. Dylan's refusal to name or voice Beckwith adopts Strawson-style objective attitudes, making blaming itself a political act aimed at the system rather than the shooter.
- ch. 13 "So, What’s the Bottom Line" **·** Socrates, John Locke — Natural Rights; Moral Relativism; Man Is the Measure (sophistic relativism); Civil Disobedience — James Klagge, defining terms in Socratic fashion, distinguishes bootlegs like Great White Wonder from piracy and argues unreleased Dylan material (the New York Blood on the Tracks, "Blind Willie McTell," the 1966 Live concert) has genuine musical and historical value. He rejects the Sophist view that morality is just self-interest and the relativist "everybody's doing it" defense, then weighs the artist's Lockean property and privacy rights against fans' rights of access, noting legal-but-immoral and illegal-but-moral cases in the spirit of Martin Luther King's civil disobedience. His verdict: bootlegging is morally acceptable when royalties are fair, stolen tapes are not used, and mainly public performances circulate.
- ch. 14 "Sick of Love" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Simone de Beauvoir, Walter Benjamin — Bad Faith; Woman as the Other; Existence Precedes Essence; Authenticity — Krein and Levin read Dylan's portraits of fallen socialites in "Like a Rolling Stone," "Just Like a Woman," and "To Ramona" as critiques of bad faith - characters who hide from freedom and responsibility in consumerist status games, in Sartre's sense that existence precedes essence. But Beauvoir's Second Sex exposes the cost: the women Dylan praises ("Girl from the North Country," "One More Cup of Coffee") are praised precisely for staying in immanent, pastoral passivity, denied the transcendence that for Beauvoir constitutes full humanity. Borrowing Benjamin's and Buck-Morss's notion of modern "dreamworlds," they finally read Dylan's pastoral utopias as a counter-dreamworld spun after the collapse of the mass-utopian promise of consumer happiness.
- ch. 15 "Both Sides of Dylan and Politics" **·** Immanuel Kant, Michel Foucault, Jacques Derrida, Jean-François Lyotard, John Stuart Mill — The End of Grand Narratives; Autonomy of the Will; Power/Knowledge; Deconstruction — Jordy Rocheleau reads Dylan's protest era ("The Lonesome Death of Hattie Carroll," "Hurricane," "Chimes of Freedom") as Enlightenment social criticism in Kant's and Mill's sense: reason can expose injustice and secure autonomy against majority tyranny. The post-protest Dylan ("My Back Pages," "Desolation Row," "It's Alright, Ma") turns postmodern - Lyotard's incredulity toward metanarratives, Derrida's ever-deferred meanings, and Foucault's power/knowledge, where ideals of freedom become instruments of domination. Yet Dylan keeps crying for justice ("Political World," "Dignity"), so the chapter concludes postmodern skepticism can only serve as a cautionary check within, not a replacement of, Enlightenment politics - the thief's line in "All Along the Watchtower" as commitment amid confusion.
- ch. 16 "Synthesis" **·** Isaiah Berlin, G.W.F. Hegel, Ralph Waldo Emerson, John Stuart Mill, Friedrich Nietzsche — Two Concepts of Liberty; Self-Reliance; On Liberty; Authenticity — Elizabeth Brake asks what freedom Dylan's outlaw persona - refusing Maggie's Farm, possessive love, and conformity - actually wins, framing the answer with Berlin's two concepts of liberty. Dylan's negations look like Berlin's negative freedom, but Hegel's critique of the "freedom of the void" shows mere refusal ends in emptiness, so she reconstructs a Millian and Emersonian ideal of self-realization for which negative liberty is only the condition. "To live outside the law, you must be honest" supplies the synthesis: outside social law the free person answers to the Emersonian law of his own nature and to undistorted honesty with others, a more severe morality Dylan shares with Emerson and Nietzsche.
- ch. 17 "Man Gave a Name to a Lake" **·** Walter Benjamin — Danieli and Biletzki read the abrupt ending of "Man Gave Names to All the Animals" - man never names the snake - through Walter Benjamin's essay "On Language as Such and on the Language of Man," where man's naming completes God's creation and the knowledge of good and evil is precisely the nameless, the birth of judgment. Read biblically, the failed naming means man lacks dominion over the evil inclination; read with Benjamin, refusing to name the snake suspends the Fall and keeps man a namer rather than a questioner. The chapter argues Dylan is reclaiming the creative potency of the logos, while mourning the poet's inevitable failure to recapture the divine word.
- ch. 18 "Side 2" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "The Blessings of Tranquility" **·** Sextus Empiricus, Pyrrho, Protagoras, Marcus Aurelius, Diogenes the Cynic — Pyrrhonian Skepticism (suspension of judgment); Philosophical Skepticism; Moral Relativism; Man Is the Measure (sophistic relativism) — Michael Chiariello takes Dylan's "the truth is true whether you wanna believe it or not" as a correspondence theory of truth, then rejects both the gospel-era absolutism of "Precious Angel" and the relativism (from Protagoras' man-the-measure to modern subjectivism) that would make Dylan's changes of course harmless. The alternative he defends is Pyrrhonian skepticism in Sextus Empiricus's sense: truth is objective but never fully assured, so suspending judgment yields tranquility. Dylan's career - from the ambiguous "Blowin' in the Wind" through "Watching the River Flow" and "Things Have Changed" to the two versions of "Every Grain of Sand" ("the reality of man" vs "a perfect finished plan") - embodies this balanced, non-dogmatic search the way of God designed it.
- ch. 20 "Moral Autonomy" **·** Harry Frankfurt — Free Will vs Determinism; Compatibilism; Second-Order Desires (personhood) — Martin van Hees reads "Who Killed Davey Moore?" and "Ballad of Hollis Brown" as arguments that shared consent generates shared responsibility for tragedy even when fate makes the events inevitable - the referee, crowd, manager, and gambler all endorsed the fight, and we who tolerate poverty consent to the next Hollis Brown. He distinguishes a "limited optimistic view" (twisting fate by preventing particular tragedies) from the proclaimed inevitability of "The Times They Are A-Changin'" and the pessimism of "Blind Willie McTell," then defends compatibilism through Frankfurt's Jones/Black case: responsibility needs no alternative possibilities, only that one act from one's own will. Dylan's "moral autonomy" is taking a stance - singing the blues - inside a determined world.
- ch. 21 "“Nobody’s Bob Dylan”" **·** John Locke, David Hume, The Buddha, René Descartes, Plato — Personal Identity (memory theory); Personal Identity; Bundle Theory of Self; Non-Self (anatman) — Vernezze and Lulewicz take Dylan's 1964 Halloween quip "I got my Bob Dylan mask on" as an entry into personal identity, testing each theory against his life: the bodily view (Zimmerman to Dylan) survives cell turnover but stumbles on brain-transplant cases; Locke's memory theory handles them but faces Reid's brave-officer regress and Alzheimer's cases, and Dylan's reincarnation lyrics ("Shelter from the Storm," "I and I") ignore memory altogether. The Christian soul view of the gospel albums ("When He Returns," "Gotta Serve Somebody") follows Descartes and Plato but cannot justify the swirl-to-filling inference that souls exist. The chapter ends sympathetic to Hume's bundle theory and the Buddhist no-self: Dylan's "I is another," his name as fabrication, and the cover album Self Portrait all deny a fixed self-created essence.
- ch. 22 "Look Up, Look Up" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Socrates, Immanuel Kant, Alvin Plantinga, Thomas Aquinas — Fate and Foreknowledge; Immortality and the Soul; Compatibilism — James Spiegel reads Dylan's God-haunted songwriting through philosophy of religion, sorting strong Augustinian-Aquinian providence ("Every Grain of Sand," "When He Returns") from the weak, risk-taking God of "Highway 61 Revisited" and "Joey." Dylan's moral judgmentalism combined with theological determinism makes him a compatibilist in Augustine's mold, while "Disease of Conceit" and "Pressing On" ("it run in my vein") press the compatibilist puzzle that Plantinga's libertarian power of contrary choice is meant to solve. Original sin becomes the "disease of conceit," and Socrates' Phaedo plus Kant's three postulates - God, freedom, immortality - ground Dylan's hope that "death is not the end," closing with Love and Theft's "Look up, look up."
- ch. 23 "A Call to Unchanging Truths" **·** Moral Relativism; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Virtue Ethics — Francis Beckwith argues the 1979-81 Christian albums are no aberration: Dylan's whole career presupposes moral realism, an objective moral law built into the universe - "Dignity never been photographed" names an irreducible moral property, and the judgments in "With God on Our Side" and "Blowin' in the Wind" only make sense if right and wrong are unchanging across eras. The six-month sentence William Zanzinger receives in "The Lonesome Death of Hattie Carroll" is condemned on retributive, lex talionis grounds rather than any utilitarian calculus of consequences, and "Forever Young" and "What Good Am I?" urge virtue practiced for its own sake. The chapter thus reads Dylan's conversion as the surfacing of commitments - against relativism, for virtue and unchanging truths - always latent in songs like "The Times They Are A-Changin'."
- ch. 24 "Tarantula’s End" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Plato, Jacques Derrida, Michel Foucault — Ressentiment; Amor Fati (love of fate); Perspectivism — Goldblatt and Necarsulmer pair Dylan's Tarantula with Nietzsche's "The Tarantulas" from Zarathustra: both are books of advice, renaming, and stream-of-consciousness flow whose sections cohere through signature and attitude rather than narrative (the same Foucauldian author function). Both embody the intuitive artist of Plato's Ion and Nietzsche's Ecce Homo - the poet as medium whose language arrives as inspiration, all language being fresh or stale metaphor. Tarantula's vengeful vignettes and "Idiot Wind" dramatize the ressentiment Zarathustra warns against, its impenetrable juxtapositions ("harmonica battalions," "funeral landlord") work as Derridean catachresis - metaphor whose transfer fails yet exerts aesthetic force - and the book's dream of the burning tenement leaves Dylan unable, and absolved, to look back.
- ch. 25 "Last Thoughts" **·** Immanuel Kant — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Theodore Gracyk asks what makes Dylan an artist rather than a "song and dance man," testing three theories of art. Expression theory (Collingwood's standard, applied by Greil Marcus to Self Portrait and by Heylin to the two Blood on the Tracks versions) explains Dylan's rasp and "nasty" remakes but cannot separate genuine from conjured emotion in live performance. Formalism praises the verbal patterning Ricks and Gray admire but undervalues Dylan's improvisatory aesthetics of imperfection; Kant's theory of genius - free play of imagination constrained by taste, from "Slow Train"'s metaphor to "Knockin' on Heaven's Door" - is judged the most satisfying account of the career.
- ch. 26 "“The Foggy Ruins of Time”" **·** Plato, Friedrich Nietzsche, Boethius, Aristotle — The Consolation of Philosophy — Kevin Stoehr stages Plato's ancient quarrel between philosophy and poetry over Dylan: from the Republic's banishment of the poets, through Boethius's Consolation where Lady Philosophy banishes the Muses yet the book stays a prose-poem satura, to Nietzsche, who attacks philosophy philosophically just as Dylan discounts thinking while waxing philosophical about it. Dylan's own remarks ("the song wrote itself," "trust my intuition") side with poetic intuition against analysis, and Holderlin's question "what are poets for in a destitute time" frames Dylan as the smirking antiquary of a nihilistic age who makes us feel the darkness philosophy can only point at. The chapter concludes that the ambiguity of songs like "Lay, Lady, Lay" resists universalization yet occasions the wonder in which philosophy begins - poetry as the bridge to philosophy.
- ch. 27 "The Bob Dylan Discography" *(notes)*
- ch. 28 "The Mongrel Dogs" *(notes)*
- ch. 29 "Reciting the Alphabet" *(notes)*
- ch. 30 "ALSO FROM OPEN COURT" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "Bob Dylan and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 32 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Breaking Bad and Philosophy - Badder Living through Chemistry

- ch. 1 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The editors' introduction frames Breaking Bad as a recession-era morality play whose overqualified, marginalized Walter White is heir to Macbeth, Faust, and Milton's Lucifer - a fallen anti-hero whose doom we foresee yet whose success we root for. It argues Walt's appeal lies in rebellion against a system that cheated him out of his just reward, while he convinces himself that family, not ego, drives him. The piece surveys the volume's guiding questions: Walt's real motives, his troubled relation to science, the ethics of drugs, existentialism and redemption, Gus Fring as a study in evil, and the show's male-female relationships.
- ch. 8 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Compatibilism; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Utilitarianism; Free Will vs Determinism — Audits Walt's killings through Season Four - Emilio by phosphine gas in the RV, the captive Krazy-8, Jane left choking in 'Phoenix', the dealers he rams with the Aztec to save Jesse, ordering Jesse to shoot Gale, and the wheelchair bomb that kills Gus and Tyrus - grading each with Aristotle's criteria of capacity, motive, consequences, justification, and proximate cause. It argues self-defense excuses Emilio but dissolves for the imprisoned Krazy-8, that Walt actively created the danger he then refused to relieve when he let Jane die, and that special-relationship duties to Jesse plus a utilitarian ledger justify running down the dealers. Compatibilism is invoked to show guilt survives determinism, while the remote Wayfarer 515 crash is pinned mainly on Donald Margolis's own choices rather than on Walt.
- ch. 10 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil; The Divided Will; Free Will vs Determinism — Sets Walt's 'There's nothing but chemistry here' materialism - the missing 0.111958 percent of the human body that Gretchen calls the soul - against Augustine's Confessions, arguing both men want redemption without giving up their sins: 'give me chastity, but not yet.' It maps Augustine's problem of evil (evil as corruption of the will toward self-destruction) onto Walt's pros-and-cons list over killing Krazy-8 and his 'no-rough-stuff-type deal' fantasy after Tuco. Werner Heisenberg's uncertainty principle then turns chemistry's laws into tendencies, opening room for free choice inside a materialist world - so Walt's remission in 'Over' saves the body, while only changed choices, which he keeps deferring, could save the man.
- ch. 11 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — Autonomy of the Will; The Harm Principle; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — A palliative-care bioethicist dissects the 'Gray Matter' family meeting, arguing Skyler's ambush coerces Walt's autonomy through an implied threat of emotional isolation, while Walt Jr.'s honest 'I'm pissed off!' and Marie's defense of his choice are non-coercive. The chapter frames the issue with Kant's ideal of choice free from emotion and circumstance and Mill's insistence that even self-destructive decisions are beyond legitimate coercion, and indicts doctors too for 'selling' futile treatment rather than admit mortality. The verdict: Walt accepted chemotherapy through 'autonomous beneficence' - a freely made sacrifice for his son - so Skyler's manipulation was unethical even though the decision it produced was genuinely his own.
- ch. 12 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Albert Camus, Jean-Paul Sartre — The Myth of Sisyphus; Bad Faith; Authenticity; The Absurd — Argues Heisenberg is not an alter ego but Walt finally escaping the Prufrock-like bad faith of his pre-cancer life: the pork-pie-hat persona acknowledges the absurd, owns his freedom and responsibility, and so achieves the authenticity Camus and Sartre demand. Walt becomes Camus's Sisyphus - rebelling against death, social norms, and Skyler, owning his rock with silent joy - citing the fulminated-mercury showdown with Tuco in 'Crazy Handful of Nothin'', running down Gus's dealers in 'Half Measures', and confessing to Skyler in 'No Mas'. The chapter closes that, like Sisyphus, we must imagine Walt happy.
- ch. 14 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger — Being-Toward-Death; Thrownness (Geworfenheit); Authenticity; The Question Concerning Technology — Uses Heidegger to read the show's very structure: the pilot's in-medias-res Winnebago crash throws us into Walt's timeline the way Dasein is thrown into time, and the flashbacks to Sandia Labs and 'nowhere to go but up' show his has-been shaping his futurity. Walt's diagnosis makes being-toward-death an overwhelming presence and exposes the failure of his calculative, goal-setting thinking, with the 'Fly' monologue about the perfect moment to die as his turn toward Heidegger's meditative thinking. By dwelling on his own death, the chapter argues, Walt inches toward an authentic being-toward-death - living each moment as if it were his last.
- ch. 15 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Explains why we believe a chemistry teacher could become a drug lord by reading Walt through Shakespeare's Macbeth with Aristotle's Poetics: drama must make a particular psychology feel universal, so interiority - here Walt's wounded masculinity (the veggie bacon, Hank's 'that's why they hire men', post-murder aggression toward Skyler) - supplies the hamartia of insecure pride. The chapter tracks Aristotle's reversals (crime restores manliness while breeding shame, driving ever more viciousness), casts Jesse as Walt's Lady Macbeth and Walt as his own Macduff, and reads the staged psychiatry lie as Walt deceiving himself about resenting his family. It predicts that if the show is Aristotelian tragedy, Walt's final reversal will come from a recognition, with cancer as the evil he embraces 'in' him and catharsis our pity and fear.
- ch. 16 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Will to Power; The Übermensch — A close scene-reading of the pilot argues Nietzsche's will to power explains Walt's transformation: he is repeatedly 'out willed' by Chad's chair-scrape, Bogdan's register demands, and Hank's birthday-party toasts, until the cancer diagnosis powers his 'Fuck you... and your eyebrows!' reversal at the car wash. Walt's decision to cook, declared to Jesse as 'I am awake', is presented as the Ubermensch's creation of his own morality - family provision over society's 'drugs are bad' - and the phosphine-gas killing of Emilio as his ultimate exertion of will. The chapter concludes Nietzsche would have been a fan of the show.
- ch. 18 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; The Last Man; Eternal Recurrence; Will to Power — Scores Walt and Mad Men's Don Draper against Nietzsche's Overman and Last Man: Roger Sterling (and the early, passive Jesse) are Last Men, while Walt moves from weak negation of the status quo to genuine self-affirmation - 'going under' in the 'Crawl Space' crawl hole and emerging in 'Face Off' having poisoned Brock and out-created Gus, which the chapter takes as proof that in a capitalist world two creators cannot coexist. Killing Gus is framed as accepting the eternal recurrence of joy and sorrow, willing even his own bad acts. Don, by contrast, negates far more than he creates, and the chapter ends by asking why viewers empathize with the one failed Overman and find the other inspiring.
- ch. 19 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Thomas Hobbes, Niccolò Machiavelli, Theodor Adorno, Jürgen Habermas — Humanity as an End in Itself; The Ends Justify the Means; The Categorical Imperative; The State of Nature — Solves the 'riddle' of Gus by showing his empire runs on corporate instrumental rationality: the careful, emotion-disciplined Los Pollos CEO launders meth through legitimate business, treats chemists as replaceable parts (the Gale plan), and exacts revenge on the cartel in 'Salud' by drinking his own poisoned tequila. The chapter defines this as the critical theorists' amoral means-end reason - Kant's hypothetical imperative without the categorical - making Gus a sociopath who violates the injunction to treat persons as ends, and reads his cartel war through Machiavelli and Hobbes's power-first picture of the state. Gus's Two-Face-like corpse reveals the split between the rage-driven avenger of Max and the respectable mask, foreshadowing what Walt becomes.
- ch. 21 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Jeremy Bentham, John Stuart Mill, Peter Singer — Consequentialism; The Drowning Child (obligation to aid); Utilitarianism — Runs Walt's choices through consequentialist machinery: his cost-benefit habit (the $737,000 target, 'non-sustainable business model'), the Krazy-8 pros-and-cons list, and whether manufacturing meth harms users or merely enables them. It argues act consequentialism fails on unforeseen consequences (Hank killing Tuco helps Heisenberg; the tow-truck driver aids a murder), defends rule consequentialism via Tuco - rational for him, disastrous as a universal rule - and weighs agent-centered views for Walt's family-first logic. It closes with Singer's drowning-child standard: Walt could have saved Jane and accepted Elliot's charity at little cost, and so could we by redirecting wasted money to OXFAM - so Walt breaks bad, and maybe we do too.
- ch. 22 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** John Stuart Mill — Utilitarianism; Existentialism; The Greatest Happiness Principle; Ethical Egoism — Pits act utilitarianism against existentialism to ask whether moral rules change at life's end: briefly cooking meth can maximize happiness (family provision against limited street harm), while a long career cannot, and utilitarians are trapped by the limits of their own foresight. Walt is read as the existentialist converting mortality into meaning - money, power, and masculinity in a godless world - while Skyler mostly calculates like a utilitarian (white lies to Marie, Ted's accounting), Hank mixes both codes, and Marie's kleptomania marks her an egoist until she defends Walt's right to choose his own death at the intervention. A marathon-runner metaphor lands the thesis: to be a utilitarian in life's early miles is itself an existential decision, and the last mile is when we 'wake up' like Walt.
- ch. 23 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — Autonomy of the Will; On Liberty; Utilitarianism — Takes apart Walt and Jesse's 'legalize it' excuse: Kantian autonomy would license adult drug choices, but knowingly self-harming meth use is a performative contradiction no rational adult can will, and Mill's utilitarianism condemns cooking because harms to all users must be weighed against one family's benefit. It reads the 'Cancer Man' revenge on Ken's BMW and the Tuco deal as proof that the meth world's criminality spreads like a cancer into Walt's ordinary life, and 'Gray Matter' as Greek tragedy - pride refusing Elliot's money when a way out existed. Against On Liberty's libertarian pull, it finally argues addicts forfeit full rational autonomy and so may be 'caged' by paternalistic law.
- ch. 25 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Answers Walt's claim that drug laws are arbitrary by separating legality from morality and defending objective moral principles, chief among them do-no-harm and a 'clear-headedness principle' obligating us to shun mind-clouding substances - a principle it attributes to thinkers from the Buddha to Kant while borrowing Aristotle's ideal of becoming brave, temperate, just, and wise. It sorts drugs by whether they cloud the mind rather than medical-versus-recreational, weighing coffee, Hank's cigars and homebrew, Skyler's cigarettes, and meth's 1200-percent dopamine spike (Spooge's neglected kid in 'Peekaboo'). Verdict: meth use is straightforwardly immoral, and therefore so are selling and cooking it - meeting Douglas Husak's challenge to actually give reasons for condemnation.
- ch. 26 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Utilitarianism; The Harm Principle; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Asks what exactly makes a meth cook a 'bad guy' and finds the line hard to draw: Walt forces no one to use, so like tobacco farmers and gun makers he merely enables self-harm - yet Saul's proposed prison shanking and Walt's order to murder Gale show culpable enabling without direct harm. Gale's libertarian purity argument ('they're getting exactly what they pay for') and Prohibition economics even suggest that on utilitarian grounds skilled cooks might do less harm than worse ones, though the industry as a whole remains indefensible. The chapter's moral center is Jane's death - Walt's monstrous 'standing by' - and the closeness effect that makes the personal Krazy-8 murder feel worse than the gas; it concludes the good-guy/bad-guy frame itself fuels the failed war on drugs.
- ch. 27 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal) — Reads Walt's descent through Lacan's distinction between constructed reality and the Real: the cancer diagnosis is the first irruption puncturing his veil of ordinary family-and-job reality, and each trauma after - dissolving Emilio, killing Krazy-8 with an 'I'm sorry', the escalating 'memory lapse' lie - re-codes his reality further into the harsh 'Desert of the Real'. The chapter stresses that choice persists at each rupture (Walt could still confess), shows illegality itself becoming his true self (car sex after the school meeting, 'because it was illegal'), and reads the nameless horse of 'Caballo sin Nombre' as an image of the unspeakable Real itself.
- ch. 29 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Kuhn, Paul Feyerabend — Paradigm Shifts; Anything Goes (epistemological anarchism) — Surveys the philosophy of science through Breaking Bad: Walt's authority trades on the trusted white-coat stereotype (Jesse's 'you're supposed to be a scientist', Tuco's CPR demand), while the chapter traces how modern science distinguished itself from alchemy via Kuhn's structure of scientific revolutions and Lakatos's research programmes, and how Feyerabend and the Traditional Knowledge movement challenge Western science's monopoly - echoed in Jesse's chili powder and the ingenious 'shake-and-bake' method. Walt and Jesse's quarrel over method resolves into an apprentice-master reconciliation ('you're an artist'), mirroring science's own shifting self-image. Ellul's grim prediction that technology, once embraced, cannot be made humane explains the ending: remission comes too late, and Walt may as well try to un-invent a weapon of mass destruction.
- ch. 30 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Feminism; Heteronormativity — A feminist media-studies audit: the first two seasons flunk most feminist criteria (Skyler the pregnant homemaker, Marie the kleptomaniac, Jane existing mainly to develop Jesse), but seasons three and four deepen everyone - Walt's agonized Krazy-8 decision curdles into the lily-of-the-valley child-poisoning, while Skyler seizes the household, launders money through the car wash, and fixes Ted's IRS crisis, 'breaking bad' herself. Gus's hinted homosexuality with Max is praised for quietly rocking heteronormative assumptions. Conclusion: by Season Four the title applies to Skyler too, and her depth alone nearly passes any analytical test a feminist scholar might employ.
- ch. 31 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Categorical Imperative; Utilitarianism; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Stages the standard three-way ethics debate on Walt: deontology's universalizability (Kant) absurdly forbids killing Krazy-8 even to save Walt's family, utilitarianism (Mill) licenses both cooking meth and murdering dealers - a outright contradiction - while Aristotle's virtue ethics judges character rather than acts and convicts Walt of vice: he endangers his family, misses Holly's birth, habituates himself into Heisenberg, and defrauds his school. Because acts and consequences alone cannot see character, virtue ethics is offered as the needed complement. The closing move widens the lens to America itself, arguing the capitalist success ethos and our appetite for anti-heroes like Tony Soprano and Dexter reveal a society that praises viciousness - 'we are what we repeatedly do.'
- ch. 33 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" **·** John Stuart Mill — On Liberty; The Harm Principle; Democracy — A provocatively sympathetic application of On Liberty: Mill's harm principle protects Jesse's drug use from punishment, and Mill's celebration of individuality and 'experiments of living' makes Walt's cooking an 'individual spontaneity' valuable precisely because it defies custom - Marie's anti-pot panic and Hank's beer-and-Cub-cigar double standard illustrating the 'magical influence of custom' and democracy's drift toward 'collective mediocrity'. Mill's temperance-era remarks that intoxication 'is not a fit subject for legislative interference' are extended from use to manufacture. The chapter's thesis: even when Walt is wrong, mere nonconformity 'is itself a service', making Heisenberg an unlikely hero of liberty and human progress.
- ch. 34 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 36 "Breaking Bad and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Bruce Springsteen and Philosophy - Darkness on the Edge of Truth

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Bruce Springsteen and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Popular Culture and Philosophy Series Editor George A. Reisch" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "“I Got this Guitar and I Learned How to Make It Talk”" **·** Embodiment (the lived body); The Extended Mind — The editors unpack Springsteen's 1988 speech inducting Bob Dylan: Elvis freed America's body from Cold War-era repression (duck-and-cover fear, Calvinist stiffness), Dylan freed the mind by bringing ambiguity ('Mr. Tambourine Man,' 'Maggie's Farm'), and Springsteen's own project was to free heart and soul — 'heartwork' in the early songs, 'soulwork' from Nebraska on. Their key philosophical move is embodied: you are not under the school desk because you are afraid, you are afraid because your body is under the desk, so intellectual freedom grows out of bodily freedom, and the guitar — flesh stretched over wood — literally talks for and with the audience. The revolution still fails, they concede, since hearts stay broken and the soul finds only hope in hard work, decency, family, and conversation.
- ch. 7 "I - Born to Run the USA" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "And the Good News Second" **·** Cornel West, Richard Rorty, Hilary Putnam — Pragmatism — Auxier tests Cornel West's four marks of prophetic thinking — discernment of who bears the social cost ('Ghost of Tom Joad,' 'Born in the U.S.A.'), empathy as human connection, self-critical tracking of hypocrisy, and audacious hope (the E Street concert as galvanizing the world-weary) — and finds Springsteen nearly unmatched, yet something is missing from reading 'The Rising' as prophecy. He stages the pragmatist debate between Rorty's poetic pragmatism (poets create the vocabularies democracy lives by), West's prophetic pragmatism, and Putnam's sober reasonableness, then uses Abraham Joshua Heschel to distinguish poet from prophet: inspiration versus revelation, gratitude versus dread, the seduction-and-coercion of the call he comically retells as Jonah in Asbury Park. A true prophet would sing, with Isaiah and Jeremiah, that even the 9/11 hijackers were the rod of America's own God against its greed — so Springsteen's 'The Rising' is lamentation, not prophecy.
