- ch. 9 "Quittin’ Time" **·** Karl Marx, Hannah Arendt — Alienation; Ideology and False Consciousness; Class Struggle — Michels surveys Marx (alienation, false consciousness, the Communist Manifesto's reduction of family to economics), Weber's Protestant work ethic, and Arendt's labor/work/action distinction, then reads Springsteen's whole career against them, from 'Factory' (the working life that 'takes his hearing' and leaves 'someone's gonna get hurt tonight') through 'Hungry Heart,' 'Mansion on the Hill,' and The Ghost of Tom Joad ('Youngstown,' 'Sinaloa Cowboys,' 'Straight Time'). The verdict: Springsteen is a man of the left but no Marxist — his characters struggle with their own restlessness and with each other rather than with class oppressors, the wealthy suffer their own bourgeois malaise, and work, however brutal, is no cause for revolution. Nor does he share Arendt's ideal: his realm of freedom is unapologetically middle-class — cruising in a cool car, hanging with friends, getting with your favorite girl, and always the radio.
- ch. 10 "You May Wonder How Come I Love You Care and Ethics" **·** Nel Noddings, Thomas Hobbes, John Locke — The Ethics of Care; The Social Contract; The State of Nature — Gimbel asks what the E Street Band's marathon multiple encores mean, starting from social contract theory: Hobbes's state of nature ('no home, no job, no peace, no rest') and Locke's contract explain the ticket as a financial IOU — so a drunk, short Hendrix show wrongs you financially but not morally — while the contractual view of moral duty (the 'Hungry Heart' narrator who abandoned his wife and kids in Baltimore) cannot explain why a missing encore feels like personal betrayal. The gift theory fails too, since encores are expected, and expected things are not gifts. Care ethics (Ruddick, Noddings) fits instead: devoted fans make the band part of their identity, Springsteen sings 'to you about you,' and the No Nukes 90-minute set became permanent because he would not give his people less — while 'Pink Cadillac' shows the limit case, since you cannot love someone for her Cadillac.
- ch. 11 "Unity Born from Diversity" **·** John Dewey — Democracy; Democracy as a Way of Life; Cosmopolitanism — Shook narrates the 1984 Born in the U.S.A. tour — George Will's 'Yankee Doodle Springsteen' column, Reagan's Hammonton speech, Springsteen's refusal to be either campaign's tool — and reads the song as the hopeless story of a Vietnam-generation man's living death (the refinery that won't hire him, the dead brother and the Saigon woman) mistaken by flag-waving crowds for a patriotic anthem. He then maps the song's possible meanings onto democracy's possible destinies: Carl Schmitt's friend–enemy politics of national homogeneity and permanent emergency, Schlesinger's assimilationist Disuniting of America, and liberal cosmopolitanism's citizens of the world. Dewey's The Public and Its Problems wins: democracy must intelligently manage rather than eliminate diversity, each citizen holding a double loyalty to subgroup and to the democratic 'great community,' so being born in the U.S.A. is a responsibility and pride is properly placed in democratic ideals.
- ch. 12 "Springsteen’s Revolution" **·** Herbert Marcuse, Theodor Adorno, Socrates — One-Dimensional Man; The Culture Industry; Socratic Method (elenchus) — Anderson and Anderson open with the Che Guevara t-shirt problem and Marcuse's one-dimensional man: the system assimilates protest, so internal critique (Springsteen on the cover of Rolling Stone, its Chevrolet calendar, the Eagles sold at Wal-Mart, Adorno's verdict on co-opted popular music) is a sop to Cerberus. Against this they set Springsteen's own account of his music as 'a very long conversation with my audience' — a transactional public service — and argue he survives the co-optation charge as a 'Socratic revolutionary' who, like Socrates refusing to leave Athens, works from within: listening without obeying ('I liked the old Bruce better'), refusing to ride the Born to Run wave, turning to Guthrie and Seeger, and drawing no hard conclusions, only opening spaces of reflection ('Born in the U.S.A.', 'Radio Nowhere', 'Long Walk Home'). Revolution is just another form of work — practice, sweat, and the personal job of 'growin' up.'
- ch. 13 "II - When We Were Wild and Innocent" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "You Can’t Tell the Players Without a Program" **·** Plato, Socrates — The Philosopher King; Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); Theory of Forms — Auxier revives Plato's ancient quarrel with the poets — the Republic's banishment of imitators whose pretty words are copies that stir the masses' feelings, the philosopher-king's city, the Ion's contempt for rhapsodes, Socrates' wisdom as Delphic acknowledgment of ignorance — while branding Plato an elitist and 'Poetry NIMBY.' He then stages a fantasy dithyramb contest at the Meadowlands where Dylan ('Subterranean Homesick Blues') and Springsteen ('Blinded by the Light') trade internal rhymes and alliteration before Weird Al's palindromes deflate Bob's seriousness. The virtuous poet wins all nine Muses by wooing their mother Mnemosyne — tapping the collective memory of the race with gratitude and discipline — which explains why Springsteen, unlike the prideful Dylan who lost the Muses, retains moral authority; the philosophers, needless to say, are just jealous of the bar.
- ch. 15 "A Fork in the Road" **·** Martha Nussbaum, Henry David Thoreau — The Capabilities Approach; Feminism — Anderson contrasts two American freedoms through Springsteen and Tracy Chapman: the negative freedom of 'Racing in the Street,' 'Thunder Road,' and 'Born to Run' — Thoreauvian wildness, the outlaw who blows off duty, wins the dude from L.A.'s money and 'little girl' as a prize, then finds her crying on her daddy's porch — against the positive freedom of Chapman's 'Fast Car' heroine, who initially sees the fast car as a way to 'be someone' but finally sends the aimless drinker off and seeks empowerment of her own abilities. Drawing on Margaret Fuller (men squander freedom on appetite and domination), Charlotte Perkins Gilman (male-built structures channel women's freedom through men), and Nussbaum (flourishing, failure built into the structure), he argues that male negative freedom is dialectically coupled to women's oppression, that justice must trump the call of the wild, yet that men's own collateral damage — desolation, alienation — must also be addressed.
- ch. 16 "The Kiss" **·** René Descartes — Philosophy of Love; The Cogito — Auxier reads 'Born to Run' as Peter Pan's seduction of Wendy, drawing on Barrie's novel: Mrs. Darling's withheld kiss becomes Peter in Wendy's imagination, the narrator at the nursery window ('Wendy let me in') offers the everlasting kiss, and the glockenspiel is Tinkerbell. The philosophical war is imagination versus reason: Descartes — Captain Hook — wields the chiliagon and the cogito to convict imagination of unreliability, while Vico's imaginative universals (Wendy, Captain Hook, the city-as-altar with its downtown heart and necropolis, which is why Newark and Camden's decay strikes us as defilement) show that reason itself is a modification grown from myth, memory, and Darwin's crocodile of natural history. Since 'I imagine, therefore I am' grounds what 'I think' presupposes, the lonely rider can take Mrs. Darling's kiss 'quite easily' — the song's climax teaches Wendy to fly to a Neverland outside time where the kiss lasts forever.
- ch. 17 "III - The Dark Edges" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "Reason to Believe When There’s None" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Friedrich Nietzsche — Existentialism; The Leap of Faith; Authenticity — Couch takes a night drive through Springsteen's road songs guided by Karl Jaspers's 'ultimate situations' — death ('Wreck on the Highway,' the bottomless gorge under the cruise-controlled life), suffering (the never-ending badlands, shared in Tom Joad's ghost), struggle (violent like 'Johnny 99,' loving as the search for 'one clear moment of love and truth'), and guilt ('Everybody's got a secret, Sonny'; 'you learn to sleep at night with the price you pay'). Jaspers's Existenz-philosophy — built on Kierkegaard's inward self and Nietzsche's diagnosis of the herd, against Kant's optimism about knowledge — explains the songs' endless border-crossings as attempts to evade limits that cannot be evaded, and the fear that turns 'your God filled soul' to 'devils and dust.' The answer to how there's a 'Reason to Believe' is Jaspers's leap of philosophic (not religious) faith toward the unknowable Transcendent, chosen in complete freedom.
- ch. 19 "Case the Promised Land" **·** Ralph Waldo Emerson — Personal Identity; Self-Reliance — Herstein reads 'Thunder Road' as an event, not an object: the screen door slam and Mary's waving dress form a single event that language can only serially narrate, and Whitehead's event ontology (with Ricoeur's Time and Narrative on how we discover and rewrite ourselves in stories, and Emerson's 'monstrous corpse' of memory from 'Self Reliance') explains why the Seanachie's tale creates a world rather than describing one. His own autobiographical turn — first hearing the song while stuck in a dead-end life, then at the 1980 Los Angeles 'Factory' performance watching Springsteen's voice resolve two warring rhythms by adding a third — shows narrative running backwards, retelling and redeeming the past, which is why the burnt-out Chevrolets are already behind the dirty hood. The solo-harmonica version strips away porch, vision, and redemption to desolation, yet the conclusion is freedom: because the events of the world await our creative retelling, you are the Seanachie of your own life, and 'maybe I am that young, after all.'
- ch. 20 "Celebrating Transience" **·** Martin Heidegger, Albert Camus, John Dewey, William James — Being-Toward-Death; The Absurd — Kaag reads 'Atlantic City' (Nebraska) as a parable of fatalism: after the Chicken Man bombing in Philadelphia and stuck with 'debts no honest man can pay,' the narrator takes the Coast City bus and finally agrees to 'do a little favor' for a criminal. Following the American philosopher John J. McDermott's philosophy of transience, the refrain 'Everything dies, baby, that's a fact / but maybe someday everything comes back' holds Heidegger's ownmost death together with tentative pragmatic hope. Suburban 'winners' who leave before dark are dead to experience, while the transient, like Camus's indifferent Stranger in 'Nebraska' ('I guess there's just a meanness in this world'), must celebrate meaning in the fleeting qualitative moments of the journey itself.
- ch. 21 "Back from the Edge" **·** William James, John Dewey — Pragmatism; The Will to Believe; The Meaning of Life — McKenna and Pratt use McDermott's 'Promethean self' and meliorism to answer the riddle of Nebraska's closer 'Reason to Believe': among the dead dog, the deserted bride and the groom left at the altar, 'still at the end of every hard earned day people find some reason to believe.' They trace Springsteen's shift from escape songs ('Born to Run,' 'Thunder Road') to life at the edge ('Badlands,' 'Darkness on the Edge of Town,' and the two midnight drivers of 'State Trooper' and 'Open All Night'), arguing meaning is made by cutting and connecting relations at the margins. Belief without evidence of salvation is legitimate hope but illegitimate certainty; the reason to believe is Jamesian meliorism — making things 'not perfect, simply better.'
- ch. 22 "Hometown Security" **·** John Dewey, Aldo Leopold — Democracy as a Way of Life; The Land Ethic; Pragmatism — Keith reads 'My Hometown' — whitewashed windows, the textile mill closing, Bruce telling his son 'take a good look around' — through Putnam's Bowling Alone, Dewey's socially constituted self, and Mead, to argue that Bruce and Kate should recommit to their dying town rather than flee in the moving van. Leopold's 'two spiritual dangers in not owning a farm' and Berry's healthy community as ecosystem ground an 'enlightened localism' that avoids both the town's racist parochialism and rootless cosmopolitanism. Volunteering, local food, and town-meeting democracy are the hometown security the song's sadness calls for.
- ch. 23 "Moments of Redemption" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Plato — The Problem of Evil; Platonic Love (the ladder of love) — Ventimiglia sets Augustine's three answers to the problem of evil (free will, the toddler-at-the-doctor 'greater good,' evil as privation) against Nebraska's title track, where mass murderer Charlie Starkweather's 'I guess there's just a meanness in this world' makes Augustine's metaphysical gymnastics look like sophistry. 'Atlantic City' and 'Johnny 99' show free will compromised by debts, job loss and winners-and-losers, and Springsteen neither glorifies nor explains evil away. What Bruce and Augustine share is the yearning for redemption: Plato's Symposium pictures Eros — poor, barefoot, homeless, a hunger for the beautiful — and Springsteen answers that hunger with temporary moments of deliverance ('meet me tonight in Atlantic City') rather than guaranteed salvation.
- ch. 24 "IV - Tom Joad’s Ghosts" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Driftin’ Off to a Foreign Land" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Albert Camus — The Myth of Sisyphus; Intentionality; The Absurd — Dick explains how 'Straight Time' makes us see Charlie's cycle of drudgery through Sartre's The Imaginary: the title is a Sartrean sign that welds prison time, rendering-factory time and straight-and-narrow time into one image of endless oppression, and Bruce's first-person characterization works like Dana Carvey impersonating Jimmy Stewart — consciousness overlooks discrepancies until we recognize 'our' Charlie. Against the escape fantasy of 'Thunder Road,' Camus's Sisyphus frames Charlie as swapping rocks (prison, factory, his uncle's car-theft heist) while the hill remains and 'there is no top of the hill.' Springsteen's answer to the absurd is to climb alongside his characters in empathetic brotherly love.
- ch. 26 "The Final Word That’s Right, I Said Preliminary Thoughts" **·** Socrates, Friedrich Nietzsche, Michel Foucault, Jacques Derrida, Augustine of Hippo — Socratic Method (elenchus); Perspectivism; Deconstruction; Power/Knowledge — Patton starts from wildly disputed Springsteen interpretations — Reagan's 1984 campaign use of 'Born in the U.S.A.,' Flint protesters playing 'My Hometown' as civic pride, Kerry's 'No Surrender' — and asks whether any reading is better than another. He frames the issue as Socratic dialectic versus Gorgias-style rhetoric, passes through Augustine's polysemy (multiple intended levels of meaning, as in 'I'm on fire') to Nietzsche's claim that the text disappears beneath its interpretation, extended by Foucault (truth as an instrument of power) and Derridean deconstruction. His conclusion: authorial intent cannot settle meaning, but some readings ('Born in the U.S.A.' as pro-Reagan anthem) are non-starters, otherwise it is rhetoric all the way down and the sophists win.
- ch. 27 "Lonesome Day" **·** Aristotle, Plato, Jean-Paul Sartre — The Knowledge Argument (Mary's Room); Existence Precedes Essence — Calef asks how fictional story-songs can teach: lyrics yield neither know-how nor know-that, since Plato's Theaetetus shows true belief is not knowledge (the 'Meeting Across the River' narrator's plan works only by luck). The answer is a third kind — knowing what something is like: 'The River' lets a comfortable listener inhabit Mary's husband's slide into a dry marriage, and 'Nebraska' lets us feel Starkweather's numb void, 'what it's like to have no soul.' Aristotle's Poetics (we delight in imitation because learning pleases; poetry is more philosophic than history because it speaks in universals) explains why sad songs satisfy us, and Sartre's universal human condition grounds the empathy that makes The Rising render 9/11 ('You're Missing,' 'Empty Sky') thinkable at a personal scale.
- ch. 28 "Springsteen’s Martyrdom" **·** Socrates, Plato — Socratic Method (elenchus); Justice in the Republic; Ring of Gyges (why be moral?) — The Bells parallel Socrates' forcible 'abduction' to the house of the arms merchant Cephalus in Republic Book I with Springsteen's 1984 political abduction, when Reagan and Mondale both pretended he had endorsed them and George Will invited him to be laureate of 'joyous overconsumption.' They read the Republic's luxurious city — its Ring of Gyges parasites, censorship of poets, and wars born of appetite — as an elenchus run against the control (epoche) of the healthy working city of Book II, so readers who take the 'ideal' city at face value err exactly like fans who hear patriotism in 'Born in the U.S.A.' Songs like 'Youngstown,' 'The Angel' and 'Galveston Bay' likewise critique overconsumption, imperialism and the censorship of artists, and both artist-critics risk the martyrdom Socrates suffered for speaking truth to the powerful.
- ch. 29 "Epilogue" **·** Martin Heidegger, Michel Foucault, Immanuel Kant, Aristotle — The They-Self (das Man); Authenticity — Auxier's epilogue teaches readers to build playful 'ontologies' from the book's list of Springsteen's proper names — imagining Elvis's .44 Magnum ('Betsy') traveling from a Vegas evidence room into 'Johnny 99' and '57 Channels,' and asking 'Would Bruce ride a Honda?' He borrows Heidegger's idea that the answer already lives in the question, and uses das Man — the inauthentic 'they-self' that chooses by appearances — to contrast Harley riders who confront an existential decision with Honda's self-conserving middle-class pragmatism. The Harley as 'suicide machine' (like Captain America in Easy Rider, like Jesus's passion) embodies freedom and authenticity; Foucault's Borgesian Chinese encyclopedia reminds us that orders of things are conventional, so the ontology itself is one imaginative path among many.
- ch. 30 "V - Greetings from . ." *(front)*
- ch. 31 "Things" *(notes)*
- ch. 32 "Hey, That’s Me" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "Bruce Springsteen and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Buffy the Vampire Slayer and Philosophy - Fear and Trembling in Sunnydale

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Buffy the Vampire Slayer and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Popular Culture and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "What a lot of fun—you guys have been real swell" *(notes)*
- ch. 7 "And was there a lesson in all this" **·** Aristotle, Plato — Editor James B. South's introduction explains how the Season One finale 'Prophecy Girl' turned him from a skeptic into a viewer who found the show 'worth my thinking about.' He states the book's two-way thesis: philosophy can illuminate BtVS, and the show offers genuine occasions for philosophical reflection on problems like nihilism, human irrationality, friendship, and punishment. Against the intimidation of technical academic philosophy (he cites Rawls's A Theory of Justice), he appeals to Plato's point that we cannot escape popular culture and to Aristotle's distinction between leisure and relaxation, arguing BtVS shows that television-watching can itself become the leisure activity in which philosophy is done.
- ch. 8 "Codex 1 - It’s kind of a Slayer thing. Buffy, Faith, and Feminism" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "The Buffyverse’s Moral Compass" **·** Plato, Friedrich Nietzsche, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Tripartite Soul — Greg Forster argues the Buffyverse's moral structure is neither Christian nor Nietzschean vampire fiction but Platonic eudaimonism: the just person is always happier than the unjust. Faith is the test case — her pleasure-seeking turn to evil with Mayor Wilkins, her body-swap theft of Buffy's life in 'Who Are You?', her self-disgust ('You're nothing! Disgusting, murderous bitch!'), and her begging Angel to kill her in 'Five by Five' enact Plato's Republic examples of the Ring of Gyges and the unjust person mistakenly honored while the just one is punished. Buffy's invisible crime spree in 'Gone' and her 'the work that I have to do' sacrifice in 'The Gift' display the same eudaimonist pattern, with the caveat that the Buffyverse follows a Rousseau-like moral psychology in which compassion, not reason, does the moral work.
- ch. 10 "Persuasion As Drama" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Socrates, Aristotle — Master and Slave Morality; The Übermensch; Ressentiment; Will to Power — Karl Schudt contends that Nietzsche's genealogy of slave morality cannot be refuted by reason (Nietzsche denies reason itself), so drama must persuade where dialectic cannot: a believable story of a Nietzschean life acts as an empathic experiment. Faith is constructed as the Nietzschean ideal — strong, healthy, ruthless ('Find a couple of studs, use them, and discard them'), dismissive of reflection ('You think too much') — serving Mayor Wilkins, whom Schudt reads as a genuine Übermensch sacrificing mankind's mass for his ascension. Watching her world collapse (the accidental killing in 'Bad Girls', her mirror scene in 'Who Are You?', the torture of Wesley and confession on Angel) gives viewers an Aristotelian pity-and-fear warning that the unjust life ends badly, completing her 'leap from Nietzsche to Socrates.'
- ch. 11 "“The Graduation” Autonomy and the Relational Self" **·** Carol Gilligan — The Ethics of Care; Feminism; Utilitarianism — Jessica Prata Miller reads Buffy through Carol Gilligan's care perspective: Buffy's self is relational (the Scooby Gang, 'Dawn is a part of me' in 'The Gift'), her moral reasoning contextual rather than rule-bound (caging Oz instead of killing the werewolf, rejecting Riley's Initiative 'demons bad' abstraction), and her emotions a moral resource ('My emotions give me power'). She also weighs the criticisms — Buffy's secret harboring of Angel exposed in 'Revelations,' and Faith's utilitarian 'plus column' defense of hiding a killing, which Buffy rejects ('I am' the one who cries over the bystander). Comparing the three Slayers, Miller argues only Buffy attains moral autonomy: Kendra's unquestioning obedience and Faith's hyper-individualistic action-hero isolation both fail, while Buffy's 'I like to think of it as graduation' break from the Watchers' Council shows autonomy achieved within, not against, relationships.
- ch. 12 "The Noir Consequences of Sex Eros, Death, and the Possibility of Love" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Philosophy of Love; Feminism — Thomas Hibbs reads BtVS as feminist noir: like classic noir it traps characters in labyrinths where the past bears down on the present (Buffy torn from heaven in 'After Life,' Angel's gypsy curse in 'Innocence,' the psych-ward ending of 'Normal Again'), and it rejects both Enlightenment optimism and the Nietzschean beyond-good-and-evil self-creation Hibbs finds in neo-noir like Body Heat and Thelma and Louise. Sex in Sunnydale always carries noir consequences — Angel losing his soul at the moment of 'true happiness,' Buffy's self-lacerating Spike affair ending in his attempted rape — so the show is skeptical of sexual liberation while recovering a classical eros as longing for wholeness. The Season Six finale locates the 'true essence of magic' in sacrificial love: Xander's yellow-crayon plea in 'Grave' reaches the 'spark of humanity' in Willow where all force fails.
- ch. 13 "No Damsel in Distress" **·** Mahatma Gandhi, Hannah Arendt — The Ethics of Political Violence; Feminism — Mimi Marinucci argues Buffy's violence tracks what agents do, not what they are: souled Angel and chip-enabled Spike receive moral consideration, willing human evildoers like Ford ('Lie to Me') and Ethan Rayne are fought, and Buffy's 'impression of Gandhi' in 'Anne' claims violence can rightly answer injustice. Building on William R. Jones's King–Malcolm X–Mao spectrum and Hannah Arendt's warning that doing violence habituates the agent, she reads the Slayer's fight as symbolic resistance to rape and women's oppression (bloodsucking as rape metaphor in 'Buffy vs. Dracula,' Spike's assault after Buffy ends the affair). The cost is real — 'Restless' reveals the First Slayer's primeval rage and staking with 'Mr. Pointy' mirrors sexualized violence — so she ends by endorsing tempered, appropriate violence (Buffy's Tai Chi training with Angel) over both passivity and aggression.
- ch. 14 "Codex 2 - Don’t speak Latin in front of the books Knowledge, Rationality, and" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "Smelly Knowledge" **·** René Descartes, Paul Feyerabend — Philosophical Skepticism; Scientific Realism vs Anti-Realism — Andrew Aberdein sorts the Buffyverse's science-supernatural encounters into three grades — reduction to present science, reduction to a future science, and the irreducibly supernatural — illustrated by Sunnydale's rationalizers and Giles's inner skeptic in 'Tabula Rasa,' the Initiative's DNA testing of Oz in 'New Moon Rising,' and the show's characteristic 'creepy mix of magic and science' (Jenny Calendar's 'the divine exists in cyberspace'). He reads 'Normal Again' through Descartes's deceiving-demon skepticism, links Spike's chip and Walsh's Adam to the Phineas Gage case (emotion is needed for rational decision), and maps the grades onto the ancients-versus-moderns quarrel and Feyerabend's methodological anarchism. Giles's 'smelly knowledge' speech about books versus computers anticipates a pluralist, socially sensitive epistemology that enriches rather than rejects science.
- ch. 16 "Pals" **·** William James, Paul Feyerabend, Karl Popper, Charles Sanders Peirce — Pragmatism; Anything Goes (epistemological anarchism); Falsificationism — Madeline Muntersbjorn uses Season Four's Initiative-versus-Scoobies conflict to dissolve the Science Wars: Professor Walsh's secret, unfalsifiable Project 314 makes her Karl Popper's bad scientist, while Buffy's gang embodies Feyerabend's pluralism and Peirce-and-James pragmatism — Buffyverse rules are probabilistic rather than deterministic, and the gang welcomes anyone willing to ask questions and do research. Her three ideals — pluralism, pragmatism, pals — culminate in 'Primeval,' where the combo-Buffy enjoining spell (Willow's spirit, Xander's heart, Giles's mind, Buffy's hand) literally enacts James's meliorism: the world may be saved if each agent does their level best, together. Walsh dies alone because she has no pals; Buffy saves the world 'a lot' because she does.
- ch. 17 "Conquering the Inner Demon" **·** Immanuel Kant, Plato — Humanity as an End in Itself; Transcendental Idealism; Autonomy of the Will; Allegory of the Cave — James Lawler casts Buffy as a contemporary allegory of Plato's cave-escaped philosopher and reads the show through Kant: the Slayer moves between a hellish lower world of instrumental appetite and an intelligible higher world of the soul, and Kant's Copernican revolution ('objects must conform to our knowledge') explains Marcie's invisibility in 'Out of Mind, Out of Sight' and the fear-made-flesh Master of 'Nightmares.' Kant's formula that every rational being exists as an end in himself grounds Buffy's duty in 'Anne' ('I am Buffy, the Vampire Slayer'), her rejection of Spike's chip as no substitute for a soul in 'Crush,' and her 'As You Were' admission that using Spike treats him as a mere means. Season Six explores 'too much heaven': Buffy's blissful death in 'The Gift' makes earthly life hell, and only conquering the inner demon — Willow's fear-driven dark magic defeated by Xander's yellow-crayon love in 'Grave' — restores the balance between heavens and hells.
- ch. 18 "Possibilities of Postmodern Education Beyond Control" **·** René Descartes, G.W.F. Hegel — The End of Grand Narratives; Deconstruction; Binary Oppositions — Toby Daspit reads Season Four as a drama of postmodern education: the Initiative's will to master and dissect — Walsh's Frankenstein-project Adam, who murders a boy to see how he works, fulfilling Wordsworth's 'we murder to dissect' — embodies the Cartesian split and the Hegelian drive to negate the other, while Buffy's world deconstructs fixed identity (Darla reversing the horror-movie opening, the Faith/Buffy body swap in 'Who Are You?', Jonathan's 'Superstar') and thwarts prophecy's metanarrative in 'Prophecy Girl.' Against modernist schooling's 'controlled circumstances' and Freire's banking model (Professor Riegert ejecting Buffy from class, Walsh's transmission lectures), the show models knowledge as collective work — the Scoobies' research sessions in Giles's library, Giles's fluid mentorship versus Kendra's by-the-book training, and the relational magic of 'Primeval' — offering education beyond control in an emergent, postmodern world.
- ch. 19 "Making the Pain Go “Poof”" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — James B. South argues Willow's Season Six turn — magic addiction, torturing Warren with a 'bored now,' attempting to end the world in 'Grave' — is tragic precisely because it is unintelligible: against Aristotle's account of tragedy, where pity requires seeing an intelligible mistake, Willow's destruction flows from Freudian depths (via Plato's 'lawless' desires and Jonathan Lear's 'enigmatic signifiers') that cannot be explained, only pointed to. The chapter traces the available evidence — her sidekick insecurity ('I'm not your sidekick!' in 'Fear, Itself'), her 'spurty knowledge' slip, making pain go 'poof' in 'Something Blue' — while insisting these unconscious swerves never add up to a reason for the final break. Xander's yellow-crayon memory in 'Grave' works not as explanation but as a second kind of break that opens space for Willow to regroup.
- ch. 20 "Codex 3 - You’re really enjoying this whole moral superiority thing, aren’t" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "Buffy As a Watcher (of Sorts)" **·** Virtue Ethics — Jason Kawal tests virtue ethics's 'act as the virtuous person would act' (Hursthouse's criterion) against Xander's 'What would Buffy do?': Buffy qualifies as a moral exemplar with heroic actions (nightly patrols, the 'Gift' plunge) and saintly sacrifices (the 'Doublemeat Palace' McJob for Dawn), but imitation fails — Xander lacks her abilities, tastes, relationships, and pure bravery, and stripping those away one by one turns Buffy into a copy of Xander. Worse, much of what Buffy does is supererogatory (Giles: 'She's a hero... She's not like us' in 'The Gift,' before killing Ben himself), so ordinary people are not morally required to do what she would do. His fix: ask what a fully informed, unimpaired virtuous observer — an 'angelic Buffy looking over our shoulders,' never drugged into 'Beer Bad' cave-Buffy — would deem right for us, given our own abilities and commitments.
- ch. 22 "Emotions and Aesthetic Response" **·** Epictetus, Plato, Aristotle, Martha Nussbaum, Immanuel Kant — Stoicism; Philosophy of Love; The Dichotomy of Control — Carolyn Korsmeyer uses BtVS to stage philosophy's ambivalence about emotion — dangerous passion versus intuitive sagacity — through three cases: anger as beast within and practical power (Xander's love spell in 'Bewitched, Bothered and Bewildered,' Willow's wish-spells in 'Something Blue,' 'My emotions give me power' to Kendra), grief as the one emotion no magic can undo (Epictetus's jug-and-child counsel set against 'The Body' and Dawn trying to resurrect Joyce), and love's non-fungibility (Anya's vengeance-demon past leaves her without the wider affection that makes singular love credible, while Spike's messy human history makes his obsession believable). She draws on Plato's wild-beast appetites and spirited element of the Republic, Aristotle on anger and emotional disposition as character, Kant's reason-above-nature picture, and Martha Nussbaum on art's particular cases. Finally she turns to aesthetic response: the show's comedy ('She saved the world a lot') regulates audience emotion, keeping the serial's catharsis from tipping into manipulation.
- ch. 23 "Erotic Love and Friendship" **·** Aristotle, William of Ockham, Augustine of Hippo — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Philosophy of Love; Ockham's Razor — Maps Aristotle's three levels of friendship onto Buffy's three boyfriends: Riley is a utility friendship chosen for the rational goal of a 'normal' boyfriend (echoed by Warren's robot April and the Buffybot, which prove programmed perfection cannot be loved), Spike is a pleasure friendship driven by passion and danger that Buffy ends in 'As You Were,' and Angel is a complete friendship where each values the other for his own sake despite the gypsy curse. This lets the authors solve Aristotle's love paradox (is erotic love chosen or does it choose us?) by adding Augustine's and William of Ockham's third power of the soul, the will: an arational faculty that values the other as an end in himself, neither rationally chosen nor irrationally caused.
- ch. 24 "Following the Moral Law" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself; Autonomy of the Will — Applies Kant's Formula of Universal Law and Formula of Humanity on two levels. Individually, Buffy's sacrifice in Dawn's place in 'The Gift' and her post-resurrection return to duty in 'Once More, with Feeling' show her will determined by duty rather than the inclination to a normal life, while Oz's werewolf episodes show inclination swamping reason. Systemically, the Slayer's coercion of demons is justified only because their actions destroy a Kantian state of right in Sunnydale, and Kant's account of radical evil — subordinating the moral law to the maxim of self-love — explains why demons and human villains like Warren's Trio are evil; the chapter closes worrying that the Slayer, like the Initiative's Adam, answers to no one.
- ch. 25 "Codex 4 - That’s the kind of wooly-headed thinking that leads to being eaten" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "Against Fascism" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil; The Ethics of Political Violence — Neal King argues the Buffyverse is proto-fascist: Buffy kills demons and vampires on sight within a race-like hierarchy, tempered only by an Augustinian split between natural and moral evil (soulless vampires cannot choose otherwise; soul-bearing humans like Faith, Willow, and Warren can repent), she derives authority from an ancient calling rather than democracy, and the graduation battle of 'Graduation Day, Part Two' stages citizenship forged in mass combat. He imagines a fascist Buffyverse of 'brownskirt' death squads and concludes only the show's secrecy prevents full fascism, finally recommending that the racism be dropped but the ritual sacrifice of evildoers kept — with Willow's flaying of Warren as the model.
- ch. 27 "Note to Self Religion Freaky" **·** Karl Marx — Ideology and False Consciousness — Surveys religion across BtVS and Angel to show it is almost always demonic — the Master's Order of Aurelius, Ethan Rayne's chaos god Janus, the First Evil's eyeless priests in 'Amends,' and Ken's fundamentalist 'Family Home' in 'Anne,' read through Marx's definition of religion as the opiate of the masses — while the heroes use crosses, holy water, and prophecy only instrumentally and never pray. Genuine miracles like the snow that saves Angel are rare and unattributed, prophecies are routinely evaded ('I flunked the written'), and evil is defeated by self-reliance and friendship, as when Xander's unmagical love stops Willow in 'Grave.' The conclusion: the moral center of the Buffyverse is human — neither secularized nor sacralized.
- ch. 28 "Buffy Should’ve Killed Ben" **·** Robert Nozick — Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Utilitarianism; Consequentialism — Jacob Held pits retributivism against utilitarian theories of punishment using three Buffyverse cases: vengeance demons Anya and Halfrek show that 'just desert' is arbitrary, relative, and subjective; Angel's gypsy curse (punishment as desert) secures society less reliably than Spike's Initiative chip (pure deterrence), while Oz's preventive caging raises the problem of punishing the innocent. The hard case is 'The Gift': Buffy refuses to kill the innocent Ben, but Held sides with Giles, who strangles him — arguing that since the state's end is security and freedom, one life may rightly be traded for six billion.
- ch. 29 "No Big Win" **·** The Meaning of Life; The Problem of Evil — Reads sacrifice, salvation, and redemption in the Buffyverse as secular myth: vampires manifest the shadow-side of the human (Liam's resentment becomes Angelus, William's repressed romanticism becomes Spike's rebellion), and the contrast between soulless Spike's genuine moral growth through love of Buffy and human Xander's arrested development shows redemption is measured by 'what have you done with what you've been given.' The Purpose of Evil section argues good and evil must stay in balance because without evil there is no free choice and hence no redemption, and Angel's 'no big win' epiphany — no grand plan, only lessening suffering one kindness at a time — locates life's meaning in the journey of becoming rather than final victory.
- ch. 30 "The Superhero As Liberal Reformer" **·** Karl Marx — Class Struggle; Alienation; Commodity Fetishism; The Ethics of Political Violence — Jeffrey Pasley traces the show's radical surface — capitalism equated with vampirism (Anya's greed, Glory the über-consumer, the Master's blood factory in 'The Wish'), Big Bads tearing down dimensional barriers exactly as Marx's Communist Manifesto describes the bourgeoisie dissolving feudal ties into free trade, Buffy refusing to commodify slaying into wage labor, and Giles's Magic Box selling mystified junk at inflated margins — but concludes the heroes are not revolutionaries. In the comic-book lineage of Superman and Spider-Man, Buffy and Angel are liberal reformers bound by a superhero code against killing, so Giles must murder the innocent Ben precisely to preserve Buffy's heroic mercy; Angel's 'champions' speech in 'Deep Down' states the incrementalist creed.
- ch. 31 "Codex 5 - You’re all slaves to the television Watching Buffy" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "The Moral Sophistication of Buffy the Vampire Slayer" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle — Humanity as an End in Itself; Deontology; Consequentialism; Animal Rights (subjects-of-a-life) — Greene and Yuen reconstruct seven moral principles from Buffy's conduct — slay those who typically harm humans, never harm humans (Faith's accidental killing of the Mayor's henchman), spare the defenseless and the controllable (chip'd Spike, caged Oz) — and note that blackmailing Spike and roughing up demons would violate Kant's Formula of Humanity if the patients were rational persons, exposing the show's human/non-human moral hierarchy and its animal-rights parallel. 'The Gift' dramatizes deontology (Buffy refuses to kill Ben) against consequentialism (Giles strangles him), and the chapter ranks BtVS above after-school-special, culpable-clown, and faux-realist TV morality because it supports its stands by appeal to general moral principles while keeping Aristotelian habituation and real-life moral complexity in view.
- ch. 33 "The Evolution of the Metaphor" **·** Jean Baudrillard — Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal) — Tracy Little argues the show takes teenagers' metaphorical speech — 'high school is hell,' 'after we slept together he became a different person,' 'graduation is the end of the world' — and makes it literal: hyena-possessed bullies in 'The Pack,' invisible Marcie whom nobody noticed, Angelus replacing Angel after Buffy and Angel's first night together, and the Mayor's ascension timed to graduation. Invoking Baudrillard's simulacra and hyperreality, the copy becomes more real than the original — the monsters are real but the social horrors they literalize are worse — and the seasons-one-through-three survey traces identity, love gone wrong, and end-of-the-world metaphors as they evolve from monster-of-the-week camp to weighty questions of life and death.
- ch. 34 "Psychical Dynamics of Love and Lust" **·** Philosophy of Love — Levine and Schneider apply Freud's 1912 essay on the debasement in love — 'where they love they do not desire and where they desire they cannot love' — to argue that BtVS succeeds not through innovation or moral seriousness but through the conventional 'girl next door': Angel's forbidden-vampire sex is the show's best because forbiddenness heightens desire, Xander's pedestal-placing of Buffy forecloses satisfaction, and Drusilla and Cordelia are already 'debased' sexual objects. Along the way they polemicize against Buffy scholars for projecting their own fantasies onto a show that reinforces, rather than subverts, gender stereotypes and the forbiddenness of sex for young women.
- ch. 35 "I have a hard enough time remembering what happened last week" *(interlude)*
- ch. 36 "Oh yeah Let’s look at your bio" *(notes)*
- ch. 37 "We’ve got a lot of important work here, a lot of filing, giving things names" *(notes)*
- ch. 38 "Buffy the Vampire Slayer and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Bullshit and Philosophy - Guaranteed to Get Perfect Results Every Time

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Popular Culture and Philosophy®" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "On Bullshitmania" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Bullshit and Philosophy" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — First part of the editors' introduction, recounting how Harry Frankfurt's slim On Bullshit, after two decades circulating only among academic moral philosophers, spent twenty-six weeks on the New York Times bestseller list and outsold any commercial philosophy book. Reisch and Hardcastle treat the book's popularity as a puzzle worth explaining: a technical exercise in conceptual analysis struck a public nerve. The chapter uses Frankfurt's own definition of bullshit as indifference to truth as the anchor for everything that follows.
- ch. 6 "Bullshit and Philosophy" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — The editors answer why On Bullshit hit a nerve using Jon Stewart's Daily Show interview with Frankfurt, where the audience laughed at the claim that the bullshitter does not care whether what he says is true. The unspoken elephant in the room was the Iraq war: the claims about weapons of mass destruction, remote-controlled delivery devices, and 9/11 complicity turned out, in the editors' words, to be best described by that word. They argue readers embraced the book as 'bullshit without tears,' a cushion of humor letting them approach the war's deceptions abstractly rather than face the disturbing political questions directly.
- ch. 7 "Bullshit and Philosophy" **·** Harry Frankfurt, Karl Popper — On Bullshit; Falsificationism — 'The Year in Bullshit' catalogues the fraud climate surrounding the book's success: James Frey's fabricated memoir A Million Little Pieces exposed on Oprah, the wholly invented author J.T. Leroy, product placement and paid word-of-mouth advertising blurring the line between ads and ordinary conversation, and scientific fraud in pharmaceutical and federal research. It invokes Karl Popper's claim that science deserves respect precisely because it falsifies its own claims, presenting headlines about fabricated results as that ideal fading. Frank Rich's verdict that 'such distinctions have long since washed away in much of our public life' supplies the chapter's explanation of the book's appeal as cultural despair.
- ch. 8 "Bullshit and Philosophy" **·** Socrates, René Descartes, David Hume, Martin Heidegger — On Bullshit; Methodological Doubt (evil demon); Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know) — 'The Dream of a Bullshit-Free Culture' argues Frankfurt revives a centuries-old philosophical tradition: Socrates searching for genuinely wise people rather than purveyors of hearsay, Descartes' Meditations discarding every belief short of certainty, and Hume's forkcondemning divinity-school metaphysics that contains neither abstract nor experimental reasoning to the flames. The twentieth-century apotheosis is the Vienna Circle, whose Rudolf Carnap dissected Heidegger's 'Das Nichts selbst nichtet' as mere appearance of meaning, calling metaphysicians 'musicians without musical ability'. The editors cast Frankfurt as the latest carrier of this Enlightenment anti-bullshit torch, with the book's popularity reconnecting seminar-room philosophy to the marketplace and voting booth.
- ch. 9 "I - To Shoot the Bull" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — The Part I introduction, 'How This Book Came to Be,' explains what the volume is and is not: not a bullshit-detector manual but discussions and criticisms of Frankfurt's essay, including what it means to propose a theory of bullshit or articulate the structure of a concept. It previews each contributor: Kimbrough on why we let bullshit slide, Preti's use of G.E. Moore as a bullshit-free exemplar, Reisch's two-conversations account, Taylor on cognitive foibles, Bernal on personality pathology, and the Part II definitional debate between Frankfurt and G.A. Cohen's 'unclarifiable unclarity'. It frames philosophy itself as one of our best defenses against bullshit.
- ch. 10 "Chapter 1 - On Letting It Slide" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "Tolerable Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Scott Kimbrough opens his essay with his six-year-old daughter shouting through a window that the house painter's smoking is unhealthy, and William Ian Miller's Faking It dictum that truth is seldom a defense. He takes up the puzzle Frankfurt left as an 'exercise for the reader': why our attitude toward bullshit is more benign than toward lying, even though Frankfurt defines bullshit as indifference to how things really are and therefore the greater enemy of truth. The chapter promises to solve the puzzle Frankfurt posed but never attempted.
- ch. 12 "Intolerable Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Applying Frankfurt's definition strictly, Kimbrough argues much of civility is bullshit: coerced childhood apologies are 'humiliation rituals' where truth is the last thing wanted, politeness is benign hypocrisy that saves people pain, and the Super Bowl ad or the silly communist-professors editorial is valued as entertainment. We also tolerate PR and advertising bullshit out of respect for the ends served, from Exxon's green image to spin by teachers and military recruiters. His answer to Frankfurt's puzzle: we sympathize with bullshitters doing their jobs and contemptuously dismiss their victims as suckers who deserve their fate.
- ch. 13 "Bullshit and Self-Deception" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Kimbrough turns to when bullshit becomes intolerable: the philosophy-slam regulars' dismissive 'That's bullshit,' the girlfriend's indictment in The Black Eyed Peas' 'Shut Up,' and the boss's team-builder rationalization for passing you over for a promotion. He leans on Orwell's 'Politics and the English Language' and Nineteen Eighty-Four to show political euphemism ('collateral damage,' 'smart bomb') as the language of power, tolerated because repeating spin is easy and going along with bullshit is going along with power. The chapter closes on the danger of politicians believing their own bullshit, setting up the self-deception question of how one person can play both bullshitter and dupe.
- ch. 14 "Blameless Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Kimbrough resolves the self-deception paradox using Alfred Mele's Self Deception Unmasked: like the cuckolded husband who masks evidence of infidelity, the self-deceiver biases evidence-gathering rather than playing deceiver and dupe at once. Since Mele shows unintentional motivated belief is common (94% of professors rate themselves above average), bullshit like the entrenched prejudice that 'smart boys are smarter than smart girls' can arise without intent, which is G.A. Cohen's opening against Frankfurt's process-centered definition. Against Cohen's output-centered alternative, the conservative-talk-radio fan who builds his own conclusions on cherry-picked propaganda shows a product can be perfectly clear yet still bullshit, produced through self-deception.
- ch. 15 "Bullshit and Truth" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Kimbrough defends calling alchemy bullshit even though Isaac Newton pursued it blamelessly: the charge's function is to excuse us from taking hopeless methods seriously, not to insult the theorist. His killer analogy sorts cases: the intentional bullshitter is the murderer, the self-deceived talk-radio fan the negligent driver who kills while reading, and Newton the blameless driver beside whom the pedestrian darts, with the pejorative resting on the method or theory rather than the person. This methodology shift lets him keep Frankfurt's insight that how bullshit is produced matters while conceding Cohen's point that it can be unintentional, though he admits even philosophy's status may never be settled.
- ch. 16 "Chapter 2 - A Defense of Common Sense" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Concluding his essay, Kimbrough observes people call bullshit whenever their values are threatened, so the bottom-line businessman dismisses ethics talk and investigations of his dodgy accounting as bureaucratic bullshit, and the linguist may dismiss armchair conceptual analysis itself. He rejects a subjective definition of bullshit as whatever provokes disdain, endorsing Frankfurt's anti-subjectivist closer that 'sincerity itself is bullshit,' and concludes bullshit must be recognized and restricted to justifiable uses rather than eradicated. He closes with Orwell's advice from 'Politics and the English Language': simplify your English so that a stupid remark's stupidity is obvious even to yourself.
- ch. 17 "The Truth Matters" **·** G.E. Moore, Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Consuelo Preti lays out Frankfurt's indifference-to-truth account and G.A. Cohen's academic bullshit as disregard for meaning, then argues both leave out bullshit's third element: the audience it needs. Bullshit, she says, sells us on our own estimation of what matters, from the Dior handbag to the guy bullshitting his way into bed or tenure. Reviewing the James Frey affair and Oprah's initial claim that truth was irrelevant because the story inspired, she proposes G.E. Moore's uncompromising commitment to truth and meaning as the model for resisting bullshit in the street and the seminar room.
- ch. 18 "“What Exactly Do You Mean ”" **·** G.E. Moore, Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Preti dissects l'affaire Frey: if Frey intended to palm off invented events as memory, he lied and his publishers committed fraud, but if they did not care either way, it was bullshit, and what exposed the menace was Oprah's own avowal that it did not matter whether the story was true. She argues inspiration tied to an assumed true story is sabotaged by discovering it false, so Oprah's retraction ('I left the impression that the truth does not matter') shows bullshit nearly turned even her head. The strategy she draws is to turn bullshit's need to be believed against itself, and for an exemplar of resolute caring about truth she reaches to G.E. Moore.
- ch. 19 "Let’s Stop Bullshitting Ourselves" **·** G.E. Moore, Bertrand Russell, Ludwig Wittgenstein — On Bullshit — Preti builds the Moore exemplar from biography: with Russell and Wittgenstein at Cambridge, Moore founded analytic philosophy, demolished Absolute Idealism in the 1899 'The Nature of Judgment,' and wrote Principia Ethica, which the Bloomsbury group treated as their religion. Russell's autobiography joke about the only lie Moore ever told and Woolf's 'tenacity of a bulldog' portrait of his passion for truth set up Keynes's description of Moore's method, 'What exactly do you mean?'. The key episode is Moore's public apology for losing patience with a question he took to be merely vexatious, showing that what Moore could not tolerate was precisely bullshit.
- ch. 20 "Chapter 3 - The Pragmatics of Bullshit, Intelligently Designed" **·** G.E. Moore — On Bullshit — Preti's conclusion deploys Moore's 'A Defence of Common Sense' against philosophers who answer 'Do you believe the earth has existed for many years past?' with 'It all depends on what you mean,' a view Moore called as profoundly mistaken as any can be. She extracts a practical anti-bullshit strategy: dogged Moorean insistence on clarity, since bullshit's scorn for truth must stay masked and needs us to be less inquisitive. Her 'paradox of bullshit' is that truth and meaning do not matter to bullshit but bullshit must make itself matter to us, so caring about the truth-false distinction puts it on a short leash.
- ch. 21 "The Example of Intelligent Design" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — George Reisch opens his essay with Frankfurt's unsolved puzzle: if bullshitters are more dangerous to truth than liars, they should be punished more, yet we are more tolerant of them, a puzzle Frankfurt admitted on radio he really does not understand. He diagnoses the puzzle as a symptom that Frankfurt's definition took a wrong turn. The remedy requires distinguishing the semantic from the pragmatic analysis of language, and the new definition it yields will both cover Frankfurt's own examples and explain our tolerance of bullshit.
- ch. 22 "A Definition of Bullshit—New and Improved!" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Reisch offers the Intelligent Design movement as a paradigm of bullshit Frankfurt could not have used in the 1980s: like advertisers and politicians, ID urgently needs public approval ('teach the controversy') and is trying to get away with something. He traces ID's descent from constitutionally defeated creation science through Philip Johnson's Darwin on Trial, showing how Johnson speaks of evidence and naturalism to scientists while telling evangelical audiences that the real enemy is cultural modernism's tolerance of promiscuity and atheism. Judge Jones's Kitzmiller ruling that ID is 'a mere re-labeling of creationism' confirms what the bullshit was covering.
- ch. 23 "The Truth in Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — If ID is bullshit, Reisch argues, Frankfurt's definition fails, because Johnson and his followers are not indifferent to truth; they are devoted to other, rotten truths about modernism and even to elevating regard for truth in the culture. Frankfurt's error was writing of 'the truth' as a single unitary thing everyone but the bullshitter agrees on, when our beliefs about how things really are are diverse and contradictory. Bullshitters conceal a commitment to other truths and an agenda inspired by them, since revealing those truths would be embarrassing, unpersuasive, or, as with ID, unconstitutional.
- ch. 24 "The Truth about Semantics" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Even Frankfurt's bull-session example (with its Animal House atom-of-a-fingernail bit) shows relaxed talk serving other truths: self-image, reputation, and whether the apple of one's eye is impressed. Detergent slogans and politicians' rolled-up sleeves likewise run two conversations at once, one overt (clean clothes, policy) and one concealed (self-image, likeability), each resting on truths the bullshitter takes quite seriously. Reisch's definition follows: bullshit is the orchestration of at least two engagements between bullshitter and bullshitee, one concealed within or downplayed alongside the other, so indifference to truth is absent or only partly in play.
- ch. 25 "Solving Frankfurt’s Puzzle, or, Baseball, Hot Dogs, Apple Pie, and Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Reisch explains Frankfurt's blind spot through the historical neglect of pragmatics: even Carnap took for granted that any complete theory of language must include how language is used in context, yet analytic philosophy came to favor semantics, as the Cambridge Dictionary's twenty-six semantics entries against seven pragmatic ones attest. Since bullshit's essence lies in using speech to hidden purposes, it is an essentially pragmatic, not semantic, phenomenon, which also shows the Frankfurt-Cohen debate (truth versus meaning) is narrow. From the recipient's side, bullshit works best when its two coordinated concerns seem maximally unconnected, as when a science-education advocate turns out to be an evangelical in disguise.
- ch. 26 "The Case for Purism about Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — The pragmatic definition dissolves Frankfurt's puzzle: the liar sins against the semantics of truth, while the bullshitter sins against pragmatics, disguising the effect his speech is designed to have (advancing science education) behind another (promoting evangelical Christianity). We can safely ignore bullshit, as with the coworker's Darwin rants, because without a gullible audience it has no effect, unlike the lie about whether the office milk has soured. Reisch adds that Stephen Meyer's post-Kitzmiller defiance, Willy Loman's optimism, the robber in Dog Day Afternoon, and Mamet's Glengary Glen Ross salesmen show why we even sympathize with bullshitters clinging to their creations, and that our tolerance extends to the pluralism of beliefs bullshit exploits.
- ch. 27 "Chapter 4 - Bullshit and the Foibles of the Human Mind, or What the Masters of" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Reisch concludes that on the pragmatic definition there is less bullshit than 'Bullshit!'-slinging suggests: your partner announcing the relationship is over and a White House claim about an oil-rich nation eager for democracy are merely true or false announcements, not bullshit. Real bullshit is the partner engineering a blame-free breakup through talk of feeling smothered, or the claim that a dictator possesses nuclear weapons about to be used. He closes by comparing bullshit to a poem or symphony: a specific, artfully layered creation intelligently designed for pragmatic effect, which our casual use of the word obscures to the bullshitter's advantage.
- ch. 28 "Some Cognitive Foibles of the Human Mind" **·** On Bullshit — Kenneth Taylor opens his essay on the debased stew of bullshit, propaganda, and spin in public discourse, arguing that in an open public square the bullshitting arts must become highly refined, whereas a totalitarian state can afford crude bludgeoning, and that the marketplace of ideas has not spontaneously driven out bad discourse. His central claim: purveyors cannot sell what we do not buy, so the fault lies partly in ourselves, in built-in architectural foibles of the mind that make it permanently susceptible to manipulation. bullshit works best when we do not recognize it, and susceptibility to comforting falsehood is rooted in the mind's very architecture.
- ch. 29 "Reclaiming the Public Square" **·** On Bullshit — Taylor illustrates the mind's foibles with the Iraq war beliefs that held a vice-grip on believers long after disconfirming evidence, explained by confirmation bias and information cocoons like Fox News and talk radio. He walks through the classic framing experiments, the avian-flu programs that are identical when described as '2,000 saved' or '4,000 die,' showing how politicians Smith and Jones exploit framing to jointly mislead voters, and the Wason selection task, where subjects fail on abstract cards but succeed when reframed as bartender cheater-detection. Citing Stephen Pinker on evolved, domain-specific modules, he concludes that whoever controls the frame may control all, which is exactly the leverage the masters of the dark arts exploit.
- ch. 30 "Chapter 5 - Bullshit and Personality" **·** On Bullshit — Taylor draws the hopeful and bleak conclusions: our minds are fine instruments for hunter-gatherer information environments, but the modern mismatch between savannah-designed cognition and deliberately framed misinformation explains why bull, spin, and propaganda abound. The masters of the dark arts are now astute students of cognitive foibles and wield means of persuasion with unprecedented reach. His two-front response is education that instills suspicion of comforting falsehoods and the habit of re-framing, plus widening access to the means of public representation now monopolized by a self-serving elite, because 'only those who seek more than bullshit can drive it out.'
- ch. 31 "Does the Bullshitter Pay Attention to the Truth" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Sara Bernal distinguishes public, interpersonal, and private bullshit (bullshitting oneself) and argues that in an era rich in public bullshit like the Iraq war, private bullshit should not be overlooked, since it is at the core of personality disorders. Departing rapidly from Frankfurt, she rejects his claim that bullshit is essentially unconnected to a concern with truth, proposing instead that core cases of bullshit have a stronger connection to truth than Frankfurt allows. Applying her revised analysis to personality disorders will, she claims, explain why their core features produce social difficulties.
- ch. 32 "Two Modes of Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Against Frankfurt's picture of the bullshitter who pays no attention to truth at all, Bernal argues the typical bullshitter is strongly connected to truth via a desire to obscure a specific part of it, conscious or not. Her prototype is the undergraduate who, knowing she has nothing thoughtful to say on an assigned essay, throws up a screen of verbiage, sometimes with malice aforethought, sometimes through a barely conscious motive to get an undeserved A. Unlike the liar she never denies the hidden truth directly but works indirectly, through pompous tone, reluctance to get to the point, and windiness, or simply by distracting the audience.
- ch. 33 "Bullshit and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "Bullshit and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 35 "Bullshit and Personality Disorders" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Bernal gives the Bush administration's yellowcake-from-Niger and Saddam-9/11 red herrings as distraction bullshit concealing the antecedent intention to invade Iraq, then states five features of her two-part conception: the bullshitter must attend to the one 'target' truth she wants to hide, bullshit shades into lying as awareness increases, unconscious motives do the work, non-verbal means suffice (the Newport ads), and self-bullshitting is a species of self-deception. Split-brain patients who confabulate 'that funny machine' to explain unaccountable giggling at a picture shown only to the right hemisphere ground the claim that unconscious motives are respectable neuroscience. Her account, unlike Frankfurt's, applies in psychiatry.
- ch. 36 "Bullshit and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 37 "Some Examples of Bullshit in Personality Disorders" **·** On Bullshit — The essay defines personality disorder via DSM-IV as an enduring, inflexible pattern deviating from cultural expectations, and catalogs the ten disorders in three clusters: the erratic (antisocial, with the Enron crew and glib con artists as textbook cases; borderline; histrionic, from the undergrad who visits professors' office hours to talk about herself; narcissistic), the anxious (avoidant, dependent, obsessive-compulsive), and the eccentric (schizoid, schizotypal, paranoid). The clue to where bullshit lurks: wherever the afflicted is out of touch with some key aspect of reality or where her stock behavior leads others to false beliefs. The disorders' rigidity makes life 'a bad one-act play that repeats again and again.'
- ch. 38 "Bullshit and Philosophy" **·** On Bullshit — The essay works through the disorders case by case: the antisocial's glib charm implicates 'I am not a nasty piece of work' without saying it, the borderline's suicidal gestures implicate 'life without you is not worth living' that her contempt belies, the histrionic's shallow opinions and sexual provocativeness implant false impressions of depth, and the compensating narcissist's charade of superiority hides a dimly glimpsed core of low self-esteem. Self-directed bullshit follows: the fanatic paranoid's superhero fantasies deny that she is not extraordinary, the phobic avoidant displaces anxiety onto elevators and dogs, the self-deserting avoidant escapes into fantasy, dependents distract themselves from doubts about their caregiver, and the obsessive-compulsive buries big-picture anxiety in rules and lists.
- ch. 39 "Perfect Partners Bullshit and Distorted Social Perceptions" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Surveying the catalog, the essay finds bullshit strategems that are first-rank symptoms in eight of ten disorders, quoting Frankfurt's 'each of us contributes his share' to observe that pathological personalities are notably generous contributors. Bullshitting is notably absent from the eccentric cluster: the schizoid's flat affect removes the pain-avoidance motive, and schizoids and schizotypals lack the mindreading facility successful bullshit requires. Other-bullshitting (mostly falsehood-implicating) marks the erratic cluster, self-bullshitting (mostly distraction) the anxious, and the essay closes by stressing that the disordered person is afflicted, her bullshit a personal tragedy as well as an infliction.
- ch. 40 "The Threat Posed by Bullshit" **·** On Bullshit — The essay's final section argues distorted social perception and bullshit are perfect partners that reinforce each other: the paranoiac imagining insults, the histrionic inhabiting a world of admirers, and the borderline seeing abandonment everywhere each see less of social reality, supplying more 'easy targets' to obscure without the sting of conscience that aware deception provokes. The compensating narcissist's glimmering of her charade and the self-deserting avoidant's glimmering of her escape show how dim awareness eases bullshitting. The street runs both ways, since hiding parts of social truth from yourself further impairs your perception, so some disorder bullshit is causally downstream of distortion while self-bullshitting deepens it.
- ch. 41 "Chapter 6 - Performing Bullshit and the Post-Sincere Condition" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Concluding an essay on personality disorders, this chapter argues that pathological personalities (the antisocial, borderline, dependent, paranoiac) are especially good bullshitters and that their bullshit and distorted perceptions are what wreck their social relationships, since good relationships thrive on truth as much as on truthfulness. Against Frankfurt's claim that bullshit is the greater enemy of truth because indulging it erodes attention to reality, the author counters that the bullshitter does track truth at some level; bullshit's distinctive danger is that, unlike lying, it can be unconscious, so the sting of conscience never checks the habit.
- ch. 42 "Mission Statement" *(front)*
- ch. 43 "Shitty Attitudes On the Use and Misuse of Bullshit in Life" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — This mini-essay opens by parodying a puffing university mission statement, aspiring to be 'one of the world's best philosophy essays' in the UBC mold. It then sets the agenda: Frankfurt left unanswered why our attitude toward bullshit is generally more benign than our attitude toward lying, and never considered bullshit's rhetorical uses and misuses - the two gaps the rest of the essay fills.
- ch. 44 "Bullshit as a Condition of Life" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Reworking Frankfurt's Fania Pascal anecdote (Wittgenstein's disgusted 'You don't know what a dog that has been run over feels like'), the chapter argues that the bullshit that earns the retort 'That's bullshit!' is speech whose truth is irrelevant and which aims to evoke a positive attitude toward the speaker - hypochondriac pain complaints, boilerplate about graduate training in grant proposals, praise of the Dean to pass a hiring motion. It separates this from bullshit-as-entertainment and from courtesy conventions like thanking the gift-giving aunt, concluding against Frankfurt that pretentiousness is constitutive of at least this central kind of bullshit.
- ch. 45 "The World as Will to Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — This section answers Frankfurt's exercise about benignity: bullshit is 'dishonesty without tears' because whole genres - grant proposals, Fourth of July oratory, letters of reference - generically require performative values everyone knows count for nothing, like compulsory figures in figure skating. Rejecting free-riding as the origin of bullshit genres (bullshit letters get found out), it explains the reference letter instead by its multiple interested audiences: the writer must satisfy the student, the admissions committee, and her own conscience at once, and only the right dose of bullshit accomplishes that.
- ch. 46 "Overcoming Overwhelming Bullshit" **·** On Bullshit — The chapter indicts an expanding class of 'performative bullshit': the video-store service pledge and the UBC mission statement, where a commitment is treated as fulfilled in the very act of being made, so no complaint can even be lodged. George W. Bush's fabricated WMD case for the Iraq war shows the political version - an argument counts as good simply because it was offered as the reason, and when the intelligence proved false another reason was fabricated. In such a world sincerity, irony, and even cynicism become impossible, since only pseudo-motives remain, lacking even sincere self-interest.
- ch. 47 "Chapter 7 - The Importance of Being Earnest A Pragmatic Approach to Bullshitting" **·** On Bullshit — Closing the essay, this section argues the bullshit world is inherently stable rather than unstable: the administrator whose whole professional being is appending mission-talk to whatever the university already does is its emblem. The one live remedy is the Jon Stewart gambit - The Daily Show's openly fake news outperforming 'serious' sources, showing by self-consciously flouting the conventions of truth-telling what those conventions were for - and beyond that, actually taking a mission statement seriously enough to rethink higher education's values rather than settle, undeserving, for bullshit.
- ch. 48 "Two Tauroscatological Schools" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — De Waal's opening salvo: Frankfurt analyzed the concept of bullshit by ordinary language, but the interesting question is the intention with which bullshitting is produced. He announces a contrast between bullshitting and genuine inquiry, and blames bullshit's prevalence partly on a lost confidence that figuring out how things really are is possible or worthwhile.
- ch. 49 "The Epistemic Imperative" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Building on G.A. Cohen's split of bullshit thinking into a structuralist school (bullshit is an intrinsic feature of products, e.g. texts unclarifiably unclear whatever the author intended) and an intentionalist school (Frankfurt: bullshit is the act done with indifference to truth), the chapter defends intentionalism against the 'insight problem'. The sham astrologer whose Tarot-based celebrity-death prediction happens to come true, and Perry's undergraduates who aced an exam they bullshitted, show that a claim stays bullshit until someone not bullshitting has affirmed it and thereby transformed it into knowledge - just as one person's trash is another's treasure without the chair itself changing.
- ch. 50 "The Problem with Bullshitting" **·** Charles Sanders Peirce, Harry Frankfurt — The Pragmatic Method; On Bullshit; Philosophical Skepticism — Against the bullshitter the chapter recruits Peirce: genuine inquiry is defined not by method or results but by the attitude of truly wanting true answers, so homicide detectives, philologists, and car mechanics all count as scientists in this sense. Bullshitting is a free-rider riding the bus of real inquiry - even the bullshitter wants his doctor not to bullshit - and the chapter formulates an epistemic imperative: proceed on the hope that there is a true answer and act from the desire to find it, with fallibilism, not skepticism, the viable stance (the passenger who can occupy any vacant seat cannot occupy them all).
- ch. 51 "So Why Bullshit" **·** Charles Sanders Peirce, Harry Frankfurt, Richard Rorty — The Pragmatic Method; On Bullshit; Truth as What Works — Violations of the epistemic imperative are sorted into Peirce's 'sham reasoning' (the creationist who reasons only to prop up a fixed conclusion), Haack's 'fake reasoning' (corporate-funded research, vote-seeking politicians, the temporary-insanity plea), and the skeptic's premature 'we'll never know' - none of which is bullshitting, which is precisely not caring about truth, like the sales clerk praising shoes at random. Even philosophy can slide into bullshitting when, per Rorty, it becomes 'carrying on the conversation' with truth as what your peers let you get away with; and brainstorming differs from a bull session because brainstormers actually want to discover something.
- ch. 52 "II - The Bull by the Horns" **·** Harry Frankfurt, René Descartes, Charles Sanders Peirce — On Bullshit; The Pragmatic Method — Why do people bullshit? Because liberal democracy, heir to the Cartesian rejection of authority and the Enlightenment's think-for-yourself ideal, expects instant opinions on everything from people detached enough that nothing they say makes a difference; and because a failed search for certainty breeds prophylactic pessimism about inquiry itself. The cure is earnestness - scientists take colleagues' results on trust - though the chapter concedes defensible cases: the formulaic 'It's nice to see you!' as fake reasoning for civility, and friends horsing around for fun.
- ch. 53 "Chapter 8 - Deeper into Bullshit" *(front)*
- ch. 54 "1 Without the Shit of the Bull" *(front)*
- ch. 55 "2 Two Species of Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Cohen's autobiographical preface: his youthful struggle with Althusserian French Marxism of the 1960s - interpellation, contradiction and overdetermination - taught him that writings attracting reverent attention can still be bullshit, a lesson sealed when his Marxist discussion group took the name Non-Bullshit Marxism Group, 'Marxism without the shit of the bull'. He also diagnoses the psychology (cognitive dissonance reduction plus adaptive preference formation) by which readers of rebarbative texts overvalue the banal sense they finally extract.
- ch. 56 "3 Bullshit and Lying" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Via the OED's two entries, the chapter finds that 'bullshit' denotes two different things: nonsense or rubbish (output-centered - the shit wears the trousers) and insincere talk (producer-centered - the bull wears the trousers, Frankfurt's kind). Frankfurt's fault is not error but generality: he cast his account of one flower as the 'essence' of the whole garden, ignoring the academically more significant kind that is defined without any reference to a producer's state of mind.
- ch. 57 "4 Bullshit as Unclarifiable Unclarity" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Cohen's main critique: Frankfurt identifies the liar by his goal (misleading about reality) but the bullshitter only at the level of tactic (indifference to the truth of what he says), leaving the bullshitter no distinctive goal - so advertisers and politicians, Frankfurt's own paradigm bullshitters, actually pursue the liar's goal and their bullshitting and lying coincide. Freud's favorite Pinsk/Minsk joke shows tactic and goal can come apart (deceiving by telling the truth), and the Fourth of July orator, contra Frankfurt, might care very much that the audience believe things about the Founding Fathers.
- ch. 58 "5 Bullshit as Product and Bullshit as Process" **·** Harry Frankfurt, Martin Heidegger, G.W.F. Hegel, Ludwig Wittgenstein — On Bullshit — Cohen's positive account: bullshit is a feature of texts, paradigmatically 'unclarifiable unclarity' - discourse that cannot be rendered clear without ceasing to be a version of what was said, which is why the way to defend Hegel or Heidegger is to show their texts make sense, not that they cared about truth. A sufficient test is the negation sign: if adding or subtracting it makes no difference to plausibility, as with Sokal's hoax physics, the text is bullshit - though suggestively valuable poetry is spared. 'Bullshitter' then splits into a dispositional reading and an aim reading, only the latter of which always bullshits when producing bullshit.
- ch. 59 "Chapter 9 - The Unity of Bullshit" **·** On Bullshit — Cohen concludes that because bullshit can be emitted innocently - even by honest acolytes - the Sokal-style struggle against academic bullshit must target the visible product rather than the producer's hidden intentions, lest innocent traffickers be slandered as insincere or deliberately obscure. Fun as it is to write 'bullshit' over and over in an academic article, the bullshitter/bullshit distinction matters because the justice of the polemic depends on it.
- ch. 60 "No Bullshit, Please, We’re Austrian" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Hardcastle proposes to settle the Frankfurt-Cohen quarrel by adding logical positivism to the mix: the Vienna Circle's war on metaphysics was the 1920s-30s' great anti-bullshit program, and both Frankfurt's negligence-of-truth and Cohen's unclarifiable unclarity have precedents in it. Laura Penny's Your Call Is Important to Us supplies the moral heat ('when you fuck with English, you are money-changing in my temple'), while the summary of both positions - the liar needs the truth, the bullshitter does not give a hoot - sets up the Carnap chapters.
- ch. 61 "A Little Carnap in Everyone" **·** Martin Heidegger, Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Carnap's 'Overcoming Metaphysics through Logical Analysis of Language' supplies the unification: his verificationism renders the invented word 'teavy' meaningless for the same reason Cohen's texts are unclarifiably unclear, while Heidegger's 'The nothing noths' exemplifies the pseudo-sentence whose utterance expresses disregard for meaning - Frankfurt-style bullshitting. The 'toovy' example drives the point home: once a term's synonymy with 'quadrangular' is fixed, professing a further hidden meaning reveals a speaker who never intended to convey information at all.
- ch. 62 "The Unity of Bullshit" **·** Martin Heidegger, Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Meaning, for Carnap, is bearing the right deductive relations to other claims - exactly Cohen's insight that clarity is relational, so that 'clarifying' an unclarifiable text mangles it into something unrecognizable. Carnap's diagnosis of why metaphysics flourishes - metaphysicians express a general attitude toward life (Lebenseinstellung), better expressed through art, and mistake it for a state of affairs - explains bullshit's ubiquity and tolerance; but the metaphysician who knows he says nothing, like Heidegger after Carnap called him on it, is Frankfurt's bullshitter pure and simple.
- ch. 63 "Chapter 10 - Raising the Tone Definition, Bullshit, and the Definition of Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — The unification: Frankfurt's intentionalist bull and Cohen's structuralist shit are two sides of one thing, since a language amenable to misuse meets the bullshitter's ends halfway.
