The response is gun control for language - not Carnap's ideal language, but shared responsibility for English in our presence, from writing letters to casting votes - ending with Otto Neurath's anti-pseudorationalist insistence that the world's shape reflects our own choices rather than reasons beyond our control: it really is up to us.
- ch. 64 "Semantic Negligence" *(front)*
- ch. 65 "A Caricature History of Semantics" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — The chapter christens Frankfurt's innovation 'semantic negligence' on the legal model: the deceptive bullshitter has a duty of care to truth and breaches it without intending the resulting deception, which is why lying (intentional) is more culpable, yet bullshit more insidious because it degrades the commitment to truth on which public discourse depends. Terry Pratchett's characters who applaud 'any man who can tell an outrageous whopper with a gleam in his eye' mark the complicit bull session - bullshit deceives only when it escapes the session and masquerades as ordinary assertion.
- ch. 66 "Persuasive Definition" **·** Gottlob Frege — Sense and Reference — A caricature history of semantics centered on Frege's threefold distinction of sense, reference, and tone (Faerbung): 'the evening star' and 'the morning star' share a reference but differ in sense, and 'and' versus 'but' share logical form while differing in tone, which formal logic must suppress. The chapter ends by deploying Stevenson's persuasive definition against the Thouless-style Orwellian fantasy of purging tone-laden words like 'freedom' and 'democratic' from political language: the real remedy is taking tone seriously, since tone does real work in ordinary argument.
- ch. 67 "Broadening the Analysis" **·** Gottlob Frege, Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit; Sense and Reference — Stevenson's persuasive definition - changing a term's descriptive meaning while keeping its emotive force - is restated in Fregean terms as changing sense or reference while representing tone as fixed, yielding a sixteen-fold table of definitional moves (PD, PQD, and beyond). Semantic negligence then grades PDs: the aphoristic 'Conservatism is true socialism' deceives no one, but the definer who hides an argument inside a definition gains rhetorical advantage while risking that his audience endorses conclusions they were never given a fair chance to appraise - analogous to bullshit rather than lying.
- ch. 68 "Bullshit and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 69 "Bullshit and Philosophy" **·** Sense and Reference — Low and high redefinition - widening or narrowing a term's reference - are defined and illustrated by the convicted drunk driver who recasts himself as someone who merely 'had a miscalculated drink', a self-serving move aimed at dodging the pejorative tone of 'drunk driver'. The move partially overlaps persuasive definition (each is a high redefinition of the other's complement) and typically rests on wilful blindness to conventional usage.
- ch. 70 "Bullshit and Philosophy" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit; Sense and Reference — Flew's No-True-Scotsman Move - retreating from 'No Scotsman takes sugar with his porridge' to 'No true Scotsman...' when Hamish MacTavish of Inverness falsifies it - is analyzed as a redefinition that converts a bold, falsifiable claim into a vacuous necessary truth. Because the two statements look and sound alike, confusing them (from carelessness as easily as deceit) is semantic negligence, and pressing on as if the original claim still stood is classic Frankfurt bullshit.
- ch. 71 "Bullshit and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 72 "Bullshit and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 73 "Bullshit and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 74 "Backfire" **·** Sense and Reference — Euphemism - the Furies as the 'Kindly Ones', 'sex workers', 'superprompt critical power excursion' for nuclear meltdown - resets a term's tone while preserving its sense and reference, but Germaine Greer's 'euphemism treadmill' shows the pejorative tone rapidly migrates onto each replacement unless underlying attitudes change. The comparative stability of 'gay' and 'black' marks real progress, while most politically correct and nukespeak coinages simply backfire as Greer describes.
- ch. 75 "Good Definitions" **·** Catharine MacKinnon — Dominance Approach (pornography and gender); Feminism — The definitional taxonomy at work: MacKinnon and Dworkin's Model Law defines pornography as 'the sexually explicit subordination of women', but backfires in Canadian law into raids on gay bookshops while mainstream pornography survives - reference drift from an intended sense-only change. The three theories of rape (traditional property crime, liberal sex-without-consent, radical feminist 'terrorist institution' of male subordination) show definitions track theories and must be argued for, with the radical redefinition risking a tone-dilution that would 'trivialize legitimate rape'.
- ch. 76 "Is Frankfurt’s Definition of ‘Bullshit’ Itself PD" **·** Aristotle, Plato — How backfire is avoided: definitions that stipulate meaning are disguised arguments, legitimate only when the concealed argument is made explicit to all parties and actually won - as with the hard-won consensus definition of 'planet' and the liberal definition of rape. The chapter dismisses essence-based 'real definition' talk, from Plato and Aristotle through the logic-textbook rules, as metaphysically discredited, and proposes 'semantic diligence' in its place.
- ch. 77 "Chapter 11 - Different Kinds and Aspects of Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Closing chapter 10's circle: Frankfurt's definition of 'bullshit' is itself a persuasive definition of case d - it changes the term's sense and reference while keeping the pejorative tone, as the well-meant 'Help is on its way' to the critically injured shows. Because Frankfurt argues explicitly for his departures from conventional usage, his definition is semantically diligent rather than negligent, though Cohen's complaint that it omits academic bullshit remains to be answered - with which Chapter 11 opens.
- ch. 78 "Harry Frankfurt on Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Maes and Schaubroeck open on the bookstore irony: Frankfurt's slim bestseller On Bullshit shelved beside Chakra Balancing Kit, The Hidden Messages in Water Crystals, and Astrology: A Cosmic Science - almost as if placed there to illustrate the very bullshit Frankfurt diagnosed as the most salient feature of our culture. They then introduce Cohen's admiring complaint that Frankfurt examined 'just one flower in the lush garden of bullshit', teeing up the academic kind Cohen will define.
- ch. 79 "A Different Take on Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — A faithful reconstruction of Frankfurt: the bullshitter is a slovenly craftsman whose laxity concerns truth rather than detail - spin doctors control every tiny thing except whether what they say is true. The bull session shows indifference alone is not enough, since bullshit adds the pretence that hides that indifference (the Fourth of July orator misrepresents his own state of mind, not American history), whence Frankfurt's conclusion that bullshit is the greater enemy of truth - and the announcement that all three claims (unconcern, concealment, blameworthiness) will be contested.
- ch. 80 "A Different Kind of Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt, Ludwig Wittgenstein, Thomas Nagel — On Bullshit — The Pascal anecdote returns to undermine Frankfurt: Wittgenstein's disgust at 'I feel just like a dog that has been run over' involves no fakery, so pretence is not an essential ingredient of bullshit - mere indifference to truth suffices, as the hypothetical politician who openly stops caring would show. Bullshit emerges as often benign: Ishiguro's butler Stevens in The Remains of the Day comes to see bantering as 'the key to human warmth', Nagel's polite 'How nice to see you!' is convention-governed and no deception, and Wilde's epigrams - nearly all bullshit - explain why our attitude toward bullshit is softer than toward lying.
- ch. 81 "III - It’s All Around Us" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Cohen's product-based kind (unclarifiable unclarity, probed by the negation-sign test) is summarized, and the chapter asks whether Frankfurt-bullshit and Cohen-bullshit exhaust the lush garden. They do not: pseudoscience like astrology, chakra balancing, and bogus healing is a third, damaging kind - its practitioners sincerely believe (failing Frankfurt's indifference test) and predict specifically and clearly (failing Cohen's unclarity test) - so insensitivity to evidence and fallacious reasoning must be central to its analysis, better to be vaguely right than precisely wrong.
- ch. 82 "Chapter 12 - The Republic of Bullshit On the Dumbing-Up of Democracy" *(front)*
- ch. 83 "Bullshitting and Lying in Politics" **·** Harry Frankfurt, Plato — On Bullshit; Democracy — Opening Mark Evans's Chapter 12: Frankfurt's claim that democracy especially breeds bullshit, since it charges every citizen with having opinions about everything - the 'Frankfurt thesis' that citizens do not care about truth. It is contrasted with the 'cynicism thesis' (citizens do not care about politics, out of well-founded disillusion with a system that fails to deliver), and both are framed by the objection as old as Plato that political understanding is an expertise we cannot all share - theses argued about, not against, democracy.
- ch. 84 "The Myth of the ‘Well-Informed’ Citizen" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Mark Evans opens the book's political section by insisting that politicians mostly produce bullshit rather than lies in Frankfurt's strict sense: lying requires knowing and concealing the truth, while the Bush administration's WMD case for invading Iraq shows truth-indifference, with post-hoc 'rejustifications' (humanitarian intervention, democratisation, Saddam-9/11 hints) spun as needed. Using G.A. Cohen's distinction between aim-bullshitters and disposition-bullshitters, it reads Winston Smith's routinized rewriting of The Times in Orwell's Nineteen Eighty-Four as falsehood that slides from lying into bullshit, since the Party aims to destroy the very true/false distinction. Totalitarian regimes (Havel's 'living in truth') thus embed themselves not in a web of lies but in a morass of bullshit insulated from any possibly refuting facts.
- ch. 85 "‘Dumbing-Up’ Some Distortions of Democratic Equality" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit; Democracy — Evans argues that democratic citizens do their own share of Frankfurt-bullshitting about politics: because electoral democracy cannot admit how complex politics really is, it institutionalizes the fictional ideal of the 'well-informed' citizen, obliging voters to hold over-simplified views and politicians to pander to them. Campaigning forces candidates into dodges like the 'politics of distraction' and the empty promise to be 'a different sort of politician', producing 'Governor Schwarzenegger Syndrome' and opening space for dangerous demagogues and fundamentalisms. Following Churchill, the chapter concludes that this citizen-side bullshit vindicates the cynicism thesis only partially and gives no reason to reject democracy as 'the worst form of government except for all the others'.
- ch. 86 "Philosophy versus Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt, Friedrich Nietzsche — On Bullshit; Man Is the Measure (sophistic relativism); Democracy; Ressentiment — The chapter claims that 'equal respect' in modern democracies has been distorted into a 'dumbing-up' relativism — 'I'm valid, you're valid' — which conflates the equal right to voice opinions with their equal intellectual merit and so leaves nothing that counts as bullshit. It illustrates this through the 'democratization of expertise' and management-speak ('downsizing', 'mission statements'), which mimics profundity while evading awkward facts, and it tests and rejects the quasi-Nietzschean ressentiment story of timid citizens dragging discourse down to a common denominator. To get the full measure of the phenomenon it expands Frankfurt's and Cohen's typologies into seven kinds of bullshit, adding clarifiable unclarity, plain rubbish, irretrievable speculation, pretension, and evasion.
- ch. 87 "Chapter 13 - Political Bullshit and the Stoic Story of Self" **·** Socrates, Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit; Socratic Method (elenchus) — Evans concludes that the antidote to political bullshit is a discursive culture trained in Socratic skills of self-examination — citizens taught to recognize how their own views may be imperfect and how to refine them — together with the analytic-philosophical style his Oxford tutor called 'a very good bullshit detector'. Experts may lead debates by their greater factual knowledge and critical acumen, but the debates must stay democratically inclusive rather than become an elite monopoly. Citing G.A. Cohen, he warns that too much fashionable philosophy disparages truth and so itself produces bullshit, and that using the ubiquity of bullshit to justify the cynicism thesis's crippling inertia would be potentially catastrophic.
- ch. 88 "How to Analyze Bullshit" *(front)*
- ch. 89 "Stories Shape Our Feelings" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Vanessa Neumann sets out a third strategy for analyzing bullshit, beside Frankfurt's agent-centered and G.A. Cohen's content-centered definitions: studying the mechanism of bullshit, the speaker-listener relation through which it works in political discourse. Bullshit need not be false — even true statements can be non-germane or obfuscatory — and it serves two purposes: depicting the speaker as desirable ('I'm one of you') and targeting groups ('they are different from us, so we should treat them like this'). Its key trick is moral language without moral commitment, as when a politician condemns a rival's corporate free trip without explaining why it is wrong, scoring points while preserving wiggle room for future favors of their own.
- ch. 90 "Bullshit Around the Globe" **·** Martha Nussbaum — Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Building on Peter Goldie's account of how life-narratives shape emotion, and Martha Nussbaum's view of emotions as judgments of value and importance (which the chapter identifies with the Greek Stoic view), it explains where bullshit gets its power over us. Because an episode's place in a narrative determines what we feel — Oedipus could be delighted to have married Jocasta but not his mother — bullshit works by 'educating' our emotions, telling us what should make us proud, patriotic, angry or frightened, and what belongs in a eudaimonistic life. It closes by warning that bullshit appeals to the base and non-rational part of us and so degrades us, before turning to examples from international politics.
- ch. 91 "Chapter 14 - Bullshit at the Interface of Science and Policy Global Warming," **·** On Bullshit; The Ethics of Political Violence — The chapter dissects 'we are waging a war on terror' word by word: the inclusive 'we' conscripts the listener, 'war' triggers unquestioning patriotism, and 'terror' — an emotion, rather than 'terrorists' or 'terrorism' — keeps the enemy suitably vague, a bogeyman Other to hunt. The phrase is bullshit not because fighting terrorism is wrong but because it replaces the thought and debate that democratic policy and military decisions require with fear management, and militant jihadists' 'God's will' rhetoric works the same way, never explaining how bombing or torturing the uninvolved will end fear. Both sides use bullshit to supply identity and purpose while evading genuine moral justification, which degrades our capacity to be moved by ideas.
- ch. 92 "Bullshit of the Isolated Fact" **·** Plato — On Bullshit — Heather Douglas opens her chapter on science advice by framing the problem with Plato's parable of the stargazer as expert navigator for the ship of state in the Republic, tracing the debate from excluding pseudoscience through 'junk science' to 'politicized science'. She identifies two kinds of bullshit at the science-policy interface: true-but-misleading statements that exploit technical complexity to confuse the public about uncertainty, and appeals to a nonexistent universal standard of 'sound science' that actually smuggle in judgments about the consequences of error. Remedies include constant vigilance and reframing debates around the explicit weighing of evidence, uncertainty, and the risks of getting things wrong.
- ch. 93 "Bullshit of Universal Standards" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — The chapter explains 'bullshit of the isolated fact' through the climate-change debate: skeptics like Robert Balling and Fred Singer repeatedly cited the 1940-1975 cooling trend as incompatible with human-caused warming while omitting the aerosol research — confirmed when models correctly predicted the cooling after Mt. Pinatubo's 1991 eruption — which shows sulfate scrubbing had been masking the greenhouse signal. Likewise the satellite-versus-ground temperature discrepancy, which skeptics took as disproof, dissolved once instrument errors were re-examined. Repeating an isolated fact after its debunking has been publicized in Science or National Academy reports is, with Frankfurt, to reveal oneself unconcerned with the truth — willing to spread bullshit to win.
- ch. 94 "Combatting the Two Kinds of Bullshit" **·** Karl Popper — On Bullshit; Falsificationism — Douglas argues that 'universal standards bullshit' — the assumption that evidence must meet one threshold before it counts as scientific — comes from textbook schooling and has no warrant: there is no such standard, and we shouldn't want one. She shows how it distorts debate on all sides, from Bush administration uncertainty claims to Michael Gough's Popper-inspired insistence that climate models cannot be tested, and argues that whether evidence suffices always depends on the risks of error — the gas-gauge warning versus the bomb in your car. Genuine politicization (suppressing evidence, firing researchers) must be condemned, but deciding how much uncertainty is acceptable is an ineliminably political and ethical judgment.
- ch. 95 "Chapter 15 - Rhetoric Is Not Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Douglas concludes that the two kinds of bullshit need different remedies. Isolated-fact bullshit — Fred Singer's commentaries resting on selective omission and emphasis — can only be met with vigilance, since the public forum must remain open and even its spreaders may not grasp the nature of their claims. Universal-standards bullshit, by contrast, can be permanently undermined once we recognize there are no such standards: asking instead about the strength of evidence and the risks of error, and getting used to expert disagreement, would deflate the persuasive power of a single isolated fact.
- ch. 96 "The Problem (and Politics) of Rhetoric" **·** On Bullshit — David Tietge opens his defense of rhetoric with the aphorism that rhetoric isn't devious and untrustworthy — those are features reserved for language itself — and reports a semester-long count in which 156 of 157 media uses of 'rhetoric' were pejorative, the sole exception being George Carlin telling Jon Stewart that a comedian is essentially a rhetorician who unpacks how words mean. He traces rhetoric's decline from its honored place in the Trivium of the Seven Liberal Arts, notes the societal paradox that we condemn 'bullshit' yet richly reward lawyers, politicians, and advertising executives for producing it, and promises to dissolve the crude truth/falsity dichotomy that fuels the identification of rhetoric with bullshit.
- ch. 97 "The Truth about Postmodernism" **·** Aristotle — The chapter distinguishes Aristotle's rhetorica utens — his On Rhetoric as a civic how-to primer — from rhetorica docens, the modern theoretical study of how language means, arguing that Aristotle's practical bias left the public unable to see rhetoric as a method of textual analysis. It then performs such an analysis on George W. Bush's statement on the Alito nomination ('bring dignity to the process... a fair hearing and an up-or-down vote'), showing how 'dignity' stigmatizes extended debate, 'fair hearing' is subjective, and the demand for decisiveness rails against contemplation — the reading The Onion satirized as 'Fair, Quick, Unanimous Confirmation'. Tietge defends his own admitted dislike of Bush as proof that declared subjectivity need not compromise a reading's integrity, since all language reflects personal and collective orientations.
- ch. 98 "Rhetoric and Bullshit" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Plato — Perspectivism; Theory of Forms — Following Jeremy Campbell's The Liar's Tale, the chapter argues that the truth/lies binary is oversimplified: even 'the sky is blue' depends on contested definitions and 'God is good' on the speaker's concepts, so we must separate capital-T Truth — the immutable, incorruptible kind Plato located beyond the flawed physical realm of perfect Forms, which made him scorn rhetoric and the Sophists as teachers of disguise — from lower-case, situated, perspectival truths. It then recruits Nietzsche, for whom humans have no 'organ' for Truth but a natural instinct for falsehood (shared with camouflaging animals), and Ken Gemes's reading of doctrinal Truth as a ritualized fiction manufactured to maintain institutional and religious power. On this view postmodernism studies truth within the shifting domain of language rather than denying objective reality.
- ch. 99 "Chapter 16 - Just Bullshit" **·** Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — Tietge argues that postmodernism, far from denying objective reality, supplies a vocabulary for how language and interpretation shape small-t truths, and that rhetoric — Kenneth Burke's 'logology', words used to talk about words — is the discipline uniquely built to study this; hence rhetoric and bullshit are 'not even distant cousins'. He concedes Frankfurt's 'talk without knowing what he is talking about' catches genuine bullshit, like the student paper opener 'In today's society, there are many things that people have different and similar opinions about', and allows that 'salesman syndrome' rhetoric can be bullshit when its pitch is too transparent to persuade anyone. His remedy is to learn rhetoric — through hypothetical situations, deconstructing model essays, and keeping a commonplace book — since navigating the gray area between Truth and bullshit without understanding how language works leaves us to avoid the pitfalls by sheer chance.
- ch. 100 "Bullshit as a Call to Open-Mindedness" **·** Harry Frankfurt, Friedrich Nietzsche, Socrates, Protagoras, Plato — On Bullshit; Man Is the Measure (sophistic relativism) — Steve Fuller opens by asking whether bullshit detection coarsens the detector: Nietzsche and H.L. Mencken embraced the debunker's role (Mencken coining 'bunk'), while Ibsen's The Wild Duck and Hedda Gabler dramatize the need for a 'life lie', and detectors who find bullshit everywhere slide from nihilism into dogmatism. He re-reads bullshit as sophistry — the Sophists taught that securing your audience's belief is the only knowledge you need, against Socrates's attack and Plato's coining of 'rhetoric' to demonize them — and faults Frankfurt for calling the bullshitter 'indifferent' to truth when the accomplished bullshitter must be a keen student of what people tend to regard as true. The Sokal affair and Benjamin Franklin's 'economical with the truth' lead to his Kantian mapping of bullshitters (rationalists selecting among already-imaginable alternatives) against bullshit detectors (empiricists demanding evidence), which the scientific method will dialectically synthesize.
- ch. 101 "Wittgenstein Ultimate Bullshit Detector—or Bullshitter" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Thomas Kuhn, Blaise Pascal, Harry Frankfurt — Scientific Realism vs Anti-Realism; Pascal's Wager; Paradigm Shifts; On Bullshit — Fuller argues that mastering a discipline converts critics into loyal insiders — Thomas Kuhn's 'normal science' enshrines the tendency, cognitive dissonance explains it, and Blaise Pascal's wager is the general model of betting on a way of life that makes you receptive to its confirming signs. Against this he sets the Protestant tradition of Luther's conscience, 'playing it by ear', which like the Sophists concedes that reality rarely yields yes-or-no answers — the gap we fill being bullshit — and he frames the dispute as realism versus antirealism, reading Frankfurt's On Bullshit as an ad hominem attack on antirealists. He then re-examines Frankfurt's star witness: Wittgenstein's rebuke of Fania Pascal for saying she felt 'like a dog that has been run over' now looks like a spectacular lack of imagination, since evolutionary psychology, being forged at that very moment, has been narrowing the human-animal mental gap ever since.
- ch. 102 "Bullshit as Deferred Epistemic Gratification" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Paul Feyerabend, Harry Frankfurt — On Bullshit — The chapter reverses Frankfurt's verdict: Wittgenstein may himself have been a grandmaster of bullshit who practiced 'ventriloquism', speaking through the authority of ordinary language and 'paradigm case' examples, and whose scolding of Fania Pascal strikes some readers today like a priest fussing over a parishioner's impure thoughts. His bullshit was backward-looking ('you already know this'), unlike the forward-looking promissory bullshit of Galileo, whose deferred vindication Paul Feyerabend used to argue that the Papal Inquisitors were originally justified in their skepticism. Ernest Gellner's ostracized diagnosis — that Wittgenstein fed off British class anxiety most clearly marked in language use — is resisted both because careers ride on his authority and because his erratic 'idiot savant' persona keeps bullshit from sticking to him.
- ch. 103 "The Scientific Method as a Search for the Justice in Bullshit" **·** Thomas Reid, Søren Kierkegaard, Harry Frankfurt — Utilitarianism; Common Sense Philosophy; On Bullshit — Fuller portrays the bullshitter as the consummate hypocrite, bluffing from behind genuine uncertainty and exploiting the sophistic principle that controlling the moment of decision controls its outcome — calling the question when the balance of arguments favors you. Since we can rarely audit anyone's track record, epistemology leans on 'trust', 'witness' and 'testimony', read optimistically through Thomas Reid's divinely guaranteed common sense or, à la Kierkegaard, malignly as cowardly delegation of belief; either way 'reliability', which papers over the gap between evidence and belief, is itself a bullshit concept. He then analogizes the epistemic economy of evidence to the moral economy of sensation: as utilitarianism defers immediate gratification (taxation insuring against discounting our future selves), so the bullshitter's imperviousness to current evidence-weightings is prudence — an 'affirmative action' for weaker-evidenced positions that may prove the better bet in the fullness of time.
- ch. 104 "Our Distinguished Panel of Incomparable Geniuses" **·** Francis Bacon, Karl Popper — The Open Society; On Bullshit — Fuller's finale casts Francis Bacon's scientific method as an inquisitorial trial designed to manage bullshit: everyone may produce it, provided a third-party judge frames a 'crucial experiment' that decides between the parties — a vision only partially realized by the Royal Society and later positivism, and tempered by Karl Popper's open-society point that all may bullshit to their heart's content given agreement on how to clean up after it. He tests the model on the Lomborg affair (initially found 'scientifically dishonest', then acquitted on appeal because politicized research is the environmental norm), on Zola's J'Accuse and Galileo's vindication in Newton's Principia as productive bullshit, and on his own service as expert witness: McLean v. Arkansas cut through creationist and evolutionist bullshit with a neutral definition of science, while Kitzmiller v. Dover adopted a 'methodological naturalism' custom-built to exclude Intelligent Design — justice losing out to the side whose bullshit is piled higher and deeper.
- ch. 105 "Our Index, Exquisitely Crafted for Your Illumination" *(notes)*

## Cannabis – Philosophy for Everyone - What Were We Just Talking About_

- ch. 1 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — In this memoir (book Chapter 1, 'A Cannabis Odyssey'), Harvard psychiatrist Lester Grinspoon recounts how his 1967 review of the anti-marijuana literature convinced him he had been 'brainwashed' by a fear-based 'cannabis catechism,' coining 'cannabinophobia' and likening it to the 'madness of crowds.' He argues from personal use — hearing Sgt. Pepper for the first time, quitting psychoanalysis after 'stoned' self-critiques — that beyond recreation and medicine there is a third category, 'enhancement.' The piece is autobiographical drug-policy advocacy rather than the application of a specific named philosopher.
- ch. 14 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Bertrand Russell, William James, Friedrich Nietzsche, Michel Foucault — Reliabilism; Philosophical Skepticism; The Will to Believe — Roche's 'Seeing Snakes' attacks the psychedelicist claim (William James on nitrous oxide, Huxley's 'reducing valve,' Leary) that drugs including cannabis reveal reality-as-it-is. He deploys Russell's line that we 'can make no distinction between the man who eats little and sees heaven and the man who drinks much and sees snakes,' and a reliabilist demand that knowledge have a source proven reliable over independently verifiable cases, to argue drug 'revelations' are indistinguishable from delusion. Foucault is cited that drugs have 'nothing to do with truth and falsity,' and Nietzsche's Dionysian veil-of-Maya passage is used as an example of the mystical rhetoric he debunks.
- ch. 15 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** William James, Laozi — Stream of Consciousness; Wu Wei (effortless action); The Tao (the Way) — Hathaway and Sharpley analyze the cannabis experience of medical-'compassion club' users through Csikszentmihalyi's concept of 'flow.' They ground flow in William James's 'stream of consciousness' and in Taoism, reading Tao as 'a patterned flow of the universe' and wu wei as the effortless equilibrium users describe ('it just centers me,' 'I take an inventory on my soul'). Against Western monophasic medicine, they defend users' 'anecdotal' testimony as legitimate knowledge of a body–mind–spirit balance that cannabis restores.
- ch. 16 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Language Games — Montagne's 'Buzz, High, and Stoned' argues that the cannabis experience is largely constituted by the metaphors users apply to it. Tracing hashish descriptions from Gautier, Baudelaire, Bayard Taylor, Ludlow and jazzman Mezz Mezzrow to modern song lyrics and Robert Crumb's 'Stoned Agin!' cartoon, he maps 'buzz,' 'high' and 'stoned' onto dose-dependent levels documented by Moreau (1845) and Charles Tart, and shows the words carry more meaning than pharmacology, shaping set, setting and behavior. The Greek 'pharmakon' (remedy/poison/charm) frames the analysis of meaning-making in drug language.
- ch. 18 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Plato, Aristotle, Epictetus, William James, John Locke — Faith and Reason; Stoicism; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Taliaferro and Le Gall's 'The Great Escape' weighs four philosophical objections to cannabis-induced altered states — falsehood (Plato's ban on poetry/theatre in the Republic), distortion of reason (Euripides' Bacchae, James on nitrous oxide), undermining of agency (the Sirens, De Quincey's opium), and loss of self-determination (the Stoic Epictetus on freedom) — then surveys Jewish, Christian and Islamic treatments of wine, coffee and hashish. They rebut the objections using Aristotle's cathartic defense of theatre and Locke's imagination-based account of freedom, concluding that if cannabis is no worse than wine the philosophical and religious case against it is not decisive.
- ch. 19 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Sigmund Freud, Arthur Schopenhauer — Civilization and Its Discontents; Philosophical Pessimism; The Death Drive (Thanatos) — Clack's 'Cannabis and the Human Condition' reads intoxication through Freud's Civilization and its Discontents, where drugs are the 'crudest but most effective' palliative against a life 'too hard for us,' tied to the pleasure principle and the Nirvana/death-instinct drive to discharge stimulus. He supplements Freud with Schopenhauer's pessimism — life oscillating between suffering and boredom — to add 'stimulus hunger' Freud ignored, arguing cannabis both dampens and stokes the 'fire in the brain' depending on THC potency (high vs. stoned). He concludes that, like religion, art and love, cannabis augments a bleak existence, so 'all cannabis use is medicinal.'
- ch. 20 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Immanuel Kant, David Hume, John Locke, Aristotle — Phenomena and Noumena; Problem of Induction; Philosophical Skepticism — Kakko's 'Hallucinatory Terror' uses Fitz Hugh Ludlow's 1857 The Hasheesh Eater to ask 'How can these things be?' — the ontological status of hallucinations that neither exist nor fail to exist. He traces Ludlow's own reading from Locke's empiricism through Hume's skeptical worry that all perception may be hallucinatory with no verifiable external object, to Kant's Copernican turn: the phenomenal/noumenal distinction, synthetic a priori judgments, and space and time as pure forms of intuition. Kant's framework lets Kakko recast Ludlow's 'virtual change of worlds' as a literal, non-metaphorical restructuring of the intuitions conditioning experience.
- ch. 22 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Holt and Kaufman's 'Marijuana and Creativity' surveys creativity research (Guilford's divergent thinking — fluency, flexibility, originality, elaboration) and US drug law (Anslinger, 'Reefer Madness,' Schedule I) to test the popular belief, held via figures like Cheech and Chong, Bob Marley and Willie Nelson, that cannabis boosts creativity. Controlled THC-vs-placebo biscuit studies show no benefit — placebo groups who merely believed they were high scored higher, while actual THC reduced fluency and flexibility. They explain the persistent belief through placebo/expectancy effects, spurious correlation, and salience bias, concluding any drug–creativity link is 'manufactured in the drug user's mind.'
- ch. 23 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** John Stuart Mill — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime; The Harm Principle — Jacquette argues that cannabis opens a 'creative inner space' of loosened associations but is skeptical it yields genuine, lasting insight, using Ginsberg's claim to have newly 'apprehended the structure' of jazz and classical music and the conjecture that Shakespeare's Sonnet 76 'invention in a noted weed' hints he wrote high. He treats the aesthetic case as a phenomenology problem: separating cannabis's real contribution to appreciation from mere buzz-glow. He then defends a right to get high by invoking the Declaration's 'pursuit of happiness' and a Mill-style principle that private, non-harming pleasure should not be criminalized.
- ch. 24 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Karl Marx, Plato — Alienation; Commodity Fetishism; Allegory of the Cave — Thorsby reads 'getting high' as a rupture of ordinary, unreflective consciousness and asks why cannabis use grows fastest in the affluent capitalist West. He pairs Plato's allegory of the cave—ordinary consciousness as prisoners mistaking shadows for reality—with Marx's account of alienation and commodity fetishism, in which workers are estranged from the products of their labor and see value as inhering in things rather than in human labor. Drawing on Fromm's 'having vs. being,' he argues that cannabis offers a momentary escape from and critique of this alienated consciousness, though it cannot cure it.
- ch. 26 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); The Harm Principle — Tahko surveys the empirical evidence on cannabis from the standpoint of the individual and of society, concluding that its health harms (respiratory illness, mild cognitive impairment, possible dependence, weak links to psychosis) pale beside those of alcohol and tobacco. He argues the gravest costs—legal risk, social isolation, contaminated black-market supply—are consequences of prohibition itself, not the drug. Endorsing public-health experts, he recommends abandoning elimination-based policy for a harm-reduction approach modeled on the Dutch coffee-shop system and Portuguese decriminalization.
- ch. 27 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Philosophical Skepticism — Penrose subjects the 'soft drug / hard drug' distinction to conceptual analysis, arguing that—unlike genuinely graded soft-vs-hard eggs—it tracks no single real spectrum. He tests three candidate bases: phenomenological intensity of the high (incoherent, since 'comparable amounts' and interpersonal 'highness' can't be measured), addictiveness (fails, since the traditional distinction counts non-addictive LSD as 'hard'), and dangerousness (Nutt's Lancet study ranks cannabis as more dangerous than some 'hard' drugs, and the distinction trades on illicitly excluding legal alcohol and nicotine). He concludes drugs vary along many distinct property-lines, so the traditional binary is simply false.
- ch. 28 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** John Stuart Mill — The Harm Principle; On Liberty; Higher and Lower Pleasures — Musselman, Frohardt, and Lynch defend adult cannabis use through Mill's Harm Principle, using the hypothetical 'Patrick the pot smoker'—a professional who grows and smokes moderately on Friday nights—to argue that competent adults' self-regarding conduct is their own affair and does not violate the 'distinct and assignable obligations' Mill owes to family. They marshal health data (no lung-cancer link, negligible secondhand exposure, neuroprotective effects) to rebut the harm-to-others claim, and invoke Mill's higher intellectual pleasures plus the Michael-Phelps case to argue prohibition wastes resources better spent on the greater harms of alcohol and tobacco.
- ch. 30 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** John Stuart Mill — The Harm Principle; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Earleywine dissects the moral versus practical arguments over prohibition, attacking legal moralism—the drug-czar claim (Bennett, Wilson) that use is wrong simply because it is immoral—as circular, and noting that the law does not criminalize every immoral act (broken contracts, lying). He shows purported moral arguments collapse into utilitarian harm estimates that the data undercut, since use rates are essentially orthogonal to policy across Amsterdam, Australia, Portugal, and US states. He then weighs anti-prohibition rights arguments (religion, privacy in Ravin, property via Szasz, self-determination) and closes on proportionality: owning a harmless plant merits no punishment.
- ch. 31 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Martha Nussbaum, Aristotle, The Buddha — The Capabilities Approach; The Golden Mean; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Schick asks whether humans need to alter consciousness (Weil, Maslow's hierarchy, Siegel's animal examples of reindeer, elephants, and rats seeking intoxicants) and concludes we have no such need, though we can flourish from it. He applies Sen and Nussbaum's capabilities approach, arguing cannabis enhances Nussbaum's second (bodily health, via endocannabinoids and medical uses) and fourth (senses, imagination, thought, via enriched music perception and possible creativity) capabilities. Citing Aristotle's and the Buddha's golden mean against abuse, and evidence of no lasting brain harm, he judges paternalistic prohibition an unjustified deprivation of the capabilities needed for a good life.
- ch. 32 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Harry Frankfurt — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Second-Order Desires (personhood) — Funke examines how the 'gateway' argument is supposed to work by treating it as a claim about weakness of will, split into two mechanisms. Motivational externalism—illustrated by Milgram's obedience experiments and Doris's situationism—holds that the illicit-market situation, not the drug, pushes users toward hard drugs, which implies legalization, not prohibition. Motivational internalism, using Michael Smith's contest between normative and motivational reasons and second-order desires, locates the risk in cannabis's creative 'analysis paralysis' and idealizing imagination; Funke argues that this imaginative value, like religion, is worth its risk and that neuropharmacology (THC's distinct cannabinoid mechanism) undercuts the internalist gateway claim.
- ch. 33 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(notes)*

## Christmas – Philosophy for Everyone - Better Than a Lump of Coal

- ch. 1 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Faith and Reason — In this foreword, historian Stephen Nissenbaum frames the book's central puzzle by asking the reader to picture Santa Claus kneeling in the Bethlehem manger and feeling why the image jars. He argues Christmas began as a fourth-century compromise: the Church Fathers fixed Jesus' birthday on December 25 to co-opt Saturnalia and the solstice and to refute Docetism (the claim Christ only 'seemed' to have a body) by giving him a human birthday. He traces how the resulting tension between piety and revelry led the Puritans to ban Christmas and how the secular, commercial Santa of Moore's 1823 poem came to dominate the American holiday, leaving the manger and the sleigh perpetually unreconciled.
- ch. 9 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Faith and Reason — Editor Scott Lowe's introduction surveys the volume, using the disputed origins of Christmas – the December date borrowed from Sol Invictus and Yule, the Christmas tree from Germanic custom, and a Santa descended from Odin, Thor and Krampus rather than any Christian saint – to show how little about the holiday is settled. It previews each contributor's argument, from Hume-based attacks on the virgin birth and Aiken's denial of a 'War on Christmas' to Foucauldian readings of Santa's surveillance, the ethics of the Santa lie, and atheist or Festivus alternatives, positioning the book as a set of philosophical debates over what Christmas actually means.
- ch. 10 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** David Hume — On Miracles; Problem of Induction; Philosophical Skepticism — Jurgensen and Southworth apply Hume's argument against miracles to the virgin birth, treating it as a claimed violation of the natural law that virgins do not conceive. They attack the credibility of the only witnesses, Matthew and Luke, noting their third-hand sources (Mark, Q, L/M), their contradiction over whether Mary and Joseph were married, and the mistranslation of Isaiah's ambiguous 'almah' as the Greek 'parthenos' (literal virgin). Invoking Hume's four defects of miracle testimony and his maxim that no testimony suffices unless its falsehood would be more miraculous than the event, they conclude a rational person must reject the Christmas miracle.
- ch. 12 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** David Hume — On Miracles; Faith and Reason — Theologian Victor Lyons defends the virgin birth against the Humean empiricist objection voiced by Dawkins, Hitchens and Harris. He marshals the creeds and Church Fathers (Ignatius, Justin Martyr, Irenaeus, Origen's reply to Celsus) and argues Matthew's and Luke's independent nativity narratives point to a pre-gospel tradition of the virginal conception. Against Hume's claim that a wise man proportions belief to evidence, Lyons contends experience is not an infallible guide and that ruling out the miraculous a priori is a circular limit on inquiry, casting Mary and Joseph as the first skeptics who nonetheless accepted an event outside natural experience.
- ch. 13 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Boethius — The Consolation of Philosophy; Faith and Reason — Medievalist Todd Preston argues that 'Put Christ Back in Christmas' misreads the holiday, which in the English tradition was always a balance of Christian and non-Christian elements. He shows Augustine (in On Christian Doctrine) and Boethius (in the Consolation of Philosophy, guided by Lady Philosophy) licensed Christians to absorb useful pagan philosophy, and that this apologetic openness let King Alfred, Pope Gregory and St. Boniface repurpose rather than erase heathen practice – turning Thor's oak into the Christmas fir. Tracing the Old English 'Cristesmæsse', Yule/Jol and Modraniht, and the secular revelry of Sir Gawain that Wyclif condemned, he concludes an authentic Christmas keeps its pagan half.
- ch. 14 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Faith and Reason; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Scott Aiken argues the conservative 'War on Christmas' pushed by Gibson and O'Reilly is illusory on both fronts: cultural Christianity has already won, and keeping nativity scenes out of public schools protects minority rights under the Establishment Clause rather than persecuting the majority. He then mounts a separate attack, claiming both Christmas myths are bad on their merits – the nativity (star that leads wise men, vanishing Magi, Joseph's pointless genealogy) promotes mere credulity rather than tested faith, while the Santa myth glorifies a slave-owning surveiller who makes goodness merely instrumental. He adds that teaching the false Santa story undercuts parents' testimonial authority to teach the gospel as true.
- ch. 16 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Paul Tillich, David Hume, Bertrand Russell, Hans-Georg Gadamer — Russell's Teapot; On Miracles; Faith and Reason — Guy Bennett-Hunter treats both the Santa story and the virginal conception as mythologies whose value lies in meaning, not literal fact, drawing on Barthes and Gadamer's dictum that a myth needs no verification outside itself. He deploys Hume on miracles and Russell's Teapot to argue it is no more rational to believe the sacred myth literally than the Santa one, then turns to Tillich's religious case: taking the virgin birth literally is idolatry that drags the ultimate God into the chain of cause and effect. He urges 'breaking' the myth – keeping its significance while dropping its literal truth – a ladder to be kicked away like Edmund Gosse's childhood test of idolatry.
- ch. 17 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Michael Sandel — Communitarianism; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Mark Mercer argues that a now largely secular Christmas can help build a liberal, rather than communitarian, multicultural society. He distinguishes liberal multiculturalism, which prizes each individual's flourishing and rests on respect, equality and decency, from communitarian multiculturalism, which treats cultural groups as basic units and breeds identity politics and envy. He detaches Christmas from its Christian origins – trees, lights and Santa now embodying good will and generosity rather than doctrine – and against those who would ban public displays or say only 'Season's Greetings', contends that a shared secular Christmas is one of the few common holidays that can bind citizens of a diverse society together.
- ch. 18 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Eudaimonia (flourishing); Virtue Ethics — Haire and Nelson read the film A Christmas Story as evidence that consumerism has replaced religion as America's faith: the Parker family shows no religious markers, and Ralphie's single-minded quest for the Red Ryder BB gun mirrors the identity-defining, transformative role religion once played. Using Aristotle's virtue ethics, they name consumerism a Christmas vice opposed to candidate Christmas virtues of being religious, traditional and family-centered. When the BB gun nearly blinds Ralphie and the hounds ruin the turkey, the consumerist ideal fails; the family's makeshift Chinese-restaurant dinner shows the family-centered virtue overcoming the vice, so 'all was right with the world'.
- ch. 19 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Plato, René Descartes, The Buddha — Karma and Rebirth; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Deal and Waller use Lakoff and Johnson's conceptual-metaphor theory to explain how we grasp Santa's God-like moral omniscience through human metaphors. Santa 'sees from above' (North Pole, flight), judges with 'cool' reason yet remains jolly, and – echoing Plato's Phaedrus charioteer and Descartes' solitary reasoner – masters his appetites and decides free of bias. His gift-giving runs on a 'moral accounting' metaphor where good deeds are commodities exchanged for presents and 'moral authority is parental authority'; they read A Christmas Story's BB-gun lesson through this frame and finally liken Santa's impartial, dispassionate sorting of naughty and nice to the impersonal law of karma.
- ch. 21 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Michel Foucault, Jeremy Bentham — The Panopticon (surveillance); Power/Knowledge; Discipline and Docile Bodies — Hancuff and O'Connor read the song 'Santa Claus Is Coming to Town' and films like The Polar Express and Santa Claus Is Comin' to Town through Foucault's Discipline and Punish. Santa's all-seeing list-checking is a version of Bentham's Panopticon: children, watched but never seeing the watcher, internalize permanent visibility and discipline themselves. Invoking Foucault's Power/Knowledge and discursive practices, they argue the surveillance does not actually make children 'good for goodness' sake' – punishment is rarely delivered – but instead disciplines them into wish-lists and consumption, training them to be good consumers in late-capitalist culture, with Santa doubling as a corporate pitchman (Coke, Budweiser).
- ch. 22 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Faith and Reason — Will Williams recounts the legend that St. Nicholas of Myra, the historical basis of Santa Claus, attended the Council of Nicaea (325) and punched the heretic Arius, who taught that the Son was created and not fully God ('there was a time when the Son was not'). Setting the fight to a parody of 'The Night Before Christmas', Williams explains via Athanasius why the divinity of Christ mattered – only if Jesus is truly God can his death secure salvation. He contrasts this fiercely theological bishop with the de-Christianized modern Santa, using the syncretism of 'Here Comes Santa Claus' to argue Nicholas would deplore what consumerist Christmas has become.
- ch. 23 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Immanuel Kant, Karl Marx, John Locke, Robert Nozick, John Rawls — Humanity as an End in Itself; Utilitarianism; Ideology and False Consciousness; The Veil of Ignorance — Brophy prosecutes Santa for 'elfploitation,' treating the North Pole workshop like an overseas sweatshop (with Willy Wonka's Oompa-Loompahs and Rowling's house-elfs as parallels) and dismantling a Nozickian entitlement-theory defense: the elfs' 'consent' is coerced by their bleak alternatives, so absolute property rights fail just as Locke's desert water-hole owner must grant access. He diagnoses the elfs' apparent contentment as Marxian false consciousness, invokes Kant's humanity formula to condemn using rational elfs as mere means, and rejects the utilitarian 'greatest happiness' justification via Le Guin's Omelas and Rawls's veil of ignorance. He concludes with a call to boycott Christmas until Santa's labor practices become transparent.
- ch. 24 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Immanuel Kant, William James, Plato, Socrates — The Noble Lie; The Will to Believe; Socratic Method (elenchus) — Johnson argues that the Santa Claus lie is immoral and should be abandoned, giving three reasons: it is deceptive lying (Kant's absolute prohibition is too strong, but the lie lacks life-saving justification and functions as a Platonic 'noble lie' no more noble than a cult leader's); Santa robs parents of credit and warps children's moral development and trust, illustrated by anguished 'finding out' stories and Virginia O'Hanlon's letter; and it damages credulity, invoking Clifford's 'Ethics of Belief' against belief on insufficient evidence while James's 'Will to Believe' cannot rescue Santa since evidence against him is overwhelming. He recommends revealing the truth early, even via a Socratic-Method questioning game.
- ch. 26 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Plato, Aristotle, Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — Virtue Ethics; Deontology; Utilitarianism — Gavrielides argues that going along with the Santa custom (leaving cookies, filling stockings, providing 'evidence') genuinely is lying, but that it is not wrong. She tests three ethical frameworks: a Kantian deontological analysis (with the 'murderer at the door' case) makes the lie at best 'wrong but permissible,' failing to capture parents' intuition of innocence; a Millian utilitarian analysis reduces character to a mere tool for good action; only virtue ethics, grounded in Plato's Republic account of edifying storytelling and Aristotelian character formation, captures why Santa stories are good — they cultivate kindness, charity, and self-reflection, and the myth is self-destructing as children's critical thought naturally dismantles it.
- ch. 27 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Death of God — Hales uses Nietzsche's verdict that truth matters less than a myth's usefulness (The Gay Science, The Anti-Christ) to argue Santa Claus is a truer exemplar of Christmas than Jesus. He traces Nietzsche's account of God's degeneration from a proud tribal mascot like Yahweh into a trivialized 'domestic servant,' and the 'death of God' as belief becoming useless. Documenting Christmas's commercial, pagan origins (Sol Invictus, Saturnalia, Mithras on December 25), he casts Santa as a practical, this-worldly demigod propped up by movies and NORAD — a giver who, unlike the credit-demanding Christian God, wants no thanks, embodying anonymous selfless generosity and thus the god Nietzsche awaited.
- ch. 28 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Aristotle, Alasdair MacIntyre — Virtue Ethics; Eudaimonia (flourishing); Virtues, Practices and Traditions — Scott reads Dickens's A Christmas Carol through virtue theory, treating Scrooge's transformation as a lesson in the virtues of generosity and gratitude that constitute eudaimonia (human flourishing). Drawing on Niebuhr, he explains Scrooge's greed as an anxious, insecurity-driven quest for security through wealth that fails because flourishing depends on relationships, not money (the poor-but-happy Cratchits). He invokes MacIntyre's 'just generosity' and Aristotelian habituation to show virtues are cultivated through practice, and uses Charles Taylor's weak/strong evaluations to warn that Christmas consumerism threatens 'keeping Christmas' by reducing moral worth to preferences and purchases.
- ch. 29 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 30 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Baruch Spinoza — The Meaning of Life; Faith and Reason — Tallman offers a naturalistic (atheist) justification for celebrating Christmas, arguing first that the holiday's date and rituals (December 25 solstice, Christmas trees, Odin-derived Santa) predate and are not exclusively Christian, so non-believers may find their own meaning in them. Drawing on George Santayana's and Steven Cahn's 'religious naturalism,' she argues rituals and moral communities give meaning without supernatural belief, so atheists engaging even overtly religious rites (like Westerners doing Hindu-rooted yoga) are not being disingenuous. She locates a secular 'spirit of Christmas' — giving, love, community — in It's a Wonderful Life, where humans, not God, save George Bailey, and closes on a Venn-diagram overlap of Christian and naturalist Christmases.
- ch. 31 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Sigmund Freud — Faith and Reason — Mason, a developmental psychologist writing as a Pagan, narrates her decades-long religious journey through James Fowler's Stages of Faith model, using each stage to reinterpret Christmas. She recounts childhood confusion over carols and chimney-invading strangers, fundamentalist-Christian zeal (stage 3 group belonging, then stage-4 doubt as she 'crusaded' against pagan Christmas trees), Unitarian Universalist questioning, and finally Wiccan/Pagan practice centered on Hecate and the winter-solstice Wheel of the Year (rediscovering Christmas's pagan roots in holly, mistletoe, and Yule). Reaching Fowler's stage 5 'Conjunctive Faith,' she concludes Christmas is neither hers nor anyone's to own but a self-perpetuating commercial 'Hallmark' phenomenon she can now greet with equanimity.
- ch. 32 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" **·** William James — Pragmatism; Truth as What Works — Holt argues that Seinfeld's Festivus, invented by Frank Costanza as an anti-consumerist 'holiday for the rest of us,' is grounded in American pragmatism: truth is 'whatever works,' malleable and tested by results. Because commercialized Christmas fails to deliver the altruism it demands, it 'does not work' and by pragmatist standards ceases to be true, so Costanza pragmatically forges a holiday that fits his needs. Holt reads Festivus's elements pragmatically — the maintenance-free aluminum pole as a bare functional symbol, the airing of grievances as a needed cathartic release from everyday restraint, the wrestling as self-perpetuating ritual — and warns that as Festivus merchandise spreads it will lose its practicality and, being no longer pragmatic, be discarded; he closes with a mock 'airing of grievances' against philosophers including Plato and Bertrand Russell.
- ch. 33 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(no index entry yet)* — Scheopner argues that the secular Santa Claus can serve as a cross-cultural, cross-religious point of connection, using a barefoot, shaka-waving Santa at Honolulu Hale in ethnically and religiously plural Hawaii as her central example. She contends Santa sidesteps the Establishment Clause conflicts that dog nativity displays — reviewing Lynch v. Donnelly and County of Allegheny v. ACLU, where crèches on public property were scrutinized while 'diluted' or tree-accompanied displays passed — and notes that early Puritan and Hawaiian missionaries actually banned Christmas. Because Santa is a universal yet customizable figure (signing Santas, ethnic Santas, a hypothetical Cowboy or Steeler Santa), he can represent a whole civic community's shared identity without marking citizens as religiously different.
- ch. 34 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(no index entry yet)* — In a comic first-person afterword, Santa Claus reviews the book, recounting how he was 'guilt-tripped' into an annual gift-giving role that outlived even his death, distinguishing himself from pretenders like Odin, Thor, and the demonic Krampus. He endorses Steven Hales's claim that he outdoes Jesus at Christmas and accepts charges of consumerism and even elf 'labor exploitation' (with a jab at Marx over variable costs), but objects to the one assumption every contributor shares — that he does not exist — pleading for a little blind faith and threatening to put every doubting philosopher-contributor on the Naughty List.
- ch. 35 "Christmas Philosophy for Everyone" *(notes)*

## Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy - The Real and the Cereal

- ch. 1 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — Authenticity; Phenomena and Noumena; Being-Toward-Death; Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal) — Vannatta's introduction frames the volume with a traffic-intersection metaphor: Klosterman's campaign against the phrase 'guilty pleasure' questions whether the one-way roads of high and low culture are real at all, and the contributors deliberately violate the traffic rules (Kierkegaard meets KISS, Baudrillard meets Britney Spears). It reads Klosterman's line that 'nothing is ever in and of itself' through Kant's noumena-phenomena distinction: like Kant's knowing subject, we never reach the in-itself but only perspectives refracted through rock bands, sports teams, and TV. The introduction presents the Guns N' Roses tribute band Paradise City (identity effaced to imitate an equally constructed identity) and the rock-death road trip of Killing Yourself to Live as Klosterman's way of recovering authenticity, arguing that living with the possibility of one's own death is more authentic than fleeing it.
- ch. 8 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Immanuel Kant — Tripartite Soul — Auxier argues that Klosterman's signature lists always carry an 'immanent order' of value, illustrated by the twenty-two teenagers' simultaneous classroom thoughts in Downtown Owl and his own contested ranking of the five greatest rock bands. He reads the seventeen-page sober conversation between Julia and Vance, printed with 'what she meant' and 'what he meant' under each remark, through Kant's extensive versus intensive magnitudes: the spoken words are an extensive whole, but the multiple unshared perspectives form an intensive whole no list can exhaust, which is why philosophers crave the 'tug of necessity' that intensive relations never deliver. Plato's Leontius, who rubbernecked at corpses despite his disgust, explains both Klosterman's TMI confessions in Killing Yourself to Live and the 'recoil of the shit-canon' by which we learn to order our desires, while Julia's carbon-monoxide death in the blizzard shows the world converting intensive possibilities into extensive necessity.
- ch. 10 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Socrates — Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); Authenticity; The Unexamined Life — Vannatta explains why Seinfeld reruns now fall flat through Klosterman's neologism 'unironically': since about 1991 irony has become the invisible background of American comedy, a pervasive detachment that David Foster Wallace named and that Jedediah Purdy's For Common Things attacks as the gap between phenomenon and essence. Against Purdy's ironist (Seinfeld's Jerry, mastered by irony, believing in nothing but his own interests), the chapter sets Kierkegaard's Socrates, whose 'infinite absolute negativity' and confessing ignorance serve a truth he cannot define, and asks whether Klosterman, probing the gap between Bono's rehearsed car-ride persona and his inner self, is master of irony or mastered by it. The answer places Chuck midway between the two jailed ironists, Socrates and the Seinfeld finale: like Socrates he knows his own ignorance and serves the undefined idea of self-understanding, but only through pop-culture resemblance (KISS where Jerry used Superman), unable to master irony any more than he can bite his own teeth.
- ch. 11 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Reisch rebuts Klosterman's Esquire claim that culture, unlike people and movements, can never be wrong because no larger framework stands outside it: he argues something can also go wrong relative to its own internal values, betraying itself as a self-sabotaging painter does. His exhibit is Phil Collins's 'In the Air Tonight', scored by a jokey 'popular music vapidity index' that divides emotional drama by lyrical substance, on which Springsteen's 'Born in the U.S.A.' rates near a healthy 1 while Collins's operatic anguish over a contentless 'it' divides by zero and becomes infinitely vapid. Since the song is beloved cultural wallpaper, its awfulness shows that culture as a whole can betray itself and its members, which is why serious fans feel genuinely let down by hit movies and best-seller lists.
- ch. 12 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Socrates, Epictetus, René Descartes — The Apollonian and the Dionysian; The Dichotomy of Control; Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); Stoicism — Curko and Greguric read Klosterman's glam-rock fandom as a problem of identity and masks: Nietzsche's Apollonian-Dionysian split casts Chuck as a self-controlled Apollonian writing about Dionysian excess in Guns N' Roses, Motley Crue, and Whitesnake, while Descartes's remark that actors wear masks to hide their shame introduces the tribute band Paradise City, a 'real copy' whose members would have no personalities left if they fully succeeded. The authors extend the mask question to Facebook and online avatars, where media saturation makes us 'copies without the original' and identity stops being self-same. They end by crowning Klosterman a modern Socrates of 'Know thyself' and a Stoic exemplar: admire your heroes like Satriani but do not steal their identities, control what you can (never your reputation or your time of death), and keep daring the endless search for self-knowledge.
- ch. 14 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger, Michel Foucault, Immanuel Kant, René Descartes — Authenticity; Being-Toward-Death; Thrownness (Geworfenheit); The They-Self (das Man) — Vannatta starts from Klosterman's confession that his first dates run on three and a half dates' worth of contrived material, and asks how self-knowledge is possible at all if, as Errol Morris tells Klosterman, we have no privileged access to our own minds. After a tour through Descartes's cogito, Kant's unity of apperception, Freud's unconscious, and Foucault's discourse-constructed subject, the chapter argues with Foucault's Ramist logic of similarity that Klosterman's thought really works by resemblance, mapping his exes onto members of KISS and hearing a dead friend in every Replacements song. Heidegger supplies the resolution: thrown into the crowd's world, we recover ourselves only through its inauthentic pop culture, and Klosterman's 'living is dying, little by little' and obsessive rock-death writing enact being-toward-death as the condition of authenticity.
- ch. 15 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Jean Baudrillard, Plato, Jean-Paul Sartre, Judith Butler, Immanuel Kant — Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal); Allegory of the Cave; Bad Faith; Gender Performativity — Rood unpacks Klosterman's 'This Is Emo' thesis that no woman will ever satisfy him: women love Lloyd Dobler rather than John Cusack, and everyone measures real relationships against Hollywood fake love (Coldplay songs, the Woody Allen Personality Type) until, as Klosterman puts it, real people actively trying to live like fake people are no less fake. He reads this through Sartre's cafe waiter and Butler's gender performativity to argue identity is learned performance, and through Plato's cave updated to a reclining-seat movie theater where we mistake projected shadows for real love. The deepest diagnosis is Baudrillard's hyperreal, glossed as a social-constructionist rerun of Kant's Copernican revolution, against which Rood's own objection lands: Klosterman covertly assumes a pure unmediated self that cannot exist, so the answer to fake love is not escape from mediation but self-control within it.
- ch. 16 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Fate and Foreknowledge; Free Will vs Determinism — Sienkiewicz takes Klosterman's hyperthetical of a crystal ball showing an unchangeable future in which you are blissfully obsessed with CFL football as a secular version of the traditional Jewish and Christian problem of divine foreknowledge versus human freedom. He offers the 'movie' solution: as when watching the chest-wax scene in The 40-Year-Old Virgin, a vantage point outside the event's time (God's, or yours before the fixed future) can behold it without having scripted it, so foreknowledge and free will remain logically compatible. Since we already live toward the one absolutely certain future, our own deaths, the portal should change nothing about how you act, though you still should not watch a league that plays with only three downs.
- ch. 17 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Virtue Ethics (arete); Authenticity — Flanders reads Killing Yourself to Live as asking whether dying can be good for the person who dies: Jeff Buckley's 'Hallelujah' and Kurt Cobain's myth became deeper and more successful only after the fact, as if death were a career move. He answers with Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics, where the fortunes of descendants can affect the happiness of the dead: our life-stories outlive their authors, so fans' posthumous devotion can really benefit Buckley, though no posthumous fame can add virtue that was never lived (Michael Jackson's rehabilitation cannot touch how he actually lived). Klosterman's road trip ultimately undercuts the question: the earnest Great White concert mourners remember what truly was there, while Cobain's fans retroactively invent meaning to gain 'credibility by mourning retrospectively', so worshipping the rock dead manufactures an inauthentic afterlife, and Klosterman must kill the unserious fan in himself to live.
- ch. 19 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Søren Kierkegaard — Despair (sickness unto death); Authenticity — Brittingham reads Klosterman's celebrity interviews through Kierkegaard's The Sickness unto Death, taking the self as a synthesis of the immediate and the symbolic, finite and infinite. Britney Spears, who claims never to have thought about the sexual iconography behind her curtain, embodies the despair of ignorance in not knowing one has a self; Bono, embarrassed by his messianic 1980s posturing and wishing to shed that iconic self while remaining 'self-conscious about being self-conscious', embodies despair at not willing to be oneself; Metallica's Some Kind of Monster breakdown, with rehab, a hired therapist, and Kirk Hammett dreading having to take out the trash, embodies defiant despair, the 'king without a country' who would be absolute master of himself. Jeff Tweedy's uncool acceptance of both his suburban dad-life and his silly symbolic persona, plus Kierkegaard's remedy of resting transparently in the power that established us, mark the only way to be 'for real' rather than full of shit.
- ch. 20 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Vosen confesses her own case of Klosterman's fake-love thesis, hundreds of viewings of Say Anything for Lloyd Dobler and his boom box, and accepts that everyone from Huxtable-watching parents to Real World casting wants Hollywood love and one-dimensional archetypes. Her framework is Bakhtin's carnival: Sex, Drugs, and Cocoa Puffs is carnivalesque literature that suspends cultural hierarchies (thirty million Americans of every class watching American Idol), crowns low culture like Pamela Anderson and Saved by the Bell, and makes Klosterman both carnie and king through his self-awareness. The dark twist is Klosterman's Real World point that we sacrifice our complicated personalities to play flat archetypes like Puck because communication becomes easier, 'we must kill ourselves... in order to live', and knowing it, as Klosterman does, wins the crown without granting immunity.
- ch. 21 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Priebe answers Klosterman's dream-VCR hyperthetical, would you tape your dreams knowing family and closest friends must watch along, with an enthusiastic yes, defining dreams as mini-movies directed by the unconscious with a nod to Freud's repressed thoughts and Jung's dream symbols, and arguing that lucid control would let her censor or spice the footage mid-dream. She pitches the device as entertainment gold, surreal dreams, post-drinking dreams better than reality television, and nightmare reels her sister could buy, plus an intimacy bonus: after watching, friends and family would finally understand her eccentricities. The punchline is that she does not dream eighty-five percent of the time, so her tapes would cure insomnia rather than scandalize anyone.
- ch. 22 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 23 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Jürgen Habermas — The Public Sphere; Democracy — Sienkiewicz argues that Klosterman's LeBron James 'Decision' episode on Bill Simmons's B.S. Report modeled genuine public-sphere deliberation: where ESPN's Skip Bayless scored points over whether James looked remorseful, Klosterman proposed, via Tupac's 'Only God can judge me', that the televised announcement was a statement of confidence and a refusal to play the one-dimensional reality-TV role America demanded. The chapter lays out Habermas's eighteenth-century public sphere, its salons and 'public sphere of letters' where low-stakes aesthetic arguments (Mozart then, the Guns N' Roses tribute band Paradise City now) train disinterested rational debate, and concedes Habermas's golden age is idealized historical fiction much like a Klosterman hyperthetical. Though Habermas blames commercial media for corroding rational discourse, Sienkiewicz contends that long-form podcasts like The B.S. Report and Contexts give Klosterman's pop-culture arguments the time and independence the old salons had, so arguing about Billy Joel or Ace Frehley strengthens rather than absolves democratic culture.
- ch. 24 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Lisle tests Klosterman's sports essays with Gadamer's hermeneutics: interpreters bring cultural 'prejudices' to a text (the '86 Celtics, soccer, gridiron football) in an open dialogue the text can resist. He argues Klosterman's soccer essay fails as interpretation — it builds a totalitarian-sport thesis ('Mussolini... Khmer Rouge') on his Wyndmere youth baseball coaching grievance, whacking a straw man the text never endorses. The 'Football' essay fares better, but its claim that the read option proves the game's hidden liberality is undercut by the play's 1950s lineage (Arthur Daley's keep-or-give option, Bud Wilkinson's Split-T, Belichick's 'single wing') and by Walter Camp's Taylorist codification of football as managed bureaucracy versus soccer's horizontal, player-driven flow.
- ch. 25 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Richard Rorty — Pragmatism — Brooks argues Klosterman is an unacknowledged heir of McLuhan's media ecology: his Fargo Rock City account of hair metal's rise through FM radio, metal magazines, and MTV's 'collective unconscious,' Britney Spears as a low-definition 'cool medium' everyone rebrands, and the 'singular club scene' as global village all do media-ecology work. But Brooks docks him fifteen percent for clinging to an authenticity fantasy — the Unabomber-essay claim that technology makes us 'never been less human' — which McLuhan's dictum that 'there is absolutely no inevitability as long as there is a willingness to contemplate what is happening' refutes. In Rorty's spirit of persuading by joshing rather than arguing, he challenges Klosterman to drop the ontology and turn his interpretive powers on real problems like compassion fatigue and Darfur.
- ch. 26 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Reisch treats Klosterman's Jack-and-Jane hypothetical (Jane dumps Jack because he watched a woman masturbate) as a trap Otto Neurath called pseudorationalism: in ethics we invent objective-sounding rational laws ('thou shalt not watch thy neighbor masturbate') that collapse under counterexamples about porn, Madonna, and zoo baboons, since only logic and math sustain such systems. The defensible breakup would rest on honest personal reasons — the incident shaking Jane's faith in Jack's commitment — not a nonexistent cosmic rule; the chapter closes by imagining Jane escaping pseudorationalism in 2008 and realizing what her 2005 decision was really worth.
- ch. 27 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Friedrich Nietzsche — Master and Slave Morality; Will to Power — Framed by Dick's wait in a Klosterman signing line, the chapter asks how a critic acquires rock-star power. Plato's Ion supplies the magnet chain of inspiration (muse, poet, rhapsode, spectator), which puts Klosterman near the spectator end — he 'only got to second base' with the muse Thalia until Downtown Owl. Nietzsche's master and slave moralities then explain the critic's ascent: Klosterman is the cunning slave-priest who seizes power from warrior-artists through wit and articulate opinion, exactly as priestly values overthrew warrior values. Dick half-endorses the diagnosis, admits he prefers the slave's world of safety, and concludes that power — even the power to sell books and pay rent — is how we stay alive.
- ch. 29 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** John Stuart Mill, Jeremy Bentham, Aristotle, Plato — Higher and Lower Pleasures; On Liberty; Eudaimonia (flourishing); Theory of Forms — Fitzpatrick asks whether Klosterman's low-culture project (tribute bands, Toby Keith, the McNuggets diet) is mere titillation or genuinely valuable. Bentham's pushpin-versus-poetry hedonism matches Klosterman's rejection of 'guilty pleasures,' while Aristotle's eudaimonia — and Plato's forms, on which a tribute band is a copy of a copy of a copy — condemns a life of amusement.
