Mill resolves the question twice over: higher and lower pleasures justify ranking Klosterman's contemplative criticism above mere sensation, and On Liberty's free market of ideas shows that even false or partial low-culture claims (about Garth Brooks, Spurlock's Super Size Me) keep high culture's truths from decaying into unexamined dogma.
- ch. 30 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Judith Butler — Struggle for Recognition; Philosophy of Love — Mistich and McCullough read Klosterman's desire essays through the psychoanalytic-philosophical tradition. 'This Is Emo' — 'No woman will ever satisfy me' — is glossed with Žižek, Lacan, and Butler: desire is not meant to be fulfilled, and satisfaction would only kill it, so Klosterman's failed Waldorf Astoria romance is structurally inevitable. Kojève's Hegelian thesis that all desire is desire for the other's recognition explains Billy Joel's tragic craving for rock-critic canonization, the cereal-box hunger to be 'cool,' and Klosterman's three-week ultimatum to Diane, while Freud's theory of fantasy shows how pop culture (Lloyd Dobler, internet porn) scripts the romantic and sexual desires it then frustrates.
- ch. 31 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** The Meaning of Life — Vosen answers Klosterman's hyperthetical about cats that suddenly read at a twelfth-grade level against his implied expectation: they would love Garfield rather than find it an insulting caricature. Reading the strip through Dan Walsh's Garfield-minus-Garfield project, she argues the comics reveal that Garfield is the glue keeping Jon Arbuckle from a full existential breakdown, so literate cats would find hope, renewed purpose, and the comfort that life could always be worse — they could be Jon. The exercise turns a throwaway joke into a small meditation on meaninglessness, comparison, and what keeps a life from collapsing.
- ch. 32 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 33 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Pragmatism; Truth as What Works; Fate and Foreknowledge; Faith and Reason — Vannatta reads Klosterman's born-again pop culture — the Left Behind novels, Victory TV's Steven, Kurt Warner's Super Bowl, Jesus Camp — as living Puritan Calvinism: original sin (Klosterman's cruel third-graders), justification by faith alone, unmediated access to God ('every man was his own priest'), and predestination that leaves uncertain whether our actions matter in the divine equation. The chapter's pivot is truth versus peace: Puritan-born-again certainty sacrifices peace for antecedent truth, from the 1675 Massachusetts settlements to Robertson and Falwell on 9/11. Against this Vannatta sets the pragmatist alternative — truth as the outcome of communal inquiry in service of peaceful living — recasting God as the web of relationships and dissolving his own childhood camp's contradiction between 'I'm Third' and its cult of competition.
- ch. 34 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** The Meaning of Life; Immortality and the Soul — Priebe builds a tongue-in-cheek 'Chuckism,' showing that Klosterman's books accidentally supply every element of a religion's formula: a bearded dude-deity, scripture, interpretive community, rules, and an afterlife (heaven as an always-neon Las Vegas, earthly life as purgatory sized by SIDS and 102-year-olds, plus a theory of reincarnation). Her ten commandments — thou shalt rock, swear, name-drop, be illogical, use footnotes — are mined from his actual writing style and its deliberate bad syllogisms. Under the blasphemy sits a serious claim: religion's structure is arbitrary and its orthodoxy enforceable, while Chuck's creed that 'living is dying, little by little' and 'life is tourism' answers the meaning-of-life question the older faiths evade.
- ch. 35 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Consequentialism — Fitzpatrick uses Klosterman's hyperthetical — kick a shackled Clydesdale to death in twenty minutes and every political prisoner on earth goes free — to stage the consequentialism-versus-non-consequentialism dispute. He argues the scenario's framing (a warm, cuddly horse) rigs it for the anti-consequentialist, but once you grant that Amnesty International's prisoners suffer worse than the horse — the tortured Syrian teenage protestor is his example — the question becomes whether one awful act to a horse is permissible to prevent worse suffering to many humans. His only remaining hesitation is practical: could you actually finish the job in twenty minutes?
- ch. 36 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" **·** Authenticity — Klosterman's own epilogue responds to being philosophized about: he understands the book exists because he is 'famous enough' to sell philosophy to readers who do not buy philosophy, and that its favorite source, Sex, Drugs, and Cocoa Puffs, is his least logical book — which is exactly why it sold. He diagnoses his central tension (entertaining writing about unsolvable ideas, consumed by audiences who want only one half), admits via the Lennon 'the pseuds will love it' anecdote that he is an unreliable narrator writing willfully misleading art, and disclaims any intent to persuade anyone of anything. A footnote concedes the chapter on McLuhan that he absorbed McLuhan's ideas 'by accident,' while disputing that the problem of authenticity has been solved — the through-line of his claim that all fifteen hundred pages of his work are about the difference between the 'real' and 'reality.'
- ch. 37 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 38 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "Chuck Klosterman and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Climbing – Philosophy for Everyone - Because It's There

- ch. 1 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(no index entry yet)* — Hans Florine's foreword frames climbing as a way of posing philosophical questions rather than settling them, echoing the book's 'it's the journey' ethos, and previews debates the volume takes up: the sanity/morality of free soloing, hold chipping, and how routes are graded. Florine describes himself as an Ayn Rand Objectivist who also carries Buddhist concern and treats logic as a reliable tool, and calls the 100-percent focus of climbing 'physical meditation.' No taxonomy philosopher (Rand) is actually deployed in argument; the piece is a personal, agenda-setting foreword rather than a sustained application of a philosophical position.
- ch. 9 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(notes)*
- ch. 10 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Utilitarianism — Schmid's editorial introduction reads climbing through Tejada-Flores' 'Games Climbers Play,' arguing that each climbing game (bouldering, trad, expedition) has its own rules and that climbing ethically means respecting the rules of the game one plays, with 'better style' meaning voluntarily adopting more restrictive rules. Invoking Russell on the value of philosophy and Mallory's 'because it's there,' he surveys the volume's essays and their applied-ethical questions about risk, free soloing, hold manufacturing, and environmental impact. The piece is a map of the book's terrain rather than an argument for one doctrine, previewing Krein's Stoic freedom, Treanor's Aristotelian virtue, Levey's Hegel, and Swan's Zen.
- ch. 11 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Epictetus, Zeno of Citium, Marcus Aurelius — Stoicism; The Dichotomy of Control — Kevin Krein argues that the freedom climbers report feeling is not freedom of movement, political freedom, or free will—on those measures a roped climber pinned on a ledge is less free—but the Stoic freedom of aligning one's will with what nature allows. Using Chrysippus' tethered-dog image and the Stoic identification of human logos with cosmic reason, he claims the climber who understands and accepts the mountain's conditions and wills only what it permits becomes free, which is why experienced climbers feel freest when their options are fewest. He adds Epictetus' dichotomy of control: the mountain (storms, avalanches, one's own failing body) is largely outside our power, so limiting desire to what we control—composure, choices, effort—is how a climber stays free in any conditions.
- ch. 13 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** John Stuart Mill, Jeremy Bentham — Utilitarianism; Higher and Lower Pleasures — Prompted by the avalanche death of his climbing partner Charlie on the Super Couloir, Paul Charlton uses Mill's hedonistic utilitarianism to ask whether the risks of climbing can be ethically justified, insisting that blindly accepting risk is indefensible but deliberate, reasoned risk can be right. He weighs climbing's costs (frivolity, self-focus, danger to self and to grieving family) against its intrinsic value (joy, friendship, aesthetic experience) and instrumental value (cultivating human excellence, psychological growth, reprioritized lives), invoking Mill's higher/lower pleasures to argue quality of happiness matters, not just quantity. His conclusion is that climbing can be justified only when it produces more overall good than any alternative use of that time, leaving each climber to run the calculation for their own case.
- ch. 14 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Language Games — Joe Fitschen distinguishes justifying climbing (weak, self-revealing answers) from explaining why humans climb, and offers an evolutionary, naturalistic explanation: the ability and pleasure of climbing are inherited arboreal dispositions that were not selected out and can still have utility, so Mallory's 'because it's there' names a real, gene-rooted urge. He deploys Wittgenstein's analysis of 'game' and family resemblances to block the fallacy of universality—the fact that some people lack the urge to climb says nothing against its evolutionary roots—and warns against teleological thinking about traits. He also treats climbing's pleasures (physical, aesthetic, emotional) and its style-based ethics (bolting disputes, ego vs. the pleasure of the experience) as expressions of an evolved, social human nature.
- ch. 15 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Feminism — Heidi Howkins Lockwood examines the 'asymmetry thesis'—that a climber's gratuitous risk is less acceptable than an astronaut's necessary risk—and argues its two standard defenses fail: the Rule of Rescue is neither necessary (free soloists need no rescue yet are condemned) nor sufficient (Hornbein and Unsoeld's risky Everest traverse was praised because it succeeded), and the argument from compassion collapses into circularity. She then shows the intuition is really driven by external background assumptions, especially gender inequality: the media vilified climber-mother Alison Hargreaves while lauding astronaut-mother Christa McAuliffe, because ambition-for-profit violates the 'traditionally female' role of service. She concedes a limited, defensible version tied to the assumption that seeking risk as a mere end (rather than an ineliminable means) is irresponsible.
- ch. 16 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Golden Mean; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Brian Treanor argues from Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics that climbing is a crucible for cultivating virtues (arete) that contribute to flourishing (eudaimonia), stressing that virtue is acquired by habituation and lies in a mean 'relative to us'—so casting off on the Bachar-Yerian is courage for a 5.11 leader but rashness for a 5.9 climber. He shows how climbing's self-imposed 'rules' (Tejada-Flores' games) forge courage (feeling the right balance of fear and confidence, as in John Sherman's Fisher Towers epic and Steve House's judicious retreats on K7), humility (honestly measuring oneself against pioneers who used inferior gear, per Chouinard and Messner's 'murder of the impossible'), and reverence for nature (Thoreau, Muir, Brower). His thesis is that in a coddled, technological world climbing is uncommonly well suited to building these excellences of character.
- ch. 18 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Virtue Ethics; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Pam Sailors distinguishes two kinds of high-altitude climber: the goal-oriented 'summiteer,' who pursues the summit at all costs for self-affirmation and may neglect others, and the process-oriented 'mountaineer,' who seeks relationship with the mountain, attains self-transcendence, and feels moral responsibility for comrades. Drawing on J. S. Russell and Jonathan Simon, and on cases like the 2006 abandonment of David Sharp versus Dan Mazur giving up his summit to save Lincoln Hall, she argues both types find value in dangerous sport but that the mountaineer—exhibiting courage, compassion, and care—is the morally better person. She backs this with Reinhold Messner, Ed Viesturs, and Sherpa judgment that mountains are not conquered but simply allow, or do not allow, us to climb them.
- ch. 19 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Philip Ebert and Simon Robertson defend the self-sufficiency thesis (S): other things being equal, the more self-sufficiently a mountaineering objective is achieved, the better the achievement. Ranking Hermann Buhl's solo, free, alpine-style first ascent of Nanga Parbat above a hypothetical roped-team version and far above a guided, oxygen-and-porter Everest ascent, they argue self-sufficiency (alpine over siege, solo over team, free over aid) is good because it is more committing and adventurous and is partly constitutive of mountaineering itself. They handle objections about foolhardiness (Lafaille on Makalu), incomparable routes (Lhotse South Face), teamwork, and blind climber Erik Weihenmayer via an 'other things being equal' clause, and conclude self-sufficiency acts as a catalyst raising the subjective difficulty and thus the value of every other mountaineering good.
- ch. 20 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** G.W.F. Hegel — Master–Slave Dialectic; Authenticity — Ben Levey uses the fast-food (sport) versus haute-cuisine (trad) analogy and then Hegel's Phenomenology to argue that trad climbing offers a more authentic experience than sport climbing. Applying the master–slave dialectic, he casts the trad climber (like his mentor Martin risking a groundfall on 'Resurrection' placing natural protection) as the slave: through genuine fear, the setting aside of desire, and work that leaves the rock in its independent, unaltered state, the climber comes to identify with the world rather than dominate it. Sport climbing, where bolts subordinate the rock's natural features to the climber's chosen safe line, corresponds to the master who never overcomes the world's independence, yielding only the limited satisfaction of having played the game ethically.
- ch. 21 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** The Buddha — Zen Buddhism; Buddhism — Eric Swan argues that climbing embodies the principles and spirit of Zen, the experience of 'ordinary mind' finding the extraordinary in the ordinary and living fully in the present. Tracing Zen from the Buddha's Flower Sermon and Bodhidharma through Suzuki's 'Beginner's Mind' and Huineng's finger-and-moon, he maps Zen's four training paths—physical work, zazen (sitting meditation), koans, and interviews with masters—onto climbing: routes are like koans solved by insight and action rather than logic, 'being in the zone' is meditative absorption, and breath-regulation at the crux is zazen in motion. He holds up free soloist Derek Hersey, grinning hundreds of feet up Eldorado Canyon, and Chris Sharma, who writes of flowing 'like water' on the rock, as modern Zen masters who dissolve the separation between self, climb, and world.
- ch. 22 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 23 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, John Stuart Mill — The Übermensch; Will to Power; On Liberty — Scott reads three landmark climbs as expressions of the tension between individual freedom and community standards. He casts John Bachar's bold, ground-up, run-out Bachar-Yerian and its self-mastery ethic as a partial embodiment of Nietzsche's Übermensch and will to power, and Alan Watts' rule-breaking sport route To Bolt or Not to Be as an exercise of Mill's On Liberty defense of originality and diversity that benefits the whole community. Against both thinkers' negative view of community, he uses Charles Taylor's 'horizon of significance' to argue that Sonnie Trotter's The Path gains its meaning only through dialogue with the climbing community and its traditions.
- ch. 24 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Peter Singer — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); The Drowning Child (obligation to aid) — Ramsey applies the methods of practical/applied ethics to the taboo of hold manufacturing ('chipping'), using Peter Singer's drowning-child argument as his model of following an argument to its uncomfortable conclusion. He builds a syllogism: since climbers accept modifying rock (removing loose rock, vegetation) to make routes climbable, and hold manufacturing is another such modification, manufacturing is sometimes acceptable. He dismantles four common objections (safety, environment, harming future climbers, slippery slope) to argue the near-universal condemnation of chipping is an unjustified bias inconsistent with climbers' actual practices, like the chiseled Jardine Traverse on The Nose.
- ch. 25 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** John Locke, Aristotle, Jeremy Bentham, John Stuart Mill — Natural Rights; Virtue Ethics; Consequentialism — Agnafors asks whether free soloing is morally permissible, opening with soloists dropping ice chips into his coffee cup to show soloing is not purely private. He tests Locke's natural rights to life and liberty (concluding no act is truly private since rescues, taxpayers, and grieving families are affected), Aristotle's virtue ethics (worrying that habitual risk-taking forms a reckless character), and Bentham and Mill's consequentialism (Bill's confidence-boosting solo of Nutcracker versus Derek Hersey's fatal fall). He concludes no universal answer exists, offering one rule: the soloist must carefully weigh all these considerations beforehand.
- ch. 26 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** David Hume, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, John Stuart Mill — Social Darwinism — Murray argues that the ecologically conscious climber's doubt that her single climb matters is a version of the free-rider problem, which he claims is just the sorites paradox (the paradox of the heap, illustrated with trail cairns built one stone fewer each time) applied to social life. Because 'environmental degradation' is a vaguely specified term with no precise tipping point, he draws on Timothy Williamson's account of vagueness and Richard Tuck's history of free-riding to argue climbers should act 'as if' a threshold exists and see themselves as sufficient (not necessary) causes of preservation, justifying self-restraint and quota systems.
- ch. 27 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** John Locke, Adam Smith — Labor Theory of Property; Anarchism; The Invisible Hand — Halbert uses a Hawaiian route-setter friend upset that his 'secret' crag went public to argue climbing culture is a gift economy that defies Locke's labor theory of property and the self-interest of Locke and Adam Smith. Route setters spend money and labor establishing climbs, then give them freely to a commons, exemplifying Kropotkin's social-anarchist mutual aid over Social Darwinist competition. Citing the Red River Gorge Coalition and Muir Valley, she recasts Hardin's tragedy of the commons as Carol Rose's 'comedy of the commons,' where more participants enhance rather than diminish the shared value.
- ch. 28 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** René Descartes — Philosophical Skepticism; Possible Worlds — Downes argues, against a Cartesian confidence in self-knowledge, that climbers do not have reliable access to their own internal states while climbing. Drawing on Nisbett and Wilson's sock experiment and dual-process 'new unconscious' psychology, he shows self-reports are often post-hoc confabulations: climbers who declare a wobbler 'felt easy,' claim to be 'not strong enough' on moves they later top-rope easily, or unknowingly dab or stand on the rope. He recommends leaning on external 'scaffolding' (partners, chalk marks, guidebooks) and the 'you're only as good as your last problem' rule to temper dangerous self-assessments, since overrating ability sits in a deceptively close possible world.
- ch. 30 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** John Locke — Scientific Realism vs Anti-Realism — Graziano rejects the 'orthodox view' that a climbing grade like Serenity Crack's 5.10d is subjective (beauty in the eye of the beholder) and argues for realism about grades. Using Locke's primary/secondary quality distinction and his dispositional account of hardness, he defends 'relationalism': a grade is a real disposition of the rock to challenge a standard climber in standard conditions, just as hardness is a real disposition. He supports this with Pinched Rib changing from 5.7 to 5.10b when a hold broke, and argues the orthodox view confuses what a grade IS with how we determine it, rejecting even the 'weakly objective' money analogy.
- ch. 31 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle, Maurice Merleau-Ponty, G.E. Moore — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime; Phenomenology; Embodiment (the lived body) — Karlsen argues that climbing routes are genuine aesthetic objects and that judging a route beautiful is a real aesthetic judgment, not mere pleasure. Against Kant's demand that beauty require detachment (which would confine climbing to the 'merely agreeable') and against Aristotle's exclusion of the bodily senses, he distinguishes a 'line' (seen from the ground, like The Nose) from a 'route' (actually climbed) and contends that proprioception, the felt sense of bodily movement, yields information about the route itself, not just the body. Invoking the phenomenological 'transparency of experience' (Moore's diaphanousness), he concludes proprioception is an aesthetic sense that lets widely-agreed classics emerge.
- ch. 32 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "Cannabis Philosophy for Everyone" *(notes)*

## Coffee – Philosophy for Everyone - Grounds for Debate

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(notes)*
- ch. 4 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(no index entry yet)* — Pendergrast surveys coffee's history to explain why the same drink has always drawn extreme praise and condemnation — from Sufi monks and the 1511 Mecca ban, through the Women's Petition Against Coffee and Charles II's 1675 suppression proclamation, to Postum's scare ads and modern epidemiology on cancer and birth defects. It is a historical and scientific account (caffeine as an adenosine-blocking plant pesticide) rather than a philosophical argument; its closest philosophical move is the closing 'you are what you drink' hypothesis that we demonize or glorify the beverages we depend on.
- ch. 13 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Immanuel Kant — Faith and Reason — Austin uses the everyday sense that 'we need the bean' to introduce a metaphysical debate about whether we need God to ground ethics, framed through the fair-trade coffee case. He contrasts Erik Wielenberg, who treats necessary ethical truths (e.g., that unfair trade is wrong, that suffering is intrinsically evil) as brute 'furniture of the universe' and follows Kant in holding that we ought to be moral simply because it is our duty, with J. P. Moreland, who argues such objective moral values, our knowledge of them, and the normative pull of obligation fit a theistic worldview better than naturalism. The chapter presents God as the 'Necessary Ground of Being' behind moral reality without settling the dispute.
- ch. 14 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Thomas Nagel, Frank Jackson, David Hume, Socrates, David Lewis — What Is It Like to Be a Bat?; The Knowledge Argument (Mary's Room); The Unexamined Life — Phillips argues that there is something-it-is-like to be a knowledgeable coffee drinker, using Nagel's bat and Jackson's Mary thought experiments — but where the bat is 'fundamentally alien,' coffee expertise is learnable, and via David Lewis's ability hypothesis the reader can apply book-knowledge of acidity, body, finish, and flavor to abilities they already have. He then invokes Hume's 'Of the Standard of Taste' (Homer's enduring appeal, the Don Quixote wine-tasters) to claim aesthetic and moral judgments spring from the same refinable sentiments, so that examining your cup free of prejudice is, echoing Socrates' 'unexamined life,' something we almost owe ourselves: the unexamined cup is not worth drinking.
- ch. 15 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** The Buddha — The Four Noble Truths; Non-Self (anatman); Impermanence (anicca); Karma and Rebirth — Geisz reads his daily morning coffee ritual through Buddhist metaphysics: the pre-caffeine grogginess and its transient relief illustrate saṃsāra and duḥkha, the First Noble Truth, driven by self-centered craving (the Second). The transience of the perfect-temperature cup shows impermanence (anitya), and the question of whether a 'self' pre- or post-coffee exists is answered by anatman — he offers three hypotheses (full self dormant, half coffee-cyborg, self created ex Coffea) then dissolves them into no-self plus dependent arising, where the cup and drinker are empty nodes in a web of physical, economic, and karmic conditions. Each intentional sip lays down karma that binds tomorrow's 'me' further into the cycle.
- ch. 16 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Martin Buber, Søren Kierkegaard, Albert Camus — The Look (hell is other people); I–Thou Relation; Existentialism — Hernandez contrasts 'dark' existentialism — Sartre's café-bound claim that hell is other people and that the self is imprisoned in others' looks (illustrated by Starbucks' Pike Place backlash reshaping its identity), and Camus's absurd alienation — with an 'optimistic existentialism' that finds meaning in community. Drawing on Kierkegaard's Christian love, Jaspers's 'encounter,' Marcel's turn toward the other (her Sweet Eugene's pregnancy story), and above all Buber's I–Thou versus I–It, she argues the coffee community (neighborhood boards, fair-trade activism, the neighborly cup) is an existential ground where isolated bodies-toward-death create shared hope by treating each patron as a Thou rather than a discarded It.
- ch. 17 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Socrates, Plato, Robert Nozick — Socratic Method (elenchus); The Unexamined Life — Parker defends the coffeehouse as an accessible venue for real philosophy through Christopher Phillips's Socrates Café movement (and Marc Sautet's cafés-philos), arguing that Socratizing — sustained questioning that offers objections and alternatives, not mere talk — makes everyone a potential 'Socrates.' Answering the charge that amateur café philosophy is mere sophistry, he turns to Plato's Sophist to note the sophist is the one who takes payment and has an agenda, so it is salaried professors, not unpaid Phillips, who resemble sophists. Framing thoughtlessness (via Nietzsche's 'oblivion' and David Foster Wallace's cruise-ship essay) as philosophy's true adversary, he endorses a softened Socratic/Nozickian maxim that the unexamined life is simply not lived as fully.
- ch. 19 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Jürgen Habermas, Hannah Arendt — The Public Sphere — Bar-Tura applies Habermas's Structural Transformation of the Public Sphere to argue coffeehouses once formed a genuine public sphere where private people 'bracketed' status under the force of the better argument — from the 1680–1730 'golden age' and Charles II's suppression proclamation to the Green Dragon and City Tavern that brewed the American Revolution and the India Coffeehouse of Lahore. He notes Nancy Fraser's feminist critique that these spheres excluded women, then argues today's chain 'coffee shops' (Oldenburg's 'third place' hollowed into laptop isolation) have become places of 'common solitude'; citing Arendt on power arising only when people act together and on thoughtlessness, he calls for reclaiming coffee-houses as sites of discussion and social change (Café Society, Discussions over Coffee).
- ch. 20 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Aristotle, Ludwig Wittgenstein — Existentialism; Eudaimonia (flourishing); Man as Political Animal; The Private Language Argument — Sadler argues black coffee, unlike ceremonial tea, is the realist emblem of our anxious, isolated postmodern condition, and that the chain coffeehouse is a 'non-place' of public anonymity that only simulates the sociality we crave. Against Aristotle's confident view of humans as social animals whose eudaimonia presupposes a settled social order, she sets Nietzsche's alienated individual free to create values — then argues Nietzsche errs: using the trivial cappuccino-vs-latte choice, she shows (via Wittgenstein's private-language argument and Korsgaard) that even solitary choices need reasons intelligible to others, so individual choice cannot be self-justifying and our search for meaningful, non-arbitrary lives is irreducibly social.
- ch. 21 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(no index entry yet)* — Romaya argues that coffee, uniquely among beverages, best aids the act of doing philosophy: alcohol (since Plato's Symposium) dulls sobriety and yields carefree, volatile, forgettable conversation; energy drinks and soda lack a social context; and tea, though social, carries calming rather than sharpening associations. Coffee instead sharpens critical thinking, alertness, and short-term memory while carrying rich social and ceremonial dimensions (Middle Eastern hospitality, tasseography fortune-telling, Sufi ritual). Granting philosophy has no single method, he holds all traditions rely on sustained critical reflection that coffee-in-liquid-form best fuels, dismissing coffee-flavored foods and coffee-drug combinations as ineffective.
- ch. 22 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 23 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Emmanuel Levinas, Epicurus — Phenomenology; The Face of the Other; Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia) — Buckingham stages a phenomenology of a wasted café afternoon over three cups, arguing against a passage from Levinas's Nine Talmudic Readings that condemns the café as a 'non-place' where relaxing without obligation lets us tolerate the world's horrors. Against Levinas, he claims the coffeehouse's public 'solitude alongside others' sustains a courteous solidarity and, like the Epicurean Garden, offers necessary respite that fuels creative responsibility rather than evading the demand of the Other. He self-consciously adopts Levinas's own phenomenological method—attending to 'what is it like?'—to defend idling as the condition for responding to suffering with grace rather than grim duty.
- ch. 24 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Moral Relativism — Davids tastes Classic Red Brand X, Starbucks Guatemala Antigua, and the $100/lb Panama Esmeralda, and asks whether one can prove any coffee universally 'better.' He concedes taste is relative—citing East Asian dislike of acidity and the European relish for fermented 'rio-y' and Robusta notes North Americans call rotten—but escapes relativism via Stanley Fish's notion of 'interpretive communities': a dominant global community of expert cuppers shares assumptions and vocabulary (acidity, mouthfeel, sweetness, clean flavor) that let them rank the three coffees consistently. Legitimate evaluation is thus grounded not in the coffee itself but in the shared code of a community of judgment.
- ch. 25 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Immanuel Kant, Karl Marx, Theodor Adorno, Herbert Marcuse, Walter Benjamin — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime; The Culture Industry; Commodity Fetishism — Wear traces the espresso aesthetic from the idealized, egalitarian Italian caffè—which he reads as an 'aestheticized Marxist' image of identical shots for businessman and street sweeper—to its dilution under neoliberal consumer choice, epitomized by the endless-customization L.A. Story 'half-caf twist of lemon' scene. He invokes Kant's Critique of Judgement and the sublime to argue that flavor and taste-judgment are neglected aesthetic faculties, and enlists the Frankfurt School (Adorno, Benjamin, Marcuse) and Bourdieu's cultural capital to explain how the market absorbs the espresso's beauty. He concludes that a single humble shot, as a memorial to the vast exploited labor chain behind it, is a truer aesthetic object than a branded frappuccino.
- ch. 26 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Martin Heidegger, Hans-Georg Gadamer — Philosophical Hermeneutics (fusion of horizons); Thrownness (Geworfenheit); Ideology and False Consciousness — Hartmann argues the specialty-coffee 'third wave' (Stumptown, Intelligentsia, Counter Culture) could not exist without Starbucks, which taught consumers to pay for a boldly-roasted, ethically-branded, self-gifting aesthetic under neoliberalism. Drawing on Heidegger's thrownness and Gadamer's hermeneutical phenomenology, he punctures Nicholas Cho's claim that the third wave 'lets the coffee speak for itself': just as we perceive a chair as for-sitting through pre-given cultural meaning, cupping rituals actively build a coffee 'hermeneutic' that reshapes the drinker's very perception (the trained palate 'tastes' plantain and graham cracker). He likens this to ventriloquism—the coffee only 'speaks' because someone feeds it its lines—and uses Žižek's account of cynical ideology to expose the gap between fair-trade branding and reality.
- ch. 27 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Parker interviews Stumptown's director of operations Matt Lounsbury, probing for a single underlying value that unifies the roaster's ethics and aesthetics. Lounsbury insists quality—'how good the coffee can be'—is the gravitational center that pulls ethical behavior (record-breaking direct-trade prices, building washing stations in Rwanda, full health care for baristas, the Meet the Producers program) into orbit, while Parker suspects a deeper commitment to community and justice. The exchange enacts the book's recurring ethics/aesthetics entanglement: whenever asked about one, Lounsbury 'slips into' the other, framing direct trade as a win-win-win business model rather than idealism.
- ch. 28 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Bramucci and Mulholland use Kenyan smallholder farmers—Lucy Wanja, David Gachigi, Henry Maina, who earn $0.02–$0.10 per pound for coffee sold north for $12—to argue that fair-trade certification, while an improvement, secures only a 'minimum' wage and leaves growers trapped by mills, middlemen, and depressed global prices. They advance direct trade (Intelligentsia, Stumptown, and the author's own Safi Coffee, which makes farmers shareholders and pays up to twenty-fold more) as a more genuinely just alternative that invests in producers' long-term capacity. The ethical claim is that 'fair' as a sticker falls short of real equity, and that consumers' smug conscience is only earned by paying beyond the fair-trade minimum.
- ch. 30 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); The Naturalistic Fallacy — Kirkwood argues that caffeine is uncontroversially a performance-enhancing drug—boosting memory, reaction time, endurance—and that by WADA's own three-part test (performance-enhancing, potentially harmful, against the spirit of sport) coffee should count as doping, yet never has. He dismantles the standard anti-doping arguments as applied to coffee: the cheating/unfairness charge is circular and dissolves when everyone may use it; the 'unnaturalness' argument commits a non sequitur (umbrellas are unnatural too, and chewed coffee beans seem natural); and the harm test fails because the positive threshold sits at a mere 20-ounce serving, far below caffeinism. His conclusion is that coffee escapes the 'doping' label not through moral reasoning but through public acceptance—the emotive word 'drug' is withheld from the substance everyone uses.
- ch. 31 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Ecofeminism; Anthropocentrism; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Aleman, an ethnobotanist, asks 'Is coffee green?' and unpacks the many senses of 'green' (raw bean, naïve, money, and ecologically friendly) before weighing coffee's growth, organic and shade-grown methods, fair trade, globalization, and value chain. She judges coffee greenest at the source (shade-grown farms preserve migrating-bird habitat) but least green in the fossil-fueled journey from Global South farm to Global North cup, invoking Vandana Shiva's claim that 'drinking coffee is a political act' and Rigoberta Menchú on coffee and indigenous survival. She concludes the answer is partly subjective—withholding final judgment while resolving to keep drinking fair-trade, organic, shade-grown, single-source coffee—and warns of the cognitive dissonance, mirroring oil dependence, between judging coffee ungreen and drinking it anyway.
- ch. 32 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" **·** Aristotle — Eudaimonia (flourishing); The Golden Mean; Practical Wisdom (phronesis) — Keleher applies Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics to coffee, arguing that eudaimonia (flourishing, not mere pleasant feeling) is rational activity in accordance with virtue, that the human ergon is reason, and that money, pleasure, and honor are only instrumental to it. She illustrates the golden mean and phronêsis with coffee choices—the temperate drinker like Katherine or Samantha calibrates cups to her own tolerance and circumstances rather than a fixed number—and distinguishes the truly virtuous drinker from the merely continent one who still craves the forbidden 750-calorie frappuccino. She coins the minor virtue of 'javavity,' the habit of drinking good coffee the right way for the right reasons with the right feeling, and suggests that were Aristotle alive, coffee would join his 'external goods,' the proper equipment for doing noble acts.
- ch. 33 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(no index entry yet)* — The Coffee Bean Guys (James Kirkland and Dan Levy) deliver a comic afterword sending up both Hollywood hustle and philosophy itself, joking that philosophy is 'the science of getting high and asking: Why?'—which is what they do all day at a West Hollywood Coffee Bean & Tea Leaf while accomplishing nothing. Their running gag insists coffee is a drug ('wake up and smell the coffee you spilled on yourself') and that appearances matter more than reality: look busy, do nothing, and people will give you money. It is satire rather than argument, but it plays on the book's themes of coffee as stimulant, coffeehouse as workplace, and the caffeinated thinker who thinks hard and does little.
- ch. 34 "Coffee - Philosophy for Everyone" *(notes)*

- ch. 19 "College Sex - Philosophy for Everyone Philosophers With Benefits" **·** Socrates, David Hume, The Buddha, Charles Sanders Peirce — Socratic Method (elenchus); Objectification; Bundle Theory of Self; Phenomenology — This span (the book's front matter plus the Part I 'Hook-Up Culture' essays) argues that college hook-up culture can be examined philosophically rather than merely lived. Hole and Chen recast reckless sexual 'experimentation' as a Socratic self-experiment, having students name a change, run it, and interrogate a 'big question' (e.g. Chen's dare to abstain all freshman year) to become a 'wiser lover.' Romaya argues that gay and lesbian students' opposite-sex 'straight sex experiments' undermine fixed sexual identity, invoking Buddhist anatman and Hume's bundle theory to reject a static self. Bruce gives a phenomenological reading of how Facebook/MySpace profiles and text/level-jumping alienate courting students, and Lunceford treats the 'walk of shame' outfit via Peirce's index/symbol semiotics as a sign of feminine sexuality marked shameful.
- ch. 20 "College Sex - Philosophy for Everyone Philosophers With Benefits" **·** Epicurus, Lucretius, Aristotle, Charles Sanders Peirce — Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Philosophy of Love — This span closes the walk-of-shame semiotics and then applies ancient ethics to college relationships. Puka defends long-distance college relationships through Aristotle's account of philia—arguing (provocatively, per Aristotle) that love is an activity, so being apart 'murders' love unless couples make Skype, sexting, and texting into the relationship's real medium rather than a substitute. Stephens brings Epicurus and Lucretius to 'friends with benefits': since sexual passion is a natural-but-unnecessary desire that breeds anxiety and love is a wound to the mind, while friendship is necessary for ataraxia, he concludes students should skip the sex and keep the friend—'friends are the real benefits,' echoing Tina Turner's 'What's Love Got to Do with It?' Levine/Mongeau and Smith/Morrison supply communication-research data on how FWB relationships form, are negotiated (usually not), and often end when one partner 'catches feelings.'
- ch. 21 "College Sex - Philosophy for Everyone Philosophers With Benefits" **·** Socrates, Plato, Martin Heidegger, Immanuel Kant, Thomas Nagel — Philosophy of Love; Authenticity; Objectification; Humanity as an End in Itself — This span turns to the ethics and epistemology of college sex. Kania argues there is no clear line between close friendship and a sexual relationship, so condemning student-professor sex logically forces condemning student-professor friendship—rejecting Golash's split and defending carefully-conducted consensual relationships. Layne narrates Socrates/Alcibiades, Abelard/Heloise, and Heidegger/Arendt to contrast Socratic eros (Plato's Symposium, love as a friend of wisdom directed at the soul) and Heideggerian authentic love ('I want you to be what you are') against Abelard's exploitative lust. McDowell mines epistemology (justification, taking beliefs 'to heart,' reflective equilibrium) to argue better thinking yields better, more autonomous sex. Pinku weighs 'plain sex' against Nagel's interpersonal-arousal account of embodiment to ask whether sex needs love, and Brophy uses Kantian dignity (treating the body as a mere means, not an end) to argue that prostitution and webcam porn undertaken to pay tuition violate the student's autonomy.
- ch. 22 "College Sex - Philosophy for Everyone Philosophers With Benefits" **·** Immanuel Kant, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, John Stuart Mill, Karl Marx — Humanity as an End in Itself; Objectification; Ideology and False Consciousness; On Liberty — The essay argues that soaring tuition drives college students into sexual self-commodification—egg-selling ('Pay your tuition with eggs'), craigslist/backpage escorting, and dorm-porn webcam sites like VoyeurDorm and CocoDorm—and reads these cases through Kant's second formulation, condemning them as treating oneself and others as mere means rather than ends of intrinsic dignity. It applies Marx's notion of false consciousness to Natalie Dylan's virginity auction and Ashley Dupré's 'empowerment' claims, comparing them to women choosing high heels as internalized oppression. It uses Rousseau's Social Contract and Mill's On Liberty to argue that prostitution is a form of illegitimate 'voluntary slavery' because one cannot rightly alienate one's own inalienable freedom, and contrasts coerced cases (Helena in Singapore, Rosie Reid's traumatic auctioned deflowering, John Gechter's porn suspension from a Christian college) with less-coerced ones to argue moral concern should track the degree of circumstantial coercion.

## Comics as Philosophy

- ch. 1 "Start" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "index" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "index" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "index" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "index" **·** Gottfried Leibniz, René Descartes, John Stuart Mill — Possible Worlds; The Best of All Possible Worlds; The Harm Principle — This composite chapter opens with the volume's Introduction and then runs several essays. Jeff McLaughlin's 'What If?' reads DC's Crisis on Infinite Earths and Marvel's What If? series as literal illustrations of Leibniz's possible-worlds metaphysics: the multiverse of Earth-1, Earth-2 and the 3,000 doomed universes dramatizes counterfactuals and Leibniz's Theodicy argument (via the principle of sufficient reason) that God actualizes only the best of infinitely many possible worlds, with Descartes invoked on why a perfect God permits imperfection. Amy Kiste Nyberg's essay analyzes the 1954 Senate hearings on EC horror comics (Gaines's 'The Orphan') as an applied-ethics clash between publishers' free expression and protecting children, explicitly deploying Mill's On Liberty and harm principle to argue why the child-audience framing defeats the anti-censorship case. Stanford Carpenter's piece treats the Black Captain America (Truth: Red, White, and Black) as an epistemology-of-authorship problem.
- ch. 6 "index" **·** Plato, Jean-Paul Sartre, Friedrich Nietzsche, Martin Heidegger — Existentialism; Bad Faith; Allegory of the Cave; Deconstruction — This composite chapter argues philosophy through several comics. Jeremy Barris pairs Plato's dialogues with Amazing Spider-Man, using the allegory of the cave, the Forms, and Platonic love/eros to show that Spider-Man's bodily beauty, awkwardness, and self-trivializing irony draw readers toward goodness the way Plato says lust for beautiful bodies leads the soul upward to truth. Iain Thomson reads Moore and Gibbons's Watchmen as a 'deconstruction of the hero,' running Rorschach, Ozymandias and Dr. Manhattan through Nietzsche's Ubermensch and 'do not throw away the hero in your soul,' Heidegger's authentic 'choosing one's hero' and the uncanny, Kierkegaard's knight of faith, and Baudrillard's idea that an extreme realization destroys its own idea. Laura and Paul Canis read Daniel Clowes's Ghost World through Sartre's existentialism: Enid's post-high-school 'three o'clock' limbo enacts thrownness, abandonment, radical freedom and the flight from it into bad faith, contrasting Enid's authentic self-creation with Rebecca's settled conformity. Aldo Regalado's essay treats Tarzan, Superman, Captain America and the Black Panther as responses to modernity and race.
- ch. 7 "index" **·** Arne Naess, Hannah Arendt, Aldo Leopold, Aristotle, Jeremy Bentham, John Stuart Mill, Thomas Aquinas — Deep Ecology; Anthropocentrism; Ecofeminism; Totalitarianism — This file bundles three essays. Kevin de Laplante reads Paul Chadwick's Concrete story 'Stay Tuned for Pearl Harbor', treating Concrete's angry monologue about oil companies and overpopulation as enlightened anthropocentrism, while Maureen's silent reverie of merging her body with root systems and being 'raped' by bulldozers dramatizes Arne Naess's deep-ecological expanded self and an ecofeminist link between the oppression of nature-as-female and of women; the utilitarianism of Bentham and Mill and Leopold's 'think like a mountain' land ethic frame the anthropocentrism/nonanthropocentrism debate. Pierre Skilling reads The Adventures of Tintin through Montesquieu's and Aristotle's typologies of government, casting Marshal Kurvi-Tasch's Borduria as Arendt-style totalitarianism (a secret police as true executive, a bid for world domination) against the legitimate monarchy of King Muskar XII, whose Syldavian sceptre works as a check on royal power. Terry Kading argues that 9/11 collapsed the superhero genre's binary of good versus evil into reality: bin Laden's 'monomaniacs' act as supervillains with a murky agenda, yet Spider-Man, Captain America and Superman comics concede no real superhero can rescue us, leaving only ordinary heroism and the compromised politics of national security.
- ch. 8 "index" *(notes)*

## Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy - Awaken the Social Assassin Within

- ch. 1 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The editor's introduction argues that Curb-Larry is 'the Larry in all of us': a socially free figure who says what the rest of us repress, from refusing lunch invitations with 'No, I don't really like you that much' to chewing out sample abusers, so that viewers live vicariously through him. Quoting Larry David and Susie Essman on how playing TV-Larry lets the real Larry be more honest ('there's a lack of artifice about TV-Larry'), it frames the book's puzzle: some people hate Curb-Larry because he refuses to pay his dues under the social rules, while others love him because he exposes how dishonestly and un-free their own lives are. It closes by previewing the volume's chapters on ethics, authenticity, religion, race relations, gifts, and Larry's piercing stare.
- ch. 10 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Immanuel Kant, Martin Heidegger, John Searle, Ludwig Wittgenstein — Ralkowski tests the three classic theories of humor against Curb: Hobbesian superiority (we laugh down at Larry stealing a 5 wood from a corpse or ruining functions), Freudian relief (the rat-dog stomping as released repressed desire, and Larry's deathbed remarks about mayo in 'The End' as gallows humor), and Kantian incongruity (asking Directory Information for 'Krayzee-Eyez Killa,' the vow renewal about bridge tables). Each theory proves either unnecessary or insufficient, so he argues Curb's real engine is what he calls the 'unknown known': the Heideggerian and Wittgensteinian background practices and Searlean social reality we know without knowing we know, like tiptoeing at night or the 'stop and chat.' Larry's jokes work as Mary Douglas's 'anti-rites,' exposing the sheer arbitrariness of unwritten social rules, so comedy at its best both describes and undermines how we live.
- ch. 12 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" **·** Possible Worlds — Westfall asks whether the Larry we watch is a character or the man himself, since George Costanza was based on Larry yet Curb-Larry insists 'I'm George' and takes Jason Alexander's roast of George personally in 'The Car Salesman.' Sorting the season-seven 'Seinfeld' reunion into three realities—Real, Curb, and Seinfeld—he shows Jason Alexander playing himself playing George while George says Larry's 'pretty, pretty, pretty good' inside a fiction where Larry David does not exist, leaving Larry 'real in fewer ways' than his castmates. The chapter concludes that we can adore and despise Larry at the same moment only because the show makes us treat a non-existent character as real, capped by Larry's catastrophic attempt to play George himself ('There were two Darrens on Bewitched').
- ch. 13 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" **·** Bertrand Russell, Walter Benjamin, Theodor Adorno, Friedrich Nietzsche, Aristotle — The Meaning of Life — Mussell defends Larry's 'idle ideology' against the work ethic that Cheryl, Jeff, and Richard Lewis enforce when they call his car-selling experiment insane or demand he write 'for your own sanity.' Following Weber's story of the Calvinist calling, Russell's In Praise of Idleness, and Adorno's lament that small talk has been assimilated by the utilitarian order, the chapter argues Larry's active idling—leisurely lunches, poker, golf, refusing the 'stop and chat'—reclaims time and makes genuine Socratic conversation possible. His sidewalk ambling and notebook jottings are cast as Benjamin's flaneur and Aristotle's peripatetic strolling, the creative idleness in which Curb's story ideas are born, and the chapter concludes that life's too short to be wasted waiting in line.
- ch. 15 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" **·** Socrates — The Golden Rule; Consequentialism; Civil Disobedience — Svolba and Flanders read Curb as an extended investigation of civility—the rules of what people 'just don't do,' from using the doctor's phone to touring Susie's new house to singing 'Happy Birthday' at Ben Stiller's party. Larry plays Socratic gadfly: refusing the tour harms no one and treats no one unfairly, and Susie's only justification ('it's just not done') is circular, so his incivility can seem admirable, even like minor civil disobedience. Against this, the chapter argues (crediting Cheshire Calhoun) that rules of civility are conventional vehicles for communicating respect for others' feelings and interests—what matters to Susie ought to matter to Larry—and, more consequentially, that a social world full of Larry Davids would be unbearable. Larry's own Golden-Rule litmus test, by which he never talks in movie theaters because he would hate it himself, misses this point, since civility is precisely about what others happen to want and like.
- ch. 16 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Alasdair MacIntyre, Jean-Paul Sartre, Plato, Aristotle — Virtues, Practices and Traditions; Theory of Forms — Mudde and Piercey argue Curb depicts Nietzschean nihilism made humdrum: in Larry's world 'the highest values devalue themselves,' so Ted Danson's 'anonymous' donation is leaked for glory, a terrorist warning becomes leverage in a tiff with Mindy Reiser, and strangers judge everyone constantly because no one is a moral authority. The Platonic and Aristotelian answers to what 'the good' is have no purchase here; moral language survives only as a hollow shell, which is MacIntyre's 'simulacra of morality,' and his own remedy—rebuilding communities that give moral rules a point—fails because on Curb community is hell ('I'd rather have the thieves than the neighbors'). Since ought no longer implies can, the show is tragic, though the Tourettic chef's opening night in 'The Grand Opening,' where Larry leads a communal torrent of obscenities in solidarity, offers a fragile glimpse of doing a little good.
- ch. 18 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Friedrich Nietzsche, Karl Marx, Sigmund Freud — Bad Faith; Authenticity; The Death of God; Ideology and False Consciousness — Zanelotti takes up the charge that Larry is a self-hating Jew, from whistling Wagner to 'The End,' where learning he is a gentile turns him into a churchgoing enthusiast who sings 'Go Tell It on the Mountain' and gives Richard Lewis his kidney. Sartre's bad faith diagnoses both poles: Larry denying his facticity by imagining he can simply shed his Jewish past and re-adopt it at will, and critics who demand he live up to an 'authentic' Jewish essence; Marx's false consciousness, meanwhile, explains the self-hating baldy who buys into the haired agenda. Using Ronald de Sousa's rejection of the 'Just Joking Defense'—we are accountable for laughter because we let ourselves become people who cannot restrain it—the chapter argues the show sets a comic frame that mocks bad faith rather than Judaism. Its deeper target, via Nietzsche's death of God, is the overenthusiastic meaning we manufacture when faith is 'curbed,' which is why Larry ridicules only public displays of religion, not belief itself.
- ch. 19 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" **·** Kwame Anthony Appiah — Rocha argues bald Larry is an unlikely guide through America's racial minefield: because hair is an arbitrary but socially enforced line, his own slights as a member of a disfavored L-group let him grasp Appiah's account of identity labels we stereotype, treat differentially, and internalize. The 'Larry David Solution to Racial Difference' is to keep asking awkward questions—Leon's permission to say 'brother,' whether Chinese children are natural chopstick users, whether the doll is 'mulatto'—since only members of a group may define it and polite silence lets prejudice fester in the subconscious. The centerpiece is the use-mention distinction: in 'The N Word' Larry merely mentions the n-word while quoting a racist, yet Dr. Page hears a use and shaves Jeff bald, showing both the precision of the distinction and its limits without proper context.
- ch. 21 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" **·** Sigmund Freud, G.W.F. Hegel, Jacques Lacan — Struggle for Recognition; Feminism — Duncan reads Larry and Jeff as the two unlivable extremes of American masculinity: Larry the neurotic impotent, paralyzed by generalized Freudian anxiety (hiding Jeff's porn, fleeing the thong-wearing therapist, self-sabotaging with Lucy Lawless over his testicle injury), and Jeff the secondary narcissist who philanders without guilt, seducing Funkhouser's sister Bam-Bam and then dismissing her as crazy. Following Hegel via Jessica Benjamin, she argues both pathologies stem from failed mutual recognition—the man-child never learned to see others as subjects—and Kimmel's 'great secret of manhood' (we are afraid of other men) explains how masculine ideals produce the fear and judgment behind Larry's neurosis. Larry and Jeff's flawed but real friendship, and Leon's model of challenging Larry to be better while asking for help himself, offer glimpses of an alternative, ethically invested masculinity.
- ch. 22 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 23 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Golden Rule; Utilitarianism — Argues against the obvious reading that Larry is a rule-breaker: properly analyzed, he is a dedicated rule-follower, since a good rule-follower need only accept the legitimacy of some rules and consistently follow the ones he accepts. Bekka Williams works through cases like Rachel's ski-lift jump versus Jewish law in 'The Ski Lift,' the moving 10:30 pm phone cutoff in 'The Wire,' and Larry's rejection of his mother's tattoo-driven reburial in 'The Special Section' to distinguish breaking a rule you accept from rejecting a rule's legitimacy altogether. Kant supplies the point that duty is only visible when it frustrates self-interest (Larry absorbing the cost of his own waiting-room rule in 'The Interior Decorator,' refusing to fire the Tourette's chef), while J.J.C. Smart's attack on 'rule-worship' explains why Larry rejects purposeless rules like the drive-thru walking ban and the one-year wedding-gift cutoff. The underlying justification is the Golden Rule: the water bottle in 'The Doll,' the caviar allotment, and even the stolen memorial flowers in 'The Ida Funkhouser Roadside Memorial' are defended as fairness and reciprocity, leaving the jacket money in 'The Korean Bookie' as the single admitted counterexample.
- ch. 24 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" **·** Jacques Derrida, Theodor Adorno — Alienation — Argues that genuine gift-giving is almost entirely absent from Curb's moneyed world, building from Marcel Mauss's observation that gifts create binding obligations to reciprocate, Derrida's claim that a truly free gift is impossible, and Adorno's account of how modern capitalist society degenerates present-giving into a social function exercised with 'rational bad grace.' The failed presents make the case: the hole-ridden shirt of 'Chet's Shirt' that Ted calls 'a problem, not a gift,' Larry's self-serving freak book, Ted's status-reinstating pie in 'Denise Handicap,' and the bracelet-for-forgiveness economy of 'The Bracelet,' where gifts merely track the balance of power between Larry and Cheryl. Adorno's 'despicable parody' of marriage as a community of mutual interest is applied directly to Larry's ice-cream-for-sex strategy and the tenth-anniversary 'Mel's Offer' arrangement. The one near-authentic gift is Larry yelling profanities in solidarity with chef Guy Bernier at 'The Grand Opening,' and even that stays ambiguous because we never learn what the recipient thinks.
- ch. 25 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Martin Heidegger — Existence Precedes Essence; Bad Faith; The Look (hell is other people); Authenticity — Reads Larry as a textbook existentialist: his agonizing over trivia ('Doctor?... Pharmacist?') instantiates Heideggerian anxiety and Sartrean anguish, because every choice, even yanking his 5 wood out of a casket in 'The 5 Wood,' proclaims an image of how everyone ought to live. Since existence precedes essence there is no fixed human nature for Larry to appeal to; he must author his values alone, which the chapter says also makes him an optimist ('I'm not an inventor, but I'm an improver'). Larry dispels others' bad faith when he tells Susie she obviously can share the brownie recipe in 'The Benadryl Brownie,' and his baldness crusade, firing the toupee-wearing chef Phil Dunlap in 'The Grand Opening,' is a demand for authenticity about one's facticity. The 'Larry David Stare' is analyzed through Sartre's Look: the squinter tries to inspect another consciousness empirically and necessarily fails, since the other's freedom can never be objectified.
- ch. 27 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Authenticity; Bad Faith; Moral Relativism; Ethical Egoism — Grants that Larry looks like the Romantic ideal of the authentic self, Rousseau's amour de soi turned into candid self-expression, but argues his authenticity collapses into egoism: petty commandments about chicken skewers and beach jackets, one-strike firings like the toupee-wearing chef, and ceaseless sincerity tests. Through Sartre's categories Larry is bad faith in both directions, the 'cowardly bastard': he denies transcendence ('I can't help it') and hides behind fabricated personas, faking concern over a sick dog and exaggerating his Jewishness to the kidney consortium. Charles Taylor's The Ethics of Authenticity supplies the verdict that self-creation needs 'pre-existing horizons of significance,' so Larry's 'anything goes' authenticity is really moral relativism and justified self-interest, with Ayn Rand's virtue of selfishness as its unwanted philosophical ally. The chapter closes that only a relational authenticity, like the African ideal of ubuntu, could redeem Larry, and he lacks it, so 'maybe authenticity isn't for everyone.'
- ch. 28 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Plato, David Hume, John Locke — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime; Faith and Reason — Takes the show's title as a literal ethical command and asks which senses of enthusiasm should be curbed, tracing the concept's history: Plato's muse-inspired poet in the Ion and Apology, the early Christian Enthusiasts, Wesley and Shaftesbury's attempts to separate good from bad enthusiasm, and the Enlightenment ridicule of Locke and Hume, for whom enthusiasm meant fanaticism, a pretended insight into the supernatural that Larry is right to mock. Kant gets the most sustained treatment: enthusiasts of friendship, religion, or patriotism inflame a good principle beyond its proper degree, which explains Larry's failure as a friend, while 'aesthetic enthusiasm,' the sublime 'idea of good with affect' before the morally good, is valuable, and Larry's single moment of it comes when the Arizona preacher's sermon on moral goodness sends him flying back to Los Angeles to donate his kidney to Richard Lewis. The verdict: curb fanatic and inauthentic enthusiasm (Ted Danson's everything-is-heaven), keep ordinary resoluteness and aesthetic enthusiasm, and accept that a society needs a few Larrys, the Socratic fools who yell for society.
- ch. 30 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "Curb Your Enthusiasm and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — A reference glossary ('Curbology') defining roughly 150 Curb-isms with episode quotes, from 'ass lever' to 'yelling for society.' Many entries are miniature philosophy problems: disputed word meanings ('better' is not 'all better,' 'favor' versus 'tip,' whether a meeting held without the agenda is a meeting), property and obligation puzzles ('finders keepers,' 'loss and responsibility,' whether a refused gift retains its gifthood), and codified unwritten rules (the 'cut-off,' 'sampling privilege,' 'stop and chat,' the 'bald code,' the 'social assassin'). The glossary supplies the show's folk taxonomy of social norms, sins of omission, and fairness disputes that the book's essays then analyze philosophically.
